# Commencement of The Curia. [ Kujo Arc ]

Ammonia first, because that was what a room became after a man had been in it upright for eleven days with nowhere to go.

Under that, wet iron, thick enough to taste at the back of the throat. Under that, the sweetish rot of tissue that had been open too long in a warm room and had begun the work of dying at a different rate from the rest of him. And under all of it, faintly, incongruously, beeswax, because the frame they had hung him on had been a choir stall two months ago and somebody had not stripped the polish before they cut it down.

Aurelian Prudentius could smell his own legs.

They had taken the flesh off both thighs in long strips beginning at the hip and working down, six on the left and five on the right, each one lifted with a hooked blade and peeled with the patience of a man skinning a hare he intends to eat. The strips had been laid on the flags below him in the order of removal because Sesk was tidy. What remained was fascia, pearl-white where it had not yet dried and grey-yellow where it had, and beneath the fascia the long red bellies of the muscles running down toward the knee, and in three places the pale glimmer of the sheath where they had gone deeper than the trade required and had stopped, because killing him was against instruction.

The nails were the newer work.

Ten of them, one to each fingertip, driven in under the nail through the pulp and seated into the bone of the distal phalanx. They were not iron. Iron would have rusted and rust was a smell he knew. These drank the lamplight off their heads and gave nothing back, and the blood that had come out around them on the first day had run red for perhaps a minute and had then gone black, and the black had kept coming, and it came still, and it fell from his fingers onto the stone in a rhythm he had stopped counting on the fourth day.

The first nail had been the loudest thing that ever happened to him.

The third had been worse and he had understood why by the fifth: the hand learns. By the third finger the whole limb was braced for it, so the blow arrived into tissue that was already screaming, and the body had nothing left to give the new injury except the old one amplified. By the seventh he had begun to hear the tap of the mallet before he felt anything at all, which was a mercy for about a quarter of a second and then was not.

The nails were not the beginning. The beginning had been the mouth, on the second day, when somebody upstairs still believed this would take an afternoon: a wedge between the back teeth and a pair of drawing pliers, and four molars out of the lower jaw before Sesk arrived and put a stop to it on the grounds that a man who cannot form words is no use to anybody. The sockets had abscessed by the fifth day. They had drained on their own on the eighth and the taste of that had been in the back of his throat since, and he had stopped noticing it around the time they started on the thighs, which was the only benefit the abscesses had conferred.

They gave him water twice a day and they gave him enough. That was the part that had told him most about who was giving the instructions. A man being tortured for information is kept thirsty because thirst breaks people cheaply. A man being kept is kept hydrated. Somebody upstairs wanted him alive on a timeline that extended past the end of Sesk's patience, and Aurelian had been turning that fact over for six days and had got nothing out of it except a growing certainty that the questions were not the point of the questions.

His arms were overhead and had been for eleven days apart from the hours they took him down to work on him properly.

That was the part nobody wrote down about a hanging frame. The shoulders went first, the capsule stretching past what a capsule is for, and then the nerve bundle under the collarbone began to complain and then stopped complaining, and both hands had gone numb somewhere around the sixth day, which had been an unlooked-for kindness until they drove the nails in and he discovered that the numbness had never reached that far. And under all of it the ribs could not fall. A man hanging by the wrists inhales easily and exhales against his own weight, and so every breath out for eleven days had been work, and the work had a floor under it, and below that floor was the point where a man simply stops having the strength to exhale and drowns in a dry room.

He was a long way from that. That was the difficulty.

"Lux, lux," Aurelian said. "Veniet, veniet."

Blood came out of his mouth with it, more than the words justified, and hit the flags and spread.

"Omnis dolor abluetur. Omnia patefient, appendentur." A breath, dragged up against his own hanging weight. "Iudicabuntur."

Corwin Sesk had been sitting on an upturned crate for two hours writing nothing.

He was fifty and unremarkable in every particular, which was the qualification. Sandy going grey, a bald patch he combed nothing over, a paunch, forearms like a butcher's because that is what the trade is. Brown wool, good quality, the sleeves rolled and pinned above the elbow so the cuffs stayed clean. A leather apron he took off when he ate. He had a ledger on his knee and a lead stylus behind his ear and he had made four entries in eleven days, and three of them were about supplies.

He was not cruel. That was the thing Aurelian had established by the second day and had found genuinely disturbing. Sesk had no appetite for any of it. He had put in the nails the way a cooper puts in a hoop, checked his work, wiped the mallet head, and sat down.

*Eleven days. I have had bricklayers open in half this. He is not resisting. That is what nobody upstairs will understand when I write it. A man resisting has a shape to him, he is pushing against something, you can feel where he is holding and you go there. This one is not holding anywhere. It is like putting a chisel into water.*

"Word," said Sesk.

Aurelian's eyes came back into the room. They did that now, went and came, and the going was getting longer.

"Master Sesk."

"You have said that same line four hundred and eleven times. I have not been counting because I was told to. I have been counting because there is nothing else to count." Sesk set the ledger on the crate. "What does it mean."

"It means the light will come."

"And?"

"And all pain will be washed. And everything will be laid open and weighed." Aurelian's mouth moved into the small closed shape it made. "And judged."

"That is a threat, then."

"It is a description." Blood ran off his chin. "You are in it too, Master Sesk. That is the part people miss. It is not a curse laid on you by me. It is a statement about the structure of the room we are both standing in, and it applies to me identically, and I have considerably more to be laid open than you do."

Sesk looked at him for a while.

"That is the first thing you have said in eleven days that was not in Vulgar Church," he said. "I will have to write that one down."

He wrote it down.

Then he got up, took the mallet off the crate, and broke Aurelian's left kneecap with it.

The knee was not a question. The knee was a man who had run out of procedure.

It went with a sound like a fired pot dropped on a flag, and the leg swung, and the flayed muscle over the joint tore across its own length because there was no skin left to hold it together, and everything below the break turned outward at an angle that legs do not have.

Aurelian's whole frame convulsed on the chain and his shoulders took it and something in the right one let go with a wet pop that he heard through the bone of his jaw.

And he suspended it.

That was the honest description of what he did and there was no other. He did not endure it and he did not master it and he did not offer it up. He caught the entire arriving mass of it at the interval, in the sum-cham, in the half-beat between the event and the body's report of the event, and he held it there, unpaid, and he set it on the stack with the other eleven days.

The stack was very tall now.

It had been tall before he came here. Thirty-one years of it, everything from twenty-seven onward, and the bottom of it was a thing he had never written down and could no longer clearly remember, and the whole edifice released from the bottom on a schedule nobody had ever explained to him.

The lamp on the wall bracket stood up straight and thinned to a blue thread.

Every surface within a yard of him went to the temperature of a hand held too long against skin. The film of blood on the flags under his feet stopped being wet at the edges. The old choir-stall wood at his back gave off a smell of hot beeswax, and somewhere behind Sesk a rat that had been living in the corner for six days went out under the door and did not come back.

Sesk stepped away and put a hand up in front of his face.

"That again," he said. "You do that every time and you do not scream. I have taken a knee off a Dawi hammerman and he screamed until he was sick. What is it you are doing."

"I am deferring it."

"To when."

"I do not know."

*And that is the answer and it is the truth and he will not believe it, because everybody assumes a man who can put a thing off has chosen when to pick it up.*

They took him down after the knee.

Two of them, unhurried, one at each armpit, and the chain ran out through the ring in the ceiling and his own weight came off his shoulders for the first time in nine hours and the relief of it was so total and so obscene that he made a sound he had not made in eleven days. They laid him on his side on the flags in his own blood while Sesk washed the mallet head in a bucket. Nobody spoke. One of them lifted the broken leg by the ankle and set it straight with a professional disinterest that Aurelian understood, half a moment later, was so that the knee would knit crooked rather than not at all, because a joint that heals wrong can be broken again in the same place and a joint that has come apart entirely cannot.

Then they put him back up.

The bells started again around the second hour.

There were no bells beneath the Grand Church at Altherion. He had established that on the third day when he still had the coherence to establish things. What he heard was on the other side, coming through where his attention had worn the membrane thin from leaning on it, and it was not a peal and it was not a tolling. It was the small flat sound of a handbell being rung once, at irregular intervals, the way a bell is rung in an orchard to tell the pickers to move down a row.

And under it, the pruning.

That was the sound he had no account for and had stopped trying to build one. Blades. Two of them, crossing, closing on green wood and cutting through it clean, and then a pause of the correct length for a hand to move to the next stem, and then again. Somebody was working a very large garden very carefully and had been doing it for eleven days without once hurrying and without once resting, and each time the shears closed Aurelian felt something in the room's ambient give way and reseat slightly differently, the way a floor does when someone walks across it two rooms away.

He had spent thirty-one years reading the Plane of Fate and had never in that time heard anyone maintaining it.

He would have given a great deal to know what was being cut back.

"Lux," he said. "Lux."

His head had gone down onto his own chest. He got it up.

"Veniet. Veniet."

The four Attestants were alive. That was the arrangement and he had accepted the arrangement with his eyes open and had walked here on his own feet, and the arrangement was that he stayed and they lived and the scriptorium remained a scriptorium. He held that fact the way a man holds a rail. Four people and a room full of books, against one fifty-eight-year-old body that had already been running on borrowed thermodynamics for three decades. It was arithmetic. It was good arithmetic. He had checked it twice on the road and once in the cell.

"Omnis dolor abluetur."

He could have taken the block apart on the first morning. He could take it apart now, one-legged, ten-nailed, with a dislocated shoulder, in about four seconds, and he knew the number because he had worked it out on the second day and had not permitted himself to work it out again since.

That was the whole discipline. Not the pain.

"Omnia patefient. Appendentur."

Sesk had gone back to his crate and picked up the ledger and was looking at the one line he had written.

*He is going to outlast me. That is the thing I am going to have to put in the report and I do not yet know how to word it so that it does not end my contract. Whatever they think they are extracting, it is not in there, and I have opened him up far enough to see most of what is.*

"Iudicabuntur," said Aurelian.

The blood came off his fingertips in ten black threads, ten separate small sounds on the stone, out of sequence with each other, none of them keeping time.

Out in the corridor a door was unbarred, and boots came four paces closer, and stopped, and stood there a while, and did not come any nearer.

Aurelian Prudentius hung on the chain in the heat coming off his own body and set his teeth against the inside of his cheek and put the knee onto the stack a second time, because the first suspension had begun to slip, and started the line again from the beginning.

The hum was the first thing now.

It had been in the flags the whole fourteen days and he had lost it on the second, when it became his blood, and it had come back to him on the twelfth, when Sesk stopped working and nothing was left in the cell loud enough to stand in front of it. Altherion drinking. The Cloister Ward drawing on the Well under the Church through the night, through sealed conduit somewhere behind the wall at his back, a long low note with a grain in it and once in a great while, far overhead, the click of a gauge-housing taking up a notch.

Under the hum, ammonia. Under the ammonia, the sweetness that had come into the wet-iron smell on the twelfth day when the strips of his thighs on the floor stopped being meat and became something else, a smell that belonged to the bindery at Alabaster, hide-glue and old leather, and he had not decided whether that was a mercy. The fascia over the muscles had dried to the texture of vellum. When he moved on the chain it spoke.

The frame at his back had been the third stall on the decani side. He had known that on the first day from the wear on the armrest under his left shoulder, a hollow the depth of a thumb where two hundred years of boys had put their elbows through the long readings, and the polish in the hollow was the only part of the stall that still gave off beeswax. The choir had not sung since the shutdown. Nobody left in the building knew the settings, and the stall that had held the trebles was a frame in a cellar with a man on it, and it would not go back up.

The hum, and the smell, and the shears.

They had not stopped. Fourteen days, indefatigable, on the far side of the membrane where his attention had worn it thin, two blades crossing on green wood and the pause for the hand to move and the blades again, and the small flat bell once in a while telling the pickers to move down a row. He had stopped asking what was being cut. He listened to it for company, the clock in a strange house.

Corwin Sesk had not touched him since the knee.

That had been the eleventh day. On the twelfth Sesk had come down the stair with his apron off and folded over his arm, and had hung it on the nail by the door, and had sat on his crate, and had not put it on since. He fed him. He watered him twice a day from a tin cup with a bent lip, holding it to Aurelian's mouth at the angle that let the water go past the sockets. He took the chain up each morning and let it out each night so the shoulders could be put back. He had made one more entry in the ledger and had not said what it was.

He had said, on the thirteenth day, without being asked, that the arrangement stood.

"Five hundred and ninety-one," Sesk said, to the ledger.

Aurelian's eyes came back into the room.

"Master Sesk."

"Since the eleventh. I stopped writing the others down. I keep this one because it is the only figure in this building that goes up honestly." He turned the stylus once between two fingers. "You said it in your sleep on the twelfth. Twice. I did not count those."

"That was correct of you."

"I have been correct for fourteen days," said Sesk, "and I would give a year's fee to know what it has bought."

The flags under the frame lit.

The lamp on its bracket burned exactly as before, a yellow tongue, unchanged. This came up through the stone. Lines first, thin as a hair laid on a page, running out from under the choir-stall frame in a geometry that had no business under a cellar, curving, meeting, closing on themselves, and the light in them was the colour of the inside of a bell that has just been struck. It ran under the eleven strips on the floor and did not disturb them. It ran under Sesk's crate. It reached the wall behind the lamp and climbed it a hand's width and stopped, and the beeswax in the frame at Aurelian's back began, very faintly, to smell warm.

Under the lines, the hum changed key.

Sesk was on his feet. The ledger had gone off his knee onto the flags and lay open with its one line up and he had not looked at it, which in fourteen days Aurelian had not seen him fail to do.

"Fuck." He backed one pace and his heel found the crate and he did not look down at that either. "What. What the fuck did you do."

Aurelian laughed.

It came up out of the sockets first, wet, and then out of his chest, and it hurt in the shoulder that had been put back twice and in the ribs the chain had been resting his whole weight on since the evening, and it brought blood off his lower lip in a thread that ran and hung and fell into the light on the floor and went black in it. He had no idea why he was laughing. Five hundred and ninety-one, and he had said the line into a cellar for fourteen days the way a man drops a stone down a well to hear whether it has a bottom, and here was the light coming up the wall. The word had done it. He had said it enough and it had come.

"Lux," he said, and had to stop, and get his head up. "Lux. Veniet. Veniet."

Sesk had his back against the wall by the door.

"Omnis dolor abluetur." The lines were brightening. "Omnia patefient. Appendentur."

Out in the corridor, boots.

He knew the boots. Fourteen days of them, the guard's, arriving at the second hour and the sixth and the tenth, four paces short of the door, standing, going. These did not stop at four paces. They came to the door. The bar went up on the far side, badly, a hand in a hurry on the other end of it, and the door came in, and the corridor lamp behind it threw a shape onto the floor of the cell before it threw a man.

Tall once. Bent a little at the top now, the shoulders folded forward over the chest. A staff in the right hand, taller than the shape holding it, its head a thickening the lamp behind could not get through. A cap. The shape stood a moment in the door with the light of the corridor at its back and the light of the floor in front of it, and Aurelian, who had been reading the Plane of Fate for thirty-one years, felt the room's attention go to it before he had decided to give it, and go all at once, the way a congregation stands.

"Iudicabuntur," said Aurelian, because it was the end of the line and he did not stop lines in the middle.

Verinus came in.

He had dressed for the Curia. That was the first thing. The Palatine's gold, the long robe of the Alabaster Court that Aurelian had seen four times in his life and never on this man, who wore a plain coat to his own Curia and let the bench mutter about it, and it was on him now, hem to throat, and it had been put on by somebody else, because the collar sat wrong and Verinus had never in nineteen years let a collar sit wrong. Gold thread going dull at the cuffs where an earlier Palatine's wrists had worn it. The cap on top of it all, the plain grey wool one, which was absurd, and which was the only thing in the door that Aurelian entirely recognised.

Seventy-one. Hair the white of a rope's end that has been in salt water, cropped close under the cap. A long narrow face gone to vertical lines, the nose broken once a long time ago and set true, the mouth a straight line with nothing spare in it. Brown eyes. Big-knuckled hands, the right one round the staff, the fingers of it chalked to the second knuckle because he had been on the slope again, or at the basin, and had come here without washing, and had done that for six years and would do it until he died. The left knee was recalcitrant. He did not let it show, and it showed.

The staff was gold through, the metal itself or a thing that had agreed to be the metal, and it had a head on it that Aurelian could not get his eyes to hold still on, and it did not shine in the lamp. It shone in the floor's light, which went up its length along the lines of the grain, and the lines were moving.

Sesk had come off the wall. He bowed his head and held it two breaths and came up.

"My lord."

"You are the contractor."

"Corwin Sesk, my lord. Under instruction."

"I know whose." Verinus looked at the frame, and at the strips on the floor in the order they had been laid, and at the ledger open on the flags with its one line, and his face did the thing Aurelian had watched it do across a table at Alabaster on the four occasions they had sat at one, which was nothing whatsoever, because a face that softens at a reading is a lie about what the reading cost. "You kept a ledger."

"Six entries, my lord. Half of them supplies."

"Then you are the only man in this building who wrote down what he did. Stand where you are. Do not touch him. Do not touch me. Whatever you were promised for this, it does not cover the next quarter of an hour, and I will not be in a position to make up the difference."

"My lord. There is a man at the head of the stair with a written instruction that nobody comes down it."

"There was," Verinus said.

He did not explain and Sesk did not ask. The boots that had stood four paces short of that door three times a day for fourteen days stayed wherever they had gone, and in the corridor there was only the corridor.

*They will have taken the roll by now. They will have found the seat empty and the staff gone from its stand and the robe gone from the press, and somebody will have said the word abdication in a voice pitched to carry, and the Bishop will have said nothing, which is the single most useful thing he has ever done for me. Let it sit. It has sat without me in every way that mattered for nine years. There is one invoice in my drawer with my name on the bottom of it and it is nineteen years overdue and I am the last signatory still breathing.*

He came to the frame. He did not hurry, and the knee would not have let him. He stopped at the edge of the strips, at the first one, the one from the left hip, and looked down at it, and moved his foot so that the heel of the staff came down on the flag between the first strip and the second, and the light in the floor went to it and up it.

"Aurelian."

"Holiness."

"You were never a man who used that word to me."

"You were never a man who wore the robe." He could taste the sockets. "It is a strange morning."

"It is the fourteenth." Verinus's eyes went over him, hip to knee, finger to finger, the shoulder, the mouth, and Aurelian watched him count and watched him write nothing down. "They took the teeth first."

"Before Master Sesk. He stopped it."

"I have had nothing from you since the shutdown and nothing from the men who hold this Church, and I did not need it, because I have watched this order buy the same thing four times in nineteen years and I know the shape of the receipt. There is an arrangement."

"There is."

"Four people and a room of books."

Aurelian said nothing. Sesk, against the wall, said nothing, and the lines in the floor came up another degree.

"I am not going to ask you what you paid," Verinus said. "I can see what you paid. I am going to tell you what you have been paying with, because you have never let me say it to your face and I have come a long way on this knee to say it. Thirty-one years. You found the interval at twenty-seven and you have been standing in it since, putting everything on the stack and calling the stack devotion, and the Curia wrote it down as devotion, and I signed the minute." His voice did not rise. Nothing in the room rose. "I have watched three men do it before you and they are all in the day-book. A body finds a thing it does not have to pay for and it uses that thing until it stops working, and the thing always stops working, and it stops in front of whoever is standing nearest."

"It is deferred."

The flags went down.

Under the frame, under Sesk's crate, under Verinus's boots, the whole floor of the cell went down the width of a coin and stayed there, and the hum in the stone took the drop and came back a half-tone lower, and somewhere above them, a long way above, a thing that had never in fourteen days made a sound came up audible for the length of a breath and settled.

"That weighed," said Verinus, very quietly. "You believe it. I have never once in forty-five years told a man his sentence weighed when it did not, and it did. It is also the sentence I came here to answer, because deferred means that somebody pays later, and the somebody was never going to be you. It was always going to be whoever stood next to you when the bottom came out." He shifted his grip on the staff. "I have decided that it is going to be me. I have brought the paperwork."

"It is my arithmetic."

"It was. I have audited it. Four people and a room of books is a good number, and it was good on the road and it was good in this cell and I would have taken it myself at fifty-eight. I am seventy-one. I have more to put up than you have and less time to hold it, and a Court that will call whatever I do this morning devotion and put a window in for it inside a generation, which is the one outcome I have spent my life preventing and which I am, this once, going to permit." He looked at the staff. "From this hour the surety is the Palatine's. Your four and your room. This Church will find that I have signed for them in a form it cannot strike from a minute, and I want you to understand that I did it with my eyes open, and that this is worth exactly nothing to you, and that it is the only distinction I have left."

Sesk, against the wall, put his hand flat on the stone behind him.

*He is buying them. He is standing in a cellar in his Curia robe buying four people who went out through the low cloister nine days before I stood in this room and told him they were alive, and I have the roster in my coat. If I open my mouth now, whatever the old man is about to do, he does it for nothing and knows it. If I keep it shut he does it for nothing and does not. Twenty-two years. I have never in my life wanted a written instruction as badly as I want one now.*

"Do you consent," said Verinus.

"No."

"Entered."

And he began.

He had read the Plane of Fate for thirty-one years, and he read it now, once, because the old man's strings were doing a thing he had no word for in either tongue.

Verinus's threads were going into the staff and the staff's were coming into him, laid up together, turning, and the turn had a centre, and the centre was black. Present black. The black of a thing that is there, and is becoming more there, and is drawing every string the old man owned down into itself one turn at a time. Aurelian had seen a man's fate cut. He had seen it burned through and he had seen it knotted and he had seen it, once, in a bishop's household, snipped with something that had wanted to be called malice. He had never seen one wound onto a spool. It was a death being written, in the old man's own hand, and there was no other reading of it and he did not look for one.

On the far side, the shears stopped.

Fourteen days. Two blades on green wood and the hand moving and the blades again, and they stopped, mid-stroke, and the whole ambient of the room went still in a way it had not been still in fourteen days, and the bell rang, flat, once, and did not ring again.

He did not know what that was. He would have given the last of himself to know what that was.

"Hoc sit sacramentum."

The lines in the floor answered. Three marks came up in them under the frame, where nothing had been drawn: the devotion mark, the mark for structure, the third one the Rubric called binding ascent and the bindery boys called the ladder, and Aurelian had seen all three in ink a thousand times and never once lit.

"Hic sit ignis sanctus indignorum."

Let this be the sacrament. Let this be the holy fire of the undeserving. Church Latin, the Court's Latin, every ending exact, and the old man saying it in the voice he used to read a lesson, a voice that had not been raised in nineteen years and had not needed to be, and the hum in the stone climbing under it.

"Haec sit mors."

Let this be death.

The hum climbed. The Cloister Ward had been drinking through the night, and now the Church was drinking the ward, and the note that lived in the flags went up through the register, a grain in it, then a grinding, then a pitch, and the pitch was the sound of a thing at the edge of what it was made for, and overhead, a long way up, a gauge-housing somewhere in the Guild-sealed dark cracked, with the sound of a bell rung too hard in a frost.

Then the silence.

Every child in Altherion had heard it described, what happens to the sound of the city when a ward is cut off. The hum went out of the stone the way a heartbeat goes out of a hand. It did not fade. It stopped, and the flags were only flags, cold under the frame, and up every standpipe in the ward the water that had been coming stopped coming, and Aurelian had no way to see that and knew it anyway.

"Nam hic est ritus sacramenti," said Verinus. "Actus veritatis."

For this is the rite of Sacrament. The act of the truth.

And the Well came up.

It was never water, the thing under Altherion. It came up through the flags where the light had been, in a spiral, turning the opposite way from the black on the far Plane, and it was the colour of a thing on the Plane of Fate that Aurelian had been taught at nineteen never to look at directly, and he looked. The Well's own nature, the thing the Guild metered and the ward drank and nobody had ever seen, drawn up out of the ground in a column with a turn in it, and where it reached the old man's gold, it caught.

Fire. Gold fire, standing off the robe, off the staff, off the chalked hand, and it did not burn. It cracked. The air round Verinus went to fracture, a hundred jagged lines opening in it with light in the lines, the pattern of ice going under weight, of a pane struck at the centre, and the fractures were gold, and they were spreading, and the old word for it came up out of Aurelian's thirty-one years of reading before he could stop it. Judger's Flame. The old word, which had named the offering before it named the light. The Court had not lit it in three hundred years because the Court had ruled it too expensive, and here it was on a seventy-one-year-old man with a bad knee in a cellar under Altherion, and it was pulling the Well up through the floor to feed itself, and the Well was coming.

Nineteen years the Alabaster Court had seated him as the weakest Palatine in its rolls. That was the Court's own phrase and Aurelian had heard it at four tables and had not contradicted it, because it was true: a man whose entire craft was that a floor sank when somebody told him a true thing, who had expressed force so seldom that each occasion had a name. The Measurewrights called the axis Ardency and the Court kept a figure for his, and the figure was the lowest ever entered against a Palatine's name, and the man himself had read it aloud at his own investiture and asked that it be minuted. Nobody had asked what the old man did with rites, because a rite is a thing the world does, and a man only has to know the address.

Verinus rose.

The heel of the staff left the flag. His boots left the flag, a hand's width, then two, the bad knee straightening out under the hem as the weight came off it, and he hung there in the fracture-light with his cap on.

"Solvere," he said. "Solvere."

Be loosed. Be loosed.

"Et reconciliare."

And be reconciled.

"Discedite," Verinus said, and for the first time in nineteen years his voice was not level, "omnes iniquitates tuae."

Depart, all your iniquities.

The wounds went across the room.

The mouth first, because the mouth had been first. Verinus's jaw dropped a fraction and his cheeks hollowed and he made a sound, low, in the chest, a sound Aurelian knew from the inside, and then he leaned his head forward in the air and spat, and blood came out of his mouth in a sheet and four teeth came out with it and rang on the flags, four separate small sounds, out of sequence, none of them keeping time, and Aurelian's tongue went to the back of his own jaw and found gum. Closed over, no teeth in it, gum where the abscess had been, smooth and wrong and whole.

The thighs. The gold of the robe went dark from the hip on the left side in a line, and then a second line beside it, and then the right, six and five, in the order of removal, and the cloth did what cloth does over a wound that has depth. It went in. It sagged into the absence of him. And on the frame the fascia over Aurelian's own legs, that had dried to vellum, was wet, and then was skin. Hairless, pale, too smooth, the wrong skin, laid over muscle that had not been covered in nine days, and the eleven strips of him on the floor lay where Sesk had set them and did not move. The flesh stayed taken. The wound had gone across the room, and somebody else was wearing the taking now.

The fingers. Black came off Verinus's fingertips in ten threads and fell into the light.

The shoulder, a wet pop, the right side, and the old man's arm swung, and he did not lower his head.

The knee. The sound of a fired pot dropped on a flag, and the left leg under the hem went out sideways at the angle legs do not have, and it was his bad knee, the one that had complained on every cold wall in the Alabaster Court for ten years, and he did not go down, because there was nothing under him to go down to.

And then the thing Aurelian had not put on any list, because he did not have a name for it.

The stack moved.

Thirty-one years. Everything from twenty-seven onward, held at the interval, unpaid, and the bottom of it, the thing he had never written down and could no longer remember, that released on a schedule nobody had ever explained to him. It shifted. Something at the bottom of him that had been there since he was twenty-seven years old came off the floor of him and went across the room, and he felt it go, and he could not have said what it was. He would never be able to say what it was now. It had been the thing under everything, and it was under nothing, and he was resting on whatever had been under it.

On the far Plane the old man's strings went into the black at the centre of the spiral, all of them, in one turn.

Verinus's eyes were open.

Brown, and then, from the inner ring outward, gold, a warm dense metallic gold that came up through the brown and held, and behind it a light that belonged to no lamp in that building, and the fracture stood off him in every direction now, and off his shoulders, from the blades of them, in two sheets, high, that caught the Well's turning column and split it and threw it, and every painter who had ever been given a wall in Altherion had put that shape on it, and had called it wings, and had been wrong about what it was and right about the shape.

The gold of the robe was dark to the knee on both sides and shining above it, and the cap was still on.

Aurelian's feet were on the floor.

He had not felt the chain let go. The ring in the ceiling had let it go and the weight had come off his shoulders and gone down through two straight legs into two whole feet, and ten nails, seated in nothing now, came out of ten fingers under their own weight and rang on the stone, out of sequence, none of them keeping time. He put his weight on the flags. All of it, for the first time in fourteen days, and the stone under him took it and went down the width of a coin, and stayed down.

Kujo breathed in slowly as the Crevice fused deeper into him.

For the first time since tearing it from Valmir, the breath did not feel like an imitation of life. Something ancient moved with it. The black-gold fracture he had carried no longer hovered outside his body as an extracted metaphysical organ; it had been driven inward, past flesh, past soul, past every layer through which Kujo normally expressed the Void. Beneath his pale chest, a vertical seam of blackness veined with molten gold appeared for a moment before sinking deeper, finding the point where his existence touched absence itself.

The reaction was immediate. His back arched as the chamber convulsed around him, black tendrils erupting from his shadow and striking the walls while crimson channels beneath his skin illuminated one after another. The Void around him did not simply deepen. It became unstable, carrying a principle it had never naturally possessed: collapse, transition, the violent interval between structure and failure. Valmir’s ancient authority poured through Kujo in waves, but there was no Titan left to insist that ruin eventually become renewal.

Kujo exhaled, and the floor beneath his feet fractured. The cracks did not spread in any ordinary way. Stone weakened before the fractures reached it, support beginning to fail before force had actually arrived. Dust rose from the seams, only to lose cohesion halfway into the air and scatter into grey particles. Kujo watched it with widening interest as the Crevice taught the Void how to destroy without needing to erase.

“So this was what you were protecting.”

His voice doubled halfway through the sentence. One layer remained the familiar controlled register he had always worn, while something deeper dragged beneath it, rougher and less stable, as though another mouth were speaking from somewhere behind the words.

“Renewal.”

A smile slowly spread across his face.

“What an obscene waste.”

The Crevice flashed beneath his sternum. For an instant Kujo could feel everything Valmir had once meant by collapse: the mountain breaking so another landscape might rise, the dead star feeding what followed, the ruined structure surrendering itself to the possibility of a future shape. Destruction had always been permitted because something else would inherit the aftermath. Even death had been made charitable.

Kujo’s fingers curled against his chest.

“Always the apology afterward.”

The darkness around him thickened as his smile widened further.

“Always something new crawling out of the corpse so existence can congratulate itself for surviving.”

He laughed then, and the sound was different from the restrained amusement he usually allowed himself. It was harsher, more fractured, touched by genuine delight. His long black hair lifted around him as though caught in a current that did not exist, and his vast wings unfurled violently behind his back. The elegant human shape he preferred began straining against what now moved beneath it, as though the body itself was becoming too polite for the thing inside.

The Crevice continued integrating with his Void architecture. Destruction and absence remained separate principles, but now they could overlap. The Void could remove, deny, sever, and refuse continuation; the Crevice could make something remain long enough to experience its own deterioration. Kujo understood immediately what that meant. Kingdoms could stand while their foundations became increasingly expensive to preserve. Steel could retain its shape while slowly losing fidelity. Wards could continue functioning while drifting from the laws they were meant to uphold. Healing could still work, but cost more each time. Memory could survive while becoming less reliable with every retelling.

“No more.”

The word shook the chamber.

Kujo raised one hand and gathered a sphere of Void above his palm. Thin veins of molten gold appeared inside it, twisting through the darkness without harmonizing with it. The sphere collapsed inward, rebuilt itself incorrectly, collapsed again, and each time returned with less structural fidelity than before. Kujo watched it happen with an expression that became almost fascinated.

“Beautiful.”

His voice came out lower now, more pleased than controlled. The Crevice was not merely increasing the volume of his power; it was giving the Void a new way to express the worldview Kujo had always carried. Things did not need to vanish immediately. They could be made to fail slowly, completely, and while fully aware of what was happening to them.

Then one name came to him.

“Alabaster.”

The sphere vanished from his hand.

The Holy Sea. The white shelf. The institutions built on ancient resonance they only partially understood. Sanctuaries of testimony, judgment, consecration, and inherited spiritual authority. Places where civilization had convinced itself that holiness and order were foundations rather than maintained arrangements.

And above all, the churches of the Judger.

Kujo’s smile slowly disappeared. What replaced it was worse: a stare sharpened by purpose and made unstable by the power now moving through him.

“I have tolerated temples that teach mortals consequence as though consequence belongs to them.”

He stepped forward, and the stone beneath his bare foot darkened. Fine fractures radiated outward, glowing gold for a breath before turning pitch black.

“I have tolerated sanctuaries where men kneel beneath borrowed judgment and call the act righteousness.”

His wings spread farther behind him.

“I have tolerated Alabaster.”

The final word emerged through several overlapping voices.

Kujo began laughing again, this time without attempting to restrain it. The sound carried through the palace in broken layers, beautiful at first and increasingly wrong the farther it traveled. Lesser Void entities beyond the chamber went still. Servants lowered their heads without being commanded. Even the surrounding darkness seemed to recoil slightly from the being at its center, because Kujo was no longer merely carrying a stolen Titan mechanism. He was beginning to make catastrophe part of himself.

“Let them ring every bell.”

His voice twisted at the end, almost breaking into another laugh.

“Let every priest of the Judger stand before his altar. Let them open every scripture. Invoke every covenant. Measure every sin.”

Kujo spread his fingers slowly as the gold-black seam beneath his chest opened again. For one terrible instant, the chamber behind him ceased looking like architecture and resembled an endless vertical wound stretching into impossible depth. Within it came the distant impression of continents cracking, towers losing their foundations, cities sagging without understanding why, and old structures failing one principle at a time.

“Judge me.”

The word came out almost eagerly.

Kujo shuddered as the Crevice answered him like a second heartbeat. His eyes widened, and the last clean edges of his composure continued to fray.

“Yes.”

His smile returned.

“Do that.”

He stepped halfway into the opening while darkness climbed over his body and his wings remained spread behind him like a silhouette against the wound.

“Stand in your white halls and tell the world what is permitted.”

The gold within the Crevice pulsed violently.

“Then watch your holy city discover that permission has nothing to do with survival.”

For an instant Kujo’s human form failed completely. The beautiful pale face disappeared beneath the truth of him: black and grey armored flesh, ribbed sinew, exposed crimson channels, the horned crown of his monstrous head, masses of segmented tendrils unfurling from his back, and the vast execution limb twitching as gold fractures crawled through the abomination. Then the courtesy returned, but imperfectly. The eyes remained too dark, the smile too wide, and the voice too layered.

“Alabaster first.”

He looked toward the distant Material World.

“Every holy church of the Judger after.”

A tremor moved through the Void around him.

“And when their bells stop ringing…” Kujo drew another deep breath, filling lungs that did not need air while the Crevice surged inside him. “…I will teach the rest of this world what Valmir was too merciful to understand.”

His wings snapped fully open, and the surrounding darkness ruptured outward.

“Destruction does not need to lead anywhere.”

Kujo smiled into the fracture before him.

“It only needs to finish.”

Such meaningless words.

He said it and heard it leave him and go out and keep going, and there was nothing for it to come back off. Nineteen years of a building that returned his voice to him a half-second late and doubled, and he had built a whole method on that half-second, and it was gone, and the sentence went out into a distance with no far side and was still going.

The cell had stopped being anywhere.

He had felt it happen and had been unable to attend to it. The Flame had taken the choir stall first, and then the flags, and then the strips of Aurelian on the flags, and then the walls of a room that had been a chapter store since the reign before last, and it took none of it violently. The stone went pale, and then went fine, and then went. Dust off a dry hand. The rock under the Cloister Ward that had held up a church for four hundred years turned out to have no argument against purity at all, and the Flame did not push it down. It found nothing in it that had a right to continue and it stopped continuing, and Verinus, in the middle of that, kept standing because there was nothing left to stand on and therefore nothing left to fall through.

Ash on his tongue. Beeswax in it, which was the stall, and cold lime, which was the mortar, and under both the sweet dead smell of the leg-strips going, and he shut his mouth on all three and could not stop tasting them.

Then the Song.

It came up under him where the draw-hum had been, low in the sternum, and it had no source and no direction. Voices. A great many, matched, singing with each other and never at each other, holding a chord that had one more voice in it than a chord can carry, and the extra voice went through his chest and out the other side of him and he had to put his hand flat against his breastbone to keep himself from following it.

The priests at Altherion had a name for this. They put it in the mouths of the dying and in the ninth-year sermons and on four walls that Verinus had walked past every winter of his working life. The Song of Grace, given to the Judger's most devoted, and every man who had ever claimed to hear it had been sanctified inside a generation.

He heard it. He declined the name.

*A servant hears a song and calls it a reward. I have never in my life been the Judger's servant, and I have said so in front of two Curias and had it struck from the minute both times, and I will say it here where there is no clerk to strike it. A servant can resign. A servant can be dismissed and can be forgiven and can be sent home to his village to keep bees. I am not the man in the room with the Judger. I am the part of him that does the weighing, and a hand does not serve an arm.*

The Song went up a degree.

And the fourteen were in it. That was the thing the walls at Altherion had left out, and the sermons, and the ninth-year readings, and Verinus heard it and understood at seventy-one what he had been getting written out of minutes since he was fifty-two. The chord had Urion in it, and Urion alone would have been a single note held forever, which is a tone and not a song. Selhar tuned it. Maelor held what had already been sung so the next part had somewhere to stand. Auren ended the phrases, because a phrase that will not end is a scream. Kaetra put in the false interval that made the true one land. Uurgath took the whole thing apart at the turn so that it could be something else on the far side of the bar.

Balance was the whole instrument sounding, and a man who had spent his life weighing had been given one string and told it was the choir.

They would have had him on a bench for that at fifty-two. They would have had him on a bench for it this morning.

His hands were wrong.

He looked down at them because the Song had gone into his forearms and the skin there was moving. The chalk went first. Second knuckle down, both hands, six years of coming straight from the slope and the basin and forgetting to wash, gone off the skin that had been holding it. Then the liver-marks under it. Then the long ropes of tendon standing off the backs of his hands settled down into flesh that had no need to show its working, and the joints of his fingers, which had been swollen since a winter in his fifties and had made him give up the small chalk and take the large, came down smooth and narrow and quick.

He turned them over. Somebody else's palms. No callus at the base of the second finger where forty-five years of holding a stylus had built one.

The teeth came in.

That was worse than the knee and he had not expected it to be. Four sockets in the lower jaw, blown out that morning by a rite that took Aurelian's fourteen days in the order Sesk had removed them, and the bone in them went hot and then went tight and then the crowns came up through the gum with a pressure that had a sound to it in the skull, four separate small grinding arrivals, and his eyes ran and he could do nothing about that at all.

The left knee reassembled. The kneecap that had gone on the flags of a cellar that morning, and the older ruin under it, the one that had complained on every cold wall in the Alabaster Court for ten years and had made him take the second step instead of the alabaster chair, both went. The joint straightened and stayed straightened and there was no ache in it, anywhere, in any position, and Verinus stood in an infinite nothing with a knee that had stopped talking to him and missed the ache the way a man misses a dog.

The robe hung heavy and dark from both hips where eleven strips of thigh had been paid across a room. The cloth stayed dark. Underneath it the flesh came back, and the fascia over it, and skin, and the pattern of hair on it, and none of it hurt.

His hair was black.

It came down over his eye, which was a thing that had happened to him last at twenty-six, and he pushed it back with a hand that had no chalk on it and no callus and put his cap on straight, and the cap was too loose, because a skull at seventy-one is a different object.

*Twenty-five. Twenty-six. I know the hands. I have not seen these hands since the year I was made a Lector, and I remember what I thought with them, and I am not going to be permitted to think it again.*

There was a word for this and he had heard it four hundred times from the pulpit and had refused it four hundred times in writing. Apotheosis. The Court would use it before the week was out. Somebody would put a window in.

He had exactly one hour of use for it and the hour had started.

"Such meaningless words," Verinus said again, to the wound.

He said it flatly, in the voice he used to read a lesson from the low lectern on an ordinary Fifthday, and the Song carried it, and it went out into a place where a great deal was listening.

"You have spent your speech on renewal. On mercy, and the obscenity of a corpse being made to feed something. It is a good speech. I have heard four versions of it in this life from men holding considerably less than you are holding, and every one of them made the same error, and I am going to name it once and then I am going to stop being polite about the Titan."

The Song under him took a step down the register.

"I pray for no renewal. Understand that plainly, because it is the last plain thing I am likely to say. I do not want the Titan loosed. I do not want the Titan restored, or repaired, or fed, or given a shape on the far side of what you did to him. I do not ask for him to be freed and I do not ask for him to escape, and I have no interest in whether he was merciful. I have read the account. I have signed the appendix that says the account is incomplete."

His right hand had come up, palm out, without instruction from him.

"I want the wrath."

Golden fracture opened along the underside of his forearm and ran to the wrist and did not close.

"I can feel it. It is arriving in me at this moment, and it is not a doctrine and it is not the Court's Scale and there is nothing measured in it whatsoever. It is raw. He is furious, and the fury has no procedure in it, and it has come down the same line the Song came down and it is in my chest with my own. His hatred is my hatred. I have spent nineteen years insisting to a Curia that Urion is a function and does not feel, and I was wrong in the minute and I would like that entered as well."

The fracture reached his elbow.

"You will defeat me in power. That has been true since I was nineteen years old and it has been true of nearly every man I have ever stood in a room with. The Court kept a figure for me and the figure was the lowest ever set against a Palatine's name, and I read it aloud at my own investiture and asked that it be minuted, because I have never wanted a single soul in that building to be confused about what they had appointed."

The gold went into his shoulder.

"What I have instead is will. My soul is spoken for. My fate is spoken for. Neither is available to be bargained with, because neither of them is mine to bring to the table, and there is nothing you can offer a man who is already the property of the thing he loves."

The fracture crossed his back and both shoulder blades opened.

"So kill me. Erase me. Take the soul apart and file the pieces where they cannot find each other. It costs you nothing to do and I am not going to pretend it will be difficult." The hair was in his eye again. He left it. "And out of the flame of Urion I will come back up. In pieces, and late, from under whatever you leave, and I will keep arriving, and you will have to do it again, and the second time it will be more expensive than the first."

The wings came out of him.

Sheets. Two of them, high, off the blades of his shoulders, gold and cracked through with a hundred jagged lines that had light standing in the lines, and they were the same fracture that had been on him in the cellar and there was more of it now and it had found a shape to be. No feather. No pinion. Every painter given a wall at Altherion had put this on it and had called it wings and had been wrong about the object and right about the outline, and Verinus, who was twenty-six years old and had a cap that no longer fitted, understood at last that the painters had been copying somebody who saw it.

"I am Verinus," he said. "Sixty-fifth Palatine of the Holy Sea of Alabaster. I am going to be the proof of what we are, and I am going to be it here, and the record of it will be a rock in a field that nobody can lift."

He put his hand out.

There is a part of the Void that is adjoined to the thing that rules it, and there is a part that is only Void, unadministered, running on nothing, and the second is the larger. Verinus took the second in his hand.

He had no force to apply. He had never had any. What he had was thirty-one years of knowing what a rite is: an address, a bill, an order of operations, and a thing in the world that is already doing the work and only wants telling where.

He told it where.

The pressure went out of him in gold and it was not a blast and it did not travel. It arrived, everywhere it was going, at once, and the unadjoined Void felt it and had no procedure for being weighed. Reality bent downward. Spirals, wide as counties, turning down and inward with the patience of water finding a drain that has been shut a long time, and in the trough of each spiral something came into being that had been missing, and Hataraki was doing it, the Work at its barest, function performing the labour it was made for and asking nobody.

The missing pieces were made. The Void was completed.

And what is completed has weight, and weight falls.

They came down across the whole world. Black stones, none of them larger than a loaf, matte, taking the light and giving nothing back. One went into the Sound off Alabaster and the herring boat that saw it come down put about and did not report it. One landed in a Kharven woodpile at the thin end of the season and cracked the night-stone beside it and the household stood in the doorway and did not go near it. One came through the roof of a chapter hold in the Old World, and one into a salt street at Deepvein where the watch had entered a tremor and gone back to bed, and one into a physic garden that Verinus had walked in nine days ago, and thousands more, into fields, into water, into the middle of a road, into places where nobody was standing and would not be for years.

He did not see them land.

He knew that they had, the way he had known about the standpipes. He was pleased. He counted it as Balance, because a hole in the world had been filled and the ledger came out even, and he did not have the instrument to read what he had actually put on the ground, and there was no Measurewright within four worlds of him to say the word *shale* in his hearing.

Both wings were out to their full extent now and the Song had gone up into a register he could no longer hear, and could feel.

"Dilue caelum," Verinus said.

Dilute the sky.

Gold came into the nothing overhead, thin at first, a shimmer laid over absence, and thickened.

"Luceant super hunc mundum stellae mundi primi."

Let the stars of the first world shine on this one again. They came out in the gold, in the wrong places, in the arrangements that had been over the ground before Malakar and had never once been over it since, and Verinus looked up at a sky nobody living had seen and set the last three terms of the rite in order in his head: the mirror, the threshold, the anchor.

He had no mirror. The gold overhead was a still surface with a light behind it and it would answer.

He had no lintel. The tear in the world had two edges and a top, and a thing with two edges and a top is a door, and the Rubric had never specified whose.

He had no anchor, and the Rubric is not gentle about that, and he had read the note in the margin about the Fourth Prince of Rovann in a cold room at fifty-two and had been unable to sleep after it.

He took Aurelian.

He did it in the half-second available and without asking, and he knew exactly what he was doing, because there was one soul left in four worlds holding his name at weight, and it was standing in a cellar under Altherion in the wrong skin with no teeth, believing that four people were alive. The bond had been made at dawn with a surety and a Sacrament and it would hold. It would hold and it would carry, and whatever came back up out of this would come back up that line, and Aurelian would be standing at the other end of it with no warning and no procedure and no idea that he had been made a fixed point.

*I told the Bishop to spend the objection where the cost is measurable. This cost is measurable. I am measuring it. I am going to do it anyway, and I have run out of the distinction I said was the only one I had left.*

"Transponar."

The threshold took him.

There was no crossing and no interval and nothing between the two places, because there is nothing between the two places, and the Rubric says so in its first line and Verinus had read the line aloud to a class of Lectors in the year he turned sixty. His position stopped being where it had been. It was written somewhere else, at the origin, at the exact point that all of it was coming out of, close enough to reach.

The gold sky came with him. The stars came with him, wrong and old and burning in their first arrangement over a place that had never had a sky.

Verinus arrived in the dark at the root of the wound with both wings out and his cap sliding, and he reached up with a young man's hand and pulled it down hard onto his head, and set his feet.

The wind blew gently against the breeze of the vast sea, the great fissures of Malakar, the trenches that have dug and bled like a wound in the world expanding underneath her vessel. Haruki looked up to see through the sky, the piercing light waves connecting throughout the entire material world. The thinly laid lines of the marks that burned across the infinite distances. There she observed, her breath leaving her lips only for a moment before she steadied herself.

"So that is what Darius saw in you... such purity..." She said, her eyes did not need to see through the stars to understand the light it was connected to. The fine line between was all she needed to read through the blindness, the sight of Verinus and Kujo, two forces colliding, declining, and expressing. Through the threads and the stars of light she could see it all, the wounds of the earth, the heat of the battle, the coldness of the void, it all began to seem familiar to her.

"The void... is formidable, I can see why mother trained me the way she did..." She said as her arms remained folded underneath her chest, her gaze softening despite the tension of the battle ahead of her. She wanted to watch, but she knew best of the results that were bound to approach, she has experienced it before, and senses it now. The lingering dread between the ice cold darkness of the void, and the warming pure light of judgement. Such an elder rivalry between power would only result in the stronger force winning, and though Verinus was pure, he was not as blunt as the edge the void carried. 

Her eyes drifted over to the old priest, though with a misguided mouth, she could feel it in his frame. The worry, the responsibility he will soon be carrying. It was all too familiar to her... through that reflection she saw a face that connected the seams before they were formed... Niran... the same weight he carried when he deemed it necessary.

"Lord Verinus is powerful, but never I thought to this extent..." She said-her vessel swerving and shaking with the waves of the tremors. The shockwaves and wind blew past them in rhythmic and oscillating patterns. There was no true outcome yet, but Haruki knew to hold onto what was best, rather than to assume what the darkness told her.

Kujo watched Verinus arrive at the root of the wound beneath that impossible golden sky, and for several moments he said nothing. The old constellations burned over the Void in arrangements that no living generation remembered, black stones had already fallen across the world, and the Song of Grace pressed against the wound with all the weight Verinus had managed to gather into himself. Kujo merely stood there with the Crevice beating inside him, molten gold threading through the black architecture beneath his flesh while the surrounding dark repeatedly collapsed and reformed around his silhouette.

Then his head tilted.

A smile spread across his face slowly, but there was less elegance in it now. The Crevice had stripped something loose inside him. His amusement no longer remained behind the careful manners he had worn for centuries; it showed openly, twitching at the corners of his mouth while his eyes tracked the golden wings, the restored youth, the sky Verinus had dragged into the Void, and every declaration that had accompanied him.

“Will.”

The word came out layered.

Kujo breathed through his nose and laughed softly, the sound beginning low before catching strangely in his throat. His voice had changed since Valmir. There was still intelligence beneath it, still the precise consciousness that had made him terrifying long before the Crevice, but something feral had started sharing the space with it. Not stupidity. Not madness without direction. Something closer to a restraint finally deciding it had been unnecessary.

“You believe will is the one possession I cannot take from you because you have already surrendered it elsewhere.”

Kujo’s black wings opened behind him as golden fissures appeared through their darkness. The Crevice surged once through his chest, and the wound in reality behind him contracted like the throat of some vast animal.

“What a magnificent little distinction.”

He took a step forward, bare foot settling into nothing as though the Void itself had produced ground for him. His eyes traveled once across Verinus and then past him, not toward another body, but toward the Material World beyond the wound. The old sky. Alabaster. The Holy Sea. Every church that had built its authority beneath the Judger’s Name.

“You came here prepared to be the rock.”

Kujo’s smile widened.

“I am no longer interested in moving the rock.”

The gold inside his chest flashed violently. For an instant, Valmir’s Crevice became visible beneath Kujo’s sternum as a black-gold vertical fracture extending far deeper than his body should have been capable of containing. Collapse poured through the Void around him, and the Crevice’s ancient instinct toward transition met Kujo’s own refusal of continuity. Where Valmir had once permitted destruction so that something might follow, Kujo allowed the first half of the law to remain and severed the second.

“I will remove the field.”

His voice distorted on the final word.

Kujo lifted his hand. There was no elaborate rite. No Domain. No declaration to Fate that required the world’s agreement.

Blackness gathered across his palm in violent strands, so dense they did not resemble shadow anymore. Thin red fractures moved inside them, and beneath those ran flashes of Valmir’s gold. The energy did not radiate heat. It carried something far worse: an imposed ending, strengthened by the Crevice’s power to make collapse propagate through structures that had once been beyond the Void’s natural reach.

Kujo stared at his palm as the energy grew. Then he smiled.

“Alabaster belongs to ruin.”

The first surge left his hand.

It did not behave like a beam. Black killing energy erupted upward, split across the wound, and multiplied into thousands of crooked streams that tore outward through the boundaries between regions of the Inner World. They spread like cracks racing across glass, dividing again and again until the sky over distant kingdoms darkened beneath branching rivers of black.

Kujo’s fingers opened farther.

The surge intensified.

“And every church that ever taught men to kneel beneath the Judger…” His voice dipped into something almost gleeful. “…belongs to me.”

The black streams descended.

Across the Inner World, death arrived with almost no warning. In some places it passed over entire districts while leaving the neighboring street untouched. In others it swept through settlements in wide moving fronts, extinguishing lives faster than bells could be rung. Cities watched black fractures spread through the sky before those fractures descended through roofs, streets, towers, markets, barracks, farms, temples, and roads. The energy did not pause to distinguish soldier from priest, ruler from laborer, believer from unbeliever.

Kujo was not judging them. That was the point. He laughed as the first wave completed itself.

The Crevice surged inside him again, and Kujo’s arm trembled, not from weakness, but from the amount of ruin moving through it. His human face flickered around the edges. Beneath it, the horned structure of his true head appeared for a fraction of a second, crimson channels glowing through black armored flesh before the beautiful mask settled imperfectly back into place.

“Do you feel it?”

His eyes widened.

“The difference between judgment and destruction?”

More black power streamed from his palm. The currents spread farther across the Inner World, finding populated regions through presence rather than morality, carrying death into places that had never heard of Kujo and had no understanding of why their skies had turned black.

“Judgment needs a reason.”

His fingers curled slightly.

“This does not.”

The devastation continued until **nearly thirty-five percent of the Inner World’s living population had been extinguished** beneath the spreading black surge. Entire family lines ended within minutes. Governments lost rulers and servants in the same instant. Armies became fragments. Trade roads fell silent. Villages that had endured wars, famines, migrations, and Epochal disasters simply stopped answering their neighbors.

And Kujo could feel the number growing. Not as names. As absence.

Millions of interruptions entering reality at once. Bonds terminating. Households losing members. Fate-lines snapping and recoiling through the Plane of Fate. Soul traffic surging toward whatever destinations still remained available. Material systems collapsing because the hands that maintained them had disappeared.

The Crevice drank the consequence without consuming the souls themselves. It was the structural aftermath Kujo wanted, the sudden vacuum of labor, governance, memory, food production, protection, inheritance, and recognition. Death was merely the first motion.

Kujo lowered his hand slightly, but the black streams remained hanging throughout distant skies like scars.

His breathing had become audible. Heavy. Almost excited.

“Thirty-five percent.”

He tasted the number as though testing a new word.

“Enough.”

A laugh escaped him again.

“Not enough to empty the world.”

His smile sharpened.

“Enough to make the survivors carry it.”

That was where the Crevice truly interested him. A dead world taught nothing. A devastated world did.

He wanted the remaining sixty-five percent to wake tomorrow and discover that the person who maintained the ward had died, that the farmer who knew the winter planting pattern had vanished, that the judge who remembered the precedent was gone, that the bridge-master had left no apprentice, that the healer’s family had been erased while the patients remained, that the priest had survived but half the congregation had not.

He wanted systems to discover their missing pieces.

Then he wanted entropy to begin.

Kujo raised his face toward the golden sky Verinus had carried with him. Something in his expression was almost ecstatic now, though intelligence still remained cold beneath it.

“You wanted to become a testament.”

The black-gold fissure through Kujo’s chest widened.

“Then witness.”

His voice fractured into several overlapping registers.

“Alabaster will fall.”

The surrounding Void buckled.

“The Holy Sea will break.”

Another pulse passed through the Crevice.

“The churches of the Judger will become foundations for things that no longer remember why the stones were white.”

Kujo spread both arms as the last distant surges continued burning through the Inner World.

“And when the living ask what crime earned them this…” His grin became monstrous. “…there will be no answer.”

For a heartbeat, his true form tore fully through the human courtesy, horned crown, black armored sinew, crimson channels, segmented tendrils spreading from his back, the enormous bladed limb flexing beside him while Valmir’s gold crawled through every fracture of the abomination.

Then Kujo laughed into the wound between worlds.

“Because I did not judge them.”

His eyes fixed ahead.

“I wanted them gone.”

The lines began to go.

He felt them in the chest, where the Song was, because they came down the same road. One, and then eleven, and then a number that stopped being a number and became a texture, a fraying at the front of him, and Verinus stood at the root of the wound under a sky he had carried in with him and understood what was happening across the Inner World from the inside of the Plane of Fate before any bell in any tower had begun to ring.

Ash was still on his tongue. Beeswax and lime and the sweet dead smell of a cellar four hundred years old, and it stayed, and under it now came something with no smell at all.

Bonds terminating. That was the flat word for it and he reached for the flat word because the alternative was to feel it accurately.

A household in the salt cities losing three of five and the two left standing in a doorway with the light behind them. A ward-keeper in a Kharven valley going out mid-breath and the ward going out four hours later, which nobody would connect. A woman who had known the winter planting pattern for a district of nine thousand people and had taught it to nobody, because she was sixty-one and had assumed she had time.

The physic garden.

He knew that one. It came up the line with a shape he recognised and he had walked in it nine days ago with poppy heads in his hand and a letter he had already decided to send, and the boy who kept it had swept the path in front of him without looking up, and Verinus had not learned his name and had meant to.

He did not learn it now.

The Song went up a degree and told him what to do with it.

It did not use words. It had never used words. It arrived as the Scale arrived, weight in a place where weight is information, and what it gave him was this: the cries are the material. The pain is the material. Let them go down into the ground and let the ground hold them, because a Paragon is not born out of a good year. He is assembled out of what a generation could not carry, and the greater the load the sooner the thing arrives that can lift it, and every one of these deaths is a stone in a foundation that a child is going to stand on.

Verinus, who had spent nineteen years refusing to let his Court spend a single person it could not account for, heard the instruction and did not argue with it.

*I could stop perhaps four thousand of them. I have the reach for it now, and spending the reach spends the rite, and there would be no rite left afterward. Four thousand against a seal that holds for an age. The sum is sound. I have done a sound sum on a person before and I know exactly how it reads from the other side, and I have checked this one twice in the time available, which was none.*

*And I want it entered that I did not hesitate, because a man who hesitates gets to keep the hesitation afterward and call it conscience, and I am not going to be permitted anything afterward, and I would rather the record was clean.*

He let them go.

Thirty-five in every hundred, across the Inner World, out of a black sky with no argument attached, and the man appointed to weigh stood at the root of it and permitted the number because he had been told it was fuel.

Something in him that had held since he was fifty-two went over quietly and did not right itself.

"How little that is going to mean," Verinus said.

His voice went out flat and level and the Song carried it, and it did not shake, and that was the worst thing about it.

"You have made a number. Congratulations on the number. I want you to understand what you have actually spent it on, because I have the only instrument in four worlds that can read the receipt and I am going to read it to you once."

He put both hands behind his back, which was the posture, and the posture was ridiculous here and he took it anyway.

"There is a child. He is alive at this moment. He is not in the Inner World and I am not going to tell you a great deal more about him than that, because the single most useful thing I have ever done for him was to keep him out of a day-book." The wings held steady off his shoulders and the gold in them had stopped spreading. "The Church has an apparatus for finding him. Men give their whole working lives to the reading of it. I have refused that apparatus for nineteen years on the grounds that a thing declared divine stops being a thing anyone will argue with, and I have been right about that, and I remain right about that."

The fraying at the front of him went on and he made himself keep speaking through it.

"And you have just built him."

A stream that had been black over a kingdom somewhere ceased to have a kingdom under it.

"Every household that comes apart tonight is a person that child is going to meet. Every ward that fails in the third week because nobody knew it needed feeding. Every judge gone with the precedent still in his head and every bridge with no master and every congregation that kept its priest and lost its people. That is the ground he grows in. You have laid it. You have laid it thick and you have laid it fast, and you have made him eager, which is the part that should frighten you, because a Paragon assembled out of a slow century is a magistrate and a Paragon assembled in an afternoon is something else, and I do not have a word for what you have just commissioned."

The Song shifted under the sentence and did not contradict it.

"You have said that judgment and destruction differ because one requires a reason. That is correct and it is the only correct sentence in your speech, and you have drawn the wrong conclusion from it, which I have watched clever men do at four tables in this life." His hands stayed behind his back. "A reason is a constraint. A constraint is what keeps a course of action from generating the next one. You have removed the constraint, so nothing you do from this hour will be answerable to anything you did in the last one, and you will therefore make a sequence of decisions that each look correct standing alone. That is how ends arrive. A string of them, laid end to end, each one defensible, by a man who had stopped checking."

The fracture on his forearm opened another finger's width and the gold in it had gone to a deeper colour.

"And behind the speech, and behind the annihilation, and behind the laughing." Verinus let a breath out. "There is a hand in you. I can see the shape of the fingers. You have taken a thing out of a Titan and you are wearing it and you have been careful to call it yours, and it is still doing what it was made to do, and it is doing it through you, and you have organised an entire cosmology around not noticing."

He straightened his back, which no longer complained about anything.

"You are a puppet, titan. You will be reminded of your standing when the One True Judger rises, and he will not be an argument, and he will not be a doctrine, and he will not require your agreement."

The bell rang.

It came from above the sky he had brought with him. One stroke, enormous, so far down in the register that the front of his ribs took it before his ears did, and the old wrong stars shook in the gold and steadied. Verinus had heard the small version of that bell through a cellar wall three days ago and had assumed it was an orchard bell and had assumed the shears with it were pruning, and he had been unable to interpret either, and he had written down that he could not.

He read this one immediately.

*Almost. That is the courtesy stroke. That is a man being told the hour so he can put his affairs in order, and I have perhaps as long as it takes to say the thing properly, and I have never in my life needed longer than that.*

He did not ask who was ringing. He would have given the rest of it to know.

"Monstrous is the whole of what you are going to amount to," Verinus said. "A monstrosity of unusual size, filed as one, and filed in the appendix." He held the gold steady off his shoulders. "Sovereigns go in the body of the register.
