You go after the weak because the totality of the strong will not fit inside you, and I have met that in men who ran a parish and I am unimpressed to find it running a Void."

The wings came all the way out.

"So allow this weak man to leave you with one gift."

Verinus put his hands in front of him and turned the palms up, which is the oldest posture in the Rubric and means the celebrant is holding nothing and intends to be holding nothing at the close.

"Findantur caeli."

Let the heavens be split. They split. The gold went open from horizon to horizon of a place with no horizon, and behind the gold, the second thing, the join, the seam where the Void gave onto the Material World, exposed along its whole length and turned to the light.

"Claudatur ostium inter vacuum et mundum."

Let the door between the Void and the world be shut.

"Sepeliantur in veritate."

Let them be entombed in the truth.

"Ligetur hoc vas intemporali, incredibili."

Let this vessel be bound to the timeless, to the unbelievable.

He heard the last word leave him and go, and the Song took it and held it, and the four marks came up in the gold overhead where nothing had drawn them: binding, seal, threshold, reverence. The fourth was the one he had never understood until this moment. Reverence. The rite requires the celebrant to honour the thing he is sealing. The Rubric gives no reason and the Court has never asked for one and Verinus, at twenty-six years old, in a body that had spent seventy-one, found that he could produce it, and produced it, and it cost him more than the knee had.

"Fiam murus inter servata et hoc."

Let me become the wall between the safeguarded and this.

"Fiam fluxus fati. Fiam puritas vera."

Let me become the flow of fate. Let me become the true purity.

"Ritus sigilli," said Verinus. "Custodia aeterna."

The realm answered.

It came from underneath, which he had not expected and should have. The world itself took hold. Nothing of his own strength was anywhere in it, because Entombment has never run on the celebrant's power, and every practitioner who assumed otherwise is entered at Nightglass. It runs on the office. It runs on the Archon whose whole function is that everything which exists has an edge, and it asks one question of the thing in front of it, and the question is whether the thing is here.

Verinus had put that argument to a Curia at fifty-two and had been told it was elegant and inapplicable.

*A thing that exists has a boundary. A thing in a body has a further boundary, and the second is stricter, and a construct raised by an Archon and given requirements carries the requirements stamped through it like a coin. And the Void has no boundary and the Titan of nothing cannot be measured and every scholar who has looked at this has stopped at that sentence and filed the matter. They stopped one step early. I am not sealing a thing. I am sealing a doing. The skill is the limit. A performance has an edge because it is being performed, and I do not have to find the edge of him, I only have to find the edge of what he is doing with his hands.*

The flow began.

It came up through the join in a slow seismic turn, the whole substance of the realm shifting weight with the deliberation of ground about to slide, and it reached past Verinus without acknowledging him and took hold of the calamitous thing at the root of the wound.

It did not take all of it.

He felt the seal find its purchase and he felt exactly how much purchase it had found, and it was pieces. The borrowed grammar. The parts that were a doing and no part of a being: the propagation, the reach that let collapse travel through structures the Void had never been able to touch, the ancient interval between standing and failing that had come out of a Titan and had been carried here in somebody else's chest. Fate-threads went taut all across the wound and the seismic flow took them and turned them down into the join, and the gold marks overhead came together into one, and the seam began, along its full length, to close.

The rest of him stayed where it was. The seal had no grip on it. Verinus had known that would be the case since the third clause and had said the rite anyway, because half of a monstrous thing is a thing that can be fought by people who cannot fight the whole of it, and that was the gift, and he had never claimed it was generous.

Then the wall was made and he was the material.

It went into him from the hands. His substance began to go out of him and into the seam, laid along the join, spread thin over a length that had no length, and the Song went with it and became the mortar. He felt the years empty out. Nineteen at the Court, forty-five in the order, a boy on a slope with chalk on his fingers, all of it going out of him along the seam in a direction that was flat and endless and had a wall in it now with his name in the material.

And the anchor line went taut.

He felt it snap out from the middle of him and run down and back, out of the Void, through the join, into a cellar under Altherion, into a man standing on flags in the wrong skin with no teeth in his lower jaw who believed that four people were alive and had no idea in the world that he was load-bearing. It went tight. It would stay tight. Whatever the seal drew from now on, it would draw down that line as well, on the far end, into a soul that had been made a fixed point at dawn by a man who did not ask.

Aurelian would hold. That was the sum, and it was a good sum, and there had been no time to check it at all.

The bell rang the second stroke.

Verinus felt the last of himself go out along the join, and the seam took his shape, and the gold above him closed over the old wrong stars one arrangement at a time. He got his right hand up while there was still a hand to get up. He set the palm flat against the seam he had become, the way a man puts his weight on a door he intends to hold, and pushed.

Kujo did not resist when the seal first found him. The golden marks closed over the wound, Fate-threads tightened across the seam, and the realm seized the parts of the Crevice currently acting through him: propagation, structural decline, and the interval between standing and failing. He felt those functions being drawn toward the wall Verinus was becoming, watched the black-gold fractures beneath his skin dim, and allowed the rite to believe it had discovered purchase.

Then Kujo smiled.

“You found the edge of what I was doing. Clever. But you mistook the edge of an action for the edge of possession.”

Kujo stopped the working completely. He did not struggle against the seal or attempt to overpower the office sustaining it; he simply ceased performing the specific actions it had defined, leaving every binding thread correctly attached to something that no longer possessed a present tense. For one suspended moment, the Custodia held perfectly around an abandoned performance while the Crevice itself remained fused deep within Kujo’s private architecture.

“You sealed the expression. You did not remove the organ.”

The vertical black-gold fracture beneath his sternum brightened again, extending impossibly deeper than his body should have allowed. Valmir’s Crevice was no longer an external instrument that could simply be stripped from his hands; Kujo had buried it into the relationship between his own existence and the Void. He pressed pale fingers against his chest and felt the stolen principle answer him from somewhere far beneath flesh, soul, and ordinary metaphysical anatomy.

“I am wearing it, yes. You should have asked what happens when the clothing grows nerves.”

Kujo did not restart the same technique. Instead, the higher layer of Void opened inside his own architecture, the same impossible stratum from which he had once interrupted the transition between Valmir’s collapse and reconstruction. There, consequence did not need to travel through the ordinary bridge between cause and effect, so Kujo began expressing collapse as isolated local completions rather than as a force propagating outward from himself.

The seal searched for reach and found none. It searched for transmission and found none, then searched for the ancient interval moving from Kujo into distant structure and discovered that he had removed the movement entirely. Black-gold fractures began appearing throughout the surrounding Void independently, each possessing its own local beginning and each already arriving at the point where collapse had completed.

“You built your wall around the road.”

His laugh came low and distorted, intelligence remaining perfectly intact beneath the increasingly unhinged pleasure in his voice.

“I stopped using roads.”

The Custodia reacted exactly as Kujo expected. Its office began searching for the boundary of this new performance because anything actively being done possessed an edge, and Kujo did not disagree with that principle. Instead, his attention shifted away from what the seal was trying to measure and toward the fact that the seal itself was now performing transitions of its own.

The realm reached. Fate-threads tightened, Verinus’s substance entered the seam, the Song became mortar, and the distant anchor was made responsible for continuity. Each stage required something to become something else, and the Crevice inside Kujo had been torn from the Titan whose deepest authority governed precisely that interval.

“There you are. You finally became something I can touch.”

Kujo raised two fingers and touched neither Verinus nor the wall itself. He touched the transition between Verinus becoming the wall and the wall successfully becoming separation, then introduced the Crevice directly into that handoff. The effect was immediate: expenditure continued exactly as the rite demanded, but completion began falling behind.

Verinus’s substance could still leave him and become wall-material, but increasingly it failed to finish becoming a functional wall. Memory could become mortar without fully becoming reinforcement, while years, identity, spiritual office, and the Song continued pouring into the seam at full cost. Kujo watched the exchange become catastrophically inefficient, the ritual paying everything it had promised while receiving progressively less structure in return.

“Do you understand? I did not break your sacrifice.”

A warped laugh slipped through the next words.

“I broke the part where sacrifice becomes useful.”

The golden wall shuddered as the Custodia drew harder through its own architecture to compensate. That increased pressure exposed the dependency Kujo had been waiting for, because every correction traveled through the anchor Verinus had installed beyond the Void. Kujo finally turned his attention toward that distant line, not toward the person at the other end, but toward the structural declaration that one soul had been made fixed.

He could have severed it. That would have been easy, and Kujo found ease increasingly boring. Instead, he reached for the line through the Crevice and reversed the direction of the unresolved structural burden accumulating inside the unfinished wall.

“No. I will not cut it.”

Kujo’s smile disappeared, leaving something colder and more malicious behind.

“I want you to feel what you did to him.”

The anchor had been designed to draw stability toward the seal, so Kujo allowed instability to return through the same declared relationship. Every section of wall that had consumed sacrifice without completing itself, every contradiction between paid cost and unfinished structure, suddenly discovered the fixed point the rite itself had named responsible for remaining steady. The unresolved weight surged backward through the line, and the golden sky convulsed as the Custodia attempted to reinforce its own anchor.

“You made him load-bearing.”

Kujo’s voice deepened as the Crevice surged violently through him.

“So let him bear it.”

The harder the rite reinforced its distant fixed point, the more clearly it declared that point part of its mechanism. Kujo exploited that declaration relentlessly, making each new reinforcement carry the same broken transition: strength arrived, but failed to completely become support; more strength followed, and more unfinished support accumulated behind it. The ritual could not stop paying without betraying its own office, and could not continue paying without deepening the structural contradiction Kujo had planted inside its conversion.

“You built a machine that cannot stop paying because you told it the payment was holy.”

The Crevice flared through Kujo’s body and tore his beautiful human courtesy open along the chest and shoulders. Black armored flesh appeared underneath, crimson channels ignited through ribbed sinew, and segmented tendrils spread behind him while molten-gold fractures crawled through the monstrous anatomy he usually kept hidden. His enormous bladed limb unfolded beside him as the last remnants of elegance gave way to open delight.

“And holiness is such an expensive word.”

Kujo drove the blade into the seam, not through Verinus, but through the distinction between inside the seal and outside the seal. The higher Void opened along the weapon and forced the threshold to occupy two incompatible placements at once: one facing the Material World and another existing where spatial adjacency, recognition, and ordinary boundary logic did not consistently apply. The Custodia attempted to establish which side contained Kujo, and Kujo made that binary relationship unstable before it could finish answering.

“You wanted a door.”

He twisted the blade and the entire sealing geometry began rotating around its own threshold law.

“I gave you a hinge.”

Binding slid across threshold, reverence became recognition, and the wall remained completely convinced that something needed to be separated from whatever had been safeguarded. Kujo did not destroy that conviction; he changed the orientation of the unfinished enclosure until the golden structure began curving inward around the active machinery of the rite itself. The Song, the office, the anchor dependency, and the sacrificial material Verinus had poured into the Custodia became the things surrounded by the wall.

The seal slammed inward around its own mechanism. Its sacred office remained intact, still capable of performing exactly what it had been created to perform, but its active reach had now been enclosed within the very boundary it intended to impose upon Kujo. The Custodia had not failed in the conventional sense; Kujo had made it successfully entomb itself.

“You were going to become the wall between me and the world.”

Kujo stood before the sealed golden mass, breathing heavily while his human appearance slowly crawled back over the abomination beneath it.

“So I let you.”

He placed one palm against the golden surface.

“Just facing the wrong direction.”

Then Kujo gave the wall something worse than immediate destruction. He compressed the Crevice into his palm and introduced decline without release into the enclosed rite, ensuring that every future act of maintenance would demand slightly more while returning slightly less. Each correction would introduce microscopic deviation, each reinforcement would preserve a fraction less fidelity than the previous one, and continuation itself would gradually become the mechanism by which the seal exhausted whatever sustained it.

Kujo withdrew his hand and left behind only a hairline black fracture in the gold. He stared at it for several seconds, almost tenderly, because the seal might survive years or centuries depending upon what continued feeding it. Immediate collapse would have been mercy; Kujo preferred that the Custodia remain functional long enough to experience the arithmetic of its own deterioration.

“You spoke of a child. A Judger not yet risen. A Paragon assembled from suffering.”

His dark eyes narrowed while the Crevice pulsed once beneath his chest.

“Good. Build him.”

Kujo leaned closer to the gold as though speaking directly through the wall to whatever remained capable of hearing him.

“Let every dead mother, every broken household, every failed ward, every abandoned bridge, every starving district, and every unanswered prayer become another stone beneath his feet. Raise him on thirty-five percent of a world and teach him my Name before he learns yours.”

His smile widened, the voice beneath it becoming layered again as the surrounding Void responded to the pleasure moving through him. He did not sound calm anymore, but neither had he become senseless; the instability only made his intent more naked, less filtered through the elegance he had once considered necessary.

“And when your One True Judger finally stands, I will discover whether judgment screams when it meets something it cannot sentence.”

Kujo stepped backward as the higher Void opened behind him, darkness swallowing his wings and the fractured outline of his body. The sealed Custodia remained suspended at the root of the wound, its golden surface apparently whole except for the thin black line Kujo had left inside it. His laughter followed him into the dark, distorted and deeply pleased with what the encounter had become.

“You wanted to leave me a gift.”

The black fracture widened by the width of a hair.

“So did I.”

The paying went on and the wall stopped arriving.

He knew the fault the moment it opened, because he had been reading exchanges for forty-five years and the shape of a bad one is the same at every scale. Cost leaving on schedule. Value coming back short and then shorter. His years went out of him along the seam at the full rate the Rubric demanded, forty-five in the order and nineteen at the Court and a boy on a slope with chalk on his fingers, and what they became at the far end was mortar that had stopped setting.

Something had been put into the handover. Between the going out and the becoming, at the exact joint where one turns into the other, a thing had been seated, and Verinus could feel it there and could not reach it, because to reach it he would have had to stop, and the rite has no clause for stopping.

*Elegant. God help me, that is elegant. He has left every term of the contract standing and taken the conversion out of the middle of it, and I am now a man shovelling coal into a firebox with no boiler on the far side, and the office will not let me put the shovel down, because the office is not aware that anything has gone wrong. It is only aware that the payment is holy.*

The seal compensated. That was the design and the design was correct in every case the Rubric had ever contemplated. Where the wall came up thin, the rite drew harder to thicken it, and where the drawing found the vessel already spent, it went to the fixed point, because a fixed point is what a rite reaches for when its own substance runs out.

The line went hot.

Verinus felt it happen from the middle of himself outward and he had perhaps a quarter of a second of understanding before the load reversed and started down. Every span of wall that had eaten a cost and produced nothing. Every contradiction between what had been paid and what stood. All of it found the soul the rite had named as responsible for holding steady, and turned, and went, out of the Void and through the join and down into a cellar under Altherion where a man in the wrong skin with no teeth in his lower jaw was standing on flags in a puddle of his own blood believing that four people were alive.

"No," said Verinus.

He put his weight against it. There was nothing left of him to put and he put it anyway, and he tried to take the line back into himself, and the office would not permit the amendment, because the anchor had been declared at the third clause and a declaration made inside a rite is a fact about the world.

He had done that. At dawn, in a half-second, without asking, and he had known exactly what he was doing and had written it in his own head as good arithmetic, and here was the invoice arriving at the address he had put on it.

*I told the Bishop to spend the objection where the cost is measurable. This is measurable. It is measured. It has my name on the bottom of it and there is no clerk in four worlds who will file the appeal.*

The load went down the line and did not arrive.

It stopped. Mid-flight, at the full weight of it, with the whole unfinished catastrophe of the wall behind it and nothing in front, and it hung in the seam and held there, and the seam held, and Verinus, who had spent his life on the proposition that everything in the world has an edge that can be found, understood that something very large had just put a finger on the scale and stopped it swinging.

Then the sky came down.

The gold he had carried in with him, the dilution, the old wrong stars in their first arrangement, all of it drew together across the whole width of a place with no width, and the stars did not go out. They moved. They came in toward one another and went down into a shape, and the shape took them the way a body takes blood, and Verinus watched constellations that had stood over the Okuchi before Malakar broke it settle into the mass of a thing that was arriving and become the light inside it.

It had a name. He heard the name before he saw the hand, and the hearing was not a sound.

The One True Judger.

He had said that phrase from a lectern for forty-five years and had struck it from two minutes as improper and had told a Curia at fifty-two that the office does not feel and cannot be addressed as a person, and he stood in the ruin of his own working with the name going through him and had no correction to make to it.

The hand came out of the fold.

It came the way a scale-pan comes to rest, without hurry and without any possibility of being interrupted, and it was enormous past the point where enormous is a description, and the Void did not part for it. The Void stopped being the thing the hand had to move through. Reality drew back from every place the mass of it interjected, shrouding, folding away along the edges of the arriving weight, and under the fingers everything that had been at the root of the wound went to grains on a beach with the tide coming.

The gaze went past him.

It did not go to him. He was under it, in the sense that everything was under it, and it went over him and past and settled somewhere behind and above the root of the wound and stayed there.

One second. Perhaps two.

The whole Mortal World shook. He felt it through the seam, through the join, through eleven hundred years of stone at Altherion and every white shelf of the Holy Sea and the salt of the Nalūn basin and the frozen ground of the north, a shudder that went into houses that had never heard the name and cracked plaster in rooms where nobody could have said why. Bells rang that nobody was ringing. In four worlds, every scale-pan hanging on every chain came to level at once and stayed level for the length of the gaze and afterward would not agree with itself for a season.

There were no words. The office does not use them and never has and Verinus had been correct about that at fifty-two and had been correct about it his whole life and took no pleasure in it now.

What arrived instead was a finding, and it came in the only grammar the office has, which is weight.

*Unworthy. That is the whole of the reading and it is not an insult, it is a measurement, and the thing measured has been put on the pan and has come up light. He believes what he has taken makes him the heaviest object in the room and the office has looked at him for one second and gone back to what it was doing, and the chase is the flaw. The reaching is the flaw. The root that will bring it down is the root itself, and he will not be told, because nothing here is going to speak to him.*

The gaze came off.

And the hand closed on the seal.

It took the whole of the Custodia up out of the root of the wound, the golden wall and the seam and the join and all of it, and Verinus felt himself go with it, because he was the material, and the material went where the wall went. He saw the thin black line as it lifted. A hair's width of fault running through the gold, the thing that had been seated in his handover, and it did not fall out and did not close, and it went up with the rest.

The seal did not stop standing.

That was the thing he understood last and would have most liked to write down. It came out of the world and kept working, and the Custodia was no longer made of Verinus, and it was a door now, and what it stood between was the safeguarded and this, exactly as the rite had asked, and what would come through it in either direction was not going to be a matter for the Alabaster Court.

The line to Aurelian came up with the hand and stayed tied.

He tried one more time. He got a hand to it, and there was still, somehow, a hand.

Then the palm closed around him and the pressure of it took his years and set them down somewhere with the care of a man laying a plate on a shelf he can reach, and the gold went dark, and Verinus felt himself being carried, and the carrying had a direction and the direction was up and inward and toward the three sub-realms where the Lawbell hangs.

He did not die that day.

He was set down in Utopia, in the law-architecture, in a quiet the like of which he had not experienced since he was nineteen years old, and he lay in it with his eyes open and his cap gone and both hands empty, and he put his palm flat on the floor under him to find out whether it would take weight, and it did, and it did not sink.

Deeper in, where the Plane stops being a place a soul travels and becomes the thing the soul is made of, an old man opened his hand and found the print of an axe head in his palm, in the flesh, seated there since the third month and no longer sore.

Darius had been ankle-deep in the ash for a stretch of time he had stopped trying to number.

The ash had come up over the welt of both boots on the first day and had closed, and it had not let go since, and somewhere in the fourth or the ninth or the fortieth month he had stopped experiencing that as a restraint. He was seated. There was a difference and it had taken him most of a life to find it and he had found it here, standing still, at sixty-odd years of age, with a coat that had gone to felt and a beard he had given up on.

He put his fist out.

The air in front of it did not move, and the ash did not move, and three hundred feet up the branches that stopped being branches and became directions did not move, and the whole column of grey bark that ran down the nearest trunk in a hand no living scholar could read went, for the length of one breath, entirely legible.

Ruin first. The fist that finds the ending already on its way and makes it arrive correctly. Then Purity, which is the labour of what has to remain. Spirit, which is the traffic. Temporality, which is order and nothing else and is the hardest of the seven because it refuses to be impressive. Knowledge, which he had come in believing he already had and which had taken him longer than any of them. Balance, which was his, which had come to him the way a language comes back to a man who spoke it as a child, and which he had needed a year to stop over-reaching with.

And the seventh, which is the six held at once and the Plane delivering the finding.

He let the hand open.

He had not learned seven techniques. He had spent eleven hundred years of his order's doctrine reading a single string and calling it the choir, and the thing he had been given here was the whole instrument: the matrix, the Works standing in relation to each other, function itself, and what it means that a Work is a labour and a labour is performed and a performance can be done well or badly by a man with a body. Work magic. He had used the phrase in three hundred writs. He had signed his name under it. He had not known what it meant until the ash closed over his boots.

"You've got it," Gimbzo said.

He was sitting on nothing, which he did, with Tsuiji laid across his knees and the linen unwound to the beard of the blade. Square shoulders. At that mass, and with the shoulders square, he stopped resembling a man who happened to be enormous.

"I have it," Darius said.

"Say what you've got. Don't be modest, you're bad at it and it wastes my afternoon."

"All seven. The relation between them. And the reason the order has been standing on Balance for eleven centuries without knowing what it was standing on, which is that Balance is not one of the seven. It is the seven agreeing to hold still long enough to be looked at."

"HOO." Gimbzo scratched the side of his jaw with one thumb. "Four times I've said that sentence to a man and this is the first time one's said it back at me."

"Then I would like the gate."

"Aye. You would."

Kizami came down the column with too many joints going at once and landed on the ash without disturbing it and put his chisel-jaw sideways to read the trunk.

*Sixty-one arrivals and eleven gates opened and nine walked out, and now this one wants the twelfth on a completed column with the ink still wet, and there is a subsection. There is always a subsection. Nobody reads the subsection until it is standing in front of them in a coat.*

"There's a subsection," said Kizami.

Darius looked at Gimbzo.

Gimbzo did not look away from him, which he had done at every other difficult moment in the last however many months, and the not looking away was the answer before the words arrived.

"One more," Gimbzo said. "I'd have told you at the start and you'd have walked. I've watched two men take the seventh and go home the same afternoon, and both of them went back with the whole matrix in their hands and neither of them could put it down on the world, because what you've learned here is how the Works stand. You've not once been asked what it costs to stand them somewhere that doesn't want them." He turned the axe over on his knees. "That's the trial. It's not a fight and I'm not going to tell you what it is, because the last thing a man in your position needs is a fortnight to prepare a position."

"And afterward I go back."

"Afterward you go back."

"To what."

"Don't ask me that."

The ash held. Darius looked down at his boots, and at the two curls of grey bark that had lain against the toe of the left one since the first hour and had not moved, and at the print of the axe head in the packed ground beneath them, which was clean and had never filled.

He worked his right foot out of the ash for the first time since he had put it there, and set it down flat in the print, and put his weight on it.

Kujo watched the golden hand withdraw with the Custodia and said nothing. The finding had passed over him, the seal had been carried away, and the wound had been left with a history written into it: Verinus had invoked, the office had answered, the Custodia had taken purchase, and the One True Judger had removed what remained. None of those things were touching Kujo now.

Then the darkness changed.

It did not gather around him like ordinary Void. It poured down over his shoulders in a single sheet of absolute black, spreading across his chest, back, arms, and legs until his entire body disappeared beneath it except for his head. The black wore on him like an enormous cape without folds or weight, its lower edges bleeding into nothing rather than ending, and even the gold of the Crevice disappeared beneath its surface.

This was not the Void Kujo usually carried.

This was Xevorith, the Timeless Void, the Fifth Layer.

In Xevorith, before and after were not sovereign relationships. Sequence was not abolished, but it ceased possessing the automatic right to insist that because something had occurred, its occurrence had to remain part of what came next. Past events could retain consequence, memory, and record, but where a sequence had finished and no longer maintained any active claim upon Kujo in the present, Xevorith permitted him to ask a far more violent question.

Why should it remain true that it happened to him at all?

Kujo lowered his gaze toward his black-covered hand. There were no fingers visible beneath the mantle, only the suggestion of them beneath perfect darkness, and when he slowly opened his palm the Fifth Layer did not spill outward. Instead, the world around him seemed to lose certainty about which moment it was supposed to be occupying.

“So that is your answer.”

His voice had become stranger again, doubled beneath the new layer of Void until one register seemed to speak fractionally before the other.

“You took the wall away from me. You took the seal beyond the wound. You removed its pressure from my present and called that protection.”

A smile began creeping across his exposed face.

“How generous.”

Kujo lifted his hand.

The Unwinding.

It was not conventional time reversal. Kujo did not send the world backward, did not resurrect the dead, and did not replace the present with an alternate history. He reached instead into his own causal continuity and found sequences in the past whose effects no longer possessed any active dependency upon his present state.

The Custodia qualified.

It had targeted him, measured his actions, taken hold of the Crevice’s propagated function, and attempted to separate him from the safeguarded world. But the Judger’s own intervention had removed the wall, removed the purchase, and carried the Custodia elsewhere. At this exact present moment, no active strand of the rite remained embedded in Kujo, binding him, suppressing him, carrying his power, or determining what he could become next.

So Kujo voided the sequence.

Not the Custodia itself. Not Verinus. Not the One True Judger.

The sequence in which Kujo had ever been successfully made the object of the seal.

Black ripples passed backward through the wound without traveling. The past did not visibly change; instead, several completed moments simply lost their right to belong to Kujo’s history. The instant the Custodia found purchase on his borrowed grammar vanished first, followed by the moment its office identified the performance as something it could contain, then the moment the threshold established Kujo as the thing standing opposite the safeguarded.

Kujo laughed.

The sound came from the present while its echo appeared several moments behind him.

“You should have left one chain attached.”

His head tilted, black mantle hanging impossibly still from his shoulders.

“One wound. One prohibition. One grain of your wall inside me. Anything that would have made the past necessary to what I am now.”

His smile widened.

“But you cleaned the table.”

The Unwinding reached deeper.

The moment Verinus’s rite had defined a relationship between Kujo and the Custodia became void, not erased from everyone’s memory, but deprived of causal authority over Kujo’s continuity. The seal could still exist wherever the Judger had carried it, and the wall could still possess every sacred property invested into it, but its historical claim that it had once successfully enclosed, measured, or opposed Kujo no longer connected to him.

It had become a door whose other side had never been his. That was when the damage spread.

Every portion of the Custodia whose meaning depended specifically upon having been raised against Kujo suddenly lost the reference it had used to define itself. Reverence remained. Binding remained. Threshold remained. The office remained intact, but the targeted relationship that had told all those functions what they had been arranged around was now an empty address.

Kujo’s black mantle rippled once.

“You became a wall between the safeguarded and me.”

His twisted voice softened into something almost delighted.

“And now there is no past in which I stood on the other side.”

The golden history of the rite began collapsing causally rather than physically. Any reinforcement that depended upon Kujo having been its original opposed object discovered that the statement was no longer part of his sequence. Any clause whose function required continuity from Kujo was targeted to Kujo remains the thing this boundary excludes found nothing at the first term.

Kujo did not break the wall. He removed himself from its grammar.

The Fifth Layer spread farther over his body, swallowing even the silhouette of his hands until his head seemed to float above an upright mass of darkness. His long black hair moved against the impossible mantle while the Crevice surged safely underneath it, now hidden inside a layer where time could not casually use yesterday as leverage against today.

“The Judger looked at me.”

Kujo’s smile sharpened.

“He weighed me.”

His eyes darkened.

“He found me unworthy.”

A low laugh dragged beneath the final word.

“How fortunate that measurements require a sequence in which the measured remained on the scale.”

Kujo reached for that sequence next.

The finding had not harmed him. It had imposed no present binding, altered no ability, wounded no Soul Crystal, and established no continuing law that currently governed him. It existed as a completed judgment, an observation made in the past whose only surviving relationship to Kujo was the historical statement that it had happened.

Xevorith accepted it. Kujo unwound that too.

He did not change what the One True Judger had perceived. Somewhere within that sovereign office, the measurement could remain exactly what it had been. But the sequence the Judger measured Kujo → that measurement became part of Kujo’s history was voided at the second term.

The judgment no longer belonged to him.

Kujo inhaled deeply, and the black mantle swelled behind him like a cape caught by wind that did not exist.

“Keep your finding.”

His voice cracked into several registers.

“But do not confuse remembering me with possessing a fact about me.”

The Timeless Void pulsed.

Kujo then looked farther backward, toward the original contest with Verinus. He could not simply unwind Death’s Lance, old wounds that had changed him, the fusion of the Crevice, or anything whose consequence actively composed his present. Xevorith was not permission to rewrite every inconvenience; anything that remained causally load-bearing to the Kujo standing here was protected by its own necessity.

But Verinus’s successful resistance was no longer load-bearing.

The old man had been removed. The Custodia had been removed. The seal no longer touched Kujo. The finding no longer governed him.

So Kujo began removing the contest itself from his continuity, piece by piece.

The sequence in which the Custodia slowed his propagated Crevice-work vanished. The sequence in which its anchor complicated his attack vanished. The sequence in which Kujo had been forced to adapt around Verinus’s wall vanished. None of those events were undone for the world that had witnessed them; they simply ceased to count as necessary steps between Kujo’s earlier state and the being now standing beneath Xevorith.

His present remained unchanged.

That was the horror of it.

Kujo had not become weaker because the struggle disappeared from his personal sequence. He still possessed every adaptation he had learned afterward, because knowledge acquired and retained in the present remained a current fact. Xevorith removed the road while leaving the destination intact.

Kujo slowly lowered his hand.

“I remember you.”

A smile remained beneath his darkened eyes.

“I remember your wall. I remember your Song. I remember the hand reaching down and deciding what I was worth.”

The black cape spread wider behind him, its edges disappearing into the Fifth Layer.

“But memory is not causality.”

His voice descended into that warped register again.

“And I have just severed the privilege your past had to explain my present.”

The wound behind him convulsed.

For an instant, several versions of the same history seemed to overlap: Kujo before the Custodia, Kujo beneath the seal, Kujo watched by the Judger, Kujo after the hand withdrew. Then Xevorith passed through them, and all the intermediate states went black except the first and the last.

There had been Kujo. There was Kujo.

Everything between had become optional.

He looked toward the Material World beyond the wound, where thirty-five percent of the Inner World was already dead and the surviving sixty-five percent were beginning to discover what had been taken from them. The deaths remained because their consequences did not merely belong to Kujo; they existed independently across millions of lives, broken households, failed systems, and severed Fate-lines. Even the Fifth Layer could not classify that history as irrelevant to the present.

Kujo seemed pleased by the limitation.

“Good.”

His black-covered hand opened once more.

“A power without limits is merely another form of stupidity.”

He raised his face toward where the golden sky had once been.

“But a past that can only hold me while it still has a hand around my throat?”

Kujo laughed.

“That is useful.”

The darkness of Xevorith climbed higher around his neck but never covered his head. Kujo stood wrapped entirely in the Fifth Layer, his pale face and long black hair emerging from an otherwise perfect silhouette, while beneath that mantle the Crevice continued beating through him with chaotic destructive force.

His smile widened one final time.

“Now.”

The wound around him began to blacken.

“Where were we?”

She sighed, not out of sorrow or grief, but of disappointment in herself. She knew the result, even if it were not truly certain. She could not see it through any way else. This was the ultimatum, the boundary that she had to cross before she could be considered ideal. The weight pressed onto her shoulders, seeing as how it deemed her the next and only successor to the will that Venrius had undoubtedly bestowed upon her.

"And so it goes, I suppose." She said, her eyes drifted lower as her cloak flowed against the wind of the flowing holy sea, the light that once shined attempted to snuff out, disappear even, but it did not leave... no... she did not disconnect it's sanctity. The light illuminated from her center the single thread of Luminalis shined, the connection re-established with something not entirely present. Her eyebrow raised as she looked from within her soul.

She could feel his presence, the one who bestowed her the blade, the one who had given her purpose. His mighty hand reaching down and scooping up the man with concise precision. Then there was another signature... one unfamiliar, then another very familiar... her eyes saw the thread converge off as the light paved a path, or rather a thread branching off into three different bonds.

"Master Darius?" She muttered to herself, her eyes looking to where the thread lead to as the usual tension sheded off her. The weight on her shoulders eased as the gaze from her eyes widened almost in an expression of shock, but before she could fully reach out to it... the connection faded. The threads vanished as the light closed, swallowing the sky in black, white, and gold. There she remained looking up to where the threads once connected in hope, in assurance that nothing was truly lost.

"Hm... I suppose I do have something to look forward to then..." She said, a soft chuckle leaving her lips as she shifted herself. The light swallowed into her frame as the golden glimmer faded into a silent hum. The sound of-

the crashing waves slowly dying with it, leaving nothing but a tranquil peace and silence.

The silence over the Holy Sea broke without warning.

There was no opening in space, no displaced air, no warning from Luminalis that something had crossed the distance. Kujo was simply there behind Haruki, standing upon the deck as though the sequence in which he had not been present had ceased to matter. His back remained turned to her, long black hair hanging against the absolute darkness covering everything beneath his neck, Xevorith wrapped around his body like a cape with no visible folds or ending.

For several seconds, Kujo did not move. The Fifth Layer of the Void hung from his shoulders so completely that even the Holy Sea’s reflected light disappeared wherever it touched him, while beneath that timeless blackness the Crevice beat with a slow, chaotic rhythm. His pale face turned only slightly toward the horizon, never granting Haruki the courtesy of looking at her.

“Haruki Yuno.”

His altered voice traveled across the deck in overlapping registers, one calm and recognizable while another seemed to arrive slightly before it, twisted by the timeless absence Kujo now carried. The tranquil water surrounding the vessel began to tremble, though there was still no wind and no physical force had yet touched the ship. Kujo breathed in slowly, tasting the sanctified atmosphere around him as though he had entered a place whose holiness existed only for him to violate.

*“Do you feel despair?”*

Then Kujo released his magical pressure.

It descended without explosion or spectacle, an immense metaphysical weight suddenly imposed across the vessel and everything immediately surrounding it. The deck screamed beneath Haruki’s feet as reinforced timber compressed, support beams bent downward, and the entire ship sank several feet deeper into the Holy Sea under pressure that did not merely push from above but declared that everything beneath Kujo ought to occupy less space. Masts bowed, railings fractured, and the hull flattened lower against the water as waves erupted outward from both sides.

The pressure struck Haruki next. It bore through body, Essence, and spiritual posture simultaneously, crushing down upon every attempt to remain upright until her knees were forced against the deck. Timber split beneath them from the impact, while the surrounding surface continued sinking under Kujo’s presence as though the ship itself had begun kneeling with her.

Kujo still did not turn around.

“You have spent so much of your life standing beneath things you believed were greater than you. Masters. Saints. Functions. Responsibilities.”

His black-covered hand lifted slightly at his side, and another pulse traveled outward. The Holy Sea depressed around the vessel in an enormous circular basin, water pushed away beneath invisible authority while black veins briefly flashed across its illuminated surface. Kujo’s pressure continued increasing with terrible patience rather than striking all at once, allowing every layer of the ship to groan through its own approaching failure.

“And each time one of them disappears, you find another thread and tell yourself that nothing was truly lost.”

His head tilted faintly. Beneath the cape of Xevorith, the Crevice surged, and the air around him briefly lost any meaningful distinction between stillness and collapse. The pressure became so dense that loose objects on deck flattened or shattered where they lay, while the remaining mast supports bent toward the planks beneath them.

“That is admirable.”

A warped laugh entered his voice.

“It is also why I came to you.”

Kujo finally began to turn his head, but stopped before his eyes could fully meet hers. The gesture was deliberate; even now he denied her the validation of his complete attention, standing with his back mostly toward her while forcing the Holy Sea itself to bear witness to the difference between them. His pale profile carried the edge of a smile that had become increasingly difficult to distinguish from hunger.

“Verinus believed suffering would build something.”

The black pressure thickened again.

“He believed every broken household, every dead keeper, every failed ward, every person left standing after I emptied thirty-five percent of this world would eventually become material for something righteous.”

Kujo’s smile widened.

“So I thought I would inspect the material.”

His fingers flexed once, and the ship compressed another degree beneath them. Wood buckled inward through the centerline, the keel protesting far below while water climbed unnaturally high along the sides before being forced away again by the same pressure pinning the vessel down. Kujo remained perfectly upright through all of it, untouched by the destruction his presence produced.

“Look at what remains of your Holy Sea.”

His voice descended into a harsher register.

“Look at the churches that will stop answering. Look at the empty houses. Look at all those beautiful little bonds suddenly leading nowhere.”

The Fifth Layer stirred around him like a cape beneath nonexistent wind. For one instant, Kujo’s shadow became impossibly deep, and something much larger than his human form seemed to stand inside it, horned, segmented, and carrying the outline of an enormous execution limb before disappearing back beneath the black.

“Then tell me, Haruki Yuno.”

Kujo’s head turned just enough for one dark eye to become visible over his shoulder.

*“Do you still believe hope is something the world gives back simply because you refuse to let go?”*

"You mistaken the conjunction for being hope, something closely relied to faith, but I am not chosen to run due to simply because I believe it..." She said, her eyes shifting as she turned back to look at him. What he saw was nothing more than a reflection of what was shown, a darkness... but not the kind that ate or disturbed like the void... but something else.

"I've simply lost the meaning, and the reason as to why I do what I must do. I simply am what I chose to become, and that is a consequence I pay for dearly." She said, there was no grief, no fear, only an emptiness that filled the space around the light of her being. The light had shown and lived with the darkness, just as much as how the sun has existed along side the moon.

"Do I feel despair? I most certainly do not... I only feel the overbearing weight of the duties judger gives me." She said, looking down at the blade she carries, the scripture and the markings that was carved and imprinted into the material of the blade.

"I've repeated the cycle from time and time again... and it means no differently what happens now... because I understand it truly for what it is worth." She said, her blade concealed away back in it's sheathe, the silence surrounding her alternating as she faced Kujo fully.

"But make no mistake... I do not bare any hatred... nor do I bare any sense of resentment against you." She said, her eyes showing something soft, something distant and yet cold. "Because now I have seen the details before they have been embedded, the light still shines down against you... even if you have snuffed out it's brightest light." She said a smirk curving on the side of her face. The inner Darkness and Inner Light mixing into each other to form a singularity, an explosive realization and renewal of the power she had suppressed for a long time.

"Destroy our cities, pillage our homes, ruin our cathedrals, run from the fate that has been carried... it does not change the result. It only seals the fate."

She said as her smirk faded, the neutral and distant expression remaining as she sharpened her gaze to look into the eyes of Kujo. The spirit inside of her burned like a flame, and dissoluted the shadow it casted over her... replacing it with only what she had observed from Venrius.

"And if Venrius did not choose to carry the Will of Judger... then I shall take upon it's mantle, as the one true candidate."

Kujo listened until she finished, his back still turned toward her, the black of Xevorith hanging from his shoulders like a seamless cape that swallowed every trace of light beneath his neck. For several seconds he remained motionless, then his head tilted slightly as though he had heard something disappointing rather than defiant. When he finally turned, the expression on his face carried none of the anger her words might have expected; only a thin, almost exhausted amusement.

“No. There is no cycle.”

His dark eyes settled on her completely now.

“A cycle returns to its beginning. What happened before does not return merely because you recognize similarities in it. Verinus made choices. You are making choices. If you repeat his posture, inherit his burden, and walk toward the same grave, that is not recurrence imposed by the world. That is you selecting the same direction and calling the resemblance destiny.”

The black mantle moved around Kujo despite the absence of wind, its edges crawling over the flattened deck like spilled night. His smile sharpened when she spoke of choosing what she had become, because to him that declaration did nothing to escape consequence. Choice did not become freedom simply because the person making it understood that payment would follow.

“You say you are what you chose to become. Fine. Then stop speaking as though that protects the choice from examination.”

Kujo took one slow step closer.

“A condemned man can choose the scaffold. A fanatic can choose his altar. A martyr can choose the knife. Awareness does not sanctify the decision, Haruki. Sometimes it only means you walked into the mistake with your eyes open.”

Her claim that despair had not touched her brought a low laugh from him. The sound was quieter than before, almost conversational, though the magical pressure around the vessel remained heavy enough to keep its timbers bowed toward the Holy Sea. Kujo watched the emptiness around her light and regarded it not as transcendence, but as something she had renamed because admitting its shape would require conceding his question.

“You say there is no despair because what you feel is duty. That is merely despair given employment.”

His voice twisted faintly beneath the influence of Xevorith.

“You have lost meaning. You have lost reason. You admit the burden remains anyway, and then you call carrying it responsibility because the alternative would require asking whether the burden still deserves you. Emptiness is not proof that despair failed to reach you. Sometimes emptiness is what remains after it has finished.”

Kujo’s gaze lowered briefly toward the sheathed blade, then returned to her face. He seemed almost offended by the certainty that repetition itself somehow guaranteed an outcome, particularly after everything he had just demonstrated about sequence. The Fifth Layer wrapped closer around him as though the Timeless Void itself enjoyed the argument.

“And this insistence that what happens now changes nothing because you understand the pattern, that is the weakest claim you have made.”

A pale smile returned.

“I carry a layer of the Void whose entire principle is that sequence does not possess automatic authority over the present. I can remove yesterday’s right to explain what I am today, and you stand in front of me telling me repetition guarantees tomorrow. No. A sequence is not a prophecy merely because frightened people recognize its shape.”

Kujo raised one hand slightly, palm turned toward her. The black covering his body stretched from his arm without separating from him, becoming several narrow strands that hovered above the deck like shadows learning how to possess muscle. His attention remained calmly fixed on Haruki as he addressed her lack of hatred.

“You do not hate me. Excellent. Hatred would be irrelevant.”

His fingers curled.

“A blade does not become less sharp because the person beneath it forgives the hand holding it. Your resentment is not required for my actions to matter, and your mercy does not impose a law upon me. You have confused refusing hatred with escaping consequence.”

He looked upward toward the darkened sky when she invoked the light continuing to shine upon him. Something in Kujo’s expression changed there, becoming colder as the remnants of his earlier encounter with Verinus and the Judger crossed his thoughts. Being witnessed by a higher law had not humbled him; it had only given him another relationship to test.

“The light may shine wherever it pleases.”

Kujo’s smile widened.

“It looked upon me already. It weighed me. It gave its finding, and I removed the privilege that finding had to become part of my present. You are still speaking as though observation and authority are the same thing.”

The black strands abruptly moved.

They crossed the space between them with almost no visible transition, one winding around Haruki’s throat while others locked around her shoulders and torso. Kujo did not yank immediately. He let the grip tighten slowly enough for the intent to become unmistakable, then stepped toward the edge of the ruined vessel while the darkness dragged her with him.

“And as for fate being sealed…” His expression became openly amused. “You keep confusing prediction with enforcement.”

The black around Haruki’s neck pulled hard, taking her from the deck and over the railing. Kujo followed without falling, Xevorith carrying him downward as though the distance between the ship and the sea had simply been removed from consideration. They struck the Holy Sea and descended beneath it, blackness spreading around them faster than the remaining surface light could follow.

The ocean became colder and heavier with every meter. Kujo continued downward into regions where pressure should have crushed ordinary bodies, his Void preventing the water from touching him while allowing its weight to remain brutally real around Haruki. Above them, the surface diminished into a distant pale distortion, then disappeared entirely beneath the depth.

Kujo stopped only when there was nothing visible around them but darkness.

“This is closer.”

His voice reached her clearly despite the water, carried directly through the black wrapped around her throat.

“No sermons. No cathedral. No witnesses telling you what your suffering means. Just pressure, darkness, and the body discovering that conviction does not provide oxygen.”

The black tightened around her neck while the surrounding water bore inward from every direction. Kujo watched without haste, letting the suffocation and crushing depth become part of the lesson rather than merely an attempt to kill her. Thin images began flickering through the darkness around them, not illusions of false events, but impressions drawn from the consequences already spreading through the Inner World: empty homes, extinguished wards, abandoned children, silent streets, half-filled tables.

“This is sorrow without doctrine.”

Another scene flashed through the dark, a household reduced to survivors staring at spaces that would never be filled again.

“This is despair before somebody writes a prayer over it.”

Another appeared: a ward dying hours after its keeper, its people discovering protection only after losing it.

“And this is what remains when nobody arrives in time to explain that the pain was secretly building something beautiful.”

Kujo’s free hand disappeared beneath the black mantle. When it emerged again, the darkness had hardened along his fingers into a narrow, blade-like point without sheen or reflection. He drove it into Haruki’s side and withdrew it almost immediately, not allowing the injury the mercy of becoming a single decisive wound.

Then another black blade formed.

Kujo stabbed again, changing the angle before withdrawing, the darkness sealing just enough around each injury to prevent the exchange from ending too quickly. He repeated the motion with deliberate spacing, each strike separated by enough time for pain to register before the next one arrived.

“You called what you carry a consequence.”

Another thrust entered and withdrew.

“Then carry this.”

The black shifted and struck again, Kujo’s expression remaining disturbingly attentive rather than furious. He was not losing himself in the violence; that was what made it crueler. Each wound was measured to hurt, weaken, and continue without immediately ending the body experiencing it.

“You want Verinus’s mantle.”

Another stab.

“You want the Judger’s burden.”

Another.

“You want to become the candidate.”

The black point paused against her before driving forward again.

“Then understand the first requirement of inheriting somebody’s suffering.”

Kujo leaned closer through the ocean darkness, his pale face the only human thing visible inside the enormous black shape Xevorith had made of him. The grip around Haruki’s throat tightened while the pressure of the Holy Sea continued crushing inward, leaving her suspended between suffocation, depth, and the repeated violence of the black.

“The suffering does not care what you call it.”

Kujo withdrew the blade and allowed it to dissolve back into the cape. He did not release her throat, nor did he begin ascending; instead he held her there in the absolute dark, surrounded by the consequences she had insisted could not alter the result. His eyes remained fixed upon her with the cold curiosity of someone waiting to see which belief survived when every comforting interpretation had been stripped away.

“And this is not a cycle, Haruki Yuno.”

His smile returned in the darkness.

“This is simply happening to you now.”

She winced, multiple times when the blade had gone through her, though despite this she did not stutter... she did not cry. The pain was harsh, but harsher was the memory. Her eyes looking back at Kujo as his sadistic glee shined through the cracks of his already deteriorated patience. Her eyes glowed softly, not in reaction of the Danshi she possessed, but of the truth denying itself right in the grasp of his hands. He felt the irritation before the sizzling, the burning sensation that surrounded him and the darkness this was not resistance, no... resistance required retaliation, required the willingness to evade, but she refused to move.

"Spare me of your excuses... you speak as if these are observations, as if they are truths to the facts, but all I can understand is need to evade... you can't embrace it as well as Venrius or I have." She said, the light was held in a spark around her core, she felt the pain of the darkness surrounding her, it's cold and harsh grasp around her fragile frame, but embedded deep inside... he got the answer he was looking for... there was a fire, it's spark held in her palms, conjoined and yet unsure what to do with it.

"Venrius never acknowledged the will, he carried it's weight but never acknowledged the testament it brought when it was needed. This is not tracing a path, this is the continuation of a path that I am already walking. I understood the weight, and now I no longer see it as burden... this is merely the toll it takes to be where I am." She said, her hand drifting over the spot of her wound as the cascade of her two-toned hair flowed freely in the salty breeze of the wind.

"Silence me... rend me into ribbons and shackle me, it does not matter. The absolute truth lies behind your eyes... and I am the only one who has seen it until now... the insecurity behind those eyes... you are shaken because you know you have failed." She said, her eyes shining a light onto his face. 

"I do not deny your claims... perhaps they-are right to what they seem to be spoken... but tell me... what good is a truth without a conviction?" She questioned, her frame straightening as she could feel the blood dripping down her frame, though the blade has struck in the same place multiple times... the pain seemed to wash away, or rather... it was ignored.

"You speak of my mentors, but you have never seen them as I have. Their names are a privilege that has been carved into fate, you have lost that same privilege when you spoken out of false truths. So do not lecture me on what I am doing is right or wrong, I am fully aware of what I am, and what I can do to a slug like you." She said, her eyes narrowing with a mild anger in her eyes. Seeing as how he continues to chase ignorance.

Kujo regarded her for several seconds beneath the crushing dark of the Holy Sea, and whatever irritation had been present in him began to disappear. Haruki could call him frightened, ignorant, a failure, or a slug; none of it touched the thing that had actually brought him here. The black around her throat remained firm while Xevorith covered Kujo from his shoulders downward like a seamless cape, and his pale face settled into an expression almost disappointingly calm.

“You keep reaching for insult whenever your doctrine stops carrying the argument. Insecurity. Failure. Ignorance. Convenient words, because they allow you to decide what I am feeling instead of answering what I am demonstrating.”

The darkness around them contracted, and the abyssal pressure of the Holy Sea suddenly vanished. Kujo used the Void to remove the interval between the ocean floor and the distant coast, dragging both of them through absence without ascent, transition, or passage through the water above. One moment there was suffocating black ocean around them; the next, Kujo stood upon a desolate stretch of pale shoreline with the ruined Holy Sea behind him, the black tether still wrapped around Haruki’s neck as seawater struck the ground around them.

He released her only after they had arrived. The darkness withdrew from her throat and returned beneath the cape of Xevorith, while Kujo turned toward the water and breathed in the salt air as though the violence beneath it had been little more than an interruption. Behind his exposed head, the Fifth Layer hung impossibly still, swallowing every contour of his body and giving the impression that a human face had been placed atop a standing piece of night.

“You say you are fully aware of what you are. Good. Then awareness will make what comes next much more useful.”

Kujo lifted one black-covered hand.

Nothing struck Haruki. Instead, something disappeared.

The sensation began at the tips of her fingers. The Void did not visibly consume them, yet Kujo imposed the complete sensory and metaphysical conviction that they were no longer entirely attached to existence. Touch began losing certainty first; the distinction between skin and surrounding air became difficult to locate, followed by the sickening impression that the boundary separating her hand from everything around it was quietly being withdrawn.

Kujo watched.

“This is not injury.”

His fingers moved slightly.

“An injury leaves you something to identify. A wound tells you where you end and the damage begins.”

The effect crawled upward.


Her hands seemed to lose their history. Not memory exactly, but the underlying certainty that they had always belonged to her arms, that her arms belonged to her shoulders, that those shoulders had occupied a continuous body from one moment into the next. Kujo used the Void to simulate those relationships disappearing one after another, allowing every physical piece to remain visibly present while convincing every deeper faculty of perception that the connections holding the whole together were quietly being erased.

“This is closer to what you keep pretending you understand.”

The black cape shifted behind him.

“Not death. Not pain. Not martyrdom.”

His smile appeared slowly.

“Unbecoming.”

The sensation reached her arms and shoulders. Kujo allowed the illusion of bodily continuity to fray in meticulous stages, never fast enough for shock to conceal the experience. Her blood could still move, lungs could still breathe, and heart could still beat, yet Xevorith supplied the horrifying perception that these processes no longer belonged to one person and were merely unrelated events temporarily occurring near one another.

Kujo stepped closer as the effect reached her chest.

“Your heart is beating.”

His voice lowered.

“But suppose the next beat has no obligation to remember the previous one.”

The timeless black around his body stirred.

“Suppose the sequence called Haruki Yuno is not killed, because killing would acknowledge there was something coherent enough to end.”

His eyes darkened.

“Suppose it simply stops being assembled.”

Kujo introduced The Unwinding into the simulation.

He did not actually remove Haruki’s past; he made her experience what it would feel like if those past sequences began losing their claim upon the present. Memories remained visible inside the mind, but their ownership became uncertain. A face remembered from childhood could still be recalled, yet the person remembering it no longer felt unquestionably identical to the child who had originally seen it.

The sensation spread through increasingly important memories. Lessons could remain without the certainty of who had learned them, convictions without the moment they had been chosen, grief without a stable person who had suffered the loss. Even the meaning attached to mentors and vows began to feel distant, not erased, but separated from the continuity that allowed someone to say that happened to me.

Kujo’s expression became almost fascinated.

“You spoke so confidently about continuation.”

He tilted his head.

“So continue.”

The simulated erasure moved into her Name. That was where Kujo slowed down.

He allowed the words Haruki Yuno to remain perfectly understandable while stripping away, piece by piece, the feeling that the Name necessarily referred to the consciousness hearing it. The sounds became a designation belonging to somebody remembered rather than somebody present, and every attempt to reconnect identity to that designation encountered another thin layer of absolute nothing.

“Haruki Yuno.”

Kujo spoke the Name deliberately.

“Do you recognize her?”

A pause followed as the Void deepened.

“Do not answer too quickly.”

The shore around them began participating in the illusion. Footprints behind her seemed to lose their association with whoever had made them, disturbed sand ceased implying passage, and droplets of seawater falling from clothing stopped feeling like evidence that a body had recently emerged from the ocean. Kujo was constructing the experience of a world that could observe every consequence of Haruki’s existence while gradually losing the ability to recognize that there had ever been a singular person responsible for them.

He walked slowly around her.

“This is what your conviction cannot solve.”

His altered voice seemed to emerge from different positions around the shore.

“You can choose duty. You can inherit a mantle. You can declare yourself candidate, successor, testament, saint, weapon, servant, judge.”

Kujo stopped behind her.

“But every declaration requires an I.”

The blackness surged.

For several terrible seconds, Kujo simulated the disappearance of that final reference point.

Not unconsciousness. Not darkness. Awareness remained, but with nowhere to stand. Thought occurred without feeling owned. Sensation occurred without a stable body claiming it. Memory appeared without a continuous observer linking one memory to another, until existence became a collection of events with no central statement capable of saying these are mine.

Kujo let her remain inside that fabricated dissolution.

Then his pale face appeared within her vision again, the only stable reference in the experience because he deliberately preserved himself as such. His black-covered hand rested near her face without touching, the cape of Xevorith stretching behind him into a darkness too deep for the coastline to define.

“Now tell yourself this is merely a toll.”

His voice carried no laughter anymore.

“Tell yourself that you understand what you are while the meaning of you is being removed.”

Kujo tightened the simulation around the Soul rather than physically damaging it. The sense of Essence belonging to an individual pattern became vague, followed by the impression that Fate-connections were reaching toward an address that no longer existed. The cruelest part was that he kept everything functionally intact beneath the deception, ensuring the experience could continue instead of granting the release genuine dissolution would have provided.

“You accused me of evasion.”

A small smile returned.

“I have entered five layers of the Void precisely because I wanted to know what remained after every comforting answer stopped working.”

The darkness around Haruki deepened once more.

“You speak of truth as though conviction completes it. It does not.”

Kujo lowered his hand.

“Conviction merely determines how tightly you hold the truth you selected when another one begins tearing your fingers apart.”

Then he removed her simulated body from her perception entirely.

The shore remained. The Holy Sea remained. Kujo remained. But within the imposed experience there were no hands, no legs, no breath, no heartbeat, and no discernible point from which awareness originated. Haruki had not actually been erased by even a fraction, yet Kujo made every available sense insist that the process had nearly finished.

His voice reached that bodiless awareness through the emptiness.

“You wanted to know the absolute truth behind my eyes.”

The Fifth Layer spread across the horizon.

“Look carefully.”

Kujo allowed one final element to disappear: the simulated expectation that there would necessarily be another moment afterward. Xevorith stripped sequence from perception until the present seemed like an isolated point surrounded on both sides by nothing, with no past required to produce it and no future promised to follow. For several seconds, Kujo made ceasing to exist feel not like something approaching, but like something that had already begun and simply had not finished noticing itself.

Then he restored everything at once.

Body. Name. Memory. Continuity. Breath. Heartbeat. The weight of the ground. The salt wind against skin. Every relationship Kujo had only pretended to remove returned with enough suddenness to make existence itself feel violent.

Kujo stood several paces away, still covered entirely in black beneath the neck.

“I could have simply hurt you again.”

His head tilted.

“That would have been easier.”

A thin smile crossed his pale face.

“But pain lets people like you become heroic.”

The timeless cape settled behind him.

“I wanted you to experience something your martyrdom has no language for.”

Kujo turned slightly toward the Holy Sea.

“Not suffering for your conviction.”

His dark eyes moved back toward her.

“The possibility that one day there may be nobody left for that conviction to belong to.”

He came up into open air through a hole that had been a stair.

Cold on the new skin of his thighs, which had no hair on it and no history in it and reported temperature at a volume the old skin had never used. Wet ash to the ankle, sucking at each foot, giving up smoke where it broke. The smell was lime and burnt beeswax and, under those, the flat mineral nothing of a place where four hundred years of stone had stopped being stone in the course of an afternoon.

No hum anywhere. The ward was cut and the standpipes had emptied and the gauge-housings in the sealed conduit hung open and dead, and Altherion at the fourth hour was the quietest it had been since the city was a shrine and a well.

Aurelian Prudentius put his hands down at his sides and let them hang.

The dead were still going.

They came past the precinct in a slow spiral, gathering from every direction across the Inner World and turning down through the same aperture, and the aperture had not been built for this volume. He could feel the crowding in his chest where the crowding was. Souls in traffic. Dread coming off them in a sheet, and pain, most of it the pain of the last four minutes still being carried because there had been no time to put it down.

A woman going past with the shape of a task still on her, both arms held as though a basket were in them.

A man who kept stopping, which is the worst thing they do.

Children who had no idea they were moving.

They were suffering as they went in, and there was a bottleneck at the crossing, and the bottleneck was the same fact from the other side that the Measurewrights would spend a season failing to explain about their scale-pans.

His eyes changed.

He felt the moment it happened as a pressure behind the orbits and a cooling across both corneas, and the world came apart into its actual grain. The silver went out of them. What replaced it went black from the pupil outward, and around the black a corona stood in the iris, a deep violet with a light behind it, and it did not come from the sky and the sky had nothing to give it.

He saw all of it.

Every fate in the precinct, and then every fate in the city, and then the lines going out past the coast, and past that. Thirty-one years he had done this one thread at a time, at cost, with a headache after. Here was the whole cloth. Every branch that came off every choice and every branch off those, backward into what had already been decided and forward into what had not, and all the pain distributed along all of it, at once, held in front of him at a size that had no business fitting behind a man's face.

He looked at it with interest.

That was the part he noticed. Somewhere under the flood a small piece of Aurelian Prudentius stood up and observed, in the tone of a clerk finding an error in a column, that a man shown this much should be on the ground and screaming, and that he had a warm and pleasant feeling in his chest instead, and that this was worth entering somewhere.

He did not enter it.

The corona thinned and the black withdrew into the pupil and the silver came back, and the world went ordinary and small, and his hands were still at his sides and had not moved.

Boots in the ash. Several people, running badly on bad footing, and one of them well ahead of the others.

"Your holiness."

Charles Lambert went down onto one knee in the wet ash without checking what was under it and bowed his head, and the hat came off and went into both hands, and stayed there.

Forty-one, and looked older tonight. A wide, plain, north-coast face, cheeks gone red with cold and with running, a jaw that had been square before the last two years put a softness under it. Hair the brown of a wet rope, cut short and grown out of the cut, plastered flat across the forehead. Big shoulders and a big chest and the beginnings of a stomach, and the archbishop's black over all of it, sodden to mid-thigh, with the pectoral cross gone round under his arm and hanging at his ribs where he had not noticed it. Ink on the first two fingers of the right hand. A thin white line through the left eyebrow, old, the kind a child gets off a stone step.

Behind him, four of the guard, stopping at a distance nobody had told them to stop at.

The stars came out from behind the last of the ash-cloud and put light on the precinct, and Aurelian turned his face up into it, and it lay on him and reflected off him the way light lies on water and off it.

They were in the wrong places.

Every one of them. The whole vault over Altherion was an arrangement nobody living had seen, hanging there since the sky had been diluted and the hand had closed, and it had not gone back. Three of the guard behind Charles were looking up at it. One of them had been looking up at it since he arrived and had forgotten to lower his halberd.

"Your holiness, you are here." Charles's voice had gone thin at the top. "We were worried for your health."

Aurelian smiled at him.

It was warm. He put the whole of himself behind it, and it worked, because his face had always been good at that, and the four molars that used to sit in the lower left of it were somewhere in the Void in another man's jaw and the gum had closed over smooth. He kept the smile small on that side out of habit.

"You had no reason to fear, Charles Lambert."

He came forward through the ash. The wet ash took his weight and let it go and the ground under him went down a coin's width and stayed down and he did not look at it.
