"And you have no reason to fear for the safety of Dabney Lambert. My eyes have confirmed both things."

Charles's head came up.

*He said the name.*

*Nineteen years the old man would not put that name in a room with more than two people in it. He would not put it in a day-book. He sat me on a cold step and told me why and I have not slept properly since, and here it is said out loud in a burned precinct with the guard standing at ten feet and the sky in the wrong order over all of us, and he said it kindly. He said it to comfort me.*

Charles got up out of the ash. He did it slowly.

"Your holiness. Forgive me. We must get you to safety. The Ward is cut, the lower cloister is gone, there is no roof on the chapter house and the Curia has been sitting since the second hour without the Palatine and without any word of him, and there are men in this city tonight who will want you where they can see you."

Aurelian shook his head. He put his hand on Charles Lambert's shoulder, and the hand was young, and Charles looked at it.

"Mm. You have no reason to worry, Archbishop."

"With respect, holiness, I have every."

"I have seen these events." The hand stayed where it was. "Seen, Charles, with the eyes. All of them, in the order they will arrive and in three other orders they will not, and I have looked at what happens to this city and at what happens to me, and I find that I am not afraid of any of it." He let a breath out through his nose. "I am no longer afraid, Charles. Of anything. That is a new condition and I am still becoming familiar with it."

Charles Lambert did not answer.

*I have known this man twenty-six years. He was afraid of the Curia. He was afraid of the vote. He was afraid of the Seventh Objection and afraid of what would happen if he ever moved on it, and he was afraid every single day, and every single day he got up and put the vestment on and went and did it anyway, and that was the whole of what made him worth kneeling to. Fear was the instrument. He was the man who felt the weight.*

*They have taken him underground for a fortnight and given him back to me weighing nothing.*

"There is only one path to peace now, Charles," said Aurelian.

He took the hand off the shoulder.

"War."

The sky opened.

It came as a ripple first, a long slow warping of the star-field to the north-east of the precinct, and the wrong constellations bent through it and came back wrong in a different way, and then the ripple went to a mouth, and the mouth was the near end of a tunnel with nothing on the walls and no light in the length of it, and it ran back further than the sky was deep.

Kaalabad came down it.

He came head-first and fast, out of the mouth at eighty feet with the halberd already up and back over the right shoulder in the loaded position that no living halberdier used because no living halberdier could recover from it, and the plate across his shoulders sat correct on a frame that had since changed its mind about being a frame, and the helm sat a few degrees off true over a skull that helms are not made for.

Aurelian saw the whole strike before it left the top of the arc.

The read cost him nothing. That was the second thing worth entering that he did not enter. The strike was Percussion at its plainest, no Sweep and no Return, a Settling delivered in one piece from a height with the whole mass of him behind it and the tunnel's exit velocity added to the top of that, and the halberd head coming down was old, and dirty, and the marks on it were old, and it was going to arrive at the crown of Aurelian's skull.

He lifted two fingers.

Time in a sphere perhaps four yards across came apart.

It did not stop. It went to grain. The interval between one instant and the next opened up and became a thing with a width to it, and inside that width the halberd head continued to descend and did so through a substance that had begun charging by the inch. The air in the sphere went visibly thick. Ash hanging in it stopped falling and did not hover, and instead came apart into its own separate motions, individual flakes each keeping a different hour.

The halberd head reached Aurelian's two fingers and touched them.

Nothing entered him.

Every last unit of what had been coming down that shaft, the mass and the drop and the tunnel's gift and the whole standing effort of a body built at the Zenith to deliver exactly this, arrived at a point four inches wide and was not absorbed and was not turned. It was put out.

It went sideways.

The kinetic wave came out of the sphere in a flat ring at knee height and crossed the precinct in every direction at once, and the wet ash went off the ground in a wall and kept going, and the four guardsmen behind Charles Lambert left their feet and travelled and landed and slid. Charles went down on his back with his hat still in his hand. Every window remaining on the south face of the chapter house went out of its frame in one movement, glass and lead and all. The precinct wall, which had stood through the fire, took the ring at its base and shed its top three courses.

Out at the edges the ring stopped being force. It came apart into something that had no push in it, a pressure across the skin like the moment before a decision, and it went through the standing men and through the ash and out into the streets beyond and every dog in the Cloister quarter began to scream at once.

Aurelian's arm had not moved. His shoulder had not moved. His feet were where they had been.

The ground under him went down another coin's width.

Somewhere else, a great way from Altherion, along a seam that a young man in a gold robe had become and had then been lifted out of, a hairline fault the width of nothing widened by the width of slightly more than nothing.

He did not feel it. There was no mechanism by which he could have felt it. He had not been told he was load-bearing.

Kaalabad hung above him in the thick air with both hands still on the haft.

He came down onto the ash and landed heavily and stayed in the crouch, and the ground took it, and the halberd stayed in contact with the two fingers, and he set his back and his hips and his heels and put the entire architecture of himself into a shaft that had stopped being able to go anywhere.

*It did not go in.*

*Forty years. It has gone into a gate, and a mountain shoulder, and the neck plate of a thing that had no business having a neck, and it has never in forty years failed to go in. It is going in now. Every part of me is doing what it does and the arithmetic is correct and the head is not moving and there is a man on the other end of it with his hands at his sides.*

Aurelian looked up at him with mild interest.

Then he flicked the first finger.

The halberd went out of Kaalabad's grip and across the precinct and through the standing remains of the north colonnade and out the other side, and it took two columns going through and buried itself to the beard in the cathedral's west footing at a distance of ninety yards, and stood there humming.

The thick air let go all at once. Ash resumed falling. All of it landed together.

Kaalabad straightened out of the crouch. Both hands were open and neither had anything in it, and he looked at them, and then at the colonnade, and then down at the man in front of him, and the scarred band around his throat above the gorget moved as he swallowed.

"Hopefully," said Aurelian pleasantly, "I can excuse you this one."

Kaalabad's head came up.

"WHO THE HELL TOLD YOU YOU COULD LEAVE."

It went off the cathedral face and came back off the chapter house and rolled out over the quarter, and Charles Lambert, flat on his back in the ash twenty feet away, flinched at the volume of it, and one of the guardsmen made a small sound.

Aurelian let the silence sit for a moment.

He looked at Kaalabad, and the look went on past the point where a look is a look, and the silver in his eyes did not change and did not need to, and the ground between the two of them settled a third time.

"You are far too weak to give me any enjoyment," he said. "It would be a disgrace to the grace of the Judger to kill something this pathetic."

He turned his back on the halberdier.

He walked over to where Charles Lambert was getting up out of the ash on one elbow with his hat crushed in his fist, and he crouched, and he put his hand under the archbishop's arm and lifted him onto his feet with no effort at all.

She gasped for air as if it were scarce, for a moment... she had remembered something about her that she did not intend to surface. 

*'How long? How long have I endured to forget the foundation?'* She thought to herself, for a moment she saw horror, then the next she saw renewal. This was the inevitable destruction she experienced, and yet in moments she remained there, frozen and yet alive. 

She looked down at her wound, seeing as the light had shined through for a moment. It stung more than it did sear, but she saw as the wound had closed, not healed, closed. She looked over at Kujo for a moment, something reached at her as she heard his laughs... not Kujo's... one far to familiar to her... Malakar.

Almost as if it were instinct, she fled, not out of something heroic... but out of the instinctual desire of self-preservation. She did not expect this... to be broken so easily because of how evil had faced her before... this was far different... the distinction was almost uncanny, but the swagger and the movement felt all to different.

*'Why? Why do I still fear? Why do I still seem... weak?'* She thought to herself, the strength in her legs carrying her to what was to be the ruins of a gate, Aurelian, the sounds came out almost as reaction.

Another vision played through her gaze, her first arrival into Kushara, the memory remembered, relived. She flinched as she held onto her head, the effects of the almost real illusion still affecting the fragility of her mind. 

She pushed passed the gates to see the scene, Kalaabad, Charles Lambert, and Aurelian. The few standing there, the anger prominent in the air as though it had seethed through her entire frame. Though she did not recoil back in fear, despite the anxiety. The weight crumbling at her will as she leaned against the wall. The strength she had seemingly collapsing in on her, the strain on her heart pounding furiously as she felt her mind go into a haze.

"No... Not again... never again... I promised-myself to never succumb to it... and yet... I saw it all before my eyes... why do I still run... why do I still hide?" She asked herself, her hands trembled softly as she clenched her fist, attempting to recollect her pride, her confidence into the soft fragile shield she held up. It truly was embarrassing the way she appeared right now, and she knew it all too well.

Kujo watched Haruki flee without pursuing her. The black of Xevorith remained draped over him from the neck downward, motionless against the salt wind while her presence diminished beyond the ruined gate. He had gotten what he wanted from her already; not submission, not ideological agreement, but the moment beneath all of her doctrine where instinct had taken control and her body had chosen survival before conviction could manufacture a reason for it.

“I’m finished with you, Haruki Yuno.”

There was no triumph in the declaration. Kujo merely turned his head toward the Holy Sea, listening to the distant cadence of her retreat while the faintest smile touched his face. He had no interest in chasing her simply to prove he could kill her; death would have allowed her to leave the encounter with the dignity of martyrdom, and Kujo preferred the memory she now had to carry while alive.

“You wanted to know whether you could endure me. Now you know there is a difference between enduring pain and discovering something inside yourself that still knows how to run.”

The darkness beneath his shoulders rippled once as the Fifth Layer opened around him. Kujo stepped into it, but before Xevorith completely removed him from the shore, his attention shifted elsewhere. A disturbance had reached him from Altherion, not through ordinary sound, but through the enormous change in weight that had just passed through the city, the violent displacement of a strike that should have reduced a man to ruin and instead had been redirected across an entire precinct.

Kujo stopped halfway into the Timeless Void.

His smile faded.

For several seconds he simply listened through absence. Kalaabad’s familiar presence remained intact, humiliated but alive, while another signature stood where Kujo had expected far less. Aurelian Prudentius had changed. The frightened ecclesiastical sovereign Kujo’s designs had once accounted for was producing readings that no longer resembled the limits previously assigned to him, and whatever had happened beneath Altherion had returned him to the world with something far more dangerous than confidence.

“Interesting.”

Kujo withdrew fully into Xevorith, and the shoreline disappeared around him. Within the Fifth Layer there was no meaningful distance between his awareness and the ruined precinct; he did not need to arrive physically to understand enough of what had occurred. He found the broken colonnade, the embedded halberd, the displaced ash, and the temporal disturbance where an enormous quantity of force had been taken apart and redirected without ever entering Aurelian’s body.

Then Kujo found Aurelian himself.

The lack of fear interested him first.

Not courage. Kujo knew the difference. Courage retained fear and selected action despite it; what stood in Altherion had apparently lost the instrument entirely. The distinction caused Kujo’s eyes to narrow, because fear had once been one of Aurelian’s most reliable constraints, and a man who lost a constraint without losing intellect did not merely become stronger. He became less predictable.

“So they returned you without the trembling.”

Kujo’s voice echoed through Xevorith while his attention followed the changed spiritual architecture surrounding Aurelian. There was power there, certainly, but raw power interested him less than the altered relationship between perception and decision. Aurelian had seen Kalaabad’s descending strike before completion, dismantled its temporal sequence into measurable intervals, discharged its consequence sideways, and then treated the creature responsible as though it had interrupted a conversation.

Kujo smiled again.

“And now you think war is peace.”

The Crevice pulsed beneath the black covering his body. There was something almost beautiful about the progression: Verinus had been removed into Utopia, Aurelian had emerged transformed, and one of Kujo’s own generals had been immediately reduced to an inconvenience before him. Kujo did not feel embarrassment on Kalaabad’s behalf. If anything, the humiliation gave him useful information faster than a victory would have.

“Good.”

His smile widened slightly.

“Kalaabad was told to test this world. He has just done so.”

Kujo allowed his awareness to linger on the temporal sphere that had dismantled the halberd’s momentum. The technique itself could be studied later; what interested him now was that Aurelian had performed it without the strain Kujo would have expected from his previous state. Even more telling was the ground settling beneath him each time his authority expressed itself, as though the Material World were beginning to accept his presence with a weight that had not belonged to him before.

“This is not the man who went underground.”

Kujo’s layered voice became quieter.

“Something returned wearing his history.”

He did not mean possession. The distinction mattered. Aurelian was still Aurelian, but altered enough that Kujo refused to use old predictions as though continuity guaranteed sameness. Xevorith had taught him the exact opposite: the present did not owe obedience to the version of a person that existed yesterday.

Kujo raised one hand, blackness extending from his covered wrist as he traced the distant signature without touching it. He found the brief opening of those changed eyes, the black pupil, violet corona, the impossible breadth of Fate-perception that had unfolded and then receded. That earned a much more serious expression.

“There you are.”

Kujo’s fingers curled slightly.

“You looked at the whole cloth and enjoyed it.”

That interested him more than the halberd.

A man exposed to that much distributed suffering should have recoiled. Instead, Aurelian had regarded it with the quiet pleasure of someone discovering that an instrument finally fit his hand. Kujo understood immediately that whatever had returned from beneath Altherion was not simply empowered by the Judger; something fundamental about Aurelian’s internal relationship to judgment had changed.

Then came the declaration Kujo had heard through the fractured spiritual terrain.

There is only one path to peace now. War.

A laugh escaped him.

Not the manic laughter the Crevice sometimes tore from him, but something smaller and considerably more interested.

“Oh, Aurelian.”

Kujo lowered his hand.

“You have no idea how much I agree.”

Xevorith wrapped more tightly around him as Kujo turned away from the distant image of Altherion. He had no intention of rushing there immediately to punish Kalaabad’s failure or challenge Aurelian simply because the man had demonstrated new strength. That would have been exactly the kind of reaching Verinus had accused him of, and Kujo was intelligent enough to recognize useful criticism even when he despised its source.

“Keep your war.”

His smile returned beneath darkened eyes.

“Build it carefully.”

The Crevice throbbed once beneath the seamless black cape.

“I want to see what you become when you finally have enough enemies to justify everything you have always wanted to do.”

Then Kujo disappeared deeper into the Fifth Layer, leaving Altherion untouched for the moment. Haruki had been broken open enough to reveal the instinct beneath her conviction, Kalaabad had discovered a new limit, and Aurelian had announced himself as something fundamentally changed.

For Kujo, the evening had become considerably more interesting.

Kaalabad did not leave.

He stood in the ash with his hands empty and his halberd ninety yards away in the cathedral footing, and he looked at Aurelian's back for the length of three breaths, and then he turned his head twelve degrees and looked at the archbishop instead.

Aurelian saw the decision arrive. He saw it in the fate before he saw it in the body, and then he saw it in the body, which was the hips going first, which is always the hips going first.

He watched with interest.

There was a version of the next four seconds in which he put his hand out and the halberdier stopped existing in the ordinary sense, and he could see that version, and he could see the eleven that came off it, and in nine of the eleven the archbishop lived and in two he did not and in all eleven something in the north went differently in a way Aurelian could follow for a considerable distance. He held all twelve futures side by side in the front of his head with no effort whatsoever.

He chose the one where he did nothing, and he chose it in the time it takes to notice a smell, and he did not experience the choosing as a decision.

Charles Lambert had got to his feet by then, hat in the left hand, black soaked to mid-thigh, and he was still turned toward Aurelian and mid-sentence when the ground behind him made the sound of an enormous man beginning.

He did not look round. That was the first thing.

He stepped off the line before he turned, on the left foot, a short passing step out and forward toward where the attack was coming from, and the arm that came through the space he had been standing in was a knuckle too long and went past his shoulder blade at a distance the eye could not resolve into a distance.

*He is inside it.*

*Forty years and men do not step toward me, and this one has no reach, no weapon, no Stage, no shell, nothing in him that reads at all, and he has stepped toward me and put himself where my elbow lives.*

Charles turned as he stepped, which put his hip into the ground, and his boots took hold of the wet ash where a running man's would have gone out from under him, because he had put weight on the outside edge of the foot before he committed. That was a thing a body learns on a wet deck at fourteen. Aurelian watched him do it twice more in four seconds.

The second attempt came down and across, a backhand off the same shoulder, and Charles was under it and out on the other side with his chin tucked and both hands up in front of his sternum and open. He did not strike anything. He had nothing to strike with and he knew it and he did not waste the motion.

The open hands were the tell, for anyone watching who knew what a tell was. A frightened man puts his hands up closed. Charles Lambert kept them open and kept them low and kept them where they could feel a shaft coming before his eyes could confirm one, and every third of a second he shifted the line of his hips by a degree or two, keeping the great body in front of him strung out along one axis where only one limb at a time could reach him. It is the oldest solution to the polearm and it is taught nowhere in the Holy Sea, because in the Holy Sea a man who is going to be attacked by a polearm is expected to be carrying something.

The third came low, at the knee, to take the base out.

He turned his shin out of it and let the whole weight of his upper body fall in the same direction, and went down onto one hand in the ash and back up, and there was ash in his mouth and he did not spit it out.

He was not fast. That was the thing Aurelian found himself examining. A forty-one-year-old diocesan administrator in soaked wool with a stomach on him is not fast in any register that means anything, and none of the four evasions had been quick. They had been early. Each one had begun before the delivery it answered, by a margin so slight that a spectator would have called it luck and a practitioner would have called it a read, and Charles Lambert had no faculty with which to read anything.

Aurelian stood four yards away with his hands at his sides and did not move.

*How interesting.*

*There is nothing in him. I have read this man for twenty-six years and there has never been anything in him and there is nothing in him now, and he has just voided three deliveries from something at the Zenith, and he did the third one with his eyes on the wrong hand, which means the eyes were never the instrument.*

*So this is what the Lamberts are. Nobody ever told me. Edward is a clerk and Charles keeps the diocese and the whole line reads as furniture in every register the Church holds, and this is what is under it, and they have been in our building for two hundred years.*

He wondered, mildly, how many of them there were, and whether the Church had ever counted, and what the counting would have been for.

Twenty-six years of vestry meetings. A man who took the minutes and brought his own pen and never once sat where the light was good.

Charles evaded the fourth by an inch and a half.

Then Kaalabad stopped swinging and simply closed the distance, which is what all of this had been for, and brought the right knee up into the archbishop's stomach.

It went in under the floating ribs on the left side and lifted him.

The sound was not loud. It was flat and wet and short, and it was made by a diaphragm being driven up into a chest cavity that had no room to receive it, and Charles Lambert's whole frame folded over the joint with his heels a foot off the ground and his hat still shut in his fist.

Kaalabad flung him.

The arm came across and put him out sideways and he travelled twenty-two feet through the air and landed on his shoulder and his ear and rolled through the ash and stopped face down.

He did not get up.

His back moved in a fast shallow flutter that was his body attempting a breath and being refused by a diaphragm that had gone into spasm and would not answer, and his hands were somewhere under him, and after four seconds he made a sound that was air going into a man in the wrong order.

The tunnel opened above the precinct without any warping this time.

Kaalabad went up into it. He did it slowly, which was worse than speed, and he did not look back at Aurelian, and the last thing that went into the dark was the empty right hand, and then there was no mouth in the star-field and the wrong constellations closed over where it had been and did not appear to have been disturbed.

The halberd came out of the cathedral footing by itself and went after him and was gone. It took the last of the west buttress with it on the way, and the masonry came down in the dark across the transept steps in a long tumbling run, and the dust of it went up and stood in the air over the cathedral and stayed there because there was no wind in Altherion that night.

Aurelian looked up at the place where the tunnel had been for a while.

"That man is the threat," he said.

There was nobody in a condition to answer. He said it anyway, in the flat plain voice he had begun using since the cellar, the one that had no drop in it at the end of a sentence.

"The halberdier is a tool with a good edge and somebody's hand on it, and I could put him down without standing up. I am speaking of the other one. I saw him from the inside of the crossing when my eyes were open, and he is considerably more dangerous than anything I have ever been asked to weigh, and I no longer wonder that Verinus was defeated."

He turned that over.

"I do not think that at my current best I would defeat him."

Four guardsmen stood at the edge of the ash and heard the Primate of the Holy Sea say that out loud, in the open, at the fourth hour, and one of them would repeat it in a tavern in the Cooper's quarter inside nine days and would be believed by nobody, and the not being believed would save his life for a period of about two months.

He said it the way a man reads a number off a gauge. Not defiantly and not with fear, because there was no fear anywhere in the precinct to be had, and the sentence went out into the ash and lay there.

*Verinus is dead. That is what the crossing shows and the crossing does not lie about arrivals. He went out of the world sometime in the last hour and there is a shape missing where he was, and I have looked for him among the traffic and he is not in the traffic, which means it took him entirely, and the last thing that man did in his life was buy four people who left through the low cloister nine days before he signed for them.*

*I will not tell Charles. There is no version of that which helps.*

He was wrong about all of it, in every particular, and there was nothing in four worlds positioned to correct him.

"Kharven," he said.

The word settled. Somewhere under his sternum a thread went taut and then slack again, the way a line does when the far end of it takes weight, and he did not notice it, because there was no instrument in him that had ever been calibrated to notice it.

"Nalūn will need to be rebuilt. That will not begin until the end of the war and it should not be attempted before, because you cannot lay a foundation in ground that is still being argued over, and there is going to be a great deal of arguing."

He said Nalūn without any particular weight, in the tone of an item on a schedule, and the salt basin at that hour had chain-bridges hanging at their stops and every sealed instrument in the lower vaults sitting at a reading no watchman could account for, and eleven of the black stones on the floor of the Deepvein station where they had come through the roof.

Charles Lambert got a whole breath.

It was audible across twenty feet and it sounded like a man being hauled up out of water. He got a second one, worse, and rolled onto his side, and then onto his back, and lay in the wet ash with his knees drawn up and both hands flat on his ribs and his mouth open.

Aurelian walked over.

He crouched, and looked down at him with the calm interest of a man reading a parish return, and the stars in their wrong arrangement stood over the two of them and put light on his face.

"Are you all right, Archbishop."

The inflection did not go up at the end.

Charles turned his head and looked at him. Ash on the whole left side of his face and in his hair and packed into his ear. Blood at the corner of his mouth from a bitten tongue. The white line through his eyebrow had opened again and was leaking down into the socket. He was breathing in a way that used his shoulders.

He did not answer for a moment.

*You were four yards away.*

*You put a Zenith's own strike out of the world with two fingers and threw his weapon through a colonnade, and then he came for me, and you stood there. You watched me. I could see you watching me. I have known you since I was fifteen years old and you have never once in your life let a man be hit in front of you if you could reach him, and it was the reaching that ruined you, and it is the only reason any of us ever loved you.*

*And you found it interesting.*

Aurelian held his hand out.

Charles looked at the hand. It was a young hand with no chalk on it and no ink and no callus anywhere. He took it.

He came up off the ground with no effort at all involved, which was its own indignity, and stood swaying with one arm across his middle and the other on Aurelian's forearm, and could not straighten fully, and gave up trying.

"We should continue your journey to Kharven," Aurelian said. "I will be accompanying you now."

Charles nodded. It cost him something to do it.

He got the hat off the ground, which took him a while, because bending was a project now and would be for six weeks, and he turned it round in both hands and looked at it. The brim had gone at the back. He put it on anyway, and the ash came off the crown and down the side of his face, and he did not brush it away.

"If you are certain, holiness." He stopped and took another breath and waited for it. "People will find it strange. That you position yourself in Moto territory. At this hour, with the Curia sitting, and the Palatine's seat empty, and the Ward cut. They will draw a line from you to them and they will not draw it kindly."

Aurelian blinked.

It was the first ordinary thing his face had done since he came up out of the hole.

"Find what strange."

"Holiness." Charles got his weight onto the other foot and something on his left side objected in a way he did not let reach his face. "Forgive me. I have carried your correspondence into the north for nine years and I have never once done it under your name, on your instruction, or with you in the party. Every man on that road will notice which of those three has changed."

"The Moto are the most important piece on this board. Anyone with sufficient knowledge of the game understands that, and anyone who does not is being played by somebody who does." He said it without heat and without emphasis, which was somehow the worst way it could have been said. "There is a war beginning that will be decided in the north and every party to it knows where the north keeps its weight. The Church has spent two hundred years pretending otherwise in writing. I am no longer required to pretend and I find I have no appetite for it."

He turned and started walking east across the precinct toward the gate, through ash that took his weight and let it go, and the ground under each footfall went down a coin's width and stayed down, and behind him four guardsmen who had not moved since the ring went out watched the prints filling with black water.

Charles Lambert put one hand on the broken top course of the precinct wall, and held there a moment with his ribs grinding, and followed him.

Haruki shifted herself, as she stepped forward looking at Charles, then back at Aurelian. Her presence carried a softer much more vulnerable tone as she leered over, resting beside a wall. Her frame hung over as she caught her breath, calming herself as she remained there for a moment. The fear draining slowly as she kept herself steady, seeing the man approaching the gate.

She reached into her bag pulling out a bandage to wrap around her wound, she remained there for a moment as she removed her breastplate for a moment, revealing the soft and yet thin undershirt underneath. With a sharp lift she revealed the sealed wound for a moment. She wrapped the bandage around slowly, making sure it's tight, but not restricting her movements. She winced for a moment, the pain settling and searing into her frame as she let it remain for now. Lowering her undershirt and enclosing her breastplate back onto her chest.

"Perhaps I was foolish to stand there and take it... but I suppose it was to make a point that no longer existed anymore." She muttered to herself, a sigh coming from her lips as she continued to approach Charles with renewed motion.

"Archbishop Lambert, you are not hurt are you?" She asked him, her eyes scanning his frame, making sure he was not wounded or branded with any curses from the presence of Kalaabad. "I saw the scene from the shore, we must get going to Kharven."

Kujo turned his attention north.

Kharven sat beneath its mountain like a wound that had learned how to build walls around itself, its surviving lights scattered thinly through districts already carrying hunger, old blood, and the pressure of a kingdom trying to decide whether it still deserved the word. Somewhere inside that darkness were the Moto brothers, heirs to a house that had spent generations teaching itself that consequence was the same thing as destiny. Kujo looked toward it through Xevorith, and then he laughed.

The sun blackened.

There was no eclipse, no celestial body passing across it. Kujo simply laid his Eternal Darkness over the star until daylight became a dead thing trapped behind absolute black, and Kharven’s mountains vanished beneath an unnatural dusk. The cold changed almost immediately; shadows deepened across streets, towers, frozen roofs, and courtyards while the people below looked upward at a sun that remained physically present yet had lost permission to illuminate them.

Kujo did not search long for Sonzai.

The Calamity inside the Moto heir was recognizable from far beyond ordinary distance, Malakar’s old corruption acting like a stain through the Plane of Fate. Kujo followed that stain through Kharven until he found the tower in which Sonzai resided, then raised one hand beneath the black mantle of Xevorith. Darkness gathered above the tower in an enormous vertical mass and descended before any warning bell could finish its first note.

The upper structure exploded inward.

Stone floors collapsed, timber supports snapped, and the tower’s crown disappeared beneath black force that punched through several levels before spreading through the chambers below. Kujo appeared within the destruction as though he had always occupied the space, untouched by dust or debris, his entire body beneath the neck covered by the seamless darkness of the Fifth Layer. Black pressure drove down around Sonzai and pinned him into the fractured stone, suppressing movement beneath a weight that felt less like gravity than the world being instructed to keep him exactly where Kujo had placed him.

Kujo looked down at him.

“Sonzai Moto.”

His twisted voice carried more than one register now, calm intelligence threaded through something increasingly cruel.

“Greatest war commander of nothing.”

Kujo’s smile widened as the black tightened around the ruined chamber.

“You won nothing. You inherited ashes, grievances, a dead father’s ambitions, and a kingdom too fractured to decide whether it wants you. Then you wrapped Chains around yourself and convinced everyone nearby that looking dangerous was equivalent to having accomplished something.”

He took a slow step closer, pale face illuminated only by the dim crimson-gold fractures moving beneath the darkness covering his body.

“A pathetic excuse for Moto blood.”

Kujo’s eyes sharpened.

“Just like your bitch twin, Sodoku.”

The insult was spoken without heat, which made it worse. Kujo did not shout or posture; he spoke as though he were entering a conclusion into a record.

“You both stand so boldly now. Kokan heirs. Butchers. Warriors. Men prepared to restore what was stolen from them.”

His smile thinned.

“Where was all that courage when Tomuka and Ezo were slaughtered before you?”

The pressure deepened.

Kujo tilted his head as though genuinely curious.

“You were children, yes. That is the answer sane people would give you. Helpless. Young. Terrified.”

His eyes darkened.

“But you do not permit yourselves sane answers, do you?”

He crouched enough to bring his face closer to Sonzai’s level.

“You built yourselves around the belief that strength should have changed the past. So every victory afterward became an argument against two dead boys who never asked you to win anything for them.”

The black around Sonzai tightened again, crushing fractured stone deeper beneath him while Kujo’s expression grew almost amused.

“And still you have not won.”

Movement struck from the side.

Selvara came through the ruined chamber with enough force to rupture the black haze around Kujo, her attack landing cleanly against him before the last fragments of masonry had finished falling. For an instant Kujo’s upper body shifted beneath the impact, Eternal Darkness rippling around him like fabric disturbed by violent wind.

Then he slowly turned his head. Kujo looked at Selvara. There was recognition first. Then delight.

“Well.”

The darkness beside him moved before his body did.

A massive black limb erupted from Xevorith and struck Selvara across the body with brutal lateral force, catching her before she could properly reset from the first attack. She was hurled through the chamber and into the surviving wall hard enough to blow stone outward around her, disappearing briefly inside a burst of pulverized masonry and broken timber.

Kujo straightened. His attention remained on the place where she had struck.

“Selvara Kusho.”

The name left him differently. Not respectfully. Familiar.

“Malakar’s daughter.”

Kujo walked toward her through the ruined chamber, the Eternal Darkness dragging behind him like a cape whose edges erased the floor wherever they passed. His smile returned slowly as he considered her, and something about the sight of Malakar’s blood standing beside a Moto amused him far more than it should have.

“You should be thankful your father died.”

Kujo’s head tilted.

“Truly.”

His smile became uglier.

“Who knows what your life would have been if that horrible sexual deviant had actually been permitted to raise you?”

He laughed softly, the sound catching strangely beneath the influence of the Crevice.

“Malakar could corrupt continents and still somehow make fatherhood seem like the greater threat.”

Kujo glanced back toward Sonzai, then returned his attention to Selvara.

“And this is what became of his daughter.”

There was a different cruelty in his eyes now, less aimed at hurting her and more interested in exposing whatever reaction the words produced.

“You found the little piece of him still buried inside Sonzai and mistook familiarity for home.”

The black around Kujo thickened.

“Of course you did.”

His voice lowered.

“You have spent your entire life orbiting a father you barely knew.”

Kujo raised one hand and allowed the darkness above Kharven to spread even farther, covering the last fragments of natural light while the city below disappeared into total black.

“And he has spent his entire life trying to prove he is more than what that same man put inside him.”

Kujo looked between them.

For several seconds, his expression almost resembled admiration.

“What an ugly little romance.”

The Crevice pulsed beneath Xevorith.

Kujo’s smile widened.

“I almost hope it works.”

Six hundred men breathing in the cold made the sound of a tide coming in under a pier.

That was the first thing on the field and it stayed the first thing. Under it, the pressure of them, which a man feels in the chest before he counts anything, six hundred bodies packed at four feet and putting out heat that hung over the whole block in a haze and went up. Then the smell, which was horse and wet felt and old grease and the particular sourness of men who have slept in mail for eleven nights. Sight came last and came late. Wren Greymane could see about a third of his own muster from the saddle and had to take the rest on faith.

Seiji Tenrai Moto sat his horse beside him and did not look at the men.

He was twenty-nine and had the sort of face that agrees with whoever is nearest. Black hair, oiled and pulled back, cut level at the nape by somebody good. A jaw that came to a point without narrowing. Skin kept out of weather. He wore lacquered plate in a red so dark it read black until the sun found it, and the fit of it was perfect, which only ever means it was made twice.

"They are ready," Wren said.

"They are." Seiji's breath went up in a thin column and did not disperse. "The messengers went out at the second hour. Four riders, four routes, all four southern corridors. By tonight the Seat will have three separate reports that we are moving on the Stormfold road and one that we have already taken the Brine crossing, and all four will be signed by men whose hands their clerks know."

"And they will believe them."

"They will believe two of them, which is better. A man who believes everything reconsiders. A man who believes half of it commits." He turned his head. "We march directly. We put ourselves into their hands and we slaughter the last of a dead line."

*He says it the way a factor reads a manifest.*

*I have been thirteen months in this man's company and I have not once heard him raise his voice, and I have watched him give an instruction that ended a village, and the instruction was four words and one of them was please.*

"The Ice have no claim," Seiji said. "That is the whole of it and it has been the whole of it for nineteen years. A man does not become King of the North because he held a ridge and because a girl sat on a broken chair and nobody stopped her. The Tenrai recognise one heir. The Amagiri recognise one heir. The Akagane recognise one heir. Miku Tenrai Moto, trueborn to Azure Tenrai Moto, who was High King of the North while these people were still deciding what to call themselves."

Wren said nothing to that.

He had asked, once, in the fourth month, where the boy was kept. He had been answered pleasantly and at length and had come out of the conversation understanding that he had been answered pleasantly and at length and told nothing whatsoever, and he had not asked again.

He looked at his men.

He had chosen every one of them himself and he had chosen them for faith, which was the word he used in his own head and had never said aloud to any of them. Skill he had in quantity, and he had passed over men who could have held the left of any line in the North. He had taken the ones who would go on doing a thing after the thing stopped making sense.

They had played traitors perfectly. That was the part nobody would ever write down. Sixteen months of being spat at in their own villages. Eleven of them had wives who had stopped writing. Cass Holloway in the third rank had buried his mother in the autumn and had not been permitted to attend, and had asked once, and had been refused, and had said *aye* and gone back to the horses.

Three of them had been killed by their own kin and Wren had entered all three in a book he kept in his saddlebag and had told nobody about the book.

*Bram would understand the book. That is the obscene part. Of every man breathing, my brother is the one who would open it and read four pages and hand it back and say nothing and never mention it again, and Bram is at the southern reach this morning with three hundred men and a wall that has already gone.*

*I smiled at him across two hundred paces of dead men. I have never been able to account for it. I have gone at it eleven different ways and there is no version where I did not smile.*

He put his heels in and took the horse out four lengths in front of the block so that the whole muster could see him, and stood in the stirrups.

The tide-sound went out of the field.

"We rally today," Wren Greymane said.

Six hundred men waited.

"Under the banner of Greymane. The true Greymane. The true Shield of the North." His voice came off the frozen ground and went back through them. "Every one of you has been called a traitor to your face by somebody you loved. Every one of you took it. You are going to be called it again this afternoon by men on the other side of a shield wall and it is going to mean nothing at all, because they will be saying it about a thing that ended sixteen months ago and you will be looking at the end of the work."

He drew.

"RALLY."

Six hundred throats, and under them the horses going up, and under that the pressure changing across the whole block as the mass of it shifted forward one step in unison, and the ground took it and gave back a sound that Wren felt come up through the horse and into his knees.

Seiji Tenrai Moto watched him do it with his hands folded on the pommel and did not shout.

The wall had gone in the night, forty feet of it, on the fourth day of them working at it.

They had not thrown themselves at it. That was the thing the Seat's own officers would not believe when it was reported. They had surveyed it, and they had gone at the mortar course two feet above the footing where the frost had already been working for eleven winters, and they had done it in shifts, in silence, under a shed roof of their own making, and on the fourth night the face had come away in one piece and had taken the rampart walk with it.

Bram Greymane had three hundred men.

They had come through at the second hour and they were still coming through at the fifth, and there were two thousand two hundred of them, and they were the most disciplined thing he had ever seen in his life.

Sound first. Boots on rubble, all of them landing at the same time, which is a noise a man's body objects to before his mind gets to it. No shouting anywhere. A horn on a two-count, twice, and the whole front rank of them stepping off together.

Then pressure. The gap in the wall was twenty-two feet at its widest and everything on the far side of it wanted to be on this side, and Bram could feel that as a physical fact standing sixty feet back.

Then the smell, which was hot iron and lamp oil and something mineral and dry that came off their skin and that nobody in Kharven had a word for.

Then, last, what he could actually see, which was eleven feet of a breach with eight of them abreast in it and the geometric etching on their forearms catching the fire behind them.

That was the whole plan and it was the only plan.

Twenty-two feet. Eight abreast. Two thousand two hundred men arriving eight at a time, for as long as three hundred could hold a line eight wide and rotate it.

"THIS IS WHERE WE PROVE WHAT WE ARE."

Bram had a voice that carried without effort and he had never once been glad of it until this year. He rode the length of his own front at a walk with his shield on his arm, the same shield, the one he had thrown down at the ridge and picked back up, and the crack in the boss was still there and had been rebound twice and everyone in the line knew what it was.

"PROTECTORS. STEWARDS. DEFENDERS. THOSE ARE WORDS THEY PUT ON A CHARTER IN A WARM ROOM AND THIS MORNING WE FIND OUT WHAT THEY COST." The line was watching him and none of them were shouting yet. "WE DIE HERE OR WE LIVE TO TELL IT. THERE IS NO THIRD THING."

   He got to the end of the line and came back.

"RAGE. LET THE NORTHERN COLD GUIDE YOU."

They started then.

"FOR THE GLORY OF KHARVEN."

He put the horse forward.

The first of them came out of the breach at a controlled advance with the shields overlapped and Bram took the horse in on the oblique, which is the only approach that works against a locked front, and came across the end of the line at the shoulder rather than the face, and brought the axe down across and back on the return.

The head came off the second one in the file at the base of the neck, through the trapezius and the cervical column together, and the body took two more steps in formation before the man behind it walked into its back.

The line closed. The breach kept producing.

After that it was fast and it was filthy and Bram Greymane had no idea whether it was going well.

That was the part nobody ever tells you. He could see thirty feet. He knew what was happening on his own left because he could hear Renard's voice in it, and he knew nothing at all about his right for a period he later worked out at eleven minutes and experienced as either a moment or an afternoon. Men went down in front of him and he did not know whose. The horse took something in the near hind at some point and kept going for a while and then did not.

The cold did the work that three hundred could not.

Their skin ran mineral, and the mineral ran slow, and at thirty below on a fourth night with no fires and no shelter the slow got slower. They were not weak. They were three-quarters of a beat late, all of them, and the lateness was the only currency Bram's men had. A Kharven axeman who has spent his whole life at this temperature swings at his own speed on the coldest morning of his life. A Mandate man drilled to the count of a horn does not, and a formation that cannot keep its count stops being a formation and becomes two thousand two hundred individuals in a hole in a wall.

Bram's men held the gap for four hours.

They rotated on a whistle every eighty count, front rank to the rear through the gaps in the second, and it worked for the first nine rotations and got ragged at the eleventh and by the fifteenth they were rotating men who could not lift the shield to head height and were being put back in the front anyway because the alternative was a hole.

Egil Vald went down at the twelfth rotation with a hooked blade under the cuisse and the femoral opened and he was dead in the time it takes to fill a bucket, and he made no sound about it, and the man beside him stepped across the gap without being told.

Sound. Pressure. Smell. Sight, last, and less of it as the light came up and the fires went out.

The men of the Mandate were still coming through at the ninth hour, and Bram Greymane, on foot by then with a dead horse behind him and his shield strapped to a forearm he could no longer feel, put his boot on the chest of something that had stopped moving and pulled his axe out of it and turned round to face the gap again.

They took the first village at the eleventh hour and Wren Greymane understood by the second one that he had misread what he had joined.

He had expected a Northern war. Two claims, two hosts, a field somewhere flat with a wood on one flank, the thing decided by whoever held together longest, and afterward the arguing and the burials and a treaty that everybody would break in nine years. He had joined a Northern war. He had spent sixteen months on his own name for it.

Kujo's generals rode at the head of the column.

There were four of them that Wren could name and two that he could not, and none of them consulted Seiji, and Seiji did not appear to expect it. They set the pace. They chose the road. Twice they went through ground that no cavalry officer alive would have taken and the ground let them.

Kaalabad rode at the front.

The horse was not a horse. It stood nineteen hands and its barrel was plated along the flanks in a pattern that was under the skin instead of on it, and the head was long and wrong and the nostril was a slit and it did not blow steam in the cold. It made no sound on frozen ground. Six hundred men behind it made the noise of six hundred men and the thing at the front of the column travelled in silence, and the silence was ahead of the noise, and that is a thing a body cannot stop noticing once it has noticed it.

He had the halberd across his back. He had not spoken to anyone since the muster.

The first village was called Ashgate and had eleven houses and a mill.

They did not fight there. Nobody in the column drew. Kaalabad turned the draconic horse off the road at a walk and went up the length of the village street once, and by the time he came back the mill was on fire from the inside without anyone having entered it, and four of the eleven houses had come off their footings and gone over sideways in the same direction, and there were people in three of them.

Wren rode past a woman standing in her own doorway with a child on her hip. She was not screaming. She was looking at the mill.

*She has seen the banner. She has seen it and she cannot read it and she is going to describe it to somebody tonight if she lives that long, and what she will describe is a grey wolf, and my grandfather is under a stone eight leagues from here.*

Beyond the second village, off toward the west where the ground fell away toward the coast road, the horizon lit.

It came without sound for two full seconds. A white swelling under the cloud base, and then a second one, and then a third further out, each one throwing the shape of the hills up in silhouette, and the sound arrived afterward as a flat concussion that came through the frozen ground into the horses' legs and made every animal in the column check at once.

Nobody in the column looked at it except Wren.

The generals did not turn their heads. Seiji had his eyes on the road. And it went on ahead of them all morning, out past the range of anything Wren understood, opening the country between the column and Kharven-Seat as a man opens a road with a plough.

Lorn Stark had been on the outer wall since the fourth hour and had watched the west go white three times before anyone came up to tell him what it was.

Nobody could tell him what it was.

He sent two runners and got back two men who had run four miles in the cold to say that the villages on the Ashgate road had stopped answering, which he already had. The third came back at the seventh hour and reported to him with his hands on his knees that a rider had come in on the Brine crossing road claiming the enemy was moving south along the Stormfold and would be at the crossing by dark.

"That is the third one of those," Lorn said. "And they do not agree."

"No, m'lord."

"Then two of them are being paid and I would like to know which two." He turned his back on the west. "Get warm. Eat something. You will be going out again."

The yard below him had filled while he was not looking at it.

They had mustered everything. That was the plain description and it did not take long to say. Every man in the Seat who could hold a line, every one of the Ashwell tenants who had come in off the hill farms in the last four days, the Stormfold party, Osric's household, the whole of the gate guard less twenty. It came to a number Lorn had already worked out twice and did not intend to work out a third time.

Sodoku came down the inner stair into the yard in full harness.

Lorn watched him from the wall.

He had been armoured since before the light, which meant he had put it on in the dark and by himself, and the mail skirt under the plate had been rebound at the left hip in a way that said somebody competent had done it recently and that it had needed doing. He crossed the yard at his own pace. The men in the yard went quiet in a rolling wave that started where he entered it and went out to the walls, and it was not the quiet of respect exactly, and Lorn had been trying to find the word for it since the boat.

Hild was at the foot of the stair with Tabitha behind her and the broken circlet in her hand instead of on her head.

He stopped in front of his daughter and stood there a moment.

Then he put a hand on the back of her head and kissed her hair, and let go, and turned away, and did not say anything that carried as far as the wall.

*Nineteen years.*

*I stood in a room with her mother and I made an arrangement about that girl and I have kept every part of it and I would like it recorded somewhere that keeping it has cost me more than any oath I have sworn to anyone with a title. And he comes up out of the sea after nineteen years and puts his hand on her head, and she lets him.*

Sodoku said one word.

"Tōga."

The thing came over the north curtain wall.

It did not climb it and it did not jump it. It came up the outside of the wall in four movements and put its forelimbs on the parapet and brought the rest of itself over, and the parapet took the weight and shed frost in a sheet, and every horse in the yard went sideways at once and two of them went down. The cold came off it in a front. Lorn, forty feet away along the wall, felt his eyes dry in the space of a breath and felt the inside of his nose freeze on the inhale and put his forearm up in front of his face without deciding to.

It came down into the yard and lowered its head and Sodoku got up onto it.

The men who had never seen it before made a sound. Not a cheer.

Edward Lambert was at Lorn's shoulder without having arrived there, which was a thing he did.

Fifty-three. Grey at the temple and nowhere else, a long dry face, a collar done up to the throat, gloves on. He had a wax tablet in his left hand and had not written on it in some time.

"He wants Sonzai," Edward said.

"Where is Sonzai."

"That is the question I have been given." Edward looked out west, where the cloud base had gone the wrong colour and stayed it. "The last confirmed sighting is nine days old and it is from a man I do not trust, and every channel I have running north of the Ashgate road stopped reporting between the fourth and the fifth hour this morning, which is either the weather or it is not."

So you have nothing."

"I have four names and a direction." He put the tablet inside his coat. "I have been ordered to go and find him. I am fifty-three years old and I intend to do it on the Stormfold road with six riders and no banner, and if I am not back in five days you will assume the road is closed and act accordingly."

"Edward."

Lambert waited.

Lorn found that the thing he wanted to say would not come out in any arrangement that was not embarrassing, so he put his hand out instead, and Edward Lambert looked at it for a moment with an expression that gave nothing away at all, and took it, and gripped once, and let go.

Then he went down the stair and got on a horse and left through the postern with six men, and Lorn watched the seven of them out until the ground took them.

The gates came open below.

The column went out under them at a walk, the horse first and then the foot, and the thing with Sodoku on its back went out ahead of all of it and did not slow at the threshold, and the cold that came off it laid a white line down the middle of the road that the whole muster then marched along.

Lorn Stark stayed on the wall until the last of them was through. Then he went down the inner stair to the yard, and picked up the broken circlet where Hild had set it on the mounting block and forgotten it, and put it in his belt.

Kujo turned his attention back toward Sonzai, who remained pinned beneath the black mass crushing the broken tower into itself. The Eternal Darkness above Kharven had swallowed the sun completely, leaving the ruined chamber illuminated only by the faint crimson pulse of Calamity around Sonzai and the black-gold flicker of the Crevice beneath Kujo’s shroud. Selvara lay among broken stone several yards away, still conscious enough for Kujo to know exactly how useful she could become.

Kujo’s head tilted.

“Sonzai Moto.”

His smile returned slowly.

*“Do you feel despair?”*

The black around Selvara moved before Sonzai could answer. Several narrow lances formed from the darkness and suspended themselves around her, not striking immediately, but waiting in full view while Kujo watched Sonzai’s face rather than hers. He wanted the anticipation to land first.

“You care for this woman.”

Kujo lifted one finger.

“Good.”

One of the black lances drove into Selvara’s side.

“Then watch her suffer.”

Another struck through her shoulder. A third followed lower, then another, each one withdrawing before the next entered so that nothing ended quickly enough to give her release. Kujo kept the wounds controlled, not because he possessed mercy, but because killing her would finish the lesson too soon.

Sonzai’s breathing changed. Calamity began leaking from him in dark crimson waves.

Kujo noticed immediately.

“There it is.”

Another blade formed.

“That little inheritance Malakar left inside you.”

The blade drove into Selvara again.

“How obedient.”

Sonzai’s hands clenched against the black pinning him down. The first pulse of Calamity expanded across the shattered floor, violently enough to send loose fragments of masonry skipping away from his body. Kujo did not reinforce the restraint yet.

He wanted to see what happened. Another stab. Then another.

Sonzai’s head snapped upward.

“STOP.”

The Calamity erupted.

It did not simply flare around him. It detonated outward from his Soul Crystal in a violent crimson-black pressure that immediately began eating through Kujo’s darkness, hatred and aggression flooding the chamber until the rubble beneath Sonzai cracked apart. The black holding his shoulders down split first, then the bindings across his arms, then the larger mass pressing his entire body into the tower floor.

Kujo watched him rise.

Sonzai’s eyes burned purple through the Calamity surrounding him, crimson hair whipping around his face while the Chains of War dragged themselves upright behind him. Every link radiated the memory of conflict stored within it, inscriptions burning hotter as his fury fed the battlefield around him.

“I’ll give you despair.”

Then Sonzai disappeared.

Kujo’s smile sharpened. Fast.

Far faster than the ruined tower had prepared the eye to understand.

The first Chain struck Kujo from his right side before the air had finished reacting to Sonzai’s movement. Kujo’s black covering buckled beneath the impact, and before he could appear to turn, another Chain descended from above, another swept low from behind, and three more crossed diagonally through the same space.

Then the entire room became Chains.

Sonzai moved through them rather than simply commanding them, appearing between attacks only long enough to change the angle of the next one. Calamity saturated each link, transforming the barrage into an omnidirectional storm of war-pressure, metal, aggression, and accumulated violence. Chains came from the walls, the collapsed floors, above the broken tower, beneath Kujo’s feet, behind his head, across his ribs, and through every line where movement seemed possible.

Kujo allowed the first dozen to land.

Then thirty.

Then hundreds.

The seamless black of Xevorith began tearing from his body.

One strike ripped a long section from his shoulder. Another peeled the darkness from his torso. A crossing barrage hit him from six directions at once and tore away nearly everything covering his chest, exposing pale flesh beneath and the black-gold seam of the Crevice pulsing violently under his sternum.

Sonzai did not slow.

His speed increased.

The Chains became difficult to distinguish individually, their attacks blending into a single field of continuous impact. Every time Kujo appeared to shift his stance, Sonzai had already attacked the location he was moving toward, and every recoil became another opening for a Chain to strike from the opposite side.

Stone disintegrated around them.

The remaining tower walls collapsed outward.

Sonzai came through the falling debris with one Chain wrapped around his forearm and smashed it across Kujo’s face hard enough to twist his head sideways. Another struck his ribs before the first impact had completed, followed by a vertical blow that drove him through the ruined floor.

Sonzai followed.

The Chains descended after Kujo like an execution.

**“I SAID I’LL GIVE YOU DESPAIR!”**

Calamity erupted downward with them.

Kujo disappeared beneath the barrage.

Every Chain Sonzai possessed hammered into the crater in rapid succession, striking from alternating vectors until the surrounding stone became powder. Crimson-black Essence filled the ruins, and the shockwaves traveled through Kharven hard enough to break windows several streets away.

Then Sonzai dropped into the crater himself.

He caught one Chain in each hand and drove both downward.

The impact tore the remaining black completely from Kujo’s body.

For a moment there was only dust.

Sonzai stood over the center of it, breathing heavily, Calamity pouring from his body while the Chains of War hovered around him like enraged serpents waiting for another command. The destruction had stripped Xevorith away completely.

Then the dust moved.

Kujo stood inside it. Untouched. No torn skin. No broken bones. No blood. Even the black covering his body had returned.

Sonzai’s expression changed.

Kujo smiled.

“You are very fast.”

His voice carried that strange doubled quality again.

“Very, very fast.”

The darkness around his shoulders settled like a cape.

“And none of it happened to me.”

Sonzai’s Chains shifted.

Kujo raised one hand. Xevorith opened behind him.

“You struck me. You tore away the black. You drove me through the tower. You broke the ground beneath me.”

His smile widened.

“All true.”

The Fifth Layer pulsed.

“And all safely in the past.”

The Unwinding moved. There was no flash. No reversal. The battlefield did not reconstruct itself.

Instead, Kujo reached backward through his own sequence and began removing each completed exchange that no longer possessed any active claim upon his present. The first Chain strike disappeared from his continuity, then the second, then every impact afterward, each moment severed from the causal road leading to the Kujo standing there now.

Sonzai could still remember hitting him.

The tower still carried the damage. The ground remained shattered.

But Kujo no longer possessed the sequence in which those attacks had successfully altered him.

The wounds vanished because, from Kujo’s present state, there was no longer a past in which they had remained relevant enough to produce him.

His head returned to the position it had occupied before the first strike.

The Crevice beat beneath his chest. The black cape of Xevorith was whole. Kujo opened both arms slightly.

“Do you understand now?”

His smile grew increasingly twisted.

“You can hit me.”

A quiet laugh followed.

“You can break me.”

Another.

“You can even watch yourself do it.”

Kujo’s dark eyes fixed on Sonzai.

“And when the moment is finished, I decide whether it is allowed to belong to me.”

The Calamity around Sonzai surged again.

Kujo’s smile sharpened at the sight.

“There.”

His voice lowered.

“That anger again.”

He looked toward Selvara, then back toward Sonzai.

“You thought caring for her made you stronger.”

Kujo laughed.

“It made you predictable.”

The black beneath him spread across the ruined tower floor.

“You thought rage freed you from my restraint.”

His head tilted.

“It merely showed me what button to press.”

The Fifth Layer expanded farther around him, swallowing the broken stones and the last natural light trying to enter the ruined tower.

“All of it is futile, Sonzai Moto.”

Kujo’s voice became deeper, several registers speaking through the same words.

“Your speed. Your Chains. Your Calamity. Your rage.”

The darkness climbed behind him like an enormous wall.

“Futile.”

Kujo raised his hand toward Sonzai.

*“And now…” His grin became monstrous. “…prepare for despair.”*

Kujo did not give Sonzai the opportunity to recover from the revelation. The black beneath the ruined tower erupted upward in a dozen thick bands, wrapping around his shoulders, waist, and legs before one isolated strand coiled around his right arm. Kujo closed his hand slowly, and the darkness answered with increasing pressure until bone finally surrendered beneath it, the arm buckling under a crushing force that turned the limb useless.

Sonzai’s Calamity surged violently in response, crimson-black pressure boiling against the restraints as the Chains of War snapped outward searching for Kujo again. Kujo allowed them to move just far enough for Sonzai to believe another opening existed, then folded Xevorith across the space between each Chain and himself. Every route disappeared before completion, leaving the weapons striking empty black while Kujo remained directly in front of him.

“You wanted to give me despair.”

Kujo crouched slightly, his pale face level with Sonzai while the black tightened across his broken arm.

“Then understand what despair actually is.”

The pressure shifted from physical restraint into something deeper. Kujo let the darkness invade Sonzai’s perception without touching the Soul Crystal itself, constructing a world where every possible answer arrived too late: Kharven burning after he reached it, Sodoku dying just beyond the reach of the Chains, Selvara screaming from somewhere he could hear but never locate, the Moto banner hanging over a kingdom where no living person remembered why it mattered. Each possibility changed whenever Sonzai tried mentally reaching toward a solution, not predicting his future but simulating the experience of every road closing because he had chosen to walk it.

Kujo watched the anger fight against it.

“That is the cruel thing about men like you.”

His fingers tightened again, drawing another sharp contraction through the ruined arm.

“You have spent so long surviving through anger that you began confusing anger with an answer.”

The darkness forced Sonzai lower. Kujo showed him Tomuka again, then Ezo, not as resurrected phantoms begging for vengeance but as memories continuing perfectly well without Sonzai’s war ever repairing what had happened to them. He made victory feel empty, revenge feel late, and survival feel indistinguishable from simply being the last person left to remember why any of it had mattered.

“You cannot punch backward through time.”

Kujo’s smile returned.

*“You cannot conquer a grave.”*

Another surge of Calamity fought through the room, powerful enough to distort the black around Sonzai, yet Kujo simply let the pressure reform. He wanted Sonzai conscious, furious, and trapped inside the realization that sheer willingness could not force reality to become fair. Every time the Chains moved, the darkness tightened somewhere else, making resistance itself another mechanism through which pain returned to him.

“Keep fighting.”

Kujo’s voice softened almost pleasantly.

*“I want you awake when hopelessness finally stops being an idea.”*

Then the temperature dropped.

Frost entered the ruined tower through every opening at once, crawling across fractured stone and silvering shattered beams beneath a sudden northern cold. Kujo’s smile vanished as his head turned slightly, recognizing the signature before its owner crossed the remaining distance.

Zaehaerys Stark had arrived.

He did not receive a chance to speak.

Kujo disappeared from beside Sonzai and was already standing before Zaehaerys when the frost thickened around the newcomer’s boots. One black-covered hand closed around his throat and lifted him from the ground in the same motion, Xevorith erasing the interval in which Stark might have reacted.

Kujo studied him closely.

“Zaehaerys Stark.”

The frost around them intensified instinctively, ice spreading across Kujo’s arm before stopping abruptly where the Eternal Darkness began.

*“Your power belongs to me.”*

The words were not metaphorical.

Kujo opened the Fifth Layer against Zaehaerys’s magical expression and began stripping the relationship between the man and the authority he carried. Frost still existed. Cold still existed. The inherited discipline, the pride of House Stark, the accumulated certainty that winter answered when he reached for it. Kujo began voiding those connections one after another until the cold around them weakened into ordinary air.

Zaehaerys’s frost cracked apart across the ruined floor.

Kujo did not merely suppress the magic. He made the man feel it leaving, allowing him to recognize each capability at the exact moment it stopped answering. The instinctive authority behind the ice became silence, then the confidence built across years of successful command followed it, leaving behind the terrible sensation of reaching for a part of oneself and discovering an empty space.

“You built pride around inheritance.”

Kujo tightened his grip around Zaehaerys’s throat.

“That is always the danger of bloodlines.”

His eyes darkened.

“They convince men that what was given to them is the same thing as what they are.”

The black crept over Zaehaerys’s chest.

Kujo took the last of the frost around them and crushed it into nothing, leaving the Stark suspended in his grip without the atmosphere that had announced his arrival. For a brief moment, Kujo simply examined what remained after the power was stripped away.

Then he raised his other hand.

“You came here believing winter would protect your name.”

Blackness compressed against Kujo’s palm until it became a point smaller than a fingertip.

“Let us see what remains without either.”

The blast crossed almost no distance.

*A black eruption punched through Zaehaerys’s chest and burst from his back in the same instant, leaving a cavernous opening through the center of his torso. The heart was simply absent from the resulting wound, not thrown free or crushed inside him, but removed so completely that the place where it had been looked unnaturally empty.*

Kujo kept him suspended for another second.

Then the black around his throat opened.

The body dropped into the frost-coated rubble.

Kujo looked down at it without ceremony before turning his attention back toward Sonzai. The Eternal Darkness crept once more across the shattered tower, swallowing the remaining ice and gathering around the Moto heir while the Crevice pulsed beneath Kujo’s chest.

“One man came to save you.”

Kujo’s dark eyes settled on Sonzai again.

“He brought winter.”

A faint smile returned.

“Now he does not even have a heartbeat.”

The black around Sonzai tightened.

“Tell me, commander.”

Kujo stepped toward him.

*“Does Kharven feel safer yet?”*

The darkness crept around Sonzai, the shade closing in onto his ruinous soul. The darkness' grasp growing in strength the more the chaos and calamity had begun to spread around him. The void was not as easy forgiving as it was cold. The shade began to shroud him, almost consuming his figure entirely. Though hope was not entirely lost, from the shadows a figure kindled a light, not simply a light that could be tangile, but perhaps something much more.

"Sonzai!" Her voice called out, Emira's arms wrapped around him, tightly hugging him as the darkness began to expulse out of him. The revitalized pulse of her own heart providing both a light and warmth to him. While Sonzai almost fell into the dark grasp, Emira additionally pulled him out of that inner darkness, finding the space where Sonzai held the most light. There, **harmony** had revealed itself. 

Emira was no fighter, but that did not make her proficient nonetheless in other fields. Sonzai felt it, the condition of his body did not simply renew, it blossomed, flowered, the roots within his own body began to tap into something with her presence. The flow of his breath became an anchorage, the flow becoming calm, clear, and most certainly free. 

Sonzai saw his own aether, flourishing around his own body as the chains of war clenched tighter into his grasp. This was not simply empowerment, this was the freedom of expression, the revelation of nature in it's most vitalizing form. Her hands held tightly as the calamity inside of him was taught more than just simply disaster, it was honed to the sharpness of a blade, it did not remove the chaos, it only made it focused into a singular point of control.

"Are you okay? I have not seen you in so long!" Emira said, her cheeks growing puffy and her eyes watery. These were not tears of sadness, but of joy, of longing. Emira's presence did not suppress the chaos, it only granted it's truest form, the darkness repelled against her frame, the darkness rejecting entry into-

her frame.

"Who is this?" She asked him, her eyes looking at Kujo as he expanded the void-like energy around them, the shadow and darkness consuming them as the area around them began to become consumed. If not for her arrival, Sonzai had might've been consumed by it like many others have before.

"Is he... one of the bad people that Sodoku and you have been going against?" She asked him, her eyes sharpening as she held him tighter, her strong arms having kept firmly pressed against his ribs, almost suffocating him from her tight grasp.

"O-Oh! Shit! I'm sorry!" She eased up on her grasp seeing Sonzai having struggled to breathe, seeing as how her strength only amplified through her own training regime. Though not taught to battle, she did not excuse keeping up with her athletics and magical prowess.

The sky over the north went to work at the ninth hour and did not consult anybody.

It began as a thickening. The cloud base did not move and did not break, and through it, from horizon to horizon, purple came up in strings. They were not lights in the sky. They hung at a height the eye could not agree on twice, and they thrummed, and each thrum arrived in the chest a full second before it arrived in the ear, and the strings drew together across the whole vault of the north into folds and the folds moved, and it was an aurora, and there is no season in Kharven when that colour belongs to that sky.

For the length of one breath, everything inside a thousand miles weighed less.

Frost lifted off the road in a sheet and hung. Blood standing in the ruts came up out of them in beads and went sideways. Every loose thing in the country, ash and grit and chaff and the teeth of the dead, rose an inch and travelled, all of it in one direction, and every horse in six hundred screamed at once because a horse knows what down is and had been told otherwise.

Then it came back.

Everything that had risen went to a single point on the Ashgate road, and reached it, and stopped, and the sky's purple came down out of the folds and went to the same place, and the aurora emptied itself into one man over the course of four seconds and did not leave a residue anywhere.

The sky over the north went to work at the ninth hour and did not consult anybody.

It began as a thickening. The cloud base did not move and did not break, and through it, from horizon to horizon, purple came up in strings. They were not lights in the sky. They hung at a height the eye could not agree on twice, and they thrummed, and each thrum arrived in the chest a full second before it arrived in the ear, and the strings drew together across the whole vault of the north into folds and the folds moved, and it was an aurora, and there is no season in Kharven when that colour belongs to that sky.

For the length of one breath, everything inside a thousand miles weighed less.

Frost lifted off the road in a sheet and hung. Blood standing in the ruts came up out of them in beads and went sideways.
