Every loose thing in the country, ash and grit and chaff and the teeth of the dead, rose an inch and travelled, all of it in one direction, and every horse in six hundred screamed at once because a horse knows what down is and had been told otherwise.

Then it came back.

Everything that had risen went to a single point on the Ashgate road, and reached it, and stopped, and the sky's purple came down out of the folds and went to the same place, and the aurora emptied itself into one man over the course of four seconds and did not leave a residue anywhere.

Wren Greymane had his hand up in front of his face and did not remember putting it there.

Kaalabad's eyes had gone the same colour.

He brought the draconic horse to a halt in the middle of the road and swung down off it, which he had not done once in three days of riding, and he put his boots in the frozen mud, and the mud under both of them went down and cracked outward in a ring eleven feet across.

Then he put his right hand out into the air beside him and pushed it through.

Space parted for the arm and did not close around it. There was a seam, a hand's width and about four feet long, hanging at chest height with nothing on the other side of it, and Kaalabad's arm was in it to the shoulder, and Wren watched the wrongness gather in a ring at the join where the man stopped and the seam began, and the ring turned slowly, and the light in it came from nowhere.

He drew it out.

The old halberd, the dirty one with the marks, had never been the thing. Wren understood that the moment the new one cleared the seam. It came out head first, eleven feet of it, and the head was a spike and a fluke and a blade the length of a forearm, and it was black through, and along the shaft ran a hairline of the same purple the sky had just given up, and the road under the head began to sag toward it as it came.

Zarakal.

Wren had never heard the name and had never needed to. Some objects introduce themselves.

*Now.*

He got his hand up and made the sign and the sign went down the line.

Six hundred men turned around.

It happened along the whole length of the column at once and it took four seconds. Cass Holloway put his spear through the back of the Tenrai rider he had shared bread with for eleven weeks. The Amagiri light horse on the left flank, who had been told nothing and had suspected everything and had done nothing about it, came apart before they got their heads around. Two of Wren's men in the fourth rank went for the same officer and got in each other's way and one of them died for it.

It was ugly. Sixteen months of work and it came out ugly, and it did not need to come out any other way, because the whole of it had been designed to happen in a place with a gate behind it and no room to reform.

Wren had walked the ground in the summer under a false name with a hired cart and a load of salt fish, and had counted the road's width at the narrows, and had gone home and drawn it on the back of a psalter. Two hundred and ten feet from the ditch on the left to the drop on the right, and a column of six hundred strung out along it three abreast, and the officers riding in the middle third because officers always ride in the middle third.

Kill the middle third and the head cannot turn round and the tail cannot get past.

The noise of the column changed key. That was the sound Wren would keep.

He came down off the horse.

He hit the road and slapped the animal away and it went, and he reached back over his right shoulder and brought the shield off his back and onto his arm and got the enarmes seated with two shakes.

It was a heater, four feet, oak core and hide and a rim of iron gone silver with wear, and the boss had a crack in it that had been rebound twice, and it was the same shield, and there was no version of this morning in which he carried a different one.

Behind him the column was already doing what a column does when it has no officers left. The front third had begun to run for the gates, which were shut, and the rear third had begun to run for the ditch, which was full of men who had gone into it first.

Kaalabad looked at him for the first time in sixteen months.

"You are the traitor's traitor," he said.

His voice was flat and did not travel. He had said perhaps forty words since the muster and he was spending nine of them here.

"Aye," said Wren.

Kaalabad closed the space.

He did not throw anything and he did not swing. He simply brought the centre in, and Wren, forty feet away, went to one knee.

*It has no shape.*

The weight came from above and from every other direction at the same instant, and it was not wind and it was not force in any arrangement his body had ever been asked to receive. His shoulders went down. His neck could not hold his head at the angle he wanted it. The mail on his back gained about four times its own weight and the padding under it compressed and his ribs found the limit of where they could go, and the road under both knees cracked in a circle, and forty feet away the draconic horse folded its legs and lay down.

Every loose thing between the two of them slid inward across the mud.

Wren got his left foot flat.

That was all he could do and it took him two full seconds and both of them were free.

The horizontal came in at the height of his throat.

It came without any wind-up and that was the part his eye refused: eleven feet of Zarakal travelling flat across the road at a speed the shaft should not have permitted, with a man's whole standing effort behind it and the pull of that centre added on top of the standing effort, and it was going to take his head and the shield and the arm and about three feet of the road behind him.

"Bulwark."

The shield went blue.

It came up through the hide from the boss out, a cold clean blue the colour of ice with light under it, and it ran to the rim and reached the iron and stopped there.

Zarakal hit it.

There was no crack. That was the thing that would frighten anyone who watched it and understood shields. A shield struck like that gives one sharp report and a splitting sound and then it is firewood. This one made a low sustained note, a foot deep, and held it, and the blue went out of it from the point of contact outward in a spreading front and left grey behind.

The force went across the face of the shield instead of into the boss, every square inch taking an identical share of it, and Wren felt the whole four feet of it push against his forearm with an even pressure like a hand laid flat instead of a hammer laid on a nail. His arm held. His shoulder held. His feet did not.

He went back eleven feet with both heels ploughing two furrows through frozen mud, and the furrows stayed.

He came to a stop against something soft that had been a man an hour ago.

Grey to the rim.

Something under the hide had gone. He could feel it through the enarmes as a slackness that had not been there, and the boss's old crack had opened past its second binding, and there was a long split running up from it toward the top edge that he could not see and did not need to see.

*That is one.*

*It takes three. It has always taken three and it took three at the ridge and it took three in the Stormfold and there is no reason on this earth it should take three from that.*

He got his shoulder under the shield's top edge and drove it up.

There was no technique in it. A big man and an angle, and that was all of it. The head of Zarakal was still in contact and still pushing and Wren put the rim under the fluke and stood up out of his knees, and the shaft went up and over his right shoulder and past his ear, and Kaalabad's hands went with it, and for about a third of a second the whole of the man was open from the hip to the jaw.

Wren did not go for the opening. He knew what was in there and he had nothing that would reach it.

He went to his belt.

The charge was iron, the size of a duck's egg, with a wax seal and a brass ring, and there were four of them on the belt and the smith who made them had died in the Stormfold and had never told anyone how.

There were four on the belt and Wren had carried them for eleven months and had used two in that time, one of which had done nothing at all, and the smith had been unable to tell him why beforehand and was in no position to tell him afterward.

He pulled the ring and threw it underhand and flat.

"Compact."

It went out about nine feet and opened.

Aetheric material came out of a duck's egg in a hard sphere twenty feet across, white, dense, arriving all at once with a face on it like a wall coming forward, and it took Kaalabad across the chest and the raised arms and put him off the ground.

He went up. Sixteen feet, and back, with the shaft still in both hands and his heels above his head for a moment, and the sphere continued to expand behind him with the sound of a mountain clearing its throat.

Then the sphere bent.

They went down where they were, on both sides, Tenrai and Greymane together, hands on the mud, and one of them was sick into the frozen ruts and could not lift his head out of it. The draconic horse got up and moved away.

Wren Greymane stayed on his feet.

He did it by putting the rim of a grey shield into the road and leaning his whole weight onto the boss and locking his knee, and his nose had begun to run blood down over his mouth and chin and it was going upward across his cheek instead, in a thin line, toward the man in front of him.

He got his teeth together, and set his left foot, and brought the shield up off the ground and onto his arm again.

For several seconds, Sonzai did not answer her.

Emira’s arms were around him, and that alone seemed so incompatible with the last several minutes that his mind needed time to accept it. He had been drowning beneath Kujo’s manufactured hopelessness, watching every answer turn useless before he could reach it, and then suddenly there was warmth against his chest, a familiar heartbeat beside his own, and his Calamity was no longer screaming in every direction. It still burned, Malakar’s old ugliness had not vanished, but under Emira’s presence the chaos had stopped behaving like an open wound and begun behaving like something Sonzai could actually hold.

“Emira…”

His voice came out rougher than he intended. Sonzai looked down at her as though checking twice that she was real, crimson hair hanging over his face while the pressure of Calamity continued circulating around them. His expression hardened almost immediately afterward, not at her, but at the realization that she had walked directly into the presence of the thing that had just broken him open.

“You shouldn’t be here.”

Then she squeezed harder.

Sonzai’s entire expression changed.

“Emira!!”

His ribs compressed beneath her arms.

**“Emira, I can’t fucking breathe.”**

When she finally eased the pressure, Sonzai dragged in a sharp breath and stared at her with an expression halfway between disbelief and irritation. For the first time since Kujo descended upon Kharven, something almost normal passed through him, some tiny surviving piece of the older brother who remembered her before kingdom, Calamity, exile, Tenrai blood, and all the other things that had turned the Moto family into a ledger of losses.

“You got stronger.”

He rubbed his ribs once, then looked at her with narrowed eyes.

“Unreasonably stronger.”

The humor lasted only a moment. Sonzai’s gaze shifted past her toward Kujo, and the remnants of amusement disappeared from his face as the Chains of War tightened around his hands. He moved slightly, positioning himself closer to Emira without pushing her away, even though some part of him understood with bitter irony that she had just dragged him out of something he had been incapable of escaping alone.

“That is Kujo.”

The name left Sonzai without decoration.

“Void Regent. The thing responsible for what happened here, for Stark, and for enough other graves that counting them right now would waste time.”

Calamity moved around Sonzai again, but differently now. Where it had previously exploded from him as fury without architecture, Emira’s Harmony had caused the corruption to organize around intention, crimson-black strands tightening into cleaner trajectories instead of thrashing through everything they touched. Sonzai noticed it almost immediately and slowly opened his damaged hand, watching the aura obey without needing him to force it.

His eyes moved back toward his sister.

“What did you do to me?”

There was no accusation in it. If anything, the question carried genuine astonishment, because he knew his Calamity better than nearly anyone alive and had spent years learning how impossible it was to make the thing behave without either suppressing it or surrendering to it. Emira had done neither.

“You didn’t remove it.”

Sonzai looked down as Calamity wound along one of the Chains of War, following the individual links instead of flooding over them.

“You gave it somewhere to go.”

He flexed his fingers and the Chain responded immediately, its inscriptions burning darker while the aura narrowed along the weapon like an edge being honed. The despair Kujo had forced into him had not disappeared, nor had the memory of Selvara suffering or Stark’s body hitting the rubble, but those things were no longer competing for every inch of his mind at once. Emira had somehow returned separation to him, the ability to hurt without becoming nothing except the hurt.

Sonzai looked at her again, and his voice lowered.

“I haven’t seen you in too long either.”

His jaw tightened.

“And apparently that was long enough for you to learn how to grab something Malakar put into my Soul Crystal and make it behave.”

His eyes softened for only a second.

“I missed you.”

Then Sonzai looked back at Kujo.

“Stay close to me.”

The instruction came instantly, but Sonzai caught himself before turning it into an order spoken purely from fear. After everything Kujo had just forced him to confront, he understood how easily protection became another excuse for deciding what someone else was permitted to do.

“No…stay where you can move. Just don’t let him separate us.”

Across the ruined tower, Kujo had become very still.

The Eternal Darkness remained wrapped around him from the neck downward, that seamless black mantle consuming the remnants of Kharven’s stolen daylight while his pale head remained exposed above it. Yet his attention was no longer centered entirely on Sonzai. His dark eyes had fixed upon Emira from the moment Harmony touched the Calamity, and the cruelty in his expression slowly gave way to something far more attentive.

Kujo lifted his chin slightly.

“That…” His gaze followed the refined flow around Sonzai’s body. “…is excellent.”

There was no mockery in the compliment.

Kujo stepped through the rubble at an unhurried pace, examining the interaction rather than merely the output. He had watched people suppress corruptive forces before, purify them, seal them, oppose them with a contradictory affinity, or replace one governing resonance with another; Emira had done something considerably more interesting. She had preserved the chaotic nature of Calamity while changing the conditions under which that chaos expressed itself.

“You did not cleanse him.”

Kujo’s eyes narrowed with genuine interest.

“You did not oppose the Calamity, and you certainly did not overpower it.”

The black mantle shifted over his shoulders as the Crevice moved beneath it.

“You harmonized with the man carrying it.”

Kujo’s smile returned, though this time it lacked the sadistic width he had shown while tormenting Sonzai. His attention moved from Emira to the sharpened Calamity running along the Chains, then back again, studying the result with the clinical fascination of someone who had unexpectedly found a new principle worth remembering.

“That distinction matters.”

He raised one covered hand, pointing loosely toward Sonzai.

“His chaos was dispersing because every emotion inside him was demanding equal priority. Rage at me. Fear for the woman. Grief for Stark. Shame regarding his brothers. His Calamity amplified the conflict until he ceased commanding it and became the location where all of it was happening.”

Kujo’s gaze settled on Emira.

“You gave those contradictions a common rhythm.”

For once, his compliment became almost academic.

“Harmony without suppression. Regulation without sterilization. You allowed disorder to remain disorder while removing the waste created by internal competition.”

His smile sharpened.

“That is much harder than simply making something calm.”

The darkness around Kujo rippled once as he looked between the siblings. Sonzai had been pinned beneath Kujo’s despair moments earlier, yet Emira had entered without grand violence, touched him, and achieved something all of Sonzai’s rage had failed to accomplish. The irony visibly entertained Kujo.

“Your brother has spent years trying to dominate Calamity.”

His eyes returned to Sonzai briefly.

“You hugged him.”

Then back to Emira.

“And produced a better result.”

Kujo laughed softly.

“That is humiliating.”

His expression settled again, more thoughtful than amused.

“But impressive.”

Kujo tilted his head toward Emira.

“You are not a fighter.”

The statement contained no contempt.

“Good.”

His smile became thin.

“Too many people discover an unusual gift and immediately ruin it by asking how quickly it can kill someone.”

Kujo looked once more at the living pattern she had created around Sonzai, watching Calamity circulate with clarity instead of losing its identity.

“Whatever you call that ability, Emira Moto…” His eyes darkened with interest. “…keep developing it.”

The Eternal Darkness spread behind him like an endless cape.

“Your family has enough people who know how to destroy things.”

Kujo’s smile returned.

“You may be the first one here who understands how to make something dangerous become more completely itself without letting it consume the person carrying it.”


"I-Is my power really the most important thing here right now?!" She said, releasing Sonzai as her hands were both held out to maintain the connection and synthesis between her and the harmony. The soft light produced from her palms produced a familiar warmth, through his mind, Sonzai began to remember the goodness of what he lived through. The purity was not the weapon, it was the hope that vitalized when she brought it to them.

Though it couldn't be definitively described as 'Hope' as hope would require the presence of despair to understand where hope had began. This simply did not target and break away the darkness, it expanded more than just that. The power surrounding them began to shift into a field, a barrier of sorts that reflected the intention that Emira had placed into it. 

"Sonzai, please, I need you to stay focused. That man... Kujo... he's dangerous when he is left unchecked. If he is what you say he is... then I will not back away simply because you are afraid." She said, her palm pressing against his back as she focused on the anchor point. The lock forming onto him as Sonzai's stability began to keep it's own footing, the lock pressing down onto the point, bonding and giving it room for his own expression.

"So I am giving you something I don't usually give out freely. I know the calamity inside of you is unstable, but by releasing the good parts of you, I can counteract the weight of the corruption. It won't be permanent, but it will be more than enough to keep you going." She explained to him, though he hear the words come out of her mouth. He merely felt exactly what intention she had of doing. 

For a moment Sonzai's mind was clear, his lattice stabilized, the calamity inside of his soul began to shape and reform into the mold around his soul. The storm living inside of him became aligned, rather than discriminatory. The silence was vitalized by the awakening pulse of the essence of life flowing through his veins. The transfer was complete-


Sonzai stood in the aftermath of the transfer and simply breathed. For the first time since Kujo had descended upon Kharven, there was no violent argument taking place inside his Soul Crystal; Calamity still existed in all its viciousness, but it had settled into the contours of his will rather than clawing at them. The pain in his crushed arm disappeared as flesh and bone restored themselves beneath Emira’s vitalizing influence, and the exhaustion that had dragged through his muscles receded until his posture straightened completely.

He looked down at his hands and opened them once, watching crimson-black Calamity coil around his fingers in thin, obedient streams. It did not feel purified. It felt sharpened, as though Emira had reminded the corruption that Sonzai existed before Malakar ever touched him and there were parts of him Calamity could amplify without being allowed to define.

“Emira.”

His gaze lifted to his sister, the usual severity in his face softened by something he rarely allowed anyone outside his family to see.

“I heard you. I’m focused.”

Sonzai flexed his restored arm, then looked at her more seriously. Her refusal to leave because he was afraid irritated the protective part of him, but he could hardly argue after she had just pulled him out of an abyss that neither the Chains nor his rage could break. He gave a quiet exhale through his nose and accepted the point without pretending he liked it.

“And apparently you’ve decided being my little sister means ignoring me when I tell you something is dangerous.”

A faint edge of humor crossed his expression.

“I suppose some things survived the exile.”

Then his eyes shifted.

Selvara.

Everything else became secondary.

Sonzai vanished from beside Emira with a violent displacement of air, his flash step crossing the ruined distance in an instant. He appeared beside Selvara amid the broken stone, one arm immediately sliding beneath her back while the other supported her legs, careful despite the speed with which he had moved. The sight of the wounds Kujo had inflicted tightened something ugly behind Sonzai’s ribs, but this time Calamity did not explode, it narrowed.

“Enough.”

The word was meant as much for himself as anything else.

Sonzai flash stepped again.

He returned to Emira with Selvara held against his chest, the Chains of War trailing behind him while controlled Calamity wrapped close to his body instead of spilling across the ruins. He lowered himself beside his sister and carefully shifted Selvara so Emira could reach the injuries without obstruction.

“Emira.”

Sonzai’s voice had lost nearly all of its hardness.

“Please heal her.”

There was no royal tone in it, no command from a Moto heir and no attempt to disguise what the request meant. Sonzai looked from the wounds to Emira, and his jaw tightened once before he continued.

“Whatever you just did to me, whatever part of that you can safely give her, give it to her. Don’t hurt yourself doing it, but please…”

His eyes returned to Selvara.

“Help her for me.”

Sonzai carefully took one of Selvara’s hands between both of his. The gesture looked almost strange coming from someone whose hands were usually wrapped around Chains, weapons, or another person’s throat, and he stared at her fingers for a moment as though remembering each time he had pushed her away because distrust had seemed easier than admitting attachment. Kujo had already discovered the truth Sonzai had barely allowed himself to articulate.

He cared.

Far more than he wanted Kujo to know.

Sonzai lowered his head and pressed a quiet kiss against the back of Selvara’s hand.

“You idiot.”

The words came softly, stripped of their usual bite.

“You had no business throwing yourself at him for me.”

His thumb moved once across her knuckles. Sonzai’s expression remained controlled, but his eyes betrayed the remnants of what Kujo had forced him to watch, the repeated wounds, his own inability to immediately stop them, and the terror that underneath all his anger there had been one unbearable possibility he had refused to name. His grip on her hand tightened just enough to reassure himself that she was still there.

“I’m supposed to be the one making terrible decisions.”

A thin, tired smile briefly touched his mouth.

“You’re ruining the division of labor.”

Sonzai remained beside her instead of immediately returning to the fight. That alone said more than any declaration could have; the Blood Hound, the man who had spent years believing movement was the only acceptable response to pain, chose for several seconds to stay still. He watched Emira’s hands and the space around Selvara carefully, unwilling to interfere with whatever Harmony required.

Then his gaze hardened toward the darkness beyond them.

Kujo was still there. The fear remained too. But it no longer owned the room inside Sonzai.

“Emira.”

He did not look away from the place Kujo stood.

“You were right about one thing.”

Calamity wound around the Chains of War behind him, each link beginning to hum beneath the disciplined crimson-black flow.

“I was afraid.”

His thumb remained against Selvara’s hand.

“I still am.”

Sonzai’s purple eyes narrowed.

“But he made the mistake of teaching me exactly what I’m afraid to lose.”

Emira looked at Sonzai for a moment, pausing for a moment as she looked at him for a moment, listening rather than speaking. She shifted up from her behind, rising to her feet as she let out a soft sigh.

"You know maybe I'm starting to regret even giving you the boost in the first place." She said, her eyes narrowing as her brother's dry humor brought the opposite of the intended effect. Her gaze narrowed into something dull and yet a soft giggle left her in spite of this.

"But still, I think I overstepped it a bit with how much I put into you." She said, her legs wobbling a little bit despite her ability to still stand. She shifted herself as she backed away towards Selvara. Seeing as how exhausted she had become after her little ability, her footing shifted as she looked back at Sonzai.

"And brother, don't get yourself killed... otherwise Sodoku is going to kill me for not being there for you." She said, letting out a dry chuckle as she moved towards the door. The gate shutting behind her as the focus returned back over towards Sonzai and Kujo.

Across the halls of Kharven, the sounds of the God Eater could be heard, the howling, the waves of it's voice hit against the castle, the wind blowing violently, almost disasterously. Kujo could feel the beasts connection to the Material World, and how it had begun to be prepared for the arrival of the next layer of the void. 

The God Eater, Formly known as The Hellborne, had continued to consume the essence within the crust of the hollowed forests of Hell's Garden, feeling the connection between Kujo within the darkness of the world. It's growls crescendo as it shifted itself, it's frame leaning against the stone of the mountains. A soft rhythmic sound came out of it, mimicking a chuckle. Knowing that it was almost time.

Kujo watched Sonzai rise beneath the last remnants of Emira’s Harmony, and the amusement in his face returned almost immediately. Calamity no longer leaked from the Moto heir in uncontrolled waves; it threaded itself along the Chains of War in disciplined crimson-black currents, each link humming with violence that had finally been given direction. Xevorith still covered Kujo from the neck downward like a seamless black cape, and behind that unnatural drape the Crevice beat in slow black-gold pulses that made the ruined tower groan around him.

“Sonzai Moto. I’m glad you’re healed again. Now tell me, are you finally going to fight properly, or will you submit to Aelor before I have to break you a third time?”

Sonzai’s eyes narrowed until the purple in them seemed almost swallowed by the Calamity climbing around his face. He rolled his restored shoulder once, felt the healed arm answer him cleanly, and tightened both hands around the Chains as the inscriptions along them flared with the memory of battles survived and wars unfinished. Emira’s influence had not softened him; it had merely carved channels through the storm so all of his hatred could move in the same direction.

“Submit? You tortured Selvara in front of me, murdered Stark, crawled through my memories, and stood over Kharven like you owned the sky. If Aelor wants me kneeling, then come down here and make me.”

Kujo smiled.

“That is more like you.”

Sonzai vanished before the sentence finished.

The first Chain came from Kujo’s blind side and hit with enough force to fold the remaining wall behind him into a shower of stone. The second wrapped around his ankle and ripped his footing away while a third struck the opposite shoulder, wrenching his torso across the axis of his own fall. Sonzai was already above him by then, flash-stepping through the debris with one Chain wrapped around his forearm, Calamity compacted so tightly along the metal that the links looked edged.

He smashed the weapon down across Kujo’s face.

The impact drove Kujo through two floors of the tower.

Stone burst downward around him in slabs. Support beams snapped, the central stairwell collapsed, and the entire eastern side of the structure leaned several degrees before breaking free and crashing into the street below. Sonzai followed through the opening without hesitation, descending through the rubble while the Chains spread around him like a storm given shape.

Kujo caught the next strike against his forearm.

The Chain carved through the black mantle anyway.

A long section of Xevorith tore from his shoulder, exposing pale flesh beneath and a glimpse of the vertical black-gold fracture burning under his sternum. Sonzai twisted, yanked the captured Chain backward, and used the recoil to sling himself past Kujo’s flank while two more Chains struck from above and below at once.

The upper one smashed Kujo across the spine.

The lower one hit his ribs.

Bone cracked audibly beneath both impacts.

Kujo folded sideways and struck the floor hard enough to crater it, but his smile remained.

“Better.”

Sonzai’s answer was another barrage.

The Chains of War multiplied across the ruined chamber in every direction, not literally becoming more numerous but moving so quickly their trajectories overlapped into a dense metallic lattice. One tore through Kujo’s side, another snapped across his throat, three more hammered into his torso before his body could fully recover from the previous blow, and Sonzai himself appeared inside the gaps to drive a knee into Kujo’s stomach hard enough to lift him from the ground.

Kujo spat blood.

Sonzai caught him with a Chain around the neck before he could fall.

Then he pulled.

Kujo’s body was yanked backward through a pillar, smashed through a second, and dragged across the broken floor while splintered stone and black dust followed in his wake. Sonzai planted both feet, roared, and whipped the Chain in a wide circle before releasing Kujo into the remains of the western wall.

The entire section detonated outward.

For several seconds, only dust moved.

Then Kujo stepped through it.

His cheek was split. Blood ran from his nose and mouth. One rib pressed visibly wrong beneath the skin, and the black cape hanging from his body was shredded in several places.

He was still smiling.

“You’re fast.”

Sonzai’s expression hardened.

“Stop smiling.”

Kujo’s grin widened.

“Make me.”

Sonzai disappeared again.

This time he did not attack Kujo directly.

Four Chains shot past him and buried themselves into the surrounding ruins. Another pair wrapped around broken columns, and Sonzai used the entire battlefield as an anchor system, redirecting his own movement through sharp impossible angles while keeping Kujo trapped within the center. Every flash step was assisted by the Chains, each one yanking Sonzai into a new vector faster than a conventional body could safely tolerate.

He struck Kujo’s jaw from the left.

A Chain hit the right temple before Kujo’s head had finished turning.

Another caught the knee and broke it sideways.

Sonzai drove his elbow into Kujo’s sternum, then wrapped a Chain around his waist and slammed him headfirst into the ground.

The stone split.

Kujo’s skull left a crater.

Sonzai yanked him back up before he could settle and smashed him down again.

Then again.

Then again.

Each impact grew heavier, Calamity feeding into the Chain until the ruined district beneath the tower began trembling from repeated shockwaves. By the sixth slam, the floor had ceased being a floor and become a bowl of fractured stone filled with dust and black blood.

Sonzai stood over him, breathing hard.

“I told you. I’ll give you despair.”

Kujo looked up through blood running down his face.

“You are trying very hard.”

Then the black moved.

A spike erupted beneath Sonzai’s feet and struck him through the abdomen, lifting him several feet off the ground. Before he could free himself, two more black bands snapped around his wrists and pulled his arms apart while another wrapped around his throat. Kujo rose beneath him as though the broken bones in his own body meant nothing, every movement slow enough to make the imbalance obvious.

He pulled the spike free.

Sonzai dropped.

Kujo struck him across the face.

The blow sent him through a half-collapsed wall and into the neighboring building.

Sonzai hit hard enough to break through the first interior partition, then the second, and finally buried himself in a room that had already lost half its ceiling. Blood ran from his mouth as he forced himself back up, one hand braced against the floor while the Chains trembled around him.

Kujo walked through the hole after him.

He did not hurry.

“You are fighting much better.”

The black around Kujo’s feet crawled across the floorboards.

“But I am still deciding how much of this matters.”

Sonzai spat blood onto the ground.

“Then decide faster.”

He attacked again.

The next exchange was uglier.

Sonzai abandoned elegance and turned the Chains into pure war. One wrapped around Kujo’s forearm and broke it backward at the elbow. Another tore through his thigh. Sonzai flash-stepped directly into his chest, drove both knees into him, and used the Chains to pull Kujo toward the impact rather than away from it.

The collision folded Kujo’s torso.

Sonzai landed behind him, seized one Chain in both hands, and whipped it across Kujo’s back with enough force to tear away nearly all of Xevorith’s covering from the shoulders downward.


Kujo stumbled.

Sonzai did not let him recover.

The Chains struck from all sides. One tore through Kujo’s abdomen. Another ripped open his shoulder. A third split his lips and shattered several teeth.

Sonzai flash-stepped through the blood spray and drove his fist into Kujo’s face, then followed with a second blow, a third, a fourth, each strike carrying enough Calamity to crack the remaining stone around their feet.

Kujo allowed it.

That was when Sonzai understood. Kujo was letting him hit. Sonzai froze for half a heartbeat.

Kujo looked up through the damage, one eye swollen and the other still bright with amusement.

“Yes.”

His voice came out wet with blood.

“You noticed.”

Sonzai’s face twisted.

*“You’re fucking with me.”*

Kujo laughed.

*“Of course I am.”*

That rage cost Sonzai.

The black beneath him erupted upward before he could redirect.

One mass wrapped his legs. Another closed around his torso. A third seized both arms.

Kujo straightened despite the state of his body and stepped close enough that Sonzai could see blood running down his chin.

“You needed to feel strong again.”

Kujo tilted his head.

“You needed to believe Emira had repaired more than your body.”

The black tightened. Sonzai’s ribs creaked.

“So I gave you victories.”

The restraints slammed him down. His face hit stone. The ground fractured beneath him.

Kujo planted one foot between Sonzai’s shoulder blades and forced him deeper into the crater.

“And here we are.”

The pressure increased.

Sonzai’s Chains rose.

Kujo pinned each one separately with black masses that appeared around them like individual graves.

Sonzai struggled harder.

The black responded by crushing his healed arm until bone began bending again.

Then steel whispered behind Kujo.

Clean. Precise. Final.

Nyxion passed through his neck. Kujo’s head separated from his body. For one instant, nothing moved.

Then his head fell.

It struck the stone with a dull sound and rolled once, long black hair trailing through dust and blood. His body remained standing for another second before the knees gave way and it collapsed beside Sonzai.

Behind him stood Dawn Guard.

Dawn Guard held Nyxion extended through the completed cut, the Sword of Severance dark against the ruined light. His white mantle moved slowly in the air displaced by the strike, and there was no triumph in his face, only the hard stillness of someone who had learned not to celebrate before an enemy stopped existing.

Sonzai shoved the black off himself.

Kujo’s body lay motionless. The black sun above Kharven flickered.

For one heartbeat, the city seemed to breathe again.

Then Xevorith pulsed. The severed head vanished. The corpse vanished.

Kujo stood where he had stood before the cut.

Whole. Untouched. His neck bore no wound.

Nyxion had still completed the strike. The past simply no longer possessed the right to explain Kujo’s present.

The Unwinding.

Kujo slowly turned toward Dawn Guard.

His smile returned.

“Dawn Guard.”

Zuberi shifted Nyxion into guard. Kujo looked him over with open interest.

“You’re finally awake after our last encounter.”

Dawn Guard’s expression did not change.

“You should have stayed dead.”

Kujo’s smile widened.

“That would have been polite.”

Sonzai attacked before either could say more.

The Chains came high. Dawn Guard moved low. For the first time, Kujo’s smile faded.

Sonzai’s Chains flooded the air around him, attacking from six directions at once while Dawn Guard entered beneath them with Nyxion in one hand and Valkyros in the other. Sonzai’s violence forced Kujo to defend wide; Dawn Guard’s economy punished every opening that created.

A Chain wrapped Kujo’s right arm.

Nyxion took it off at the shoulder. Valkyros followed through the ribs.

The impact launched Kujo sideways.

Sonzai redirected him mid-flight with another Chain around the waist and slammed him back toward Dawn Guard.

Dawn Guard met him with Valkyros.

The blade struck Kujo’s sternum and drove him into the ground.

Sonzai’s Chains descended immediately afterward.

The crater exploded.

Kujo disappeared beneath metal, Calamity, and shattered stone.

Dawn Guard stepped through the blast and drove Nyxion down into the center.

The Sword of Severance pierced Kujo’s chest.

Sonzai wrapped three Chains around the embedded blade and pulled.

Kujo’s torso tore open.

For one moment, the damage was catastrophic enough to look final.

Then The Unwinding activated. Kujo stood three yards away. Untouched.

Dawn Guard’s blades still carried the sensation of contact.

Sonzai’s Chains still bore Kujo’s blood. But Kujo had no wounds.

Zuberi’s eyes narrowed.

“He waits until completion.”

Sonzai understood.

“Then we don’t let anything finish.”

That changed everything.

Sonzai stopped throwing completed strikes.

The Chains became moving conditions instead of attacks, rotating around Kujo in continuous loops that never fully resolved into impact. Dawn Guard entered through those unfinished trajectories and used Valkyros to create Material pressure while Nyxion cut at the relationships Kujo tried to establish with the surrounding Void.

Kujo stepped left. A Chain denied it.  He stepped backward.

Valkyros shattered the ground behind him. He opened darkness beneath Dawn Guard. Nyxion severed the connection before the Void could complete.

Sonzai came from above. Kujo blocked the Chain.

Dawn Guard struck from below. Valkyros cut through Kujo’s abdomen. The blow remained active.

Sonzai wrapped the Chains around Kujo’s torso before the wound could become a completed past event.

“Hold him!”

Sonzai pulled. Dawn Guard twisted Valkyros. Kujo’s body bent around the blade. Nyxion came next.

It cut across Kujo’s side, severing a section of Xevorith from his body before the darkness could fully manifest.

Kujo’s expression finally changed. Not fear. Concentration.

Sonzai saw it.

“Again!”

The Chains tightened. Dawn Guard moved in. Kujo responded with the Fifth Layer. The space between Chain links disappeared.

Several lengths of metal compressed violently into themselves, links colliding hard enough to spark and deform. Sonzai’s shoulders jerked from the sudden loss of extension, and Kujo used that half-second to open a seam of higher Void beside Dawn Guard.

The black struck Zuberi across the ribs.

He was launched through a stone wall.

Sonzai reacted instantly. Every remaining Chain converged. Kujo let them hit. Metal pierced his shoulders.

His thigh. His abdomen. His chest. Calamity ignited inside the wounds.

Kujo’s body convulsed.

Sonzai pulled all the Chains outward. Kujo was torn apart.

The blood had barely reached the floor before The Unwinding answered.

The completed sequence disappeared from his present.

Kujo stood behind Sonzai.

Whole.

He drove a black-covered fist into the back of Sonzai’s skull. Sonzai hit the ground face-first. Before Kujo could follow, Dawn Guard returned.

Nyxion passed through Kujo’s waist. Valkyros struck the opposite side. The two attacks crossed. Kujo’s body separated at the middle.

The Unwinding activated again.

Whole. Dawn Guard attacked again before Kujo could speak. Nyxion severed one arm. Valkyros broke the opposite shoulder.

Kujo countered with a black spike through Dawn Guard’s side.

Sonzai’s Chain wrapped around the spike and ripped it away before it could finish driving deeper.

The three of them collided at the center of the ruined district.

Stone ceased surviving near them. Every exchange broke something.

A house collapsed when Sonzai’s Chain missed Kujo and tore through the foundation.

A street split when Dawn Guard drove Valkyros into the ground to redirect a black wave.

Kujo opened a section of Void beneath both men, and the surrounding buildings tilted inward as if gravity had forgotten which way belonged down.

Sonzai wrapped a Chain around Dawn Guard’s arm and yanked him free.

Dawn Guard returned the favor seconds later by severing a black tether around Sonzai’s throat.

Kujo moved between them like an absence wearing a body.

He allowed Dawn Guard to cut him. Allowed Sonzai to crush him. Allowed ribs to break. Allowed flesh to tear. Allowed Chains to puncture him from six angles while Nyxion severed the Void around his neck and Valkyros smashed his chest nearly flat.

Then each completed injury vanished. One by one. The battlefield retained every crater. Every broken wall. Every pool of blood. Every shattered Chain-link.

Kujo retained nothing.

He stood in the center of the devastation immaculate beneath Xevorith.

Sonzai was breathing hard now. Blood ran from his nose and mouth. One shoulder sagged lower than the other.

Dawn Guard stood beside him with a deep wound across his side and dust caked into the white mantle, both swords still raised.

Kujo looked at them.

“You understand the cruelty now.”

His voice carried several layers beneath it.

“You can hurt me.”

The black sun over Kharven deepened.

“You can mutilate me.”

The Crevice pulsed.

“You can sever me.”

Xevorith spread wider.

“You can reduce this body to pieces and watch every piece hit the ground.”

Kujo smiled.

“But the moment you finish…” The darkness moved behind him like a continent turning. “…you give me permission to make it irrelevant.”

Sonzai wiped blood from his mouth.

“Then we keep you in the moment.”

Dawn Guard lowered his stance.

“No ending.”

Kujo’s smile disappeared. That answer interested him.

The Chains rose again. Valkyros and Nyxion crossed. Sonzai’s Calamity tightened. Dawn Guard’s Material authority pressed into the broken ground.

Kujo opened his arms.

The Fifth Layer unfolded behind him like endless night.

“Good.”

His voice dropped into something darker.

“Now make me work for it.”

Kujo’s smile disappeared.

The shift was immediate. He had spent the previous exchanges allowing Dawn Guard and Sonzai to dictate tempo, studying their coordination while permitting wounds simply to demonstrate that completed damage meant nothing beneath Xevorith. Now the Eternal Darkness drew close around his body like a fitted black cloak, and Kujo lowered both hands into an ordinary fighter’s stance.

“Enough defense.”

He vanished between them.

Kujo appeared inside Dawn Guard’s reach before either sword could properly turn, slipping beneath the angle of Nyxion with his shoulder nearly touching Zuberi’s chest. His left palm struck the inside of Dawn Guard’s elbow to spoil the blade line, his right fist buried itself beneath the ribs, and before the body could recoil Kujo rotated behind him and drove the ridge of his forearm across the back of his neck. The combination contained no wasted motion; every impact prepared the posture required for the next.

Dawn Guard stumbled one step and Kujo followed him into it. A short elbow struck the jaw, a heel hooked behind Zuberi’s ankle, and Kujo simultaneously shoved his shoulder in the opposite direction so the Sovereign was violently twisted off his base rather than simply knocked backward. Before Dawn Guard could touch the ground, Kujo caught him by the tunic, pulled him upright again, and drove three compact body shots into the same side.

Sonzai’s Chains came screaming across Kujo’s flank.

Kujo ducked beneath the first without looking. He caught the second between his forearm and bicep, stepped toward Sonzai rather than away from the weapon, and used the tension in the Chain to pull the Moto heir directly into a knee that crashed into his abdomen. Sonzai folded around the impact, only for Kujo to catch the back of his head and redirect him face-first toward Dawn Guard.

Zuberi recovered fast enough to move aside.

Kujo had expected that.

He pivoted with Sonzai still in his grasp, released him at the exact instant his momentum crossed Dawn Guard’s position, and immediately followed behind the thrown body. When Zuberi shifted to avoid Sonzai, Kujo occupied the emptied space and struck him with a rising palm beneath the chin, a knuckle-driven blow into the sternum, then a spinning elbow across the temple.

The combinations were wrong in ways both men immediately recognized.

They were not built from one martial school. Kujo transitioned between narrow infighting, joint manipulation, throws, low-line kicks, deceptive hand traps, and movements that seemed designed for bodies with entirely different skeletal proportions. He had assembled combat from centuries of observation until styles stopped being traditions and became interchangeable answers to anatomy.

Sonzai recovered behind him and flash-stepped forward.

Kujo turned at precisely the wrong time for conventional defense.

His hand slapped Sonzai’s punch aside, but instead of countering toward the exposed face he stepped outside the arm, hammered his fist into the tricep, kicked behind the knee, and caught Sonzai by the jaw as the leg buckled. Kujo dragged his head downward while driving his own knee upward, then released him only to strike the side of his neck with the opposite hand.

Sonzai’s Chains erupted around them.

Kujo moved through the gaps.

One Chain passed over his shoulder while Kujo drove two fingers into Sonzai’s wrist joint, another curved toward his spine only for Kujo to rotate Sonzai directly into its trajectory and force the weapon to abort. Kujo then hooked one foot behind Sonzai’s heel, swept the leg, and struck him across the face before he had even finished falling.

Dawn Guard entered from behind with Valkyros.

Kujo shifted half a step.

The blade missed his throat by less than an inch. Kujo trapped Zuberi’s sword wrist between both hands, slammed an elbow into the forearm, drove a headbutt into his face, then twisted beneath the arm and used Dawn Guard’s own forward momentum to throw the larger man over his shoulder.

Zuberi landed hard.

Kujo stamped toward his ribs.

Dawn Guard rolled.

Kujo’s foot cracked the stone where his body had been, but Sonzai was already descending from above with Calamity burning around one fist. Kujo met him in midair with an upward kick that caught him beneath the sternum and sent him higher before a black tendril snagged his ankle and violently returned him to the ground.

Kujo landed between them.

Neither man had fallen because Kujo was physically stronger alone. He was reading weight shifts, shoulder positioning, breath patterns, weapon commitments, and the tiny unconscious corrections both men made whenever they fought beside another person. He was beating the coordination apart one decision at a time.

“You both depend upon extraordinary things.”

Kujo raised his hands again.

“Bloodlines. Legendary weapons. Calamity. Sovereignty.”

His dark eyes moved between them.

“I learned a long time ago that gods still have jaws.”

Sonzai attacked first out of fury.

Kujo met him willingly.

They exchanged at close range with enough speed that fists stopped looking separate, Sonzai throwing hooks and elbows while Kujo redirected rather than blocking them outright. Kujo let one punch skim his cheek, slipped inside the next, struck the liver, shoulder, throat, and sternum in a four-beat sequence, then caught Sonzai’s wrist and drove him headfirst into an approaching knee.

Dawn Guard came through the opening Sonzai created.

His sword remained in hand, but Zuberi used the pommel rather than the edge, smashing toward Kujo’s temple before following with a brutal shoulder check. Kujo took the first impact partially, rotated with the second, and then abruptly trapped Dawn Guard’s forward arm while kicking the back of his knee.

Zuberi refused to fall.

Kujo smiled.

He changed combinations.

A palm went into Dawn Guard’s throat, followed by a low kick to the thigh, an elbow into the collarbone, another palm against the ear, then a short uppercut that snapped his head backward. Before Kujo could continue, Sonzai wrapped a Chain around his waist and tore him away.

Kujo landed several yards back.

He rolled his shoulders.

“Better.”

The Eternal Darkness suddenly rose from his body.

Xevorith spread behind Kujo until the ruined district seemed to lose its chronology. Broken buildings appeared briefly intact in their shadows, falling snow hung beside the memory of snow that had already melted, and Sonzai and Dawn Guard both experienced the sickening impression that the battlefield contained more than one version of the last several minutes.

Kujo lifted his right hand.

“The present is where you believe the attack must occur.”

His fingers slowly closed.

“That assumption is provincial.”

The Unwinding moved backward.

Kujo did not travel into the past. Instead, he reached into completed sequences belonging to Dawn Guard and Sonzai and found moments when their bodies had occupied positions now remembered only as history. He selected a single uninterrupted sequence within each of them and voided one fact from it: that a specific portion of their body had remained present throughout that interval.

Nothing visibly struck them.

Then Dawn Guard’s body jerked.

A perfectly circular absence suddenly existed through the left side of his torso, passing cleanly through flesh without an entry wound or projectile. It looked less like something had pierced him and more like reality had only just realized that a small section of Zuberi had not been included in the sequence leading to the present.

Sonzai staggered almost simultaneously.

A matching void appeared through his lower abdomen.

For one disorienting instant his body continued behaving as though the missing portion existed, muscles attempting to contract across an absence and Essence circulating toward pathways that no longer completed. Then pain arrived all at once.

Sonzai yelled.

Dawn Guard’s teeth clenched before a harsher sound escaped him as his body tried to reconcile itself with a wound that possessed no moment of impact.

Kujo watched them both.

“I did not pierce you.”

His black cape spread wider.

“I removed the past in which those pieces successfully accompanied you here.”

Kujo walked toward them while the two men struggled to stabilize themselves. The holes did not bleed conventionally at first because there was no torn path for the body to interpret, only missing material surrounded by tissue that remembered having once connected across the gap. Then their regenerative processes began attempting to answer the contradiction, and Kujo’s smile widened.

“Now your bodies must argue with history.”

The black around Kujo’s feet expanded.

He did not attack again.

Not physically.

Instead, Xevorith reached toward both men and built a simulation around their perception, one so complete that body, Soul, Fate, and Name all received the same false information at once. Kujo began showing them what it would feel like for existence itself to stop assembling them.

Their fingertips went first. There was no visible disappearance. They could still see their hands.

But touch stopped belonging to them.

Sonzai looked down as his fingers became conceptually distant, visible objects that his mind could observe yet no longer completely recognize as his own. Dawn Guard flexed his hand and felt the movement occur a fraction removed from intention, as though the command and the limb performing it belonged to different people.

Kujo walked between them.

“This is the part death never teaches.”

The sensation crawled upward. Hands became forearms. Forearms became shoulders.

Their bodies remained physically intact beyond the holes Kujo had already created, yet every internal system began reporting that ownership itself was disappearing. Breath entered lungs, but the mind no longer felt unquestionably connected to the one breathing.

Sonzai roared and tried to flood himself with Calamity.

The power responded.

That made it worse.

He could feel Calamity moving through his Soul Crystal while simultaneously experiencing the conviction that neither the Calamity nor the Crystal belonged to a stable identity called Sonzai Moto. The Chains of War shook around him, recognizing their bearer even while Kujo’s simulation made the bearer increasingly unable to recognize himself.

Dawn Guard drove one knee into the ground.

His Sovereignty pressed against the effect, establishing Material acknowledgment of his own form, but Kujo was not actually erasing that form. He was simulating the sensation of every relationship connecting Zuberi to that form being individually withdrawn, leaving authority with no reassuring proof that the person exercising it would remain coherent.

Both men yelled as the simulation reached deeper.

Kujo’s expression became serene.

“This is emptiness.”

The black cape behind him opened until Kharven’s ruined skyline disappeared inside it.

“Not darkness. Darkness still gives the eye something it cannot see.”

Kujo stepped toward Sonzai first.

“Not silence. Silence requires the possibility of sound.”

He moved toward Dawn Guard.

“Not loneliness. Loneliness is crowded with the memory of someone absent.”

His voice lowered.

“The Void is less generous.”

Xevorith stripped another layer from the simulation.

Their memories remained.

Ownership did not.

Sonzai remembered Muken, Ayame, Tomuka, Ezo, Sodoku, Emira, Selvara, Kharven, exile, blood, snow, Chains, Calamity, but every memory arrived like something discovered in another man’s archive. Dawn Guard remembered Kushara, family, conquest, duty, Asante, Ara, the weight of a throne, yet the connection between those memories and the one experiencing them began dissolving.

Sonzai screamed harder.

Dawn Guard tried to rise and collapsed onto one hand.

Kujo watched with open fascination.

“Nothing does not hurt because nothing is cruel.”

His smile slowly returned.

“It hurts because you are still here long enough to understand what is being taken.”

The simulation reached their Names.

Sonzai Moto.

The words remained comprehensible.

But Sonzai began experiencing them as a historical designation rather than a present identity.

Zuberi Zettari.

The Name still possessed recognition.

Yet Dawn Guard heard it internally as though it referred to someone who had existed a moment ago.

Kujo stood between them.

“You think nonexistence is peaceful because every language invented by the living describes it from the outside.”

His voice became layered beneath Xevorith.

“You imagine rest. Release. An ending.”

The Crevice pulsed beneath the darkness.

“But those are luxuries of death.”

Kujo raised both hands.

“The Void offers no grave.”

Their perception of bodily boundaries began disappearing entirely.

Sonzai could no longer tell where his chest ended and the air began.

Dawn Guard felt his heartbeat but could not locate it inside anything.

The holes Kujo had placed through their bodies remained the only visually obvious injuries, yet the simulated unmaking made every undamaged piece feel as though it were quietly preparing to follow those absences into nothing.

They could do little except endure it.

And yell.

Kujo listened.

“This is what devastation actually becomes when you carry it far enough.”

The ruined city around them darkened.

“People mistake devastation for destroyed buildings.”

He glanced toward Kharven’s broken structures.

“They count burned homes. Dead soldiers. Empty granaries. Broken governments.”

His gaze returned to them.

“That is only architecture.”

Kujo’s expression changed as the Crevice stirred more violently beneath Xevorith.

“True devastation begins when survivors can no longer trust continuity.”

The black sun over Kharven pulsed.

“When the farmer wonders whether planting matters because winter may erase the field. When a mother stops naming another child because Names have become invitations to grief. When soldiers stop repairing walls because every wall has already failed once.”

The simulation tightened. Sonzai’s cries became ragged.

Dawn Guard forced himself upright only halfway before another wave of fabricated dissolution crashed through his identity and drove him back down.

Kujo smiled.

“That is where despair begins.”

His voice softened.

“Not when something terrible happens.”

He walked slowly through the space between them.

“Despair begins when tomorrow stops functioning as a promise.”

The darkness swallowed more of Kharven’s horizon.

“When action no longer guarantees consequence.”

Another step.

“When courage becomes merely another expensive way to suffer.”

Kujo stopped before both men.

“And when the living finally understand that survival itself may only be the interval before something worse notices them.”

He let the simulation reach its deepest point.

For several seconds, Sonzai and Dawn Guard experienced awareness without identity.

No bodies. No Names. No histories. No relationships.

Only consciousness suspended over an absolute absence that provided nothing for consciousness to attach itself to.

Kujo’s voice became the only remaining reference.

“That is the Void.”

No laughter accompanied him now.

“No throne.”

The darkness stretched infinitely behind him.

“No law.”

A black-gold fracture opened beneath his cape.

“No ancestor.”

The Crevice throbbed.

“No war.”

His eyes became almost completely black.

“No victory.”

Kujo leaned slightly closer.

“No you.”

Then he restored them. Everything came crashing back at once. Names. Bodies. Pain. Memory. Calamity. Material Sovereignty. The ruined district.

The missing portions in their torsos.

The sheer violence of suddenly becoming themselves again drove both men into fresh agony, as though existence itself had been forced back through bodies too small to contain it.

Kujo stood above them. He looked almost disappointed by the world around him.

“Valmir believed devastation required renewal.”

The black cape shifted.

“He believed collapse was useful because something followed it.”

Kujo’s mouth slowly curled upward.

“That was his weakness.”

The Crevice brightened beneath him.

“I have no interest in what follows.”

The darkness over Kharven spread outward, swallowing mountain after mountain beneath the blackened sun.

“I will ruin your cities until rebuilding becomes superstition.”

His voice deepened.

“I will break your nations until history cannot remember where one kingdom ended and another began.”

The Void stirred across the horizon.

“I will make your magic expensive.”

Black veins crawled across the sky.

“Your bloodlines unreliable.”

The wind died.

“Your records contradictory.”

Snow stopped falling where the darkness touched it.

“Your gods distant.”

Kujo spread his arms.

“And your hope embarrassing.”

The Fifth Layer opened behind him like an infinite curtain being drawn across reality.

“Then I will wait.”

His smile widened.

“I will wait while devastation becomes habit.”

The Crevice pulsed again.

“While habit becomes exhaustion.”

Darkness swallowed another section of Kharven.

“While exhaustion becomes despair.”

Kujo looked down at Sonzai and Dawn Guard.

“And when despair has taught this world to stop expecting itself tomorrow…” His voice fractured into several overlapping registers. “…I will Unwind it.”

The black sky convulsed.

“Not destroy it.”

Kujo’s grin became monstrous.

“Not conquer it.”

The Void behind him seemed to deepen beyond depth.

“I will make the sequence called this world unnecessary.”

His arms lowered slowly.

“Every kingdom.”

The black moved.

“Every covenant.”

Another pulse.

“Every inheritance.”

The Crevice burned beneath Xevorith.

“Every precious little Name insisting it deserves another morning.”

Kujo’s eyes fixed on the two men still forcing themselves against the pain.

“I will remove the road.”

His smile sharpened.

“And leave nothing waiting at the destination.”

For a moment there was only the black sun, the ruined tower, and Kujo standing beneath both.

Then he laughed.

“I will make this world undone.”

Cold coming up off the road through the soles. Grease and old leather where the gorget sat under his jaw. The wind out of the northwest carrying woodsmoke from a village that had no business burning at this hour, and under the smoke the sourness of two thousand men who had slept in harness.

Then the sound of the column ahead of him changing.

Sodoku Moto sat on the Titan Droval a quarter mile short of the narrows and watched the Greymanes turn.

It went along the enemy column from the middle outward, which is where it had to start, and it was over inside the time a man needs to fill his lungs. Six hundred spears reversing on the six hundred they had marched beside. The Tenrai officers were already down. He counted eleven of them off the horses before he stopped counting, and he had been in enough of these to know what it meant that the eleven went first and went together.

Wren Greymane had built this.

That was the second thing Sodoku understood and it arrived a half-beat behind the first, and there was no pleasure anywhere in it.

Sixteen months of it. The defection at the ridge. The smile across two hundred paces of dead men that had gone into every mouth in the North and had been repeated to Sodoku three times by three separate people who each thought they were the first to tell him. Every village between here and the Stormfold saying the word traitor about a man who had been standing in the enemy's saddle waiting for a road narrow enough.

*Sonzai.*

*He put a man inside. That is his hand and nobody else's, and I have known him since we were both small enough to fit under the same table, and it did not occur to me once.*

*Nineteen years and it did not occur to me once. I have been thinking about walls. Whether they hold, whether they can be held, how many men on the wall and how long the stack lasts. Everything I have built since I came out of the water has been a thing that stands still and takes a blow.*

*A man who thinks in walls does not think of the door being opened from inside. That is the whole of the fault and it is mine and it has cost us nothing today, which is the only reason I am able to look at it.*

Breath went out of him and hung in front of his face and stayed there longer than it should have, because the Droval under him kept the air around them at a temperature that made a man's own breath into a small standing cloud.

Down at the near end of the narrows a Tenrai serjeant had got about forty men into a block facing the wrong way and was trying to turn them, and a Greymane spear went into him under the arm from behind while he was still shouting, and the forty came apart in the time it takes to notice they have.

He put that away and looked at the Seat behind him.

Kharven-Seat stood on its shoulder of rock with the light coming up behind it and every stack in the outer town smoking, because the outer town had been told to burn its winter wood this morning and had done it without argument, which was a thing Sodoku had asked for and had expected to have to ask for twice. Four hundred households putting a season on the fire in one day. If the road held, they would be through the Thin Weeks on borrowed heat and short rations and the death-house would take whatever the cold took, and that was the arrangement, and the town had made it in about an hour.

Hild was inside. Yoko with her, and Osric with both, and the order had been given in the yard before light and Osric had repeated it back word for word without being asked, which was Osric. Under the main keep, in the cut chamber below the old cistern, which was stone on six sides and had one way in. Hold the walls of the inner court. Hold the gatehouse. Do not sortie for anything, including him.

*He will do it. He will do exactly it, and he will do it while she argues with him, and she will argue with him, and he will not move.*

*And if the wall comes down he will put himself in the doorway, and that is a thing I ordered and did not say out loud.*

He swung down off the Droval.

His boots went into the frozen ruts and took his weight and the cold came up through the plate at the ankle. He put his hand flat on Tōga's flank and held it there.

The hide under his palm had frost standing on it in a nap, and beneath the frost the animal's warmth ran the wrong way, inward, so that touching it was touching the outside of a very deep cold that had decided to be still for a while. The great head came round. The eye found him and held.

"Go and help them," Sodoku said.

He patted the flank twice.

Tōga looked at him a moment longer.

There had been no promise made when the two of them came to terms on the ice and there was none now. The creature had ten thousand years of winter in it and had chosen, and choosing is a thing that can be done twice, and Sodoku had never once let himself forget which of them was the smaller party to the arrangement.

The Titan Droval went up the road.

It did not run. Nothing that size runs and nothing that size needs to. It went at a long rolling pace with its head low and the frost coming off it in a wake, and the ruts froze solid ahead of it in a line, and the water in them cracked as it went over, and the men of the broken column who could see it coming stopped doing whatever they had been doing.

The temperature at the narrows dropped eleven degrees in under a minute. Every Tenrai man in that press felt his fingers report late. Every Greymane man in that press felt the same thing and had grown up in it and knew what to do about it, which was to keep swinging and let the cold take the other side's hands first.

That was the whole of what Sodoku had sent, and it would be enough for the foot, and it would be nothing at all for the thing standing on the road with the purple around it.

He was on foot now. He had put his mount into the fight and his mount was the reason anyone within a mile of him took a step back, and he had done it without weighing it, the way a man puts his hand out to catch something falling off a shelf.

He turned his sight.

The road went out.

Reigan does not add anything to the world. It takes the world away and leaves what is under it, and what is under it is traffic, and the traffic has weight and direction and health, and a man who opens that eye stops having the ordinary one for as long as he keeps it open.

Sodoku Moto stood in the ruts with his hand still half-raised from the flank of an animal that was no longer there and could see nothing whatsoever of the field in front of him.

He found the two of them at once. That was the trouble.

He had gone looking for the road. For the six on it and the shape of the press and whether the Droval had opened anything worth putting men into, and the eye had taken him past all of that in the first instant and put him four hundred miles away without being asked, because Reigan goes to weight and weight is not always where a man wants it.

His brother came up first, north and west, at a distance he could not have put in miles, and the signature was wrong in the way that a note is wrong when the string has gone slack. It was there. It was still moving and still fighting and still absolutely itself, which was the part that told him how bad it was, because a man does not put out that much of himself unless the alternative has already been considered and rejected.

Zuberi was beside him and the reading was the same.

Two of them. Together, which meant it had come to them at the same time and in the same place, and both of them running at an output that no living body sustains, and the load coming off them was going into something that Sodoku could feel the shape of and could not measure at all.

He put the eye further out and found the edge of the thing they were standing in front of.

It had no edge.

*That is the Work.*

*The one who took the Crevice. He is out there, and he has both of them at once, and neither of them is winning and neither of them will stop.*

*And I am here.*

The read cost him eleven seconds.

He knew the number because he counted while he was blind, which is a habit and had been a habit since he was fifteen, and at nine he heard the horse.

He let go of the sight.

The world came back in a rush that put his stomach into his throat and greyed the top and sides of everything for a moment, and there was a rider forty feet away and coming, and the sword was already out of Sodoku's scabbard and low along his right leg with the edge turned out.

The horse went past.

Robin Ice hauled it round in a spray of frozen mud and got it stopped and half fell out of the saddle, and stood there holding the cheekpiece with both hands and breathing in a way that was not going to be sustainable for much longer.

Seventeen. A face still deciding what it would be, thin at the cheek and heavy at the brow, black hair cut by somebody in a hurry, ice in it. A courier's coat two sizes too big and cinched at the waist with a belt that had gone round nearly twice. Blood on his left ear that was not his. He had ridden through the narrows to get here, which nobody had told him to do and nobody would have permitted.

"My lord."

Sodoku waited.

He had learned that off Muken, who had learned it off their father, and it was the only piece of the whole inheritance he had kept without arguing. A man arriving with news will give it in his own order if you leave the silence alone, and the order he gives it in is half the message.

*Say it in the order. He told you once, in the hall, in front of everyone, say it in the order and do not say anything else, and I have got four things and I have been rehearsing the order for six miles and I have already lost one of them.*

"The southern reach is still standing. Bram had the breach at the ninth hour and was holding it and I saw him on foot. The Seat is closed and the inner court is manned. Lord Osric has the princess and the lady below the cistern and has ordered the gatehouse shut behind him." The boy got a breath in. "And Lord Lambert went out the postern before the gates opened. Six riders. No banner. He said five days."

"Wren Greymane."

"On the road. Standing." Robin Ice's jaw moved. "In front of the one with the halberd."

Sodoku looked past him at the narrows.

Lambert going out the postern before the gates opened meant Lambert had left with the road still open and would have been on the Stormfold before the aurora came up, which was either the best timing of the man's life or the worst, and there was no way to know which for five days.

Four hundred paces of road, choked end to end with two columns that had turned on each other and had nowhere to go, a ditch on the left full of men who had gone in first, a drop on the right. Somewhere in the middle of it the Droval was making a hole. And past the hole, at the far end, a violet corona standing on the road with nothing left upright around it inside sixty feet.

"Go back," Sodoku said. "Tell Osric the road is closed and I am not coming back through it. Tell him he has the gate until I do."

"My lord, the ditch is full and the narrows is."

"Then go round the ditch."

The boy stood there for a second, because he was seventeen and because the thing he wanted to say was *come with me*, and he did not say it, and that was the beginning of him being useful.

He got back onto the horse and went, and he took the long way round the ditch without being told twice, and Sodoku watched him do it and put the boy's name into the part of his head where he kept the ones who came back.

Sodoku Moto set the sword in his right hand and started up the road on foot.

The wind came round out of the north and put smoke across the whole width of the narrows and took it away again, and somewhere in the press a horn went and was cut off in the middle of the note, and the cold coming back down the road from where the Droval had gone reached him and went through the mail and found the sweat under it.

Wet wood burns eventually.

His grandmother had said it about men who could not be relied on and about the fires they made. She had said it in a doorway in a house that no longer stood, with her hands in a bowl, and she had been dead forty years, and it came up out of him now with no invitation and in her voice.

*Wet wood burns eventually. A liar never dries.*

Wren Greymane had been the wettest wood in the North for sixteen months and every hearth in the country had said so, and he was standing on that road in front of the halberd with a shield on his arm and would be dead inside the quarter hour.

*The sky does not ask whether you are ready.*

He put his shoulder into the wind and walked into the narrows.

Kujo felt the gaze before Sodoku finished understanding what he was looking at. Reigan did not touch him like ordinary perception; it arrived as a shift in metaphysical weight, a distant eye stripping away the visible battlefield and reaching for the traffic beneath it. For eleven seconds, while Sonzai and Dawn Guard endured the simulated unmaking Kujo had wrapped around them, something four hundred miles away looked directly into the shape of his presence and failed to find an edge.

Kujo stopped speaking.

The black mantle of Xevorith settled against his body, and his head turned slightly toward a direction that meant nothing geographically from where he stood. Distance mattered very little to the Fifth Layer, but recognition mattered immensely, and Sodoku had just established that recognition himself. Kujo followed the Reigan connection backward through the Plane of Fate and found cold road, packed men, Titan frost, civil war, and one Moto walking toward violence because there were still responsibilities in front of him that had not permitted him to run toward his brother.

“Sodoku Moto.”

Kujo said the name quietly.

“So you finally looked.”

A smile spread across his face.

He did not open a portal or drag Sodoku out of the narrows. That would have been easy, and there was something much more interesting in watching the third man remain where duty had placed him while knowing exactly what was happening elsewhere. Kujo tasted the contradiction immediately: Sonzai was being broken beneath the black, Dawn Guard was beside him, and Sodoku knew it, yet still there were gates behind him, soldiers ahead of him, Wren standing before Kalaabad, and a kingdom whose survival refused to become less important simply because his brother might die somewhere else.

“That hurts, doesn’t it?”

Kujo’s voice traveled along the recognition Reigan had created, not as sound carried across four hundred miles but as something placed at the far end of an already-opened line.

“To know exactly where your brother is and remain where you are.”

His dark eyes narrowed.

“You spent years telling yourself you would never again be the child who stood there while his family died.”

The Crevice pulsed beneath Xevorith.

“And now the world has arranged the same question more intelligently.”

Kujo laughed softly.

“Not because you are helpless this time.”

His smile sharpened.

“Because you are needed somewhere else.”

That distinction delighted him. Childhood helplessness had left the Moto brothers with the vulgar fantasy that sufficient power would someday guarantee they could reach everyone, punish everything, prevent every repetition, and stand in every doorway at once. Reality had finally matured enough to offer Sodoku the version of that lesson strength could not solve.

“You could come.”

Kujo’s black-covered hand opened at his side.

“You could abandon the road. Leave Wren. Leave the Seat. Leave the boy who just carried you news. Leave whatever remains of the Tenrai problem to the men already bleeding for it.”

His voice remained calm.

“You are strong enough to try.”

Then Kujo glanced down toward Sonzai.

“But if Kharven breaks because you came running to save your brother, what will you call that?”

The question lingered.

“Love?”

Kujo gave a small laugh.

“Or cowardice wearing love’s face?”

The Eternal Darkness over Kharven churned once, spreading through the clouds above the ruined district. Kujo could feel Sodoku’s sight was no longer actively upon him, but that did not matter; the recognition had happened, and Fate remembered recognition long after eyes closed. He kept speaking into the residue, less concerned with whether every word arrived cleanly than with planting the shape of the thought where Sodoku had opened himself.

“You think in walls.”

Kujo’s expression became almost thoughtful.

“That is your weakness.”

He looked toward the distant north through Xevorith as if Kharven’s mountains were transparent.

“Can it stand? Can I hold it? How many men? How much grain? How long before winter makes the argument for me?”

Kujo’s smile returned.

“Your brother thinks in doors.”

He tilted his head.

“That is why he hid Wren inside the enemy and never told you.”

There was no mockery in that sentence. If anything, Kujo sounded approving.

“Sonzai understands that wars are not won by enduring every blow.”

His voice lowered.

“They are won by deciding which structure never gets the opportunity to become a wall.”

The darkness around Kujo shifted.

“And you just learned that about him while he was dying.”

He laughed again, more openly this time.

“How cruel.”

Kujo allowed himself to look once more at Sonzai and Dawn Guard, both still trapped beneath the terrible simulated absence he had made them experience.
