He could have killed Sonzai and let Sodoku feel the severance through Reigan, but death was beginning to bore him. It ended questions too efficiently.

“No.”

Kujo’s voice became softer.

“I will not summon you.”

His hand lowered.

“I want you to finish what is in front of you.”

That was the more vicious option.

Kujo wanted Sodoku to walk through the narrows while knowing his brother suffered elsewhere. He wanted him to make competent decisions, save men, hold roads, calculate losses, perhaps even win, and afterward discover whether victory still felt like victory when another battlefield had existed beyond his reach the entire time.

“Go to Wren Greymane.”

Kujo’s dark eyes gleamed.

“Go see whether your brother’s door was worth sixteen months of being called traitor.”

The Fifth Layer stirred behind him.

“Go see whether Kalaabad can be removed from your road.”

His smile became thin.

“Go save Kharven.”

Then his voice twisted slightly.

“And every minute you spend doing it, remember that Sonzai is here with me.”

The black around Kujo’s shoulders rose like the outline of enormous wings.

“That is despair, Sodoku.”

He spoke the word with almost academic satisfaction.

“Not losing everything.”

A pause followed.

“Being given two things you cannot permit yourself to lose…” Kujo’s eyes darkened. “…and enough strength to understand why you cannot save both at the same time.”

The Crevice beat once.

Kujo’s smile widened.

“So walk.”

He turned his attention back toward the battlefield before him.

“Show me what the Kokan heir does when fate stops asking whether he is ready.”

Inside the corona the air had a grain to it.

Wren Greymane learned that in the first second of standing up in it, and he learned it through his teeth, because the violet did not press on him and did not burn him and did not do any of the things a man's body has language for. It made every breath arrive at the back of his throat about a quarter-second after he had asked for it. It made his own blood go up his face. It made the frozen mud under his boots stop being a surface that pushed back and start being a surface that agreed with him, provisionally, and could be persuaded otherwise.

The road tilted.

It did not tilt. He checked it twice, with the shield rim planted, and both times the road was level and both times his own inner ear reported a slope running away toward the man in front of him, and he stopped checking on the third and decided to fight on a hill.

That decision was the only reason he lived the next forty seconds.

Kaalabad came at a walk.

He did not close in a rush and did not need to. He came the length of eleven paces with Zarakal held low on the right in a hanging guard that no manual in the North had a name for, and every pace put a crack in the road behind him, and behind the cracks the road sagged half an inch and stayed sagged.

*He is doing that to himself and he does not care.*

*It is not free. Nothing is free. He has been on that road since the ninth hour and he cracks it every time he lands and the sky came down into him and none of that is free, and I have to live long enough to find out what it costs.*

The second delivery came underhand from the right hip, rising, aimed to take him from the groin to the collarbone.

Wren dropped the shield's bottom edge and turned the face out and got his weight forward and down and let the road's imaginary slope help him, which it did.

"Bulwark."

Grey went blue for about a third of the shield's face and no further.

That told him more than anything else in the fight had. Three deliveries had always meant three whole ones, boss to rim, and what came up this time was a third of a shield's worth and it came up sluggish, and the rest of the face stayed grey and dead under his arm like a board.

Zarakal hit the live third.

The note was there and it was shorter. The distribution held across the whole face regardless, and Wren felt it as the same even flat pressure on his forearm, an entire mountain coming through a hand laid open instead of a nail. His arm did not break. The oak did.

It went along the grain from the boss to the top edge in a single split that opened four fingers wide and let daylight through it, and the hide on the front went with it, and the second binding on the boss let go and the rivets came out of the rim on the right side and hung.

He went back seven feet and stayed on his feet by the width of a decision.

*Two.*

*And it was not a whole one. That was a third and it took the shield and there is not a third left in it.*

The corona around Kaalabad had thickened at the wrists.

Wren saw that on the recovery and did not know what he was seeing. The violet had gathered at both cuffs, denser than the rest of it, moving in a slow turn, and under the right cuff the plate had begun to stand off the arm by about a finger's width where it had sat flush ten minutes ago.

He put it away to look at later, and there would be no later, and some part of him knew that and filed it anyway because that was how he had been built.

He went to the belt.

Second charge. Ring, throw, and this time he did not throw it at the man.

He threw it low and long, past Kaalabad's left boot, twenty feet up the road behind him, into the sag.

"Compact."

The sphere opened in the cracked road instead of in the air.

It went into the fissures first because that was where the space was, and it filled them, and then it kept expanding and the road had nowhere to put it. Forty feet of the Ashgate road came up. Nothing threw it. It lifted, in slabs, frozen mud and gravel and the buried corduroy of split logs some Kharven road gang had laid in the reign before last, all of it standing up on end at once with a sound that went through the whole narrows and stopped two hundred men fighting.

Kaalabad's footing went out from under him.

He came down onto one knee in the wreck of it, and the pull round him caught the falling slabs and drew them inward, and they arrived at him from four directions and stacked against him and pinned his own weapon between his chest and about a ton of the road he had been standing on.

Wren was already moving.

He came off the imaginary slope with the ruined shield up and the arming sword in his right hand, and he did not go for the head and did not go for the chest, and he went for the one place on that man that had ever shown him anything, which was the scarred band around the throat above the gorget.

He got the point in.

Two inches, maybe three, into the side of the neck under the ear, through scar tissue that felt like driving into a barrel stave, and he twisted the wrist and put his shoulder behind it and drove.

The blade stopped.

There was nothing in there for it to go into. Wren had killed thirty men with that sword and he knew the whole vocabulary of what a body does around a point, the give and the grate and the awful ease of it, and there was none of that. The steel went two inches into a man's neck and met something that had no interest in being cut, and the last four inches of the blade bent instead, slowly, in his hand, and then went.

He got a look at Kaalabad's face from eight inches away.

*He is going to want it recorded that he did this.*

*Forty years and men do not step toward me and this one has stepped toward me twice and put a road on my back and a sword in my neck, and it is going to be a Greymane, and the Greymanes are going to be extinct by the spring, and nobody is ever going to be told.*
The slabs came off him.

They did not fall away. They went out, all of it, forty feet of a road going outward in every direction at once from a man getting up off one knee, and Wren took the whole leading edge of that across the chest and the ruined shield and went backward through the air for a distance he did not measure.

He landed on the frozen ditch bank on his back with the shield still strapped on and his left shoulder out of its socket.

The third delivery took eleven seconds to arrive.

He used them. He got the arm back in by rolling onto it against the bank, which he had done twice before in his life and which made him black out for a portion of a second, and he got up onto one knee, and he got the shield in front of him with a dead arm and a split face and four fingers of daylight through the middle of it.

"Bulwark," said Wren Greymane.

Nothing happened.

He looked at it. Grey, and split, and the boss hanging off the second binding, and the iron rim gone from the right side, and the crack he had put in it at the ridge sixteen months ago running up through the whole of the rest.

Zarakal came down.

The fluke went through the shield and through the arm behind it and through the meat of his left shoulder and into the frozen ditch bank underneath, and it did all of that in one motion and pinned him to Kharven.

The pain arrived late and enormous. Wren heard himself make a noise. He had time to be embarrassed by it.

He got his right hand up and took hold of the shaft, which was a thing to do, and he did it because a man's hands go where they go.

Kaalabad set his boot on the shield.

*He is looking at my face. He wants the face.*

*Then have it. I have had sixteen months of men spitting in it and it is the only thing I have left that is mine to spend.*

Wren Greymane looked up at him and did not close his eyes.

The spike came down through the collarbone and the top of the chest and the whole architecture under it, and it went into the bank, and everything below Wren's throat stopped being a place he lived.

He had about nine seconds.

He spent four of them getting his right hand off the shaft and down to the belt, and the fingers had nothing left in them and had to be dragged, and he got two of them under the ring of the third charge and could not pull it.

So he brought it up to his mouth.

*Bram.*

*The book is in the left saddlebag under the oilcloth and the horse went south when I slapped it and somebody will find the horse and nobody will open the book, and you will go to your grave believing it, and there is no arrangement of this in which I get to fix that.*

*Sixteen months and I never once got word to you. Every route ran through somebody, and every somebody was a way in, and it was the correct decision. I would make it again. I have made it every single morning since the ridge and I made it again this morning.*

*I am sorry about the smiling. I have never been able to account for it.*

He pulled the ring with his teeth.

"Comp."

It went off in his own hand.

Twenty feet of aetheric material opened at the level of Wren Greymane's chest and took his arm off at the elbow and put Kaalabad backward off the ditch bank and into the road, and the shaft came out of the bank with him, and out of the shield and the arm and the shoulder and the chest, and Wren was already gone by then and did not have to feel any of it.

The sphere bent around the man as he went, as it had the first time, and closed behind him, and travelled on.

It went up the narrows and through the press.

Sixty-odd men were still on the ground in the corona's radius where the fever had put them, Greymane and Tenrai together, and the wave came through under them and picked them up and rolled them forty feet toward the Kharven end of the road, and it broke three legs and a jaw and it did not kill any of them, and every one of them who could stand afterward was standing on the far side of the man they had been lying in front of.

They ran. All of them, both colours, in the same direction, and nothing came after them, because the thing that would have come after them was face down in the road with a road on top of it.

Cass Holloway got up out of that and ran with the rest and did not know whose wave it had been.

Kaalabad came out of the mud in his own time.

He put the shaft across his knees and knelt on the broken road for a moment and did nothing at all, which was a thing nobody had seen him do.

Then he stood, and shook the right hand once, and looked at it.

The gauntlet would not come off. The plate had stood off the arm by a finger's width at the wrist and now it had stood off further and the leather under it had gone tight, and the whole hand from the knuckles to the forearm had begun to swell against the steel from the inside, purple under the nail beds, the skin between the fingers gone shiny and full.

He looked at it for a while.

Then he put the shaft in the left hand, and walked back down the narrows toward the Kharven gates, and did not look at the body on the ditch bank, and did not look at the shield either.

Four hundred paces up the road, on foot, alone, coming the other way, a man in Moto harness with a sword low along his right leg had stopped walking and was watching him come.

The snow at Sum-gol was eleven days old and had a crust on it that took a man's weight for one step in three.

Kwon Mu-jin came up the orchard road with his right arm hanging.

It had been hanging for six weeks. There was no wound in it and there had never been a wound in it, and the fingers were warm, and the nails grew, and the physician at the school had spent four days on it and had written *no finding* in a hand that got smaller toward the end of the report.

He had stopped trying to lift it in the second week. Geuk-hon had told him to stop, in a room with a table in it and two cups on the table, and had said it in the voice of a man describing something he had already watched happen once.

*You are still holding that arm the same way you held the roster.*

*You want it back the way it was, annotated and gradeable, and you want the annotation to say that it is yours. It is not going to say that again. Put it down. What you have left is enough, and it has always been enough, and the reason you never found that out is that nobody in four hundred years has taken enough away from you in one afternoon to make you count what was left.*

He had counted.

The orchard came up on his left, thirty-one trees, eleven of them down at the root since the summer and none of them cleared, because nobody at the school had been willing to be the one who cleared them. The wall that had stood since before the family kept a ledger was still open at the place where a live pine had gone through it end over end.

Raga came out from under the trees.

Three-limbed, and the left forequarter rebuilt out of somebody's cartwheel and a length of mill shaft and two hundredweight of the wall, and the socket where the green eye had been packed with orchard clay and river stone and burning, faintly, from the inside. The compact had held. The animal had never once asked him why the arrangement had changed.

It put its head down beside him and walked.

There was smoke on the western sky where no town was, and there had been since the ninth hour, and under Mu-jin's boots the ground had been carrying a note through the crust since before dawn, low and steady and wrong, and he had walked eleven miles toward it instead of away.

He stopped at the top of the orchard road.

Below him the Ashgate valley lay open all the way to the Kharven narrows, and there was a violet standing in it, and a road that had come apart, and something on a ditch bank with a shield pinned to it.

Mu-jin looked at that for a long moment.

Then he lifted his left hand, which was the only one he had now, and drew the circle in the air in front of him, and it took under three seconds, and it opened.

The fighting stopped in pieces.

Sodoku Moto stood four hundred paces up the road and watched it go out along the whole length of the narrows the way a fire goes out of wet straw, in patches, unevenly, with men in the middle of it still swinging at nothing for a second or two after the men beside them had lowered their arms.

It started at the ditch bank.

A Greymane serjeant with half a helmet got up onto the bank and looked down at what was pinned there and made a noise that had a word inside it.

"Wren."

It went up the road. Nobody organised it and nobody made a chant of it. It went from mouth to mouth in both colours, because half of the men on that road had spent sixteen months hating that name and the other half had spent sixteen months carrying it, and every one of them turned to look at the same place at the same time.

*They are going to break.*

*Both sides at once, in a defile, with a ditch on one flank and a drop on the other and four hundred paces of dead men between them and any ground worth standing on. Two thousand men are about to find out that there is nowhere to run to, and the ones at the back are going to push, and it will be the ditch that kills most of them.*

He got his hand up and put two fingers in his mouth and whistled, twice, long, and the sound went down the road ahead of him.

Then the second figure came onto the field.

It arrived at the far end of the narrows, past Kaalabad, past the wreck of forty feet of lifted road, and it did not walk up out of anywhere and it did not step through any seam. It resolved. A man's shape stood there at about half the density of a man, gleaming, edged in a light that had no source and cast nothing, and the ground under it was undisturbed and the snow on the ground under it was undisturbed, and for perhaps three seconds Sodoku could see the broken cart on the far side of the road straight through the middle of its chest.

He had spent thirty years being told what that was and had seen it done properly twice.

*The figure comes first and the body follows. That is a working walking out ahead of its own owner, and it means the man himself is somewhere behind it and closing, and it means he has enough to spare that he can afford to arrive twice.*

Nobody on the road knew the shape. Sodoku watched two thousand men fail to recognise it in the same instant, Tenrai and Greymane together, and something went out of the press that belonged to neither army.

Down at the ditch bank, the corona thrummed.

It went through the ground and came up the road four hundred paces and into the soles of Sodoku's boots and up the long bones of his legs, and every horse still standing in the narrows put its head down.

The sword went back into the scabbard.

*Leave it. Whatever that is, and whatever the other one is, I have four hundred paces of open road and no mount and one arm's worth of anything that would matter, and there are eleven hundred men on this road who can still be got off it.*

He turned his back on both of them and started shouting names.

The road smelled of opened men and frozen mud and the particular hot-slate smell that comes off a working that has spent everything it had.

Kwon Mu-jin came the rest of the way in his own body four seconds later and the shape that had been standing there went into him without any ceremony at all.

His right arm hung at his side. Snow on the shoulder of the coat. Raga had stopped at the top of the orchard road because he had told it to and because the animal had understood which part of this was not for it.

"So you have returned."

The purple around Kaalabad had thickened at the wrists and gone dark at the right one, and the gauntlet on that hand no longer resembled a gauntlet, and he was holding Zarakal in the left with the right laid along the shaft for balance and taking no weight.

"Kwon Mu-jin." The head came up. "YOU HAVE RETURNED TO DIE."

Mu-jin did not answer him.

He had come eleven miles toward a sound in the ground and had spent the last of those eleven miles building four sentences, and every one of them had gone out of his head the moment he saw the ditch bank, and what he had instead was a man he had never met.

Big. Forty-odd. A shield split along the grain from boss to top edge, still strapped to the arm, and the arm gone below the elbow and the stump cauterised by whatever had taken it. The chest opened from the collarbone down through the middle by something with a spike on it. A face turned up at the sky with the eyes open.

He knew about the grey wolf on the surcoat. Everybody between here and Sum-gol knew about the grey wolf and what had been said about the man wearing it.

The soul was still coming away.

That was the thing that stopped Mu-jin where he stood. It had begun the parting and had not finished it, and the parting had a shape, and the shape was slow and orderly and going up in a way that souls on a battlefield almost never do, and there was no fear anywhere in it and no confusion in it and a great deal of something that Mu-jin had no name for and had spent forty years being unable to name.

His eye took it in.

The left one. It had been an ordinary brown eye six weeks ago in a room with a table and two cups on it. The white of it had gone violet, all of it, corner to corner, and the pupil at the centre of that had gone a deep dense white with no reflection in it whatsoever, and the physician at the school had spent four days on the arm and had not been shown the eye.

He walked over to the ditch bank.

The men on both sides of the road watched him do it and none of them moved.

He got down on one knee beside the body in his own snow-wet coat and put his left hand out and drew the lids down with two fingers, and held them a moment until they stayed.

"Rest in peace," said Kwon Mu-jin.

"PEACE."

The corona came up four feet.

"There will be no peace where he is going. There is hell and there are labours and there is nothing else on the far side of that road, and that is the destiny of every traitor who ever drew breath, and he will be a long time finding it out."

Mu-jin stood up.

Nothing about it was fast. He came up off the knee and got his boots square and stood there on the ditch bank with his back to the corpse, and the calm man who had run a school for eleven years and had been described in four separate Accord assessments as *temperamentally unsuited to field deployment* was somewhere else entirely.

The valley began to shake.

It came up out of the ground under the whole of the narrows at once. The lifted slabs of the road ground against each other and settled. Frost came off the ditch bank in sheets. Every loose stone in four hundred paces began to travel in small hops, and the men who had been standing went to their knees, and the men who had been on their knees went flat, and somewhere behind Mu-jin a supply cart came apart at every joint at the same moment and dropped its load into the mud.

It was leaking.

He knew that with the flat certainty of a man who has lived inside an arrangement his whole life and can feel when a seam has opened. Small, dense pieces of the thing he carried were coming out of him in a way that had nothing deliberate about it, and they were going into the ground and into the air and into the cold, and they were very small, and there were a great many of them.

The shaking went out past the narrows.

At the Kharven gates the bar in its iron cradle jumped a quarter inch and came back down. Eight miles south at the reach, in a breach twenty-two feet wide, three hundred men and two thousand two hundred felt the wall move against their shoulders and did not know why. Further out, in a salt basin, chain-bridges hanging at their stops for eleven hours swung four degrees and came back.

*Stop.*

*You will crack the world open across four hundred miles for one man you never met, and he would not have wanted it, and you do not even know his name.*

Mu-jin closed his left hand.

The shaking came down over about six seconds, as a bell comes down, and the ground went quiet and stayed quiet, and every man on the road inside a hundred feet of him stayed exactly where he had fallen.

Kaalabad started laughing.

"HAHAA." It went off the drop on the right and came back. "There you are. THERE you are. I put a spike through your bear's eye and took your arm off at the shoulder without touching it and you stood in an orchard and cried, and I have wondered all summer what it would take, and it was a stranger. A stranger on a ditch bank."

He set the shaft.

"GEUK-HON."

Mu-jin looked at him.

"That is not my name."

He said it in the voice he used for a boy who had put the wrong glyph in a Draft-circle for the third time in a term, and every man on the road who could hear it heard something in it that made them wish they had not.

"You should get the name of your killer properly. It is the one thing a man in your position can still get right, and it will be the only lesson I give you today." He stepped down off the ditch bank onto the road. "I am Kwon Mu-jin."

The arms came out of him.

They came out of the right side, out of the dead one, along the line the shoulder had stopped being able to move six weeks ago, and there were nine of them and then there were more than nine. Each was longer than a man and made of the same half-density light the figure had been made of, and every inch of every one of them was covered in glyphs.

Nothing was laid on the surface. The arms were made out of the glyphs, wrist to elbow to knuckle, Parunic in the hand the Accord used and letters in three other hands under it, and the letters moved and rearranged and settled, and any Measurewright alive would have recognised what they were doing, because that is what a grading mark does. Every called thing that answers him arrives annotated. It arrives readable and correctable, which is how he has been read and corrected since he was fourteen.

They closed into fists.

Kaalabad brought the pull in.

Mu-jin felt it arrive and felt what it was doing, and it was a good working and it was elegantly made and it had a centre and everything near the centre stopped choosing its own direction, and the arms took hold of it.

They did not break it. They bound it, and then they turned it, and the sphere the man had been holding in perfect balance for eleven hours went into a spiral with nobody driving it. The weight in the road had stopped being his. It had belonged to Mu-jin from the moment he stood up off that knee, and the pull had no way of knowing and went on pulling, and what it pulled on was a mass it could not lift, and the working came apart along its own axis with a sound that made two thousand men put their hands over their ears.

The fists landed together.

All of them, in the same instant, from nine directions, on a man at the Zenith who had killed everything that had ever come at him in forty years. Plate went in at the chest and the hip and both shoulders. Kaalabad left the ground and went a hundred and ten feet back up the road, and he went through the wreck of the slabs and through a cart and out the far side, and he did not tumble, because he was still holding on to the shaft.

He came down on his boots and skidded eleven yards and stayed up.

*It bound me.*

*Nothing binds me. The office does not bind me and the seal did not bind me and the wall in the Void did not bind me, and a schoolmaster with one arm has just turned my own centre inside out and hit me nine times before I finished the thought.*

He brought Zarakal round in the left hand and cut.

The Ruinic edge went through four of the arms and they came apart into their letters, and the letters hung in the air for a moment in the shape the arm had been, and then went out.

Mu-jin walked forward.

He did nothing else. He did not replace them and did not answer the cut and did not raise the remaining arms, and he came down the middle of the Ashgate road at the pace of a man crossing his own yard, and behind him the ditch bank and the shield and the body got further away, and in front of him the corona came up to meet him.

Kaalabad threw the arrows.

The purple came off him in bolts, forty of them, then eighty, and each one duplicated as it flew and each duplicate came with the weight of the original poured into it, so that what left his hand as forty arrived as several hundred and every one of them carried the whole of the first.

Half of them never got there.

They reached the air around Mu-jin and bent, wide, all in the same direction, and went out into the ditch and the drop and the frozen fields and opened forty holes in the Ashgate road that would still be there in the spring.

The rest went into his eye.

They did not strike it. They travelled toward the left side of his face and their line shortened and their line went on shortening past the point where anything should have been left to travel, and they entered the violet and the white at the centre of it and were gone, all of them, in the order they arrived, with no light and no sound and no report of any kind.

Kwon Mu-jin did not slow down.

He put his left hand out into the air in front of him and drew the circle.

Kujo watched the Ashgate road through Xevorith without moving. The Fifth Layer held the entire narrows before him as though distance had become a minor clerical problem, and through that impossible closeness he saw Wren Greymane’s ruined shield, the lifted road, the ditch bank, and Kaalabad still standing after everything that should have forced something lesser to remain down. More importantly, he saw the swollen hand, the damaged plate, the thickened violet at the wrist, and the way Zarakal had shifted into the left hand without Kaalabad ever once pretending the cost was not there.

Kujo smiled.

“Kaalabad.”

His voice did not need to travel to the road for the approval to be real.

“You did well.”

There was no mockery in it. Wren had made him pay for every step, had buried him beneath the road, driven steel into his throat, blown himself apart to create one final opening, and Kaalabad had still risen from the mud and continued forward. Kujo did not measure the achievement by how untouched his general remained; he measured it by whether Kaalabad could continue functioning after the world had finally forced him to acknowledge consequence.

“That one deserved to hurt you.”

Kujo’s smile sharpened.

“And you killed him anyway.”

His attention lingered on Wren for only another moment. The man had given everything to a deception sixteen months in the making and had died still carrying the knowledge that Bram might never learn what had actually been done. Kujo understood immediately why Sonzai had chosen him, and why the choice had worked.

“A useful man.”

The black mantle shifted slightly around Kujo’s shoulders.

“A pity his usefulness required everybody to hate him.”

Then Mu-jin arrived.

Kujo’s expression changed.

The half-density figure caught his attention first, but not for long. The right arm hanging uselessly, the altered left eye, the widening perception around him, and the way his presence leaked into the road itself mattered far more than the spectacle of arrival. Kujo had seen injured men compensate before; what stood at Ashgate did not feel like compensation.

It felt like redistribution.

“Oh.”

The word left him quietly.

“There you are.”

Kujo watched Mu-jin kneel beside Wren and close the dead man’s eyes. He watched the valley shake afterward, not from a formal working but from something inside Mu-jin failing to remain contained by the limits it had previously obeyed. The tremor traveled far beyond the narrows before Mu-jin deliberately brought it back under control, and Kujo’s pale eyes narrowed with increasing interest.

“That is not grief.”

The Crevice pulsed beneath Xevorith.

“That is architecture failing under load.”

Then the arms came out. Kujo leaned forward slightly.

He watched them form from living annotation rather than ornament, every piece readable, every surface already carrying the logic necessary to explain and correct what had been called into existence. When Kaalabad brought the pull inward and Mu-jin did not overpower it but instead seized the structure, rebound its center, and forced it to collapse along its own governing axis, Kujo’s smile vanished completely.

Not from concern. From concentration.

“That is very good.”

The fists landed.

Kaalabad left the ground.

Kujo watched him travel through the broken road and shattered cart, watched him land upright despite the simultaneous impacts, and a slow smile returned to his face. Being thrown did not diminish the general in Kujo’s eyes; what mattered was that Kaalabad remained capable of answering after a working he believed fundamentally unbindable had been bound.

“Excellent.”

Kujo’s voice became almost pleased.

“Now we are learning something.”

When Zarakal severed four of the manifested arms and reduced them back into their letters, Kujo noticed that too. Kaalabad had not frozen after encountering an unfamiliar principle; he had tested its material expression, found what still admitted separation, and cut it. The response was simple, violent, and entirely appropriate.

“That is why I keep you.”

Kujo’s dark eyes remained fixed on the road.

“You do not need to understand everything before you try to kill it.”

Then came the arrows.

Kujo watched hundreds of violet bolts bend away from Mu-jin before impact, while the remainder traveled directly toward the altered eye and vanished into shortening trajectories. There was no collision, no conventional absorption, no visible destruction of the projectiles; their distance simply diminished until arrival ceased having anything left to complete.

Kujo became very still.

“Hm.”

The Fifth Layer tightened around him.

“That eye is new.”

His gaze sharpened further as Mu-jin continued walking.

“He is not stopping the attack.”

Kujo’s voice lowered.

“He is making the attack run out of road.”

That interested him considerably more.

For a moment, Kujo raised one hand.

Xevorith reacted immediately. The dark around his fingers deepened, and the distance between himself and Ashgate became thin enough that intervention would have required almost nothing. One motion would have been enough to place blackness between Mu-jin and Kaalabad before the circle was completed.

Kujo did not do it. His hand remained suspended. Then slowly lowered.

“No.”

The word was for himself.

“Not yet.”

He had given Kaalabad discretion because he wanted to know what happened when one of his generals was forced into conditions Kujo had not personally arranged. Intervening the moment those conditions became genuinely dangerous would reduce Kaalabad to nothing more than an extension of Kujo’s own caution, and Kujo had no interest in surrounding himself with weapons that only functioned while protected

“If he kills you…” Kujo’s gaze settled on Kaalabad. “…then I learn something valuable.”

His eyes shifted to Mu-jin.

“And if you kill him…” A faint smile returned. “…I learn something else.”

Kujo let the battlefield continue without him.

That was harder than violence.

The Crevice inside him wanted completion. Xevorith offered him too many ways to collapse distance, erase sequence, or turn the developing exchange into something decided before either man understood the decision had been made. Yet Kujo kept both hands lowered and watched, because the most important information on Ashgate road would disappear the instant he interfered.

Kaalabad’s damaged right hand. Mu-jin’s altered eye. The living arms. The binding of the pull. The shortened trajectories.

The circle now being drawn with the only physical hand Mu-jin still trusted.

Kujo catalogued all of it. Then he smiled again.

“Kaalabad.”

His tone carried genuine approval.

“You have already justified every wound on your body.”

The black mantle settled against him.

“Now justify surviving them.”

Kujo’s attention remained fixed upon the circle taking shape in Mu-jin’s hand.

He did not move. He did not warn. He did not rescue. He waited.

“Go on, Kwon Mu-jin.”

The Crevice gave one slow pulse beneath the Eternal Darkness.

“Show me what you became when losing an arm finally forced you to discover how much of yourself was never inside it.”

Kujo’s smile became thin. Then he said nothing else.

*He simply waited.*



"Lord... Kujo..." A voice spoke out, as from the corner of their sight, emerged a figure. It was a corpse, or more accurately... a zombie. His grotesque head lowered as his jaw dangled, mangled, a few inches off it's hinges. His lowered head rising up to look into his master's master's eyes. A generous cackle came from his maw.

"The Brotherhood of Apokalypse... has arrive to your disposal... Lord God Eater... sends his regards purposefully." The elder maw of the undead shawman shifting almost impatiently, his decayed fingers brushing against his tattered robe, the old markings and ointments still stained over the threads of this old shawman's robe. His nasty glare directed towards Sonzai as he risen to his feet, arms spread wide and open.

"Rejoice, Sonzai Moto! You'll finally be able to reunite with your brothers... IN HELL!" His eyes widened manically as his dilapidated figure hung over in a hunch, limping to his side as his laughter rang out as his figure began to swell. The old body and figure of the undead shawman shifting and turning as his figure began to adjust to the essence provided by the God Eater's generosity. His figure twisting and turning, gaining a shape uncanny to his previous form.

Bones cracked and shifted, wet crunches and churning flesh began to twist as the zombie morphed into a creature resembling a mixture between the God Eater, a Wyvern, and a Bird, the figure resembling something monstrous as the creature let out it's deathly caw. The Crow of the Apocalypse has come, to do continue his labors for his master. His? No... the voice didn't match the new figure, perhaps... it was better to call it a she. The voice was much lighter, despite her new horrific form. 

"Brothers of Apokalypse! Reign chaos on Kharven! Do not allow the Moto Scum to leave this earth untouched! Pillage their houses, Kill their best men, do not let a single man leave untouched!" The caws echoed out, albeit not as strong as the God Eater, but quite far from it.

The Kingdom of Kharven began to flood as an army of darkness swarmed around the city's walls. The roars and howls of the monsters outside of the castle walls sent a deathly call down to the group. The presence of the void had never felt this close in merely eons. The darkness swarmed around the city. The void had sent it's army, now it was time for the Sons of Muken to understand what it truly felt to experience, despair.

"I am Bryndís, Master of the Skies, Lady of the Seven Winds, given by my one true savior... The Eater of Gods. It is a pleasure doing business with you, Lord Kujo." She lowered her head in a respectful manner, despite the obvious lack of her own humanity.

Kujo did not answer when his name was called.

He stood beneath the blackened sun with Xevorith hanging from his shoulders, the Fifth Layer covering everything beneath his neck in featureless darkness. His eyes moved once toward Bryndís as the corpse reshaped itself into that grotesque avian thing, then toward the armies beginning to flood Kharven’s approaches. There was no greeting, approval, or command from him; Kujo simply watched, content to see what another servant of destruction intended to add to the ruin already unfolding.

Sonzai heard the name of his brothers.

Everything else disappeared.

The armies beyond the walls, the monsters crawling through the dark, the ruined tower, the blood on the stones, even Kujo standing somewhere beyond Bryndís became secondary to that single sentence. Tomuka. Ezo. The names did not need to be spoken for Sonzai to hear them, because Kujo had already torn those wounds open once, and now this malformed thing had reached into the same grave and laughed.

Sonzai’s head lowered. The Chains of War went still. Not slack. Still.

Every link suspended around him as though the battlefield had been caught between heartbeats, and the refined Calamity Emira had given structure to stopped moving with them. The crimson-black currents running across Sonzai’s body compressed inward instead, disappearing beneath his skin one strand at a time until the air around him became disturbingly quiet.

“Say it again.”

His voice was low.

Bryndís’s army continued spilling toward Kharven while Sonzai slowly raised his head. The purple of his eyes remained, but darkness had begun collecting around the irises, thin crimson fractures branching through the whites like the Soul Crystal’s corruption was becoming visible through the body carrying it. His expression held no theatrical rage anymore; it was the rigid face of someone containing enough violence that even breathing had become deliberate.

“Tell me again where my brothers are.”

Calamity erupted.

The blast did not spread chaotically.

It climbed.

A crimson-black pillar tore upward around Sonzai and punched through the darkness above Kharven, the surrounding rubble lifting several feet before being pulverized into dust inside the pressure. The Chains of War snapped outward around him, every link burning with molten red inscriptions while old battlefield memories awakened along their surfaces in overlapping whispers of steel, screams, marching feet, and dying armies.

Sonzai screamed.

The sound tore through Kharven.

Calamity poured from his Soul Crystal faster than his body had ever permitted before, but Emira’s Harmony remained embedded in the architecture she had stabilized. Instead of allowing the corruption to consume indiscriminately, that anchorage forced every ounce of rage, grief, hatred, shame, and murderous intent into the same direction until Malakar’s corruption stopped resembling a storm.

It became a blade.

Sonzai’s body began changing inside it.

His crimson hair rose from his shoulders and lengthened beneath the violent current, darker at the roots while the ends burned almost blood-red in the Calamity surrounding him. Black markings crawled from beneath his collar and spread across his throat, chest, and arms like fractures forming beneath the skin, crimson light moving inside them each time his heart struck. His muscles tightened rather than grotesquely swelling, every part of him being reorganized for speed, violence, and the intolerable density of power now being forced through his frame.

Then the Chains changed with him.

Calamity poured through their links until portions of them ceased behaving like ordinary metal. Black-red vapor dragged from every movement, while additional lengths seemed to emerge from the darkness behind Sonzai not newly created Chains, but battlefield echoes of the Chains of War overlapping the originals, remembered versions from previous conflicts briefly granted enough substance to fight beside the present ones. Every war they had tasted seemed awake at once.

Sonzai looked toward Bryndís.

“You came into my home.”

The ground cracked beneath his boots.

“You brought an army to my walls.”

The Chains rose higher.

“You said my family’s name with that thing you call a mouth.”

His teeth clenched.

“And then you laughed about my brothers.”

A tremor moved through Sonzai’s body.

For an instant he saw Tomuka again. Not Kujo’s manufactured despair, not some false resurrection made to torment him, but memory, his brother before death had become the defining fact about him. Then Ezo followed, and the grief that had spent years festering behind Sonzai’s violence finally collided with the thing Emira had given him: the ability to feel all of it without letting any single wound take possession of the rest.

His back arched.

Calamity exploded from his shoulders like two enormous, ragged projections that looked almost like wings from a distance, though they possessed neither feathers nor stable anatomy. They were streams of compressed catastrophe, black on the outside and burning crimson within, continuously breaking apart and rebuilding as the corruption searched for a form capable of expressing Sonzai’s rage. Behind his head, the aura gathered into a broken circular crown before splintering outward into jagged arcs.

Sonzai inhaled. The entire transformation compressed. The pillar vanished.

The screaming aura vanished. Even the huge projections collapsed back toward him. What remained was worse.

Calamity sat directly against Sonzai’s body in a thin black-red sheath, so dense that the edges of his silhouette distorted everything behind him. His hair drifted despite the absence of wind, the fractures across his skin glowed softly, and the Chains of War hovered around him without shaking once.

His rage had become quiet.

“Tomuka and Ezo are not yours to speak about.”

Sonzai’s voice had changed slightly. Another resonance followed beneath it, not Malakar speaking through him, but the Calamity reproducing the violence of his intent as an audible second layer.

“They were my brothers before they were your joke.”

He took one step forward.

*“And if there is a hell…” Every Chain rotated toward Bryndís. “…then you should start praying it is far enough away that I cannot follow you there.”*

Sonzai’s eyes moved beyond her.

He saw the darkness flooding Kharven. The creatures at the walls. The Brotherhood of Apokalypse.

His home being turned into another battlefield by strangers who thought Moto suffering was simply old history that could be reopened whenever useful.

Something in him finally snapped.

Not his restraint. His hesitation.

“No.”

The word traveled through the Chains.

“No more.”

Sonzai raised one hand and hundreds of overlapping Chain echoes rose throughout the ruined district, emerging from streets, rooftops, shattered towers, and the darkness over Kharven. Calamity ran through all of them in one synchronized pulse, and for the first time the battlefield did not feel like a place where Sonzai happened to be fighting.

It felt claimed by war.

“You want chaos?”

His hand closed.

“Fine.”

Sonzai looked directly at Bryndís.

“I was raised in it.”

The Chains tightened around the sky.

“I buried family in it.”

Calamity surged.

“I became a monster in it.”

His voice rose.

“And I came home anyway.”

Sonzai slammed his fist against his chest, directly over the corrupted Soul Crystal.

“So bring every brother of your little Apokalypse.”

The crimson fractures across his body blazed.

“Bring your God Eater.”

His gaze moved briefly toward Kujo.

“Bring the fucking Void.”

Then back to Bryndís.

“I will bury all of you under Kharven before I let another thing come through these walls and tell me what I failed to save.”

Only then did Kujo’s expression change. Barely. A slight narrowing of the eyes.

He had watched Sonzai lose control before. He had watched Calamity respond to grief, watched anger turn the Moto heir predictable, and deliberately exploited that weakness until Sonzai had collapsed beneath it.

This was not the same thing.

Sonzai was furious beyond reason. Yet the Calamity was obeying him perfectly.

Kujo remained silent beneath Xevorith, watching the crimson-black figure standing amid Kharven’s ruins with far more attention than before.

Because Emira had not removed the monster. She had taught Sonzai how to hold its leash.

A laugh resonated from her frame, amused by his bravado, the vainglory in his tone. All of it was something she had already heard merely fourteen times, truly some things never did change. Bryndís' wings lifted up as the hall was filled with a torrential gust from her laughter, the walls shaking and the earth tremoring underneath it's influence.

"Good... that fury... that anger... it is just as ripe as Lord God Eater anticipated... such confidence... such anger... yes... YES!" She looked at him maniacally as she cackled, her beak shifted open, opening in six angles as it's long tendril-like tongue stuck out as it laughed at him.

"Focus it on me... feed me the righteous edge of your blade... stab the beast over and over until she falls over dead!" She said, the space folded inward as the calamity distilled and dissoluted, transmuted and given shape. The maw determined the wolven calamity to be familiar, the scent of Malakar was firm, but it did not stop her euphoria. Each edge of her beak began to consume the calamity inside of her. The chaos and the entropy hit her instantly, the disasterous energy of calamity flooded her body, but it did not stop there.

The calamity began stab into her, hurt her, but her nerves had long lost their senses. The calamity began to collect into the center of the mouth, Sonzai saw it clearly before it could fully release. The mixture of Blackish-Red energy swirling in it's maw, the black and crimson stirring and discharging as the terrain around them shifted and cried. The Avian's head aimed towards it, mimicking the movements a Wyvern would hold. 

The release came out in a burst, the mix of the calamity, the essence, and the fire released outward in a sea of flames. The explosion happening outward as material around began to melt, dissolute and corrupt. The space around began to invert and bend, the silence following after broken by the cataclysm of thermal collapse. The blunt edge of the kinetic force striking against him with-no mercy, the front gate collapsing inward as the blast's force broke through the barrier. The army flooded the kingdom, Sonzai watched as the turmoil and chaos continued. The scene of death, the smell of decaying flesh and the rotten sight of carnage filled the scene in stains of red. 

Bryndís could only giggle  with glee. "See now Lord Kujo? Allow me to occupy the pest, it is an obligation that I must hold as per my master's requests. And besides... I am quite the fan of your... mannerism~" She said with sadistic glee, her frame shifting left in right in glee.

Sonzai watched his own Calamity disappear into the six-part maw and did not flinch. The black-red fire swelling inside Bryndís told him everything he needed to know: she could ingest the corruption, survive its violence, and turn what he gave her into ammunition. A younger Sonzai would have answered that insult by pouring more into her until one of them broke.

This Sonzai simply stopped feeding her.

The crimson-black sheath around his body contracted until almost none of it extended beyond his skin, every loose strand of Calamity abruptly withdrawn into the disciplined circulation Emira had given him. The Chains of War remained suspended around him, but the corruption coating their links vanished as well, leaving only ancient metal, battlefield inscriptions, and the weight of every conflict they had survived. Bryndís had opened her mouth expecting a river and found the source cut cleanly at the throat.

“You think because you can swallow my anger that it belongs to you.”

Sonzai’s purple eyes narrowed.

“You’ve mistaken access for ownership.”

The blast came anyway.

Black-red fire swallowed the approach to Kharven, the stolen Calamity mixed with Essence and wyvern heat until the front of the world became a single advancing catastrophe. Sonzai did not attempt to overpower it head-on; six Chains buried themselves behind him in surviving foundations, two more speared into the road, and another wrapped around his waist as the entire network went taut.

He let the blast strike the battlefield.

Then the Chains ripped him sideways.

Sonzai disappeared from the centerline an instant before the thermal collapse reached him, slinging himself through the exploding gate while the wave continued past and broke stone rather than body. He rotated through the air, planted one foot against a falling section of masonry, and used a returning Chain to redirect himself upward through smoke dense enough to make the sky disappear.

The gate came apart beneath him. The army entered.

Sonzai saw both facts at once. The war commander overruled the brother.

“Chains of War.”

Every link answered.

“Battlefield Seal, The Closing Front.”

The Chains did not form a conventional barrier. They shot into the collapsing gateway, walls, streets, towers, and buried foundations, creating overlapping lines of tension that turned the breach into a controlled killing corridor instead of an open mouth. Falling stone was caught, redirected, and dropped into lanes Sonzai chose, narrowing the invading front while leaving paths open for Kharven’s own defenders to withdraw and reorganize.

Then Sonzai looked up. Bryndís was still the problem. But now she was his problem.

The Calamity she had consumed remained in her attack and body as something changed, processed, no longer under Sonzai’s command. The Chains did not need ownership of it; they only needed to remember the signature of the battlefield from which it came, and the ancient weapons had spent their existence learning exactly that distinction.

The inscriptions along every link changed. Not crimson. Iron-white. The color of remembered war.

“You wanted me focused on you.”

Sonzai vanished.

“Congratulations.”

The first Chain did not carry Calamity.

It carried momentum.

It tore through the smoke and hammered into the joint of Bryndís’s left wing with enough physical force to make the air burst around the impact. A second Chain crossed immediately from beneath and wrapped the opposite wing nearer the base, while a third struck the avian body across the ribs before either previous line had finished tightening.

No corruption. Nothing offered to the maw. Only metal. Only violence.

Sonzai flash-stepped above her.

His hand closed around another Chain as it returned from behind, and he swung the weapon downward like an enormous flail. The links smashed across the crown of Bryndís’s monstrous head with enough force to rupture the air, and Sonzai used the recoil to propel himself past her before the beak could become the center of another counterattack.

“You wanted the righteous edge of my blade?”

Sonzai landed briefly against the side of a ruined tower.

“I’m not righteous.”

He kicked away from the wall.

“And I’m done putting food in your mouth.”

The Chains became omnidirectional. One attacked from beneath the wing. Another returned from behind the shoulder. Three crossed through the air above her, curved past deliberately, then snapped backward under Unbroken Link Return, striking from angles created only after the first miss. Sonzai kept moving among them, flash-stepping from Chain to Chain until there was no obvious center from which the assault originated.

Bryndís had invited fury. Sonzai gave her geometry.

A Chain hooked around one leg and pulled downward while another struck high, creating opposing vectors intended to wrench the enormous avian frame out of stable flight. Two more wrapped around the outer portions of her wings, not trying to tear them free immediately but forcing every attempt at repositioning to fight several tons of accumulated battlefield tension.

Sonzai appeared above her again.

The black-red markings across his body glowed faintly, but still he refused to release Calamity outward. His anger had not diminished; it had become so concentrated that there was no longer anything wasted enough for Bryndís to collect.

“You keep laughing like anger makes me stupid.”

His expression had gone cold.

“That was true once.”

A thin crimson line appeared around one fist.

“Unfortunately for you…” Emira’s anchorage pulsed through him. “…my sister got here first.”

Sonzai drove downward.

He punched Bryndís directly across the upper body while six Chains pulled against her in the opposite direction. The combined collision was not an Essence blast or Calamity discharge; it was brutal mechanical violence, his body accelerated by the Chains while the Chains simultaneously denied the target the ability to travel naturally with the impact.

Then Sonzai released the real counter. Not more power. A command.

“War Echoes.”

The Chains remembered.

Phantom impressions of previous battlefield movements appeared along their trajectories, not full duplicates with independent power but remembered attack patterns replayed over the current war. Where one Chain had struck, the tactical memory of another strike arrived from an altered angle; where Bryndís had been pulled downward, another remembered vector pulled sideways, layering old wars over the present one until the sky around her became crowded with inherited violence.

Sonzai landed atop a broken section of the wall. His chest rose once. Calm. Controlled. Furious.

He looked toward the monsters flooding through the narrowed breach, then back toward Bryndís without losing track of either. That was the difference Kujo had missed when he tortured him and Bryndís had missed when she laughed at his anger: Sonzai did not need rage to occupy his entire mind anymore.

“You called it vainglory.”

One Chain returned into his hand.

“Maybe.”

He wrapped it once around his forearm.

“But you came into a starving kingdom with an army behind you and started screaming about how much you wanted me angry.”

His mouth twisted.

“That isn’t strategy.”

The Chains around Bryndís tightened.

“That’s appetite.”

Sonzai planted one boot against the stone and pulled.

Every established Chain-line contracted at once.

The goal was not simply to drag her down. Sonzai changed the direction of each line independently, forcing the bindings to disagree with one another so that every wingbeat, turn, or shift had to solve several competing vectors simultaneously.

“And I have spent my entire life around creatures with appetites.”

His eyes hardened.

“Malakar had one.”

Calamity flickered beneath his skin.

“Kujo has one.”

The Chains groaned under the tension.

“Your God Eater apparently built a religion around his.”

Sonzai raised his free hand toward Bryndís.

“So you’ll forgive me if yours does not impress me.”

His fingers closed. The Chains snapped inward.

Sonzai used the convergence as his own launch point, accelerating through the web directly toward Bryndís while keeping the Calamity sealed inside himself. He came through the storm of metal with one fist chambered, the Chains of War turning his entire body into the final projectile of the formation.

This time there was no scream. No declaration. No uncontrolled hatred.

Sonzai struck with the quiet precision Emira had returned to him. And all the rage Bryndís had wanted to eat stayed exactly where it belonged.

Inside him.

The chains struck against the surface, rather than the appendage. The mechanisms were smartly layed out, geometry, the tool of a skilled warrior, however coming as the servants of the God Eater, this was no stranger. The chains aimed at joints, locks to prevent flight and movement, clever, but clever couldn't exactly strike as weakness. 

Her eyes percevied the movements, to conserve energy she placed wards against each join aimed before impact, with a swift twist of her body she created a distortion, the wind and distance folding inward before expulsing outward throwing his chains off trajectory. His arms jolted in shock, though the grip was still strong, the joints of his shoulders rolled back from the recoil. The sky bent inward and then released out a spiral of sorts, motioning in the same  way a drill would. 

The impact entangled his chains striking directly where the two acted like independent limbs. The creature's beak grappled the chains, the pain as almost instant, the chains couldn't exactly accept something like a sadistic creature like herself, but she did not attempt to wield control of the chains, she was manipulating the way her opponents tangled form. The creature spun around, Sonzai's weight immediately shifted as he was directed away from the wall.

"Clumsy, your art would work on those weaker than you. Directing to crush rather than to calculate. You are no different than a monster, most impressive... but I'm far from amazed!" She said releasing him as he came crashing the throne room of Kharven's Royal Palace, the stone and wood carving into his frame. Though his body was carved through many battles and wars, Sonzai could still feel the pain digging into his frame. The Left and Right Hip, the back, all were aligned with shards of stone and wood. 

"Do you not see it?! You are outclassed! Your home will be consumed, ravaged, burned, and it will be no different from the sins you have committed against my people's homes." She said, her frame loom-ing above Sonzai, looking down at him as he laid there in the rubble. Her gaze filled with manic glee and animosity, it's gaze sharpening as it's wings expanded outward, the shadow of it looming over Sonzai. 

"Do you feel it? The pain you have caused onto those poor souls... hear their cries Sonzai..." She said, as her shadow began to twist and distort, the cries of women, children, and men alike began to come from them. Hands extended from the shadows as they grabbed him by the joints. Their presences felt too familiar, but where? Then... he could remember, those figures... those faces... the Tenrai?

More correctly, their shadows, their sorrows, the darkness embodied and conjoined into it as the creature's smile widened. "Do you not see it... I am the results of your failure. Is it not beautiful? What failure can do to you? What kind of monster it can change you into?" She said looming closer to Sonzai's frame as a cackle resonated from her frame.

Sonzai felt the first distortion before Bryndís completed it. The wind folded around the Chains in a spiral, distance shortening along one side and expanding along the other, exactly the sort of response he had expected from something calling itself Master of the Seven Winds. He did not fight the displacement, did not wrench against the recoil, and most importantly did not allow a single ounce of the redirected force to reach his shoulders.

The Chains went loose. Not uncontrolled…uncommitted.

The instant Bryndís twisted the trajectories, Sonzai released the physical load carried through those links and invoked Sacrificial Load. Every ounce of recoil that should have traveled from the Chains into his wrists, elbows, shoulders, and spine was accepted by the weapons themselves, distributed across hundreds of links and stored as battlefield consequence. His arms never jolted backward; he remained perfectly still while the Chains bent obediently through the distorted air as though Bryndís had successfully taken their geometry away from him.

Sonzai’s eyes narrowed.

“Got you.”

The beak closed around the Chains.That was what he had wanted.

Bryndís began rotating, using the captured links as leverage to redirect Sonzai’s supposed weight away from the wall. The Chains followed her turn beautifully, and for a fraction of a second they even carried the full sensation of Sonzai’s mass through them shoulders, torso, momentum, all of it traveling toward the enormous avian body as though she had genuinely caught him.

Except Sonzai had never attached his present body to that load.

War Echoes.

The weight Bryndís seized belonged to the remembered trajectory Sonzai had occupied one movement earlier. The Chains of War carried tactical memory, and Sonzai had deliberately impressed his previous acceleration into them before she ever caught the links, allowing an echo of his momentum to continue where his actual body had already disconnected. Bryndís spun an archived Sonzai while the real one stood several yards behind the formation, untouched.

The phantom trajectory went flying toward the throne room.

It struck.

Stone and timber exploded inward exactly as Bryndís expected, a Sonzai-shaped impression tearing through the royal structure amid clouds of rubble and broken wood. But when the dust rolled away there was no blood, no buried Moto heir, and no body pierced with splinters, only a fading crimson-black afterimage collapsing into loose inscriptions from the Chains.

Sonzai remained where he had been. Perfectly upright. His shoulders rolled once. No pain. No wound. No damage.

“You weren’t calculating me.”

His expression had gone cold.

“You were calculating the answer I gave you.”

Bryndís still had the Chains inside her beak. That was the second mistake. Sonzai closed two fingers. Unbroken Link Return.

The Chains did not rip themselves free in the direction she expected. Every link changed its declared returning route independently, some reversing through the path they had entered while others wrapped deeper around the geometry of her jaw before returning from behind. The result turned Bryndís’s own six-angled maw into the central knot of a closing network.

The stored force from Sacrificial Load came back with them.

Everything she had redirected, the spiral, the expulsion, the angular acceleration, the force intended to send Sonzai through his own palace, remained inside the Chains as remembered consequence. Sonzai did not return it as a simple blast; he distributed it through the links already touching different portions of her beak so the recoil arrived from multiple directions at once.

Her own counter became a jaw-breaker.

The Chains snapped outward. Then inward. The surrounding air cracked like artillery.

Bryndís’s head was wrenched sharply off its preferred axis as the opposing vectors hit simultaneously, one Chain pulling the upper structure upward while two dragged opposite portions sideways and another struck beneath the hinge. Sonzai still had not moved from his position, his body protected behind the fact that every exchange had been occurring through a weapon system designed to remember war better than flesh ever could.

“You called me clumsy.”

A faint smile touched his mouth.

“While biting the weapon.”

His eyes sharpened.

“That was generous.”

Then the shadows reached for him.

Tenrai voices poured from the darkness first, the cries of children, women, fathers, and dying men layered over one another until the ruined palace seemed to remember the massacre itself. Hands emerged beneath Sonzai, grabbing toward his ankles, wrists, knees, shoulders, each one shaped from grief that immediately felt familiar enough to make his stomach tighten.

Sonzai looked down. He knew those voices. That was precisely why the trick failed.

His face did not harden into denial. He did not call them lies simply because Bryndís was using them against him, because some of the screams resembled sounds he had heard with his own ears during the night he and Sodoku destroyed the Tenrai settlement. Sonzai had already decided those deaths would go into the record without excuse.

His Calamity remained perfectly still.

“I remember them.”

The shadow-hands closed closer.

Sonzai did not move.

“I remember the eastern hall.”

His jaw tightened.

“I remember putting the fire there myself.”

The darkness crawled higher.

“I remember people who had nothing to do with my father dying because I wanted somebody to hurt as badly as I did.”

Then Sonzai looked directly at Bryndís.

“So what?”

Not dismissal. Acceptance. The shadows hesitated.

“You thought guilt meant ownership.”

Sonzai’s Chains began vibrating softly.

“You thought because I regret what I did, you could put their faces on your darkness and make me obey it.”

His purple eyes became vicious.

“No.”

Calamity compressed tighter against his skin.

“They are my dead.”

The Chains rose.

“My crime.”

The inscriptions ignited.

“My responsibility.”

Sonzai took one step forward.

“You do not get to borrow them.”

The Chains of War remembered the actual battlefield.

That was their nature.

The moment Bryndís attempted to clothe her shadows in Tenrai sorrow, the Chains compared those signatures against the war-memory carved into their links, the original sounds, Essence residues, panic, weapons, temperatures, spatial arrangements, and violent history accumulated during the massacre itself. The fabricated sorrow could imitate grief, but it could not perfectly reproduce the full tactical memory of a battlefield the Chains had genuinely survived.

Every false hand acquired a mark. Sonzai saw them all.

“Battlefield Seal, Witness of the Fallen.”

The Chains struck the shadows rather than the bodies they resembled.

One link passed through a child-shaped hand and hooked the foreign signature underneath it. Another threaded through a grieving silhouette without touching the image, wrapping around the false recognition holding it together. Across the throne room, the Chains began separating memory from manipulation with surgical precision.

Sonzai refused to destroy the grief.

He destroyed Bryndís’s claim to speak through it. The screams collapsed out of her shadows one after another. The Tenrai faces disappeared.

What remained was simply darkness belonging to Bryndís. Sonzai’s expression became murderous.

“That is better.”

The Chains tightened around the stripped shadows.

“Now when I hit you…” Calamity finally surfaced along his right arm, compressed into a narrow crimson-black line. “…I know whose face I’m looking at.”

He disappeared.

No flash-step explosion marked his departure. Sonzai used the existing Chain network to pull himself through three consecutive vectors, changing direction so rapidly that his first visible position appeared above Bryndís while his momentum still registered as originating behind her. One Chain struck her wing from below, not the joint this time, but the airflow immediately beneath it, while another took the opposite current and forced both pressure fields to disagree.

Her wind became asymmetric. Sonzai exploited it immediately.

One Chain wrapped the tailward airflow and redirected it upward while another drove the forward current downward, turning Bryndís’s own aerodynamics against her without attempting to overpower the wind itself. She could distort distance or expel trajectories, but every alteration created new motion, and the Chains treated each adjustment as another battlefield event to record.

Sonzai appeared over her spine.

“You taught me the first counter.”

His fist drew back.

“Thank you.”

He struck.

The blow carried no loose Calamity for her maw to consume. Sonzai kept the corruption underneath the skin, using Emira’s stabilization to reinforce his own physical expression rather than externalizing it as a projectile. The punch landed between the upper structures of Bryndís’s back at the exact instant four Chains pulled her body upward toward it.

The collision had nowhere to travel. Her body met Sonzai’s fist while the Chains denied recoil. The air detonated.

Sonzai immediately rotated over her, caught one Chain, and drove a heel down across the side of her head. Before she could turn the maw toward him he released that line, dropped underneath her blind angle, and used another returning Chain to slingshot himself upward into a rising knee beneath the lower beak.

He never remained in one trajectory long enough to be folded. Every movement was divided into segments. Every segment had an independent anchor. Every anchor could be sacrificed before Bryndís converted it into control.

That was the correction.

She had shown him that a continuous geometry could be hijacked. So Sonzai stopped giving her continuous geometry.

He landed atop a suspended Chain for less than a heartbeat, knees slightly bent, crimson hair moving around his face while dozens of links rotated through the air beneath him. There was no wound across his hips, no timber buried in his back, no blood from the throne room, and no spiritual damage from the Tenrai shadows.

Nothing she had attempted had touched him.

Sonzai looked down at her.

“You said I was outclassed.”

The Chains spread.

“Maybe you should have waited until you actually hit me.”

His hand closed.

The entire network changed formation.

Chains that had seemed discarded returned from beyond Bryndís’s field. Chains she had redirected became incoming attacks from new angles. Chains whose momentum she had consumed came back carrying stored portions of her own wind, while others remained deliberately empty so she could no longer tell which links possessed meaningful force until they were already inside striking distance.

Sonzai’s expression remained furious.

But the rage no longer hurried him.

“You wanted the monster.”

Calamity burned through the fractures along his skin.

“You got the commander.”

The Chains descended.

“And that is much worse.”

A chuckle resonated from her frame. "You fool..." She called him out, her beak began to shift the momentum of the drill-like motion into renewal, the second then did the motion not just become physical, but reactive too. The built up force inside of the chains suffered a lock, their motion breaking and hitting against a wall as the momentum redirected opposingly, like scraping against a wall. The material around her neck began to be chewed on, the shadows consuming the link between the head and the neck. The body jolted back the shadows filling in where the flesh and bone had been severed, reforming what exactly had gone missing. 

The severed head began to morph it's form, changing its state from a head to merely a bind. taking upon the reaction that it stole the motion, her head became something that hindered it's movements. A giant ball of steel, the severed head had become not simply an evasive measure, but also a ward against his chains. 

Bryndís shifted her frame as she looked at him with disgust, and bitter anger. "You mistaken the depths for manipulation, that is the truest lie, do you not still see it? The truth presents itself before the battle even begun, did you not see it?" She said, much more calmer, collective, calculating the edge before it had struck entirely.

"You say so what... but you do not begin to measure the consequences. Some of those men... those children you slaughtered, were not as guilty as you painted them. Zealots, yes, but still... you saw their fearlessness, their nihilism, but you did not see their kindness, their generosity. A warrior clan only needing to keep in check the power that had been bestowed upon you, and the filth." She said, her head twitching slightly as an opening upon the top began to emerge a figure, a woman of sorts. The figure looked back at Sonzai, her rage, her anguish, it stained her face much like his before.

"Do you not see it? I am what's left of your slaughter... do you see what it changed me too?" She-said leaning closer as her frame controlled the neck of the beast, her maw opening as the beak shifted in a drill-like motion. "You took my life... you stole my soul! I don't even have a family to come home to! THEY ABANDONED ME FOR MY WEAKNESS!" She shifted her wings, half of the town was hit with the gust almost instantly, the housing and streets caving upward with her rage, the anger twisting on the creature's form.

"So yes... I am your responsibility... a responsibility that you will have to live knowing." She said, looking down at him with spite, disgust, something that offense would birth when envy and hate carve themselves into a soul long before it had been developed.

"Which is why I'll make sure it is slow... I'll make sure it's painful... I'll make sure every single person you once loved is crushed before your eyes, and when I'm done... I'll burn them and this kingdom all to a crisp. Like you have left me and my village... IN RUINS!" She released out the fire from her maw, the chaos and calamity raining from the sky as the jagged and unstable aether rained down incessantly.

"DO YOU NOT FEEL IT?! MY RAGE?! WHAT I HAD TO ENDURE THROUGH THE YEARS?!" She called out to him.

Sonzai stood beneath the rain of corrupted fire and listened to Bryndís speak of the Tenrai as though their deaths were supposed to become chains around his throat. The woman emerging from the monstrous frame, the grief carved across her face, and the hatred in her voice were all things he recognized immediately. For a moment his expression became almost contemplative, as if he were genuinely considering whether anything she had said had reached some hidden place inside him. Then his mouth curled upward.

“I remember them. I remember their houses, their screaming, and the eastern hall burning. I remember exactly what my hands did that night, and I would do it again.”

The black-red fire continued descending, yet Sonzai made no attempt to evade it. His transformed aura contracted tightly against his skin while Emira’s anchorage remained buried beneath the violence, keeping every fragment of his Calamity aligned rather than calming it. Sonzai raised his face toward Bryndís with a cruelty that had not been there before, the glow beneath his skin sharpening around his eyes. There was no shame in his expression now.

“If you put me back in that village with the same dead brothers, the same blood in my mouth, the same hatred in my chest, and told me those people stood between me and what I wanted, I would burn it twice. Their kindness would not have mattered to me. Their families would not have mattered to me. They were insignificant compared to what I had lost.”

Something inside Calamity changed as soon as he said it. Until now, Sonzai had treated the corruption as something to direct, compress, sharpen, conceal, or load into the Chains of War. Bryndís had repeatedly exploited that assumption because anything emitted could be consumed, redirected, distorted, or transformed into another mechanism. Sonzai finally stopped treating Calamity like ammunition.

He began mending it.

The black fractures spread across Sonzai’s body until thin crimson seams connected one unstable concentration of Calamity to another. The corruption no longer existed as thousands of competing eruptions fighting to be expressed first; it folded inward, stitching its own broken continuity back together and turning chaos into something that could survive disruption without losing itself. Emira’s Harmony had given Sonzai the mental anchorage necessary to recognize the difference between suppressing Calamity and teaching it how to remain whole. Now he used that understanding deliberately.

The change reached the Calamity Bryndís had already consumed. It was no longer merely energy stolen from Sonzai and incorporated into another attack, because the corruption itself had begun rejecting fragmentation as a stable condition. Every black-red portion inside her fire resonated with the larger governing pattern still housed within Sonzai’s Soul Crystal. He was not reclaiming ownership over her spell; he was forcing his Calamity to remember what it had been before she separated it.

“Do you know what Malakar actually gave me? Not fire, not entropy, and not some convenient red-black energy you can swallow and spit back at me. He put something inside me that survives catastrophe by making disaster coherent. You took pieces of it and assumed the pieces had forgotten the whole.”

“You said you are my responsibility. No…you are my consequence. And consequences can be killed. If your entire existence was born from what I did, then I will simply finish what I started.”

Sonzai struck.

The instant his knuckles made contact, Calamity did not explode away from him. It mended inward, spreading from the impact through every available discontinuity inside Bryndís’s reconstructed anatomy and searching for the seams between flesh, shadow, borrowed Essence, transformation, regeneration, and whatever foreign material had been used to preserve her. The corruption behaved like violent stitching forced through a body that had survived by repeatedly becoming something else.

That was the cruelty of the new method. Sonzai was no longer attempting to destroy Bryndís faster than she could regenerate or change state. He was forcing every transformation she had already used to become part of one continuous history, making each new body responsible for the damage suffered by the one before it. Shadow pretending to replace flesh was stitched back to the flesh it replaced, and reconstructed structures were bound to the injuries that had necessitated them.

“Change again. Turn into steel, shadow, wind, corpse, bird, beast…whatever your God Eater taught you to become. I will mend every version back into the same body. Then I will make that body answer for all of it at once.”
