The Chains of War finally moved, but they did not attack Bryndís. Several links wrapped around Sonzai’s arms, torso, waist, and shoulders like a brutal harness, anchoring Catastrophic Continuity to the accumulated history carried inside the weapons. Every battle stored within them became another reference proving that Sonzai Moto had remained Sonzai Moto through corruption, exile, wounds, slaughter, Calamity, and war. The Chains became evidence of continuity rather than the primary weapon.

His aura changed again.

The black deepened until it resembled an absence pressed directly against his skin, while the crimson became thinner and sharper, moving in narrow lines through that darkness. Calamity stopped spilling into the battlefield entirely and became almost economical, every fragment assigned to maintaining the relationship between Sonzai’s body, Soul Crystal, Name, and violence. The more contained it became, the more dangerous it felt.

Sonzai looked directly at Bryndís, and whatever guilt she had expected to uncover was gone. There was still memory inside him, and there was still responsibility, but neither had produced remorse in the form she wanted. He remembered the Tenrai because the massacre belonged to his history, not because he believed those deaths held authority over his future. His expression had settled into open contempt.

“You want me to hear their cries? I heard them the first time, and I kept going anyway. They screamed, they died, and I walked away while their village burned. If you think repeating those voices will make me kneel now, then you misunderstood the kind of monster that walked out of that fire.”

Sonzai stepped forward while the Chains remained wrapped around his body. The Calamity inside him pulsed once, deep and controlled, and the ground beneath his feet cracked from the pressure without any energy visibly leaving his frame. He was no longer fighting like a commander controlling a field from the center of a weapon network. He had become the weapon the network existed to stabilize.

“They were insignificant to me then. They are insignificant to me now. What matters is that you survived long enough to stand here and tell me what my slaughter made you, because that means I get the opportunity to decide whether I left the job unfinished.”

Sonzai lowered into a new stance. His shoulders relaxed, one foot settled slightly forward, and the Chains tightened around him as Catastrophic Continuity finished stabilizing the transformation. There was no wasted aura, no spectacular pillar, and no uncontrolled rage for Bryndís to consume. Everything he had become was concentrated into the next movement.

“So stop telling me what my slaughter made you. Show me whether it made you strong enough to survive the man who did it. Because if I have to turn your home into ruins again, Bryndís, I will do it with my eyes open this time, and I will not regret a single corpse I leave behind.”


"That's... ENOUGH!!" She cried out, the calamity bursting away from her as the tone shifted completely from Calamity to something else. The calamity assimilated within her did not loosen it's grip, it was restructured, reformed, transformed into something that the void took advantage of. It was not simply a piece of a whole anymore, no, it was the seed of a foundation needed to produce her own. Thus was true to the act of consumption, and even then there was still something similar that lied within itself.

The spirals of black and red swirled around her as the tears poured down her cheek, the storm inside of her swirling aggressively as the resonating furnace of her own calamity began to burn and sear through her figure. The it did not divide, it did not conquer, it simply acted as it's own figure, and built the foundation that was needed for her to take control over her own singularity. Then the obsession came, the way it tore and reshaped, crafted through the embers of her fury had made something much more different.

What came out was not fury, not anguish, but hatred. Pure, refined, unfiltered hatred. The musculature shifting to something akin to more of a wyvern, the roars out of her frame became more animalistic, the humanity fading from her slowly with the dissolution of her  emotions. The pure anger and hated began to craft exactly what the God Eater had designed, the corruption was merely the trace of the step, the calamity the catalyst, and now the neglect that Sonzai presented was merely the key. 

Instead of the corruption splitting, the attack merely parted away. The source burning and searing along the edges as though he were reaching into the center of a forge. The otherworldly energy resonated from her as the sound waves resonated out from her, the dissonance of the chains, Sonzai, and the connection between her began to rupture and burst, the roars echoed outward as the bonds became blurred. The shockwaves brushing against him as the chains of war-did not take into account of frequency of sound. The transformations of it's previous forms became more and more aware, exposed, the connection between them almost unified, but severed before they conjoined. They instead began to align, shape, and reconfigure into the shape that was entirely true. The true figure emerged out the horns, the frame, the true avian shape shifted to reveal the draconic essence underneath it all. 

This was no dragon, only the figure of what she exactly had become from the results of Sonzai's negligence. "You say you wanted me to show you exactly what I had become... well then... how is this for results?" She called out, the figure shifting as the claws extended out of itself, digging into the ground as if to anchor her being completely.

Sonzai was sent back as the heat spread across his chains, palms reddening and burning from the heat. His hands caught ablaze as of the result, the contents burning and blitzing the figure around him as the heat of the furnace directly inside of her core released out the infernal otherworldly energy. 

"Live in your ignorance, live in your lies, I do not care. I will show you regardless of your approval, that I am more than enough!" She called out as her maw opened as the dark essence around her represented a mixture of fur and scales, the messy and yet void-like armor surrounded her body in a black exoskeleton. The crest and multiple lingering eyes laid onto him as they locked on, never leaving her sight. The growls of anger came from her  as the air was filled with a toxic, and yet sweet aroma. Sonzai felt it reach him as his body began to hinder, muscle joints began to loosen, bonds began to weaken. The ashes raining from it had filled the air as the black smog around it's new frame risen upward.

The neurotoxin hit, mind-numbing, maddening, the connection between his chains and his resonance had suddenly began to loose control. The Harmony within holding on tightly as the lock began to loosen on the-grip.

The neurotoxin reached Sonzai’s lungs and immediately began weakening the Harmony Emira had anchored inside him. Sensation loosened in his fingers, the muscles around his shoulders stopped answering with their usual precision, and the resonance connecting him to the Chains of War frayed beneath Bryndís’s dissonant roar. Sonzai recognized what was happening before the first Chain struck the ground, and instead of desperately trying to preserve the connection exactly as it had existed, he deliberately released it.

The Chains fell around him with heavy metallic crashes, apparently severed from the precision that had made them dangerous only moments before. Bryndís had disrupted his muscles, interfered with resonance, and forced enough separation into his control architecture that a lesser bearer would have lost the weapons completely. Sonzai merely raised his eyes through the descending ash while every fallen link began moving independently.

“You made the wrong assumption. Weakening my grip does not weaken the Chains themselves. They have been fighting longer than either of us has been alive.”

The inscriptions covering the Chains awakened with accumulated battlefield memory, recalling formations, encirclements, retreats, feints, sieges, and killing patterns Sonzai did not need to consciously reproduce. He stopped treating them like extensions of his limbs and instead gave the entire weapon system one objective, allowing centuries of stored conflict to determine how that order should be fulfilled. The Chains rose around Bryndís from every direction, no longer moving according to the simple rhythm she had already learned to disrupt.

“**Battlefield Seal, Iron Encirclement.** The order is simple. Restrict everything.”

One Chain buried itself behind Bryndís while two crossed above her wings and another threaded beneath one forelimb without touching it. Additional lengths entered the spaces around her body, dividing the battlefield into smaller and smaller regions until she technically possessed freedom of movement while almost every useful direction had been preoccupied. Sonzai had learned from their previous exchanges that a single decisive restraint gave her one obvious mechanism to counter, so he gave her dozens of incomplete restrictions instead.

Bryndís shifted left and found a Chain already occupying the route, then raised a wing only for two more lengths to position themselves beneath the airflow necessary for full extension. When she attempted to pull backward, another Chain tightened across the ground behind her anchored claws, converting retreat into additional tension against her own stance. None of the restraints committed enough force to become a clean reaction point, yet together they steadily reduced the space in which the transformed beast could operate.

“You wanted calculation instead of crushing force. There it is. I do not need to stop you from moving if I can make every movement belong to me before you make it.”

The dissonance erupted again, sound tearing through the formation at multiple frequencies and shaking the Chains hard enough to fracture the ground beneath several anchor points. Sonzai felt the remaining Harmony inside him loosen further as the neurotoxin continued attacking his nerves, but this time he did not attempt to restore ordinary resonance. Calamity spread from his Soul Crystal and entered the damaged relationships themselves, using every rupture Bryndís created as a fresh route through which Sonzai’s will could reach the weapons.

“**Calamity Art, Ruinous Communion.** You keep breaking my connections as though broken things are useless to me. Disaster lives precisely where things stop working correctly.”

Where resonance became unstable, Calamity occupied instability; where muscles misfired, Calamity used the failure; where dissonance blurred the relationship between bearer and Chain, the corruption flowed through the blur. The Chains stopped trembling almost simultaneously, their movements becoming unnaturally quiet as black-red light entered the inscriptions along every link. Bryndís had attacked the integrity of Sonzai’s system, only to discover that the force Malakar left inside him functioned most naturally inside damaged systems.

The first loop closed around Bryndís’s lower body while another tightened across the base of one wing and two more crossed the opposite shoulder. Every attempt to transform created additional points of contact because the Chains no longer interpreted transformation as escape, only as another movement occurring inside a battlefield already sealed. Sonzai stepped through the toxic smog while the skin across his palms reddened and blistered from the furnace heat, Catastrophic Continuity forcing the damaged flesh to remain functional as quickly as it deteriorated.

“You tried to loosen everything holding me together, and that would have worked wonderfully against almost anyone else. Unfortunately, I carry something that becomes more comfortable the worse the situation gets. You gave Calamity exactly the environment it prefers.”

The toxin continued numbing Sonzai’s nervous system, so he stopped demanding that his body function normally. Every delayed nerve signal, weakened joint, lost sensation, and misfiring muscle became a localized catastrophe for Calamity to seize and incorporate into the larger pattern of movement. He was not resisting the poison by restoring health; he was forcing failure itself to remain operational beneath his will.

Then Sonzai raised one hand and allowed the Calamity to leave his body for the first time since he had begun mending it. The corruption traveled through the Chains already wrapped around Bryndís, using physical contact, battlefield memory, transformed anatomy, and damaged bonds as entry points into the architecture maintaining her existence. It did not rush toward her like a blast, but spread inward like black-red veins discovering every seam she had spent years hiding beneath new forms.

“Now you get what you have been demanding from me. Not a projectile you can swallow, not a loose flame you can restructure, and not another piece of Calamity you can pretend belongs to you. You get me.”

The corruption flooded deeper until Sonzai no longer recognized Bryndís as merely the draconic-avian creature standing in front of him. He pressed his will through Calamity and made it acknowledge every state that had produced the present one: the Tenrai life before the slaughter, the death that ended it, the abandoned soul, the undead remains, the reshaping under the God Eater, the avian monstrosity, the draconic emergence, and the hatred that had eventually become identity. Catastrophic Continuity stitched those lives together until none of them could conveniently declare itself separate from the others.

Bryndís’s body jerked as the first memory became sensation again. The smell of fire returned with enough force to overwhelm the present battlefield, followed by smoke entering lungs that no longer possessed the anatomy they had possessed then and heat crawling across skin that had been replaced several forms ago. Sonzai was not inventing an illusion around her; he was forcing the pain already embedded within her continuity to become present enough that transformation could no longer place distance between the survivor and what she survived.

“Remember it. You told me repeatedly that I refused to understand your pain, so I am done refusing. We are going to examine every part of it.”

The village returned inside Bryndís’s perception, not as fabricated scenery but as trauma given enough metaphysical pressure to become immediate again. Burning structures, screaming Tenrai, collapsing streets, the terror of realizing there would be no rescue, and Sonzai’s own presence inside the slaughter rose through the Calamity already threaded into her existence. The Chains tightened whenever the beast attempted to move, denying her the physical escape her transformations had always supplied.

The moment of death arrived again. It completed, faded for barely an instant, and then Calamity reopened the same catastrophic imprint before the pain could become safely historical. Sonzai was not reversing time; he was repeatedly forcing the existing wound in her continuity to report itself as unfinished.

“Again. You wanted me to know what happened to you, so you are going to know it too. Every time.”

The memory struck again with all the old terror intact, followed by another repetition and another as Calamity refused to allow the catastrophe to settle into the past. Each cycle ended only long enough for Bryndís to recognize that it had ended before the same sensations returned, making survival itself the mechanism by which the next repetition remained possible. The beast’s claws tore trenches through the ground while her wings strained against the encircling Chains, yet Sonzai kept every restraint calculated enough to hold without giving her one clean force to reverse.

“You keep calling yourself the result of my failure. Fine. Then the result can remember the cause.”

Sonzai pushed the corruption deeper into the Obsession feeding Bryndís’s hatred and found the place where memory had stopped being something she carried and had instead become the foundation carrying her identity. Every thought of revenge pointed toward Sonzai, every resentment kept his Name present inside her, and every year spent imagining what she would do when she saw him again had strengthened that connection. He seized that fixation and reversed its usefulness, turning her hatred into the road by which his Calamity returned directly to the wound she had built herself around.

“You made me the center of your grief. You remembered my face, carried my Name, and shaped your entire second life around the moment you would stand in front of me again. Thank you for maintaining such an excellent connection.”

The torment expanded beyond the original massacre. Sonzai forced the continuity to include the abandonment afterward, the realization that survival had restored nothing, the shame of being considered weak, the loneliness that followed, the resurrection, and the eventual willingness to surrender pieces of herself to the God Eater so she could become something powerful enough to matter. None of those experiences were newly fabricated, but the emotional distance between them disappeared until years of suffering occupied the same immediate space.

Bryndís’s many eyes began losing coordination as incompatible stages of her own history crowded the same consciousness. The girl who had died, the abandoned survivor, the corpse, the servant, the avian horror, and the draconic beast were no longer allowed the comfort of pretending they were separate people created by separate eras. Sonzai’s Calamity held every version together and forced the current body to carry the complete accumulated weight.

“You asked me to look at what my slaughter made you. I am looking now. I see everything.”

The Chains pulled Bryndís lower as Sonzai walked closer through the infernal heat, his transformed aura remaining thin and controlled against his body. He refused to crush her consciousness because unconsciousness would have defeated the point of what he was doing; he wanted her aware enough to recognize every remembered sensation and every connection that allowed it to return. Each time her body weakened toward collapse, the Chains supported just enough weight to keep the torment from ending through physical failure.

“You survived me once and spent everything afterward becoming strong enough to ensure you would never feel that helpless again. So I am going to remind the thing you became of what the girl underneath it already knows. Power did not make that day disappear.”

Calamity surged again and the massacre reopened. Fire returned, panic returned, death returned, and Sonzai himself stood at the center of the memory with all the merciless significance Bryndís had preserved for him across the years. When the experience finished, Catastrophic Continuity simply mended the catastrophic imprint back into an active wound and started the sensation again.

“You could not stop me then. That is the truth you spent all these years trying to bury beneath strength, resurrection, hatred, and your God Eater. I am simply uncovering it.”

The beast convulsed against the restraints while black-red corruption moved through every transformed layer of her existence. Sonzai did not increase the physical damage because the battlefield had already shifted away from flesh; instead he tightened the existential continuity connecting each version of Bryndís until no transformation could escape responsibility for another version’s suffering. Her hatred itself became self-defeating because every attempt to focus on Sonzai strengthened the connection through which his will was reaching her.

Sonzai stopped directly before the restrained creature and looked upward without guilt. His purple eyes remained bright beneath the black-red fractures crossing his face, while the Chains stretched around Bryndís in every direction like the architecture of a cage made from remembered wars. The rage inside him had not disappeared, but it was quiet enough now to become deliberate cruelty rather than uncontrolled violence.

“And I still do not regret you. I do not regret the village, I do not regret the fire, and I do not regret being the reason you learned what helplessness feels like.”

Sonzai opened his hand and every catastrophic imprint inside Bryndís began aligning. Death, abandonment, resurrection, transformation, rage, and obsession ceased presenting themselves as separate events and collapsed toward a single continuous disaster. Calamity entered that continuity completely, ensuring that each painful part reinforced the next rather than permitting the mind to separate them into different lives.

“**Calamity Art, Everlasting Ruin.** No more distance between what happened and what you became because of it. Carry all of yourself at once.”

The accumulated torment descended through her existence as one uninterrupted experience. The massacre became abandonment, abandonment became resurrection, resurrection became servitude, servitude became hatred, and hatred returned directly to the massacre that had produced it, creating a closed catastrophic circuit inside the continuity Sonzai had stitched together. Every attempt to escape through anger merely fed the Obsession that completed the circuit.

Sonzai remained perfectly still while the Chains maintained the restriction. No uncontrolled aura exploded from him, no blind rage compromised his judgment, and no guilt appeared in his face as the beast struggled beneath the accumulated weight of its own history. He had finally learned how to make Calamity express something more precise than destruction.

“You wanted me to feel what you endured, Bryndís. I understand it now, and understanding has changed nothing about what I think of you. So endure it again.”

The Chains tightened another fraction while Sonzai’s Calamity kept the torment present. He watched without looking away, refusing the distance she had accused him of maintaining since the beginning of their fight. The answer he gave her was not sympathy, repentance, or denial, but something considerably darker.

“This time I am not walking away from the flames. I am standing here long enough to watch you remember every one of them.”

"What did you do...?" She looked as she felt the metaphysical chains around her neck, the binding the ones that held her down despite all the hatred she could hold and aim towards him. "You chain me only to use me? Do you think I will be as stupid enough to willingly allow myself to be submitted by your own will?!" She looked at him in fury, she struggled, the chains around her began to rattle and violently so. The bound monstrosity shifting, crying, her hate, her pride, everything she held up until now began to weigh down on her as she struggled. The tears from the slits of those eyes shifting, turning as she struggled.

"I won't be put under submission... I am going to kill you... and I am going to..." She lost her thoughts as she continued to struggle, the furnace's heat began to slowly ease as the exhaustion sat in, the same way one becomes tired after a blind rage. Her frame collapsed as she caught her breath. Her body remembered all the transformations, but what stuck out was it's truest form, not the form that she chose to take. She began to shrink, considerably, the form reducing down to the size of your average cattle. 

There were no limbs, no wings, no legs to stand on, only boneless, jagged tendrils, and a draconic head looking right back at him. It's figure felt almost uncanny compared to something in the material world, the image of it remained as the heat from it sizzled into steam, the black tendrils shifting around her as she curled up. The tendrils and face curling perfectly into a sphere as Bryndís reached a state of dormancy. The silence followed after, the scent of the toxic smell and the sludge dripping off it's frame began to leak onto the ground.

The sludge began to slowly corrode the ground, the smell of it was unbearable, but the creature surrounding it was more interesting. The true form of Bryndís Tenrai, or rather what became of her, had remained still. The pulse still moving as there was an uncanny silence now.

Sonzai felt it-the rage, the anguish, all fading into grief, silence, and stubborn bravado. The beast was not slain, nor was she tamed, merely... silenced. The soft sounds came from the dormant sphere... ones mimicking sobs... sadness... and yet silence.


Sonzai did not move when Bryndís collapsed into herself. The monstrous body shrank beneath the Chains until only that limbless, tendril-wrapped thing remained, curled into a sphere while corrosive sludge hissed against the stone beneath it. He heard the faint sobbing from inside and understood immediately that the hatred had not disappeared; it had merely exhausted the body carrying it.

For several seconds, Sonzai simply stared at her. He could still feel everything he had forced back through her existence,the massacre, the abandonment, the resurrection, the years spent feeding that wound until hatred became enough of an identity to survive upon. He understood her rage perfectly now, and that understanding did not make his own diminish.

It made his worse.

“You think I don’t know what that feels like? You think you are the only thing that had a family taken from it and learned to hate the world afterward? I was a child when mine was butchered.”

His voice remained low at first, but the Calamity beneath his skin reacted before his volume changed. Crimson fractures brightened across his arms and throat while the Chains of War tightened around Bryndís without crushing her dormant form. The emotional pressure coming from Sonzai no longer resembled the violent flare she had shown moments earlier.

It had weight.

The Plane of Fate noticed first.

Something shifted through the invisible relationships surrounding Kharven as Sonzai’s anger crossed beyond emotion held inside a Soul Crystal and became something recognized by the world around him. Bonds, grudges, Names, memories, debts, and consequences connected to Sonzai Moto all trembled at once, as though his rage had become heavy enough that every relationship carrying his existence suddenly had to account for it.

The battlefield felt him.

Not merely his Essence.

*Him.*

The Chains of War began rattling without being commanded, their inscriptions answering something deeper than Calamity or physical tension. Every battle they remembered carried an impression of Sonzai’s rage through the Plane of Fate, and each remembered enemy, loss, betrayal, wound, and surviving connection became another thread feeding that emotional pressure.

Bryndís had hatred.

Sonzai had history.

His rage carried Tomuka, Ezo, Muken, Ayame, exile, Kharven, the Tenrai massacre, Malakar’s corruption, every battlefield where the Blood Hound had survived long enough to become feared, and every person he had failed to save afterward. It was no longer one wound demanding recognition; it was an entire life of consequence compressed into one emotion until Fate itself began treating that emotion like a defining relationship.

“You want to know why your hatred could never frighten me? Because yours points back to one night. Mine has had years to find new reasons.”

The pressure deepened.

The corrosive sludge around Bryndís flattened against the ground, not from physical gravity but from the sheer metaphysical recognition building around Sonzai. His anger was becoming a Fate-presence, something connections responded to simply because Sonzai’s Name and the emotion were beginning to overlap strongly enough that separating them required effort.

Calamity reacted violently to that realization.

The corruption inside Sonzai’s Soul Crystal surged toward the Plane of Fate through the Attraction Layer, but Emira’s Harmony prevented it from degrading into Obsession. Rage passed through without becoming captivity, retaining direction while refusing to become a single thought that owned him. That distinction made it more dangerous, because Sonzai was not trapped inside his anger.

He was wielding it.

The black-red aura returned around him, but there was almost no visible flame this time. Instead, the air around Sonzai developed an oppressive stillness as the Fate Plane recognized his hostility as an active metaphysical condition. Anyone tied strongly enough to him would have felt something wrong in the relationship itself, as though the thread carrying his Name had suddenly become hot enough to burn whatever touched it.

Sonzai stepped toward the dormant creature.

The Chains moved with him.

Every link dragging across the stone left no ordinary mark, yet the Fate beneath those places remained disturbed for a moment afterward, carrying impressions of hostility, conquest, and remembered violence. The Chains had stopped merely restraining Bryndís’s body and had begun enclosing the relationships by which she could meaningfully act against him.

“You wanted my rage. You kept begging me to show it to you, like hatred was some little contest you could win because you suffered enough. So feel mine.”

Sonzai opened his hand.

The pressure struck Bryndís without becoming an attack.

His rage simply entered the Fate-space between them, flooding the relationship she herself had created by making Sonzai the center of her hatred. Every thread of revenge she had maintained toward him suddenly carried something traveling in the opposite direction, and what returned through those connections was larger, older, and considerably less fragile than the anger she had built herself around.

The dormant sphere twitched beneath the Chains.

Sonzai felt her grief trying to retreat from him, but the relationship between them had already been declared too clearly for that. She had called herself his consequence, built a second existence around killing him, preserved his Name through hatred, and stood before him demanding recognition.

Fate had listened.

Now Sonzai answered.

His rage poured through those established connections as a metaphysical emotion, not forcing thoughts into Bryndís’s mind but making his hostility impossible for her existence to interpret as distant. The same way an oath carried consequence or a grudge altered recognition, Sonzai’s anger began occupying the Fate relationship between them until every connection pointing toward him returned the undeniable knowledge that he hated more violently than she did.


Not because he screamed louder. Not because Calamity burned hotter. Because his rage had survived more.

Sonzai remembered his mother.bHe remembered his father. He remembered Tomuka and Ezo.

He remembered the moment childhood ended beneath blood that had belonged to people whose voices he would never hear again, and then he remembered everything he had done afterward because of it. None of those memories contradicted one another anymore; murder, grief, loyalty, guilt, cruelty, love, and hatred all remained parts of the same man without requiring him to select only one.

The Fate Plane strained beneath that coherence. His rage did not fracture him. It recognized him.

The relationship reversed until Sonzai was no longer merely the man Bryndís hated; within the metaphysical exchange, Bryndís became something standing inside Sonzai Moto’s anger. His fury acquired the qualities of a recognized grievance, a living declaration that the world could record without mistaking it for temporary emotion.

“You lost one home and let it become your entire existence. I lost mine and went on to build enough hatred to burn other people’s homes afterward. You became a monument to what I did to you, Bryndís.”

Sonzai’s eyes narrowed.

“I became the thing that did it.”

The Chains tightened. Not enough to injure her. Enough to make the distinction undeniable.

Sonzai crouched in front of the dormant sphere, close enough to hear the muted sobs inside it without the furnace, screaming, or monstrous bravado masking them. His expression did not soften, but neither did he laugh at her; there was something almost offended in the way he stared down at the creature that had believed its hatred could somehow eclipse his own.

“You were angry because I ruined your life. I was angry enough to ruin lives because mine was taken from me. Do you understand the difference now?”

Calamity rolled quietly through his body.

The Fate around him answered.

For one terrible instant, Sonzai’s anger became so strongly recognized that the surrounding battlefield seemed to possess an emotional direction. Broken weapons pointed toward old enemies, Chains creaked toward unfinished grudges, and every connection touching Sonzai carried the impression of something standing behind his Name with its teeth exposed.

He could have allowed it to become Obsession. He felt how easy that would be.

One final surrender and the rage would have consumed every other relationship he possessed until the entire Plane of Fate recognized Sonzai only through hatred. Emira’s Harmony tightened around that threshold before he crossed it, and Sonzai consciously pulled the anger back from becoming a prison.

That restraint proved the point more than losing control ever could.

His rage was greater precisely because it did not own him.

Sonzai stood again and looked down at Bryndís while the metaphysical pressure remained around him, quieter now but permanently understood by the relationship between them. She had reached dormancy because her hatred had consumed every available expression and left exhaustion behind.

Sonzai was still standing. Still thinking. Still furious.

“You burned yourself empty trying to prove how much you hate me. I haven’t even begun to run out.”

His fist closed.

The Chains of War settled around the dormant beast.

“And that is why you were never going to win a contest of rage against me.”


"You don't need to explain it... I can already feel it." She said, though her voice was faint, it was breaking up a bit as she caught Sonzai off guard a bit, her frame shifted as the sludge around her shifted. Her eyes laid onto him, a pair of fourteen, two larger eyes and then the others trailing from top and bottom. She stared at him, the pain filling her mind as much as it did her body. Her frame shifted as the connection showed just how much pain they both endured. 

"I can see it just as clear as you do... how could they do that to you?" She asked, her eyes shifting as a soft steam risen from her frame, the warmth was felt between him and her the rising heat as she shifted herself to encapsulate herself. She spoke it as if she demanded answers, but she did not need to know. 

"I still hate you for what you've done, but even still... I have something to acknowledge now when I am free from this." She spoke, her tendrils extending out inches away from him, their bladed tips stopping before they could cut him as she showed little hesitation, only restraint. 

"I'm not burned out, I'm just tired. You expect me to understand you, but understanding requires something mutual and understanding. We are not mutual, no matter how you may shape it to be." She said, her eyes narrowing as she let out a soft growl, her tendrils retracting as she curled back up into a ball. Underneath the sludge he could hear a bit of muttering, some curses, other words much more softer. 

She didn't want to accept it, but part of her now knew it, the disbelief sat with her as she kept to her silence now as the air between them did not grow any less tense from before. Only deeper did the pressure become, but to her... suppose a little weight did ease her weary soul.

"Well? What are you waiting for? Are you going to keep me chained here are you going to finish me? There's no point anymore, you reduced me to what I am now. So you're better off with finishing what job you have started." She said-her eyes drifting despite the darkness she surrounded herself in. The sludge providing warmth and comfort despite the ice cold grip that was around her.

Sonzai stared down at the curled mass after Bryndís asked him to finish what he had started. The Chains of War remained wrapped around her dormant form while corrosive sludge hissed against the ruined stone beneath them. He could hear the faint sobbing inside the sphere, quiet and exhausted beneath the fading heat. His expression hardened rather than softened.

“Pathetic. You crossed years carrying my Name in your mouth, rebuilt yourself around hatred, crawled beneath the God Eater for power, and came here threatening everything I still have left. Now that you finally reached me, you want me to kill you.”

The Chains did not tighten around her. Sonzai stepped closer until the spreading sludge stopped only inches from his boots, his purple eyes moving across the fourteen exhausted eyes visible within her folded shape. There was no satisfaction in seeing her reduced to this, only irritation that someone who had screamed so loudly about survival had suddenly decided death was acceptable. Her request offended him more than another threat would have.

“You spent this entire fight telling me what you survived. You screamed about what I made you, how strong you became because of it, and how you would make me answer for everything I did. Then the moment I finally make you confront all of it, you decide there is nothing left except dying.”

Calamity stirred beneath Sonzai’s skin, but he refused to release it. The black-red seams across his arms dimmed instead, their glow retreating gradually toward the corrupted Soul Crystal beneath his chest. His rage still carried enough metaphysical weight that the Plane of Fate remembered it, but he no longer intended to place that weight upon Bryndís. He had already proved whose anger could endure longer.

“No. I am not killing you. If death is what you want because you are tired, then you are going to be disappointed.”

Sonzai opened his hand, and the first Chain loosened around Bryndís. Another followed, then another, the ancient links withdrawing from her tendrils and draconic head until their oppressive weight disappeared piece by piece. He did not hide what he was doing or disguise it as strategy. He was releasing her.

The Chains of War returned to Sonzai instead, some coiling loosely around his forearms while others hovered behind his shoulders. Nothing remained binding Bryndís except her own exhaustion and whatever strength she had left inside the dormant form. Sonzai stood directly before her without preparing another attack. The absence of restraint was deliberate enough to be insulting.

“You wanted me to finish what I started. That would be convenient for you, wouldn’t it? You get to die hating me and never have to find out whether there is anything inside you that exists without Sonzai Moto at the center of it.”

His eyes narrowed as he studied her. Bryndís had built years of identity around one massacre, one enemy, and one promised confrontation, while Sonzai had lived through countless other wars, losses, mistakes, and attachments since that night. Their suffering had intersected violently, but their lives had never been equally centered upon one another. Sonzai understood that distinction more clearly now than he had before the fight began.


“You said we are not mutual, and you were right. I did not build my life around you. You built yours around me.”

There was cruelty in the statement, but Sonzai did not decorate it with mockery. He simply believed it, and the calmness with which he said it made the words sharper. Bryndís had carried his Name through hatred long enough that killing him had become part of how she understood herself. Sonzai refused to reward that dependency by becoming the one who decided her ending too.

“That is exactly why I am not giving you death. If everything you became was built around surviving me, then survive this too. Figure out what remains when I stop giving you something to fight.”

Sonzai turned his gaze toward the ruined streets of Kharven. Smoke still rolled through broken structures, distant fighting continued beyond the immediate battlefield, and there were people inside those walls whose lives mattered to him far more than the exhausted creature at his feet. The realization settled him more effectively than any victory could have. He still had responsibilities waiting beyond Bryndís.

“I have a kingdom to defend. You apparently have a life you cannot stand carrying. So keep it.”

The Calamity around Sonzai diminished further until only thin black-red seams remained beneath his skin. He withdrew the pressure he had forced through their previous exchange, giving Bryndís back the full distinction between her own grief and his rage. Whatever pain remained inside her belonged to her now, without Sonzai amplifying it or forcing it into another cycle. He would not comfort her, but neither would he continue tormenting her.

“Carry the village. Carry your family, the abandonment, the God Eater, every transformation, and every reason you came here wanting my head. Then decide what you are when none of those things are happening directly in front of you.”

Sonzai took one step backward, leaving more space between himself and the dormant creature. The Chains hovered around him without arranging themselves into an attack formation, and there was no hidden bind waiting beneath the gesture. For the first time since their battle began, Bryndís possessed the freedom to move without immediately being punished for it. Sonzai wanted that freedom to be unmistakable.

“If the answer is still that you want to kill me, then heal. Get stronger, come back, and try again. I am not frightened enough of you to deny you the opportunity.”

His expression remained severe, but there was no challenge for the sake of pride in his voice. He was simply allowing the possibility that hatred might remain her choice even after everything that had happened. Sonzai would not demand forgiveness, loyalty, surrender, or gratitude in return for sparing her. If she chose another battle later, then another battle was what she would receive.

“But do not ask me to kill you because you are tired. That is beneath everything you spent this fight claiming to be. If you really survived me once, prove you can survive being left alive by me now.”

Sonzai turned his back on her completely. The Chains of War followed him without hostility toward Bryndís, their inscriptions dimming as the battlefield ceased demanding immediate violence from them. He took several steps toward Kharven before stopping, his back still exposed to the creature he had just defeated. The gesture carried more confidence than another threat could have.

“I still do not regret the Tenrai. I still remember exactly what I did, and if you are waiting for me to suddenly become ashamed because I finally saw what survived, keep waiting. But I am not doing it again today.”

Sonzai looked over his shoulder at Bryndís. Sparing her did not mean forgiving her assault on Kharven, sympathizing with the God Eater, or deciding that his own history required repentance. It meant only that he had looked at the consequence of the massacre and decided another corpse would accomplish nothing he actually wanted. That choice belonged to him, not to guilt.

“You came here demanding that I look at what survived my slaughter. I looked. I saw you.”

The Chains shifted quietly behind him as Sonzai turned forward again. Bryndís remained alive behind him, neither conquered into service nor bound by some new command, and Sonzai made no attempt to define what she was supposed to become next. That uncertainty was hers now. For someone who had built herself entirely around him, Sonzai considered that a harsher challenge than death.

“Now survive without needing me to finish your story. If you hate me tomorrow, then hate me tomorrow. Just make sure you are still alive to decide it.”



The leak did not stop when he closed his hand.

Kwon Mu-jin understood that about four seconds after he had congratulated himself on stopping it, which was the correct interval, because that is how long it takes a man to notice that a thing he has taken credit for has gone on happening quietly behind him.

It came off him in the seams. Out of the right shoulder where the arms stood, out of the sternum, out of the sockets of both eyes and the hinge of the jaw, and it went into the road as vapour and as light and as nothing at all, and it was not one kind of thing. Several hundred bolts of another man's essence had gone into his eye and had not been digested, and what came out of him now carried their signatures alongside his own, and there were others in it that he did not recognise and had not put there.

Every practitioner on that road who could read anything at all was looking at the richest field any of them would see in their lives.

*Saturation. That is my fault and it has been my fault for ninety seconds.*

*Aetheric Residue from the aurora, from the shield, from four hundred paces of men dying with their workings half made, from whatever the old man did in the sky at the ninth hour, and now from me, and the density in this valley is a hundred times what the district main at Sum-gol carries on a feast day. I have flooded a room and I am standing in it with a man who has spent his whole life short of a drink.*

Down the road behind him, at the ditch bank, the shield with the arm still strapped in it had begun to rime over.

Kaalabad stopped pulling.

That was the tell and Mu-jin caught it in the first half-second, because a working that has been running for eleven hours leaves a hole in the room's texture when it stops, and the hole arrived before anything came to fill it.

The centre went out. The pull went out. The spiral the bound arms had turned it into unwound and dissipated with no ceremony whatsoever, and the man let go of the whole architecture he had been holding since dawn and did not attempt to hold anything else.

*He has stopped fighting me.*

*A man who stops fighting the man in front of him has decided the man in front of him is a feature of the ground. He is going to work on the ground.*

He had time for that one sentence and then the air went white.

The water came out of it first.

No fog anywhere, and nothing settling on a surface. It went straight from vapour into ice without visiting liquid on the way, in the middle of the air, at a height of six feet, all across the narrows at once. Crystals seeded on nothing. They came up in plates and columns and long six-armed things, hanging where they had formed with no wind to move them, so that the whole four hundred paces of road filled from the ground to the height of a man with a slow-turning suspension of ice that caught what light was left and cut it into pieces.

Deposition. Vapour to solid, no intermediate, and every gram of it that changed state gave up its latent heat at the moment of crystallising.

It went into Kaalabad.

Every crystal that formed handed him what it had been carrying. A pound of water changing to ice in open air surrenders enough to hold a kettle at the boil for a quarter of an hour, and there were tons of it in the narrows, and it was all arriving at one address in the same second and it went on arriving.

Mu-jin watched him take it. That was the mechanism and it was in front of him and it was elegant enough that some detached part of him wanted to write it down. The law was not making cold. Cold is the absence of a thing and no law has ever been written that produces an absence. The law was closing the ways in which heat is permitted to be stored in a volume, one at a time, until motion had nowhere left to sit and had to leave, and every joule that came out of the air came out of it into the only object present that had been shaped to accept it.

Heat death, with a hand on the arrow.

That was the whole of Liminia and Mu-jin had read the entry twice as a first-year and had understood exactly none of it. Absence, the paper said, and void, and the end state. He had pictured emptiness. What was standing on the road in front of him was the second law with somebody holding it open at the correct page and refusing to let it turn back, and the reason it looked like emptiness was that it was the shape everything takes on the way to the same temperature.

The black came in behind it.

It amassed from the road upward, and it had no interior, and it had no edge that Mu-jin's eye could find twice in the same place. Light going into it did not come back out. Light going past it came out wrong. The remaining daylight bent as it crossed the boundary and separated as it bent, so that the shapes of two thousand men lying in the mud stood in the ice at three different offsets in three different colours, red furthest, blue least, every man on the road duplicated into a spectrum of himself.

*Refraction across a density gradient. The whole valley has become a lens and I am standing inside the glass.*

The temperature went past the point where a body reports it.

Mu-jin's fingertips stopped sending. The left hand first, then the face, then the ears, and he knew the sequence because he had grown up eleven miles from a mountain and had helped carry men down off it. Under the skin it would be running the same way it always ran: extracellular water crystallising first in the tissue spaces, drawing the water out of the cells behind it by simple osmotic difference, the cells shrinking and concentrating and dying of thirst in the middle of an ice field.

It was already too late in three fingers of his left hand and he knew that too. Once the water inside a cell has gone, nothing brings that cell back, and the pain of finding out arrives four to eight hours after the warmth does, and half the men lying in the ice on that road would lose something in the night from what was happening to them right now while they felt almost nothing at all.

The right arm went first and he did not feel any of it.

No circulation to withdraw, because it had never withdrawn anything since the summer, and no sensation to lose. It hung at his side and froze from the fingertips to the elbow while the arms of light stood out of the shoulder above it, and he found out about it later, and by then it did not matter.

Four hundred paces up the road, where a man in Moto harness had been getting eleven hundred men onto their feet and moving them south, the ice reached the back of the column and the column stopped being able to see. Mu-jin registered that and could do nothing with it and put it down.

Kaalabad tilted his head back.

*Oh.*

*Oh, that is what it was. Forty years and a hundred sites and every one of them a mouthful, and I had begun to believe the thirst was simply what a man of my kind is. It is not. It was the district. It was every district. I have been a lamp on a starved main since I was nineteen years old and this boy has opened every standpipe in the world in one place and stood in the middle of it.*

The gauntlet on his right hand came apart.

The swelling that had been building since the ditch bank reversed inside two seconds. The plate that had stood off the wrist by a finger's width let go entirely and fell in pieces into the ice, and the hand under it was a hand again, and he opened it and closed it twice and looked at it.

He said something then that Mu-jin heard as three syllables and did not catch, and there was nothing triumphant in the delivery of it. It had the flatness of a man confirming a figure he had been given years ago and had privately doubted ever since.

Ardency came up in him.

Mu-jin felt the figure move and had no instrument to put a number on it and did not need one. The Court keeps a scale for that axis and eleven men in four worlds are entered on it, and whatever reading Kaalabad had carried onto this road at the ninth hour, he was not carrying it now. He had come back to something. The corona around him stopped being violet and became a colour that the ice took and multiplied and threw around the whole valley in facets, and every crystal hanging in four hundred paces of air had a small purple man standing inside it.

Then the ground.

The Ashgate valley sits on a basalt shelf and the shelf is full of iron.

Mu-jin did not have it in those words. He had the working leaving the air and going down, and the note in the ground he had walked eleven miles toward changing key, and the whole continent under his boots choosing that instant to move.

The rock went below the point where the iron in it changes its mind.

Every grain of it, across the shelf, in the same instant. The crystal system folded from cubic to monoclinic and the easy line of every grain swung off its old orientation onto a new one, and the rock twinned against itself as it went, ridging and buckling along a million internal boundaries at a scale no eye could see and every boot could feel. The conductivity of the shelf fell through two orders of magnitude in about a second.

The ley in that valley went dark.

That was the part that mattered and Mu-jin found it out through his shoulder.

The nine arms standing out of his right side lost their line. An Animatria form lives as long as the line to its caster holds, and the line had been running through the ground because everything in that valley ran through the ground, and the medium had stopped carrying. The letters began to drift. They came off the surfaces of the arms in ones and twos and then in whole clauses, and the hands came apart at the knuckles first, and Mu-jin felt each one go as a specific thing, the way a man feels a rope part strand by strand in his grip.

He had held four before, once, in a bad month, at a cost he had entered in his own record as *not to be repeated*. He had held nine here for less than a minute and the ground had taken them off him.

Then Kaalabad took hold of all of it.

The influence had never been on the ley alone. It went into every grain of iron in the shelf, in the ore in the ground and the nails in the carts and the mail on two thousand men, and it pulled, and the ground of the Ashgate valley came apart in waves.

Nothing cracked. It rolled. The road and the fields on both sides of it lifted in long rolling ridges eleven feet high and moving, coming out from under the black in ranks, throwing frozen mud and buried stone and the corduroy of split logs and the dead, and the ridges reached the ditch and filled it and went on across the fields toward Kharven-Seat, and where they passed the ground did not come back down flat.

Kaalabad roared.

The sound of it and the colour of it arrived together and the ice took them both, and the whole hanging suspension across four hundred paces rang at one pitch and shattered inward.

He cut.

Diagonal, left shoulder to right foot, the whole of Zarakal in a right hand that worked again, and the cut did not travel through the distance between them. It closed the distance and arrived, and the space it had crossed had not been consulted.

Mu-jin went sideways.

He did it without his feet, because there were no feet available. The ground under him was a wave in motion and the ridge was already lifting his left boot off the ground when the cut came, and there was nothing to push against in any direction.

He had perhaps a fifth of a second and he spent all of it on the only asset he had, which was the thing he had spent the last ninety seconds being ashamed of.

So he stopped holding the leak.

He opened the seams on his right side all the way and let everything that had been coming out of him quietly come out of him at once, all of it, in one direction, out of the shoulder and the ribs and the hip. Several hundred bolts of another man's essence and his own and the ones he had not recognised, vented in a hard jet from one flank, and every ounce of it that left him going one way put him the other.

There was no elegance in it whatsoever. It is what a squid does. It is what a pump does. Mass out of the back, and the body goes forward, and the only thing it asks is that you have something to throw and that you do not care about getting it back.

He went eleven feet across the front of the cut with the ridge still rising under him, and the vent tore his coat off his right side from the collar to the hem and took the skin off the back of his hand where the sleeve went, and he did not slow down and could not have.

Zarakal went through the place he had been and through the wave behind it and through the ground under that, and the ridge it was riding split lengthwise for a hundred and forty feet and both halves fell away from the line of the cut, and everything inside that line simply had no further account.

Mu-jin came down on the far side on one knee in broken ice with the jet still venting off his right hip, and turned his head, and his eye tracked the whole length of the cut in about a quarter of a second and took the black at the end of it and did not blink.

He put the frozen right hand flat on the moving ground to steady himself, and it made a sound, and he set his left and stood up.

"You chose to not kill me?" She looked back at him, her tendrils shifting as her gaze laid down onto him as her gaze narrowed. The soft growling from her frame rumbled in anger as she looked at him. The way he neglected her felt so offensive and yet oddly she could feel the softness behind it, even if it denied her the privilege to see her family once more.

A soft chuckle resonated from her, despite the gaze she gave out, she curled back into a ball not even bothering with arguing about it. "You're an odd one, you know that? After all the threats I shouted, out of all the things I have done... you chose to spare me. That's uncharacteristic of you." She said, her figure not even bothering to stare at him in order to understand him. She didn't need to look in order to see what kind of man he was, he was already there.

"You're a cruel man, Sonzai Moto." She said in a bitter tone, despite the obvious amusement she got out of his reaction. She risen herself up as she towered over Sonzai's Smaller Frame, the chains might have shrouded her, but the gaze that looked back at him was something much menacing despite it's softer tone.

Her body shifted again, as she looked away from him, stubbornly and defiantly. She did not want to acknowledge anything about him, she held onto it firmly holding herself away in both a haughty and yet smug manner. She would've sunk now slowly into the sludge as she dove right underneath her shadow, remaining with him due to the connection the two enforced. 

Though it was contractual and not mutual, she knew better to obey it rather than to disobey what exactly it was he had set up between the two of them.

Kujo stood at the center of it all and watched the world attempt to become several catastrophes at once. Kharven was burning in different directions for different reasons, the Brotherhood of Apokalypse poured against its defenses, Kaalabad had found something on the Ashgate road capable of making him work for every step, and Sonzai had just chosen not to kill the surviving consequence of his own massacre. Beneath Xevorith, the darkness covering Kujo from the neck down remained perfectly still. Only his eyes moved.

For a long while, he said nothing. The Crevice beneath the black mantle pulsed slowly as if measuring the failures occurring across the kingdom, each collapse carrying a different texture into him. Buildings were failing materially, loyalties were breaking politically, old grief was opening through Fate, and individuals who should have been consumed by despair were instead producing stranger answers. Kujo found that considerably more interesting than uncomplicated slaughter.

Then he smiled.

“This is becoming excellent.”

His voice carried a quiet delight that had very little warmth in it. Kujo had expected Kharven to fracture beneath simultaneous pressure, but instead the city had begun producing contradictions everywhere he looked. Traitors had become loyalists, monsters had become survivors, schoolmasters had become battlefield anomalies, and Sonzai Moto had somehow turned rage into something capable of touching Fate without surrendering himself completely to Obsession.

Kujo’s gaze settled briefly on Sonzai.

He had watched the Moto heir explode before. That version of Sonzai had been easy to understand because anger increased output while reducing judgment, Calamity making him more dangerous and simultaneously more predictable. What stood before Kujo now was different, because the rage remained enormous while the waste had begun disappearing from it. Emira’s influence was still there, and Kujo could recognize her work without needing to see her standing beside her brother.

“You really did improve him.”

The statement was not directed at Sonzai.

Kujo’s smile thinned as he considered Emira’s intervention again. She had not suppressed Calamity, purified it, or separated Sonzai from the corruption Malakar left inside him; she had allowed the dangerous thing to remain dangerous while teaching the carrier how not to be consumed by its internal competition. Sonzai had now taken that principle further on his own, turning rage into coherence rather than simply volume.

“That is irritatingly elegant.”

His attention moved toward Bryndís.

She had arrived screaming about despair, carried the God Eater’s authority into another man’s kingdom, transformed herself through hatred, and then collapsed beneath the full weight of the history she had built her identity around. Yet Sonzai had spared her when killing her would have been easier and theatrically cleaner. Kujo did not mistake that for mercy.

He understood exactly what made it more interesting.

Sonzai had refused to complete the pattern Bryndís expected from him. She had built herself around the premise that the man who destroyed her people would eventually either die by her hand or kill her again, allowing her entire existence to close around the same catastrophe that created it. By walking away, Sonzai had denied her conclusion without denying the original violence. Kujo’s eyes narrowed slightly at that.

“Cruel.”

A faint laugh followed.

“Much crueler than killing her.”

Kujo looked beyond them toward the roads choked with soldiers and monsters. Kharven’s people were still dying, walls still groaned beneath pressure, and armies still tried to decide whether they were invading, retreating, defecting, or merely surviving the next minute. The city had become exactly the kind of place Kujo wanted the world to become: too many simultaneous disasters for one heroic answer to resolve them all.

And yet it had not collapsed. That bothered him pleasantly.

The Crevice beneath Xevorith brightened faintly along the hidden fracture below his sternum. Kujo could feel structural failures everywhere, but he could also feel the irritating persistence surrounding them, people making repairs faster than ruin could become final in certain places. Kharven was demonstrating the weakness in uncomplicated entropy: sometimes pressure did not merely destroy structure.

Sometimes it taught structure what mattered.

Kujo laughed again.

“Oh, this is wonderful.”

The darkness around his shoulders shifted as though enormous wings moved beneath a curtain. He watched Kaalabad continuing toward violence despite accumulating damage, watched Mu-jin draw workings through an altered eye Kujo still had not fully classified, and watched Sonzai return his attention toward a kingdom that had every reason to fall apart around him. None of them were responding correctly.

Which meant they were responding usefully.

“You were all supposed to become simpler.”

Kujo tilted his head.

“Fear makes most people simple. Grief makes them simple. Exhaustion makes them simple.”

His smile widened.

“But you keep becoming more complicated.”

That was the part he enjoyed most.

Despair worked best when options narrowed until survival became nothing more than delayed surrender. Kharven was doing the opposite beneath pressure, producing new loyalties, revised tactics, unexpected transformations, and decisions that would never have existed without catastrophe forcing them into being. Kujo had wanted entropy to teach the world that continuity was unreliable.

Instead, some of them were learning improvisation.

He disliked that.

He adored it.

Kujo lifted one hand slowly and watched darkness collect along his fingers. He could interfere almost anywhere now, collapse another road, remove another section of wall, strike Sonzai while his attention divided, or reach toward Kaalabad’s battle and distort the developing answer before Mu-jin completed it. Every option presented itself with exquisite ease.

He chose none of them.

Not yet.

“You see, this is why I dislike ending things too quickly.”

His fingers relaxed, and the gathered blackness dissolved back into Xevorith. Kujo’s expression became almost conversational despite the catastrophe surrounding him. There was something deeply wrong about how comfortable he appeared while entire districts burned.

“A corpse tells you almost nothing.”

He looked toward the armies pressing through Kharven.

“A survivor becomes instructional.”

Then his gaze moved back toward Sonzai.

Sonzai had become particularly instructional. Kujo had already shown him emptiness, broken his continuity, made him experience the terror of ceasing to belong to himself, and expected the experience to leave behind a more exploitable fracture. Instead, Sonzai had taken terror back into himself and emerged with a clearer understanding of what he refused to lose.

Kujo’s smile faded slightly. That deserved attention.

“You are learning too quickly.”

There was no anger in the words.

Kujo sounded almost proud.

The black mantle around him moved as the Crevice pulsed again, and for an instant the beautiful human shell around Kujo’s torso seemed too thin to hide what lived beneath it. Something jagged and red-black suggested itself under Xevorith before the darkness smoothed over the impression. Kujo stared at Sonzai with the concentration of someone examining an experiment that had begun producing unauthorized results.

“Malakar put Calamity inside you because he wanted corruption.”

His eyes narrowed.

“Emira gave it rhythm.”

A small smile returned.

“And now you are teaching it philosophy.”

That amused him enough to laugh openly.

Kujo turned his attention across Kharven again, taking in the broken gates, the corrupted streets, the Brotherhood of Apokalypse, the surviving Moto, the Greymane betrayal, the Tenrai dead, the distant clash involving Kaalabad, and the strange awakening of Mu-jin. Too many stories were colliding now for any one hand to maintain complete authorship over them. Even Kujo could feel the difference between causing chaos and attempting to dictate every consequence of it.

He preferred the former.

Control bored him when it became too complete.

“What a disgusting little kingdom.”

His smile became genuinely delighted.

“I wound it, and it produces more people.”

Kujo lowered both hands to his sides.

“Good.”

He began waiting again.

Not passively.

The Fifth Layer remained open around him, holding multiple distant battlefields within reach while Xevorith watched for completed sequences worth removing and the Crevice listened for structures nearing irreversible failure. Kujo simply refused to resolve anything prematurely.

He wanted to see who broke first. He wanted to see who adapted.

More than anything, he wanted to see whether all this resistance eventually became proof against his philosophy or merely a more elaborate route toward the same ending. That uncertainty was the only thing in Kharven powerful enough to hold his attention completely.

Kujo smiled beneath the blackened sky.

“Go on.”

His voice was soft.

“Make this difficult.”

It should have put him on his knees.

It did the opposite, and the doing of it went through him with a physical sensation he could put a hand on. Something in his chest that had been narrow since the water opened. Something in the line between his sternum and his palms that had run rough for nineteen years, that had made every working since the Crossing cost him more than it cost other men and had been recorded in three assessments as an unexplained inefficiency.

It ran clean.

*Because it is theirs.*

*That is the whole of it and I have been nineteen years and could not have said it this morning. What I have been carrying was mine and had my name on it and had one person's face on it, and I would not put it down, and a thing you refuse to put down is a thing you are holding by the wrong end. This belongs to them. It is arriving, and I am refusing no part of it, and the road it comes down is smooth.*

The surge went up his arms and out.

He was walking. He had been walking for a while and had not registered the walking, and the narrows was ahead and the ice was in it and the ridges were coming out of the black at the far end in ranks, and the ground under his boots was rising and falling in long slow swells like a deck.

He was going to have to go into that.

*And I am not to be afraid of it. That is the requirement and it is the only one. Brave is a separate question. Brave is a word for men who have somewhere to be afterward. I am required to walk into the chaos of that thing and be the one who does not run, because there is nobody else on this road who can stand in front of it, and if I am afraid where they can see me then the fear goes out along the same water that the weight came in on, and four thousand people find out in the same instant that the sky is empty.*

He was wrong about almost all of it.

He would find out how wrong over a period of years, and the testament that man made of him would take the shape of his life and turn it, and there was no version of the morning in which he could have known that, and it would not have changed his footing if he had.

Edward Lambert had turned back at the fourth mile.

The Stormfold road had gone under a ridge eleven feet high while he was looking at it, and the six riders had lost two horses in the first minute, and he had sat there on the frozen verge with a wax tablet inside his coat and made the arithmetic and turned round. Sonzai was on the far side of ground that had stopped being ground. There was no version of the errand left in the world. So he had come back to the only work that remained, and he had been in it for two hours now.

He was covered.

None of it was his. That was the part he noticed, in the small clear place at the back of his head that noticed things and wrote them down and had never once stopped operating in fifty-three years. Somebody else's, four or five somebodies, over the front of the coat and up the right arm to the elbow and across the left side of his face and into his collar, cooling and stiffening in the cold.

His men had come apart twice and re-formed twice. They were doing it a third time behind him at the base of the outer wall, forty of the household and about ninety Ashwell tenants who had never stood in a line in their lives, and the Brotherhood were coming through the broken sally gate in a column because a column is what fits through a gate.

Edward Lambert had no working worth the name.

He had a reading, and a hand that had never in his life been the hand that fired anything, and an assessment in the Accord's own file that put his output at the bottom of the register for a man of his standing and had done for thirty years.

What he had instead was forty years with a sword and no illusions about any of it.

He went forward.

The first one came at him high and he did not parry it. He stepped in on the left foot inside the arc where a downward cut has not yet acquired its weight, put his left hand on his own blade at the middle, and drove the point up under the jaw at the soft triangle behind the chin, and it went into the floor of the mouth and through the palate and the man's whole body locked and let go at once.

He did not stop to look. He turned the point out of the skull with his hip and not his wrist, because at fifty-three the wrist is the first thing to go.

The second was a spear and he took it on the outside and came up the shaft and put the cross of the hilt into the bridge of the nose, which is not a killing blow and is not intended to be, and then put the man on the ground with a foot behind his knee and killed him there in half a second while he was still deciding what had happened to his face.

Economy. Nothing thrown away. No exchanges, because an exchange is a thing two men can afford and he could afford nothing.

*Kasumi went at the fourth hour of the ninth of Tenth-month in a courtyard at Vaeloris and I have the report and I have read it four hundred times and I know the name of the man who signed the order and the name of the man who carried it out and the weather that day and what she was wearing.*

*There is no mercy in knowing. People believe there is. People have said to me, at least you know, and I have thanked them.*

He got to the front of the line and turned round.

They were looking at him. A hundred and thirty men with about eleven proper weapons between them, in the mouth of a gate, with a column coming.

Edward Lambert had never raised his voice in his life. It was a matter of some quiet pride to him. He had run a division of the Seat's correspondence for nineteen years and had never once needed to, and the men who worked under him would have told anyone that the dry voice going quieter was the thing to be afraid of.

He raised his sword over his head with both hands.

"FIGHT."

It came out of him wrong and enormous and it stopped the line dead.

"WE HAVE NOTHING LEFT TO SPEND BUT OUR LIVES." His face was somebody else's blood. "SO SPEND THEM. FOR THE GLORY OF KHARVEN."

They went in behind him and he did not look back to check.

Out on the water, four hundred paces away, the wave that had been rising in Sodoku Moto struck something and came back.

He felt it hit. It went out from him across the Plane in an expanding front, the whole surge of it, everything that had come up out of Kharven and everything he had put behind it, and it travelled, and then it reached a boundary and stopped travelling, and what came back at him was inverted.

Crest into trough. Every part of it turned over.

He knew the shape of that. Any child in a fishing family knows it and learns it on a mooring rope without a name for it: strike a rope that is tied to a post and the pulse comes home upside down, and strike a rope with a free end and it comes home the way you sent it. The difference is whether the far end can move.

The far end could not move.

The returning wave came back through him and met the next one going out, and where the two of them crossed they cancelled, and the cancellation was not peace and did not feel like peace. It felt like a stretch of himself that had gone quiet in the middle of a sound. Nodes, spaced out along a line, dead places where nothing arrived, and between them the places where the two waves added and where everything arrived twice.

He walked eleven paces through one of the dead ones.

A hand came down on his shoulder.

It was a heavy hand, and warm, and it had weight in it, and Sodoku Moto had been alone on that road for six hundred paces and had not heard anything come up behind him and there was nothing there.

"How much are you willing to give."

The old man's voice. The same one. It had not aged, and it had not softened, and it arrived at exactly the volume it had arrived at when he was fifteen years old with his hands in cold water, and it was not asking. It never had been asking. It had been telling him the shape of the account and waiting to see whether he could read.

How much.

His life. That was cheap and he had known it was cheap since the water and had been embarrassed to find how cheap. A man puts his life up when he has nothing else on the table and he calls it a great offering because it is the last thing he has, and it is worth precisely as much as the next hour, which is nothing at all to the sort of thing standing at the end of that road.

His kingdom, then. Kharven. Four hundred households burning their winter wood in a day and not arguing. The stack in every yard. The death-house with its door open. The Seat on its shoulder of rock with the light behind it. Every one of them, spent, to put that thing in the ground.

He found he could say yes to that, and he found how fast he had said it, and something in him went cold at the speed.

And then the boundary.

*I was not there.*

The trough came back through him and this time he could not step around it.

He did not know how she died. That was the fact. Nineteen years and he had never learned it, not the hour and not the room and not what took her and not whether there had been anyone in the room at all, and there was no report and no witness and no bench that had heard it and no man alive who would say. He had asked four people in nineteen years. Two of them had not known. One had lied to him, badly, in a way he had recognised at the time and had let go, and he had never worked out why he had let it go. The fourth had looked at the floor.

She had died. That was everything he had. And Hild had been raised by other people's hands, in other people's rooms, and had learned to hold a spoon and a name and a grief without either of them present, and had come out of it holding a broken circlet and putting it down on a mounting block and forgetting it.

The rope is tied to the post.

The wave will not go through and will not stop coming, and it will come back upside down every single time, and a man can drive it for nineteen years and get the same standing pattern in the same places, quiet here, unbearable there, and no motion anywhere at all. Nothing travels. That is what a standing wave is. It looks like the sea and it moves nothing from one place to another.

The hand on his shoulder was not there.

He put his own left hand up and covered the place where it had been and held it, and stood in the middle of the road with the ice ahead of him and the ridges coming and the whole valley ringing, and he was twenty eight years old and it was the ninth hour and his people were dying at a rate he could feel in his chest.

He had been in the dark before.

That was the thing he did not tell people and did not intend to and had not entirely admitted to himself, and it went past him now on its way somewhere, the way a smell goes past. The dark ahead of him at the end of that road had a quality he recognised, and the black was no part of it. What he knew was the thing behind the black. There was something at the far end of that valley that he had stood in before, once, for a length of time he had never been able to establish, in the years the record has him missing, and the reason he was not afraid was not courage and had never been courage.

It was familiarity.

That was the truth of it, and he had carried it since the Crossing, and it was harder to hold than the wife and harder than the daughter, and there was nobody in four worlds he could hand it to.

He set his sword low along his right leg with the edge turned out, and walked into the dark alone.

Kujo remained beneath the blackened sky while Kharven continued tearing itself apart in several directions at once. His eyes moved over the kingdom slowly, cataloguing each new fracture as though they were entries in a ledger rather than lives being ruined in real time.
