The Brotherhood pressed inward, old alliances were collapsing, Kaalabad had found resistance worth remembering, and Sonzai had begun changing under pressures Kujo had expected to consume him. None of it was finished yet.

“Just a little more.”

The words left Kujo quietly, almost affectionately. Xevorith shifted over his body like a sheet of living absence while the Crevice beneath it answered with a dull pulse. He did not need Kharven destroyed today; destruction was useful only insofar as it prepared the conditions for what came afterward.

“A few more pieces of chaos. A few more cities frightened enough to mistrust tomorrow, a few more kingdoms forced to choose which wound they can afford to treat, and a few more people learning that survival has become maintenance rather than certainty.”

Kujo’s gaze passed over the distant walls. Phase Two had never been about conquest in the ordinary sense, because territory meant very little if the world beneath it still trusted continuity. The purpose was corrosion: exhaust governments, damage inheritance, fracture confidence in magic, turn restoration into expense, and force every surviving institution to spend more each day simply remaining what it had been yesterday.

Then his smile returned.

“And after that…”

The darkness behind Kujo expanded slightly. For an instant, the silhouette beneath Xevorith appeared impossibly deep, as though his body had become an opening looking directly into the Fifth Layer rather than a creature merely wearing its power. The black around him swallowed the faint reflections from firelight without giving anything back.

“Phase Three.”

Kujo spoke the words differently.

There was no laughter in them.

Phase Three was not another army, another massacre, or another attack against a capital. It was the point where all the instability created beforehand would become useful infrastructure, where the weakened Material Plane would no longer possess sufficient coherence to reject what Kujo intended to place inside it. The wars were merely loosening the soil.

“The world still thinks the Void is invading it.”

His expression became almost pitying.

“That misunderstanding has been useful.”

Kujo lifted one hand and watched absolute black gather between his fingers. It did not resemble darkness anymore; darkness was merely the absence of ordinary light, while this behaved like something for which light had never been an applicable condition. Even the space immediately surrounding it appeared uncertain whether distance was still supposed to exist there.

“The Void will not remain something beyond your walls.”

His fingers slowly closed.

“It will become the condition beneath them.”

The Crevice surged.

Across Kujo’s hidden perception, he imagined the boundaries between planes thinning beyond ordinary breach and becoming permanently unreliable. Material law would begin accepting absence as native behavior, Fate would lose certainty about which connections deserved continuation, and Soul structures would increasingly have to spend themselves proving that identity remained worth maintaining. The Void would stop arriving as an event because eventually there would be no meaningful distinction between arrival and presence.

“That is Phase Three.”

Kujo’s eyes narrowed.

“Not occupation.”

A small smile returned.

“Supremacy.”

He looked over the chaos brewing throughout Kharven and seemed almost patient now. Every fire, betrayal, collapse, corrupted bloodline, broken ward, failed harvest, ruined road, and exhausted survivor was another small preparation for the same transition. The world was being taught to function poorly before Kujo asked it to function under rules that had never belonged to it.

“A healthy world would reject what comes next.”

His smile widened.

“So I will make it unhealthy first.”

The black mantle rippled behind him.

“Then tired.”

The Crevice pulsed again.

“Then desperate enough to call adaptation survival.”

Kujo lowered his hand, and the gathered absence disappeared into him. He wanted resistance to continue because resistance produced expenditure, and expenditure accelerated the decline he required. Heroes rebuilding cities, mages repairing wards, rulers feeding armies, healers reconstructing bodies, and bloodlines desperately preserving inheritance were all participating in his plan simply by refusing to die.

“That is the amusing part.”

Kujo laughed softly.

“They believe resistance delays me.”

His eyes gleamed beneath the dying light.

“It is paying for Phase Three.”

For a moment the beautiful shape of Kujo’s face remained utterly still. Then something underneath it shifted, the suggestion of the monstrous architecture hidden beneath courtesy pressing against the disguise before settling again. His voice lowered until it seemed less like speech than a promise given to the world itself.

“Just a few more catastrophes.”

He looked toward Kharven.

“A few more lessons.”

Then beyond it.

“A few more places where tomorrow stops feeling guaranteed.”

Kujo spread his arms slightly beneath Xevorith.


“And then I will open the Third Phase.”

The black surrounding him deepened until the landscape nearest his body seemed to lose definition. Kujo’s smile became serene in a way that made it considerably worse. There was no impatience left in him now.

“When that begins, there will be no question of whether the Material World defeats the Void.”

His eyes became completely cold.

“There will only be the question of how long the Material World continues pretending it is separate from it.”

Kujo laughed once beneath the blackened sky.

“Phase Three is coming.”

The Void shifted around him.

“And when it arrives…” His smile widened. “Void will reign supreme.”

Haruki's blade remained steady, the sea of monsters flooding the castle walls in an uneven and yet rushed pace. The light from her core emitted outwardly as the void repelled around her figure, the monsters and creatures clashing against her as the rows were divided evenly amongst each other. Her blade lifted up as the Luminalis thread aligned itself, the edges of it shifting as the tip began to resonate and glow. The runes conjoining in a chain reaction as the aether released upward into the sky, producing a pillar of light.

"Mother didn't die for me to just stand still and watch. Hope... perhaps I've forgotten how long it has been since I last remembered it... but I know now what I must do." She said as she shifted her footing, the Saishiki imbuing her blade, and allowing the marks down her back glow gently as she focused her edge towards the tsunami of monsters.

The environment around began to resonate, pulse with vitality and life, the area was dying, but she could still feel it's pulse. The blade began to close inward, the motion of it strong and yet quick in movement. She quickly performed two slashes without much delay, the movement almost blurred as a cross slash was formed by the motions combined.

Haruki's palm extended out as she released the slash forward, the energy wrapping around the hoard in a binding net, the energy burning and purifying the darkness inside of the sea. Haruki's footing shifting as she continued her motions to thrust forward into the anchor point. Her blade shifting into a turn as the seal exploded into a chain reaction, the energy spreading across a vast portion of the monsters ahead, the light pouring out of their body as the darkness was erased, consumed, destroyed from the inside.

"Go, fight for your kingdom. We leave the rest to you two, Sonzai... Sodoku. I will handle this like I always have... I will fight for the people, and not simply just for myself." She said, her eyes shifting as she looked down a soft chuckle left-her lips.

"Perhaps you were right... perhaps I am their hero." She said, recalling the memory of her first conversation with the Holy Sea. Her eyes focused as the Danshi shined through her as she began to roar out, her other eye began to shift and change as the awakening of a second eye began to emerge, the Saishiki began to shift as it made way for the new expression... thus the awakening of the Musushi. 

The connection shifted between the path of the monsters as it was shifted upward, the Danshi reacting in conjunction as their darkness was disconnected, conjoined and collected all into one single mass that she collected upward into the sky.

Her blade shifting upward as the pure and unfiltered light sliced through it, destroying it from the inside-out. She looked with a smile up on her face.

"I see now... what exactly you wanted me to see, Darius. For that... I am grateful." She said, with a smile on her face, knowing not the satisfaction of battle, but the satisfaction of realizing simply just how wrong she was.

A room he had never entered.

Stone. A drain in the floor with a grating over it, and the grating had been lifted out and set against the wall, which told him what the room was for before anything else in it did. Three lamps. A chair that had been made for the purpose, with the arms cut short so the wrists came past the ends of them.

Freya was in the chair.

He watched all of it. That was the arrangement and nobody had explained the arrangement to him and there was no refusing any part of it. He watched them start at the hands because you start at the hands when you want a person to keep speaking, and he watched the second day, and the third, and he watched a man he had eaten with at his own table in the year of the ice come into the room on the fourth morning with his sleeves pinned back and take the chair opposite hers and speak to her pleasantly for eleven minutes before he touched anything.

Seiji Tenrai Moto had wanted one thing.

Where is he.

That was all of it. Four days and a drain in the floor for one sentence, which she did not have, which she had never had, because Sodoku had made certain that she did not have it and had told himself at the time that he was protecting her. She had no answer to give on the first morning and none on the fourth. The whole of what was done to her was done to extract a thing that had been removed from her in advance by her husband, for her own safety, with her agreement, in a kitchen, over a bowl.

He watched her die.

It took a long time and it was not clean and there was nothing in it that any account would call dignified, and the thing that broke him was none of that.

At the end of it she was still holding him.

He could see it. He was in the Plane and it was the one place a thing like that is visible, and the bond at her Attraction Layer was intact, undegraded, unstained, and four days in that room had put nothing into it. It was the same shape it had been in a kitchen nineteen years earlier when neither of them had a title. Whatever they had done to her body, they had not reached that, and they had not known it was there to reach, and it went out of the world still burning at full strength and unbent.

That was the most beautiful thing Sodoku Moto had ever been shown.

He would have given every year he had left to unsee it.

And the only remaining piece of it in the world was asleep under a cistern with a broken circlet she had put down and forgotten, in a stone chamber with one way in and an old man standing in the doorway.


*How much are you willing to give.*

The question came back carrying the picture with it, and he understood at last that it had never once been rhetorical.

There is a state the Moto record calls Gisei. It has no activation and no training path. What it has is a price list. The Plane accepts memories and it accepts capacities and it accepts crystallised Traits, and it converts them at a rate set by what the loss costs the man making it, and it cannot be deceived, because the grief itself is what does the conversion.

And it accepts bonds.

No abandonment in it anywhere. Severance at the Attraction Layer. The anchor comes out of the Crystal and the person goes on existing and the practitioner goes on remembering them accurately, every detail, the whole history, and cannot feel any of it ever again. And what that bond was worth to him while it lived becomes density available immediately.

He had one bond of that magnitude left in the world.

*Authorship. That is the word for what is on the other side of this. I have been telling myself I want enough Ardency to put that thing in the ground, and it is a lie, and I have been telling it since the ninth hour.*

*Authority over the real. To open my chest and let it go outward until it is the size of an ocean and everything in it obeys the shape of what I have decided. That is what I want. I have wanted it since a kitchen, since a bowl, since somebody came to my house while I was elsewhere.*

*And it is available. That is the obscene part. It is not a fantasy. It is a documented state with a documented price and I have read the price list and I know exactly which item on it is worth the most.*

The shallow wall of darkness around his heart began to come apart.

It unwound. He felt it go, one turn at a time, something he had not known was there and now could not think of as having ever been absent, and what had been inside it stepped out and stood a little way off from his soul and looked at him.

It had his face.

The face from the wedding portrait that had burned with the house, thinner through the cheek, the nose still straight because the nose went in the second year of the ice.

It was not a demon and it did not pretend to be. It was younger. That was the thing that landed hardest and the thing he had least expected. Thirty-two, thirty-three, before the ice and before the Crossing and before the water, with the jaw he used to have and the hands he used to have, and a mandible of the void running down one side of it where the shape had not been finished.

It did not gloat. It sounded tired.

"Really," it said. "This is what is left."

Sodoku said nothing.

"Look at yourself. Walking up a road on foot because you gave your mount away to help another house's soldiers. Standing in a dead patch of your own grief doing arithmetic about a girl." It put its head on one side. "You have become a weak, disposable thing. And the terrible part is that you have made it a virtue. You have found a way to call this restraint."

"Say it."

"Where is the anger."

The voice did not rise. It never had. That was the whole of what made it his.

"Where is it. You watched four days in a stone room with a drain in the floor and the man who did it is eight miles from you at this moment with six hundred men and I could tell you the exact number of paces. Where is the obsession. You had it. You had it for years and it was the only thing that ever made you worth anything, and you put it down somewhere around the fourth year and you have been telling yourself since then that putting it down was growth."

*It is going to be right about the next thing. I can feel the shape of it coming and I already know it is going to be right.*

"You could kill that man in the Void." The thing came a half-step closer, which was the first movement it had made. "The one at the root of it, and you know which one I mean. Everything necessary is inside you at this moment and it has been inside you since the water and the only thing between you and it is a price you have decided not to look at. You could end the entire war by tonight. You. Alone. And instead you have arranged it so that Wren Greymane died on a ditch bank, and Bram is standing in a breach at the southern reach with three hundred men, and Edward Lambert is fifty-three years old and covered in someone else's blood in the mouth of a sally gate, and a schoolmaster with one arm is doing your work in the narrows because you would not."

"They chose that."

"They chose it because you were not available." It let that sit. "Every one of them is spending their life on a gap you left. You know it. That is why you have not looked at any of them since the whistle."

"And your correction."

"Give me the girl."

The thing said it in the flattest voice he had ever heard come out of his own mouth.

"Sever it. It is one act and it takes less than a second and she does not die and she does not even find out. She grows up. She rules. She is fed and guarded and taught, and she never learns that the man who kissed her hair this morning stopped being able to feel her somewhere between the gate and the narrows. Nobody is harmed anywhere. That is what makes it perfect, and it is why you will refuse it, and your refusal will be about you."

"Say the reason."

"You want to keep feeling it," said the thing with his face. "That is all. You have confused the feeling for the love. The love goes on working whether or not you can feel it, exactly as her love went on working in that room where nothing else survived, and what you are protecting when you refuse me is not your daughter. It is your access to your own grief. You would rather hold the ache than end the man who caused it."

The dark went very quiet.

*It is not wrong.*

*It has not said one thing that is untrue and it is not going to, because it is made out of me and it has had nineteen years of me to work with, and everything it has is something I gave it. That is what it is for.*

*And that is the answer.*

"How long have you been in there," Sodoku said.

The thing did not respond.

"You are not older than me. You are younger. You stopped at thirty-two, which is the year of the room, and you have not aged a day since, and I have been standing here trying to work out why a stronger version of a man would be a younger one." He looked at it. "You are not the strong part. You are the part that could not look at it."

"I am the part that would have burned Vaeloris to the footings."

"You are the part that took the rage off me so that I could get out of bed. Somebody had to hold it. I could not hold it and stand up, so I gave it to you, and I built a wall around you and I have been feeding you through it for nineteen years, and you have kept your side of the arrangement, and you have been the only reason I have functioned." He found his voice had not moved at all. "So thank you. I mean that. But you have never once been in the room with what happened, because holding the rage is how you avoid being in the room, and that is the whole of your function, and you cannot advise me on a thing you have spent your entire existence not looking at."

"You are afraid."

"Of course."

The thing stopped.

"Say that again."

"I have been afraid since the water. I was afraid on the boat and I was afraid in the hall and I was afraid this morning in the yard with my hand on her head, and I have been afraid every single day that I would find out what happened in that room, and now I have found out and I am still afraid, and none of it has ever once stopped me putting my boots on." He looked at it steadily. "You thought fear was the accusation. It is the condition. Every man on that road is in it. The only question anybody has ever been asked is what they did while it was true."

"They can all see it," the thing said, and there was heat in it now, which there had never been. "Every one of them. Your daughter can see it. She has been able to see it since she was four years old and it is why she does not ask you for anything."

Sodoku Moto took that.

He took it all the way in, without argument, and stood there in a place with no ground in it and let it be true, and the mandible of the void running down the side of the thing's face flexed once and did not know what to do.

"Yes," he said.

And then he turned his back on it.

That was all it took. There was no working in it and no expenditure and nothing he could afterward have described to a Bench. He stopped looking inward, which was the only ground the thing had ever stood on, and he looked at what was actually in front of him, which was a road and ice and men, and the noise in him that had been running since he was fifteen years old, the Ledger reading every room, the Shingan sorting every surface into what it hid and what it owed, the ranking and the assigning, all of it went quiet.

None of it suppressed. All of it irrelevant, the way a tool stops mattering when the hand carrying it has nothing left to hold.

He had always been this.

Behind him the thing with his face said his name, twice, and then said something about the price and about how much was still available and about the arithmetic of a single second, and it went on talking for some time, and Sodoku Moto did not turn around and did not answer and could no longer entirely hear it.

He was thinking about a bowl in a kitchen.

She had been holding it against her hip with her forearm and stirring with the other hand, and she had said the thing about the whereabouts without looking up, and he had agreed to it because it was sensible, and neither of them had said anything else about it ever again.

He was thinking that the bond in that room had been intact at the end, and that nothing anyone did to her had reached it, and that this was a fact about how the world was built and had been true before he learned it and would be true after him.

The dark ahead of him thinned.

There was a road under his boots again, and ice in the air, and the sound of two thousand men, and somebody a long way off shouting a name that was not his.

Sodoku Moto set his sword low along his right leg with the edge turned out, and walked on into the narrows.

The dark ahead of Sodoku thinned, the road returned beneath his boots, and for several breaths the world seemed willing to leave him alone with what he had chosen. Then the cold immediately behind him stopped behaving like cold. There was no approaching footstep, no displacement of air, and no shadow stretching across the road before its owner arrived.

Kujo was simply there.

Xevorith resolved behind Sodoku at full height, absolute black covering the Regent from the neck downward while his pale face remained almost unnervingly beautiful above it. One hand rested lightly upon Sodoku’s shoulder, without force, as casually as though the two of them had been walking the narrows together for miles. The contact carried no immediate violence, which somehow made it considerably worse.

Kujo had watched the darkness around Sodoku’s heart come apart. He had watched the younger face emerge from nineteen years of buried rage, watched the temptation place Hild upon Gisei’s price list, and watched Sodoku refuse an authority over reality that could have been purchased by mutilating his own capacity to love his daughter. Kujo’s expression carried quiet fascination rather than disappointment.

“You surprised me.”

His fingers remained resting upon the Moto’s shoulder as the black mantle shifted soundlessly around him. Kujo had expected obsession, because obsession was usually what survived when grief had been given nineteen years without resolution. Instead, Sodoku had looked directly at the ugliest arithmetic available to him and decided that becoming powerful enough to solve everything was not worth becoming incapable of caring why any of it mattered.

“You had the price in front of you, and you refused to pay it. Not because you doubted the power, and not because you lacked the courage to lose something. You simply decided there were things victory was not permitted to consume.”

Kujo’s smile slowly widened.

“That is dangerous.”

He leaned slightly closer, his voice dropping beside Sodoku’s ear while the narrows continued roaring somewhere ahead of them. Beneath Xevorith, the Crevice pulsed once, and for an instant the darkness around Kujo seemed to deepen beyond the dimensions his body actually occupied. His hand still did not tighten.

“You finally understood something most strong men never do. Fear is not weakness, restraint is not necessarily cowardice, and refusing a price does not mean you were incapable of paying it. Sometimes it merely means the thing on the other side was not worth what it demanded.”

Kujo laughed softly.

“How inconvenient.”

His gaze moved past Sodoku toward the road ahead, though he never removed his hand from the man’s shoulder. Somewhere beyond them waited Kaalabad, Mu-jin, broken armies, collapsing loyalties, and a kingdom being asked too many questions at once. Kujo appeared almost pleased that Sodoku intended to keep walking toward all of it rather than becoming something larger and emptier.

“You chose the bowl.”

A faint amusement touched his voice.

“Not the throne over reality. Not enough power to make the world obey you. A bowl in a kitchen, a woman holding it against her hip, and the fact that nineteen years later you still refuse to purchase victory with what remains of that room.”

Kujo’s smile became thinner.

“That is remarkably human.”

For a moment his expression lost its amusement completely. Kujo understood now why simply killing Sodoku would be wasteful, because the man had already endured the more interesting attack and survived it without pretending survival felt good. What remained was not the absence of fear, but a man who had consciously decided fear was allowed to accompany him.

So Kujo moved his face nearer.

*“My twin son of Fate.”*

The words were almost affectionate in their obscenity, Kujo acknowledging the two Moto brothers as separate answers produced by the same blood and the same ruined childhood. Sonzai had taken rage and made it increasingly coherent, while Sodoku had taken fear and grief and somehow turned them into boundaries he refused to sacrifice for power. Kujo wanted to know which answer broke more beautifully.

His fingers finally pressed slightly into Sodoku’s shoulder.

“Your brother has already tasted what I mean by emptiness. He screamed when his own existence stopped feeling like something that belonged to him, and afterward he became more dangerous instead of less. Now I find you standing here after refusing the strongest version of yourself because you still want to be capable of loving a girl beneath a cistern.”

Kujo laughed again, quieter this time.

“You Moto twins are becoming irritating.”

The darkness around his arm crawled a fraction farther across the space between them, not attacking Sodoku but making the contact feel increasingly incompatible with the ordinary world. Kujo had no intention of immediately tearing him apart, because despair worked poorly when administered before its subject understood exactly what they still possessed. Sodoku had just identified that possession for him.

“You know what you love now.”

Kujo’s eyes darkened.

“That makes this much easier.”

His head tilted slightly, and the beautiful smile returned to his face while Xevorith seemed to swallow the road immediately around his feet. The words that followed carried none of Bryndís’s shouting, none of Kaalabad’s violence, and none of the theatrical fury consuming Kharven. Kujo spoke them like a host welcoming someone into a room that had already been prepared.

*“My twin son of Fate…” His hand remained upon Sodoku’s shoulder. “Are you ready for despair?”*

He had been here before.

That was the first thing Sodoku Moto understood and he understood it with a flatness that surprised even him, because the emptiness arriving behind him was being offered as a revelation and he had already had it. Twice. Once at twelve in the four months the record has him missing, and once in the water, and neither of those had been administered by anybody. They had simply been where he was for a while.

*He thinks this is new.*

*He has done this to a great many people and every one of them has met it for the first time, and he has built his entire method on the shape of a man's face the first time, and I am not going to be able to give him that.*

*He is going to find out. And when he finds out he will change what he is doing, and what he does second will be worse, and I would rather have the second thing arrive while I am still standing than while I am on my knees pretending.*

There were four hundred paces of road behind him with eleven hundred men on it who had not finished moving south, and a girl under a cistern, and a schoolmaster in the ice at the far end doing work that was not his, and every one of those was a reason to keep a mouth shut.

He said nothing.

He wanted to. There was a sentence in his chest with weight in it and he could feel the shape of the whole thing, and he let it stay there and did not spend it, because that had been the arrangement he made with himself at nineteen and had kept in every room since. The Ice had called it coldness. Yoko had once told him, accurately and without malice, that a man who never shows the tide is a man who has decided the tide is a weapon. Freya had known the whole of it in about a week and had never once mentioned it.

The feelings moved anyway. They always did. In and out, twice an hour, the whole nineteen years, a river running under a floor.

His grandmother had had a word for it and had used it about him when he was nine, in front of his mother, and had refused to take it back. A boy who holds the tide, she said, grows into a man the tide holds. He had thought at the time that it was a compliment and had been pleased with himself for a week.

The hand on his shoulder had no weight in it.

That was the wrongness he could not stop reading. It rested there with the ordinary friction of a hand and the ordinary warmth of a hand and it displaced nothing whatsoever, and where the fingers lay across the pauldron the metal reported pressure and his shoulder underneath it reported none, and he had never in his life encountered a contact that arrived in one layer and stopped.

And the cold came out of him.

Outward, from the middle. That was the diagnostic and he made it in about a second and a half, because he had put men into cold water and pulled them out again and knew every stage of it. Cold from a room arrives at the fingers and the ears and the nose and works inward. This one started behind his sternum and went outward through the chest wall and down both arms and into the palms, closing the small vessels as it went, a wave of shutting-down travelling from the middle of him toward the edges.

His own body doing it to itself. No part of it came from the field or the hand. His own architecture reading the thing standing behind it and beginning, on its own authority and without consulting him, to prepare for a wound.

*Even my blood knows. It has gone to the middle and put a wall around the heart and the lungs and it is spending my hands to do it, and it did not ask, and it is correct.*

There would be an accounting for it afterward. The vessels in the hands do not reopen politely. Whatever this cost him would arrive in about four hours in the fingers and the ears, along with everything the ice had already done to two thousand other men, and he put that where he put such things.

He set his feet anyway.

The wind arrived from four directions and one of them was above.

It came into the narrows without a front. There was no gust and no build and nothing that moved the ice hanging in the air, and yet every loose thing on that road began to lean, and the lean was toward a point, and the point was eleven feet in front of Sodoku Moto and it was where an old man was standing.

He had not walked there.

Sodoku's whole trained apparatus went at him at once and came back with nothing. No projection, no working, no called thing. He stood on the road with the road under him and the ice hanging around him and moved it out of his way when he shifted his weight, and he was as present as any man Sodoku had ever stood in front of, and he was standing in front of both of them and around both of them at the same time.

Old. Seventy at least and hard with it, the way a man is hard when the work never stopped: bones showing through the face at the temple and the jaw, a mouth that had lost the shape of a mouth some years ago, white hair cropped to the skull with the scalp brown through it. Small. That was the surprise. He came up to Sodoku's collarbone. Wearing a working deel gone the colour of nothing at all, a hide belt with three knives on it and none of them dress knives, and boots that had been resoled by their owner more than once and badly.

His hands were enormous and out of proportion to the rest of him, and the tendons stood on the backs of them, and the last two fingers of the left one were gone at the second joint and had been for a very long time.

The eyes were the Shingan and they were open and they were not looking at Sodoku.

There was a hawk's feather through the left ear. Old, brown, the shaft worn shiny at the quill from being touched, and it was the only thing on him that had been chosen instead of used.

*Bōfū Fūkoku Moto.*

*I have known that name since I could read and I have never once expected to use it about a man standing on a road.*

*The Howling Hawk. Eleven mentions in the family literature and nine of them are the same three sentences copied forward, and the tenth is a marginal note in a hand nobody has identified, and the eleventh is the record of what was done at the Kurenai hall in the fourth year of the second Concord, which the Bench sealed and which my grandmother described to me once, in a doorway, with her hands in a bowl, and then never again.*

*He is nine hundred years dead. He was born before the Kōkan Line had a name and he held the eastern hall for forty-one years and refused the seat twice in writing, and the second refusal is the oldest document the family still holds and it is four words long.*

*He has two names because he was named at birth and named again afterward by the Plane, and the family literature keeps both because dropping either was considered dangerous. And he is called a hawk and not a wind, and I have wondered about that since I was eleven years old, and nobody at the Seat has ever been able to tell me why.*

Behind him, the hand on his shoulder did not move.
But something in the road changed, in the pressure and in the black and in the quality of the attention arriving over Sodoku's left ear, and it changed in a specific way that Sodoku had felt exactly once before, in a hall, when a man he had been negotiating with for two hours looked past him and saw somebody he had not expected to see alive.

*He knows him.*

*That thing is old enough to have met my ancestor and to remember what he looked like from the front.*

The old man grinned.

It was an awful expression on a mouth in that condition and it had a great deal of pleasure in it and none of the pleasure was kind.

"Well," said Bōfū Fūkoku Moto.

That was all he said for a while. He looked past Sodoku's shoulder at what was standing behind it and he did not hurry, and the wind kept leaning inward, and the ice that had been hanging in the air across four hundred paces began, all of it, to turn.

"They told me you would come back around," he said. "Sixty of us sat in the hall and worked it out and none of us liked the arithmetic and we did it anyway, and I have been in the wind nine hundred years waiting to find out whether the sixty were right, and here you are, and you have brought a hand and put it on my great-grandson's shoulder."

He wiped his mouth with the back of the ruined hand.

"They will tell you the sixty burned," he said, and he was still not looking at Sodoku. "They did. We burned the floor and every one of us went into the wind, and what came out the other side was not sixty men and was not one man either, and the Bench sealed the record because the Bench could not decide what to call it. I have been the wind over this country since before your family kept a ledger. I have watched eleven of you die badly and I did not come out for any of them."

He turned his head then, and looked at Sodoku for the first time, and the Shingan in the old eyes had a structure in it that no living Moto carried.

"You went quiet," he said. "That is all it ever wanted. Nine hundred years and not one of you would put the tools down."

He faced front again.

"Embrace despair."

The wind stopped leaning and started running.


There is a way a wind ends and Sodoku had never seen it done.

It came in on the point from every direction at once and it did not slow down as it converged, and that was the part his body refused before his mind caught up. A thing running inward across a shrinking circle should thin out and lose itself. This gathered. The circle closed and the speed went up, and the closer it came to the point the faster it came, and the rate was not something anybody had chosen. It was set by the medium and by the closing itself, and the old man was not driving it and had not been driving it since the first second. He had opened it and stepped back.

The condition was the silence.

Sodoku understood that at the last instant and understood that he had provided it. A converging thing of that kind can only run into ground that is holding nothing. It has to invade a quiet, and there had been a quiet in the narrows since he turned his back on the thing with his own face, and there had been a quiet in him since the same moment, the Ledger gone irrelevant and the Shingan gone irrelevant and the whole four-hundred-pace valley emptied of everything that could have pushed back.

The old man had walked into a silence that his descendant had made about ninety seconds earlier, and the silence had been the door, and refusing the thing with his own face had been the key, and nobody had told him and nobody was going to.

*He waited for it. Nine hundred years and he waited for the one man in the line who would go quiet in front of the right thing.*

The wind arrived.

Everything the Ashgate valley had in it went to that point and reached it together. Ice, the whole hanging suspension of it, four hundred paces of the stuff, drawn inward and down to a volume the size of a fist. Frozen mud. The wreck of the road. The letters that had come off nine spectral arms an hour ago and gone out. Frost off two thousand men. All of it converging, and going faster as it converged, and where it converged there was no space left to hold it and no time left to hold it in.

The speed at the centre went past what a number can be asked to do.

Then the point.

Everything that had come in came in at once and stopped being anywhere, and for a length of time that had no length the middle of the Ashgate road was a place where the whole valley existed at one address and the pressure at that address had no ceiling and the heat at that address had no ceiling and nothing that has ever been built could stand at the middle of it.

And it did not stay.

That was the thing Sodoku had not been told and had no way to know. A collapse of that kind does not end at the bottom. It arrives, and it turns, and it comes back out, and what comes back out is the same wind with every one of those four hundred paces still in it, expanding now, running outward at the speed it had going in.

He had time to understand that and no time at all to do anything with it.

The reflection hit.

It went through the narrows in a ring at head height and it went through the ice and the wreck and the ridges and the dead, and it took the black on the road and moved it, and for the first time since the ninth hour something that was not the black decided where things went.

Sodoku Moto was inside it and standing.

The wind went past both sides of him without touching him, which was a fact about the working and not a kindness, and his deel cracked flat against his back and his hair went forward across his face and his eyes ran and he did not close them.

He got his weight onto his front foot and turned his head to look at what was standing at his shoulder, and the hand was still there, and he brought the sword up out of the low guard for the first time that morning.

Haruki's frame shifted as she raised her sword, letting the light emit off it as the dark particles of the void began to convert and dissolute, the energy produced from the monster's presence had begun to siphon into purified light, the weight produced off Haruki's frame began to weigh down the battlefield as Sodoku, Sonzai and Kujo could feel her presence expanding over the field.

"Almighty True Judger... blessed be my blade... at let it strike true to all who dares questions your law... " The blade was held out as the Saishiki imbued around her, the ritual forming as the light surrounded her, the magnetic current around her began to shift as the space began to fold and bend. Her aether began to expand outwardly as it was conjoined into the flow of her blade, the field expanding as she shifted her footing to adjust her stance from he previous assault.

Haruki let the world around her slow down, her senses sharpening as the harmony flooded her frame, the silence, the soft subtle movements of each muscle, the way a monster roars when it is hurt or in pain. Within moments Haruki allowed her body to take over, the movements were swift, quick, her blade flourished as the monsters clawed and surrounded her.

The corpses flew back with each blow, the Saishiki causing them to explode into refined purity, their void-like figures combusting and collapsing underneath the pure light of her blade.

Kujo felt the light shine over his frame as the the field continued to expand, the way the void stabilized and grew into something that couldn't be wielded properly. The philosophy of something being out of control had shifted as the void began to lose it's control from Kujo. The darkness around his figure burning around him just the same as any of the monsters around Khraven. 

With a swift slash from her blade, Haruki released out the Saishiki, slaying the monsters each individually as their cries came out in unison. A choir built through their collective exorcism as the field-burst into collective purified dust, the darkness and light both faded at the same time, leaving both sides at a balance and yet disadvantageous state. Haruki remained as she caught her breath, her arms sore and weak. Her blade was lowered as she looked over at Sodoku and Sonzai.

"WHAT ARE YOU WAITING FOR?! DO IT NOW!" She said, her eyes showing how much her adrenaline had affected her during that moment. Her body felt as if it were in overdrive, but in the same time it felt almost... amazing.

Kujo did nothing when Haruki’s field reached him. The Saishiki washed across Xevorith in brilliant sheets, burning through the black around his shoulders while purified light stripped Void particles from the air and turned the creatures surrounding Kharven into drifting ash. His body remained motionless beneath it, pale face illuminated by the radiance as if he had deliberately stepped into the center of an execution. He even allowed the purification to finish.

The black mantle tore apart around him in strips. Sections of Kujo’s body became visible beneath the collapsing Xevorith, light carving through the darkness while the Crevice beneath his sternum briefly showed as a black-gold fracture. For several seconds, Haruki’s working appeared completely successful, the Void receding from Kujo just as it had from every lesser creature caught within the field. Kujo merely watched her breathe afterward.

Then the gold eyes opened.

One after another, enormous golden circles appeared inside the blackness remaining around Kujo, each containing a narrow pupil fixed not upon Haruki’s present position but somewhere behind it. Some stared seconds backward, others minutes, and several looked considerably farther into the past, Xevorith revealing itself as something that did not perceive history as a road traveled once. Kujo’s own eyes remained fixed upon Haruki while the eyes surrounding him searched for something he already knew was there.

“There.”

The word was followed by a quiet laugh as one golden eye widened more than the others. Inside it existed another Haruki, another battlefield, another moment, the prior encounter when Kujo had wrapped his hand around her throat and dragged her into darkness. He was not creating a new event or imagining an alternate history. He had found an actual relationship between them that the past had already proven.

Kujo allowed Haruki’s attack to become finished.

That mattered.

The purification had struck him, burned Xevorith, disrupted the darkness, and completed every meaningful consequence it could impose upon his present state. Once the attack became a completed sequence rather than an active restraint continuing to bind him, the golden eyes surrounding Kujo contracted simultaneously.

“The Unwinding.”

History slipped.

The world did not reverse, and Haruki’s monsters remained purified around them as proof that her attack had happened. Sonzai and Sodoku could still remember the light, the ruined field still bore its effects, and Haruki’s exhausted muscles still carried the cost of producing it. Only Kujo’s relationship to the completed sequence became negotiable.

The burning vanished from him.

Black returned across Kujo’s body without regeneration, flowing upward from nowhere until Xevorith once again covered everything beneath his neck in seamless absence. The purified sequence remained real everywhere else, but its successful claim upon Kujo’s present was gone. Her attack had happened; it simply no longer explained what Kujo was now.

Kujo smiled.

“You still misunderstand.”

The golden eyes began moving independently around him, several turning toward Haruki while others remained fixed backward through history. Kujo lifted one hand slightly, but he did not reach across the present distance separating them. Instead, the largest eye behind him remained focused upon that older moment when his fingers had already been wrapped around Haruki’s throat.

“I do not need to cross the space between us again. I have already held your neck.”

His fingers closed around empty air.

Xevorith did something considerably stranger than moving him.

Kujo reached into the sequence between that past contact and the present and removed the part that said the relationship had successfully ended. He did not erase everything that happened afterward, nor did he pull Haruki physically backward through time; the intervening history remained intact for everyone who had lived it. What disappeared was the causal certainty that Kujo’s hand had ever ceased being connected to her after that earlier grasp.

Reality corrected itself.

Kujo was suddenly behind Haruki.

There had been no flash, no rush of black, no transition across the battlefield, and no moment in which his body traveled through the distance separating them. One instant Haruki stood calling for Sonzai and Sodoku to strike, and the next Kujo’s hand was already around her neck exactly where the old sequence had once placed it. The present had been forced to inherit a relationship from the past because Xevorith had removed the history that declared it finished.

His fingers rested firmly against her throat.

The golden eyes surrounding Kujo stared backward while his own gaze remained entirely present. Haruki could feel the distinction immediately; this did not resemble teleportation because there was no arrival to perceive. It felt as though reality had suddenly remembered that Kujo had been holding her all along.

“That was beautiful, Haruki.”

His voice remained almost gentle beside her ear. The compliment carried no sarcasm, because Kujo had genuinely appreciated the scale of the Saishiki and the precision with which she had purified an entire portion of the battlefield. What amused him was the assumption that completing such an attack meant its victory was allowed to remain attached to him afterward.

“But it is insufficient.”

Kujo’s grip tightened by a fraction.

“Insufficient in the present because once your strike finishes, I may ask whether its consequence still deserves to belong to me. Insufficient in the past because I can find something you already survived and renegotiate whether it ever stopped touching you. And if your answer is simply to become stronger tomorrow, Haruki, then you have misunderstood the problem entirely.”

The golden eyes widened.

Some no longer looked behind them.

They looked along unfinished possibilities, not truly seeing a fixed future but examining the causal roads Haruki’s present actions were attempting to create. Kujo could not simply erase events that had never occurred, but he could already see the arrogance in believing future power automatically solved a relationship with something for whom completed history itself was negotiable.

“So no.”

His smile widened beside her.

“This attack is not sufficient now. It was not sufficient when you first learned to oppose me. And merely making it larger will not make it sufficient in whatever future you imagine saving you.”

Xevorith rose around them like a black tide filled with golden eyes. Each pupil stared into a different completed moment, searching through Haruki’s history for relationships, injuries, choices, releases, and separations that had already ceased exerting active claims upon Kujo’s present. For the first time, the true terror of the Unwinding was not that Kujo could survive what had already happened to him.

It was that he could make something that had already ended happen now.

Kujo lowered his face nearer to Haruki’s.

“You keep preparing better attacks.”

His grip remained steady.

“I keep acquiring more past.”

He got the chain over his head with two fingers and a thumb because the other two would not close.

Ash in his mouth. The cold of the ice-field on the ruined side of his face where the eyebrow had opened again, and under both of those the flat animal smell of a road that had two thousand men on it and had been at this since the ninth hour. His ribs on the left side had been broken for four hours and had settled into a thing he could work around by breathing shallow and not turning.

The amulet came out from under the collar and hung.

A scale. Two pans on a beam, worked in silver gone black in the recesses, no bigger than a thumbnail, and around the rim and across the face of both pans the glyph-work ran continuous and unbroken, the Urion hand, the old one, the version the Bench stopped teaching in the year Charles was born because it was too easy to get wrong. Eleven glyphs to the inch. He had counted them once, in a cold room, on the night they gave it to him, because counting was what he did with his hands when he was frightened.

Ten years ago.

They had done it in the lower chapel at Altherion with four men present and the door barred, and it had taken eleven minutes, and nobody had said a word that was not in the form. Aurelian had put the chain over his head. Darius Ignatius Sanctus Sanctorum had stood on the other side of the table with his hands behind his back and had not looked at him once, and had signed second, and afterward the two of them had been walked out of that chapel through different doors and had not been in a room together since.

The office is not held by a man. It is held between two, and it is called the God Hand in the family literature and in nothing else, and Charles had never learned why, and had asked once and been told that the reason was in the sealed part.

One to carry and one to spend. That much he had worked out for himself inside a year, from the shape of the form and from which of them had signed first. The carrier does not know when. The spender does not know where. Neither is told the other's condition, ever, on the grounds that a man who can check on his other half will eventually check instead of act.

The other half of it had been in the ash at Sum-gol for six weeks. He knew that the way he knew the weather.

*I have carried this for ten years and I have never once been able to tell my wife what it was, and she is dead, and I have never once been able to tell my son, and he is four months old and asleep on a keel somewhere north of here with a woman I paid.*

*Aurelian said it would be a thing I would know when to do. He said it in that chapel with his hand still on the chain and he was afraid when he said it, and he was afraid every day of his life, and this morning he stood four yards away and watched a man knee me in the stomach and found it interesting.*

*So there is nobody left to ask.*

He looked up the road.

Four hundred paces of it, and the ice, and the ridges, and at the far end of it the black with the gold in it, and inside that a shape with its hand somewhere it should not have been.

He could not see the eyes. He knew they were there and he could not perceive them and had never been able to perceive anything of that order, and that was the whole of Charles Lambert, fifty-three assessments deep in the Accord's file at the bottom of the register, no output, no faculty, no working worth the name.

Which was the reason.

Thirty-eight years in the Church and the only thing about himself he had ever been proud of was the handwriting.

*They did not give it to a practitioner. Everyone at that table understood why and nobody said it out loud. A man with a faculty is a man the register has a great deal of information about, and information is the thing this destroys, and it would have taken him apart before he finished the second line.*

*They gave it to the emptiest man in the building.*

He put his thumb on the beam of the little scale and pressed until it turned.

The first thing was that he could see.

Nothing of the eyes and nothing of the black. The road, and everything on the road, and everything that had not happened on the road, all of it arriving with the same weight and the same solidity and the same claim on being the case.

The Imperceptible District. That was the Bench's term for it and Charles had filed correspondence about it for nineteen years without ever once understanding that it was a place. Everything the register cannot weigh, because nothing witnessed it, because it left no mark, because it did not come out. The road not taken and the blow not landed and the word not said. Filed under the same heading and kept, and the Church's position for four hundred years had been that the District is real and cannot be entered and that the two facts together are the reason the Scale is the Church's sign.

It came up into his hand and became as real as the mud.

Every man on that road, and beside him the eleven of him that had gone the other way at some point in the last four hours, all standing, all as solid as each other. Wren Greymane on the ditch bank, dead, and beside him Wren Greymane not dead, and Wren Greymane never a traitor at all, and every one of them with the same claim. The ridge that came up under the Stormfold road at the fourth mile and the ridge that did not. Kasumi in a courtyard at Vaeloris in the fourth hour of the ninth of Tenth-month, and Kasumi walking out of that courtyard, and Charles Lambert did not look at that one and did not permit himself to look at that one and knew that he was going to look at it before the end.

The whole world stopped being one thing.

*This is what it is. Nobody told me and I understand it now with the thing in my hand and I have about a minute.*

*A thing is only definite because the world kept a record of it. That is all of it. Nothing about it happened harder than the others. It left marks in enough places that the alternatives can no longer reach each other, and once they cannot reach each other, one of them is the case and the rest are gone.*

*And that creature at the end of the road works on the record. It finds a completed thing and asks whether the completion still belongs to it. It needs the marks. Everything it has ever done, it has done to a finished sequence.*

*So do not fight the finished sequence.*

*Take the record away and there is nothing finished anywhere for it to hold.*

There would be no second attempt. That was in the form too, and it was the only sentence in the whole eleven minutes that had been spoken in the common tongue, and it had been spoken by Darius, and it was four words long.

He walked forward eleven paces and stopped in the middle of the road with his ribs grinding and the scale turning in his fist and the beam of it hot enough now to be a difficulty.

He began.

"Creatura infecta."

Unfinished creature.

The word went out and the ice took it. He said it in the Church Latin he had used at eleven hundred offices of the dead, in the reading voice, because it was the only voice he had ever used for anything that mattered, and the road under him began to lose its edges.

"Te vinciat Planities Fati. Te liget."

Let the Plane of Fate shackle you. Let it bind you.

"Arcus graduum tibi parcant."

Let the arcs of degrees spare you.

And then the two that were the working, which Charles Lambert had known by heart for ten years and had said aloud exactly once, in a cold chapel, with his hand shaking.

"Sit omne quod fieri potuit."

Let everything that could have happened, be.

"Nihil perficiatur."

Let nothing be completed.

The cold hand came out of him.

That was the honest description and there was no other. It came out through the front of his chest and it was not his and it had never been his, and the arm of it went up the road four hundred paces in the length of one heartbeat, and it took hold.

Charles could not see what it took hold of. He could see the effect: an arm somewhere at the far end of the narrows going upright, wrist first, lifted the way an official lifts a man's hand off a document he is not entitled to sign.

And there was a figure standing at the end of the arm, and it had not walked there, and the ice under it was undisturbed.

White. A white that was not light and did not illuminate the ice or the mud or anything else, standing in a corona that moved on its own account and hid everything inside it, so that the shape had a height and a set of shoulders and an outline and nothing else at all, and Charles Lambert, who had been in the Church for thirty-eight years and had heard every account, looked directly at it and could not have said afterward whether it had a face.

*I did not summon that. I opened a door and something that was already standing behind it stepped out, and the four men in that chapel knew and did not tell me, and I would have taken the chain anyway.*

The Void near it came apart.

It unspooled. That was the only word for what it did: the black at the near edge of it stopped being a mass and became a length, and the length ran out and out and went on running, unwinding from the far end of the narrows in a line that got thinner as it went and did not stop getting thinner.

And time fractured.

Charles felt it in the ribs first, which was absurd and was also the truth: the broken ends of two ribs on his left side grated in four different ways in the same instant, because there were four different ways he had been standing, and all four had become the case. Around him the road did the same. Every unfinished possibility in the Ashgate valley came up off the floor of the District together and broke against the ones that had been standing on top of them, and what had been a fixed thing with one outcome became a relative thing with a great many, and none of them completed.

Somewhere up the road the black with the gold in it was made to twist, and the twisting came from the Plane of Fate directly and had nothing local in it at all, and Charles could not see that either and knew it from the way the ice went.

Down on the road to his right, at fifty feet, a man in Moto harness with a sword low along his right leg went down onto both knees in the ice and stayed there.

*Good.*

*Stay down. Whatever you are, whatever you have become in the last hour, stay on the ground until this stops, because I do not think that a man standing upright inside this keeps the shape he came in with.*

The scale had gone through the skin of his palm.

He did not remember when. It was in the meat of his hand now, both pans and the beam, and it was drawing, and what it was drawing was the part of him the register held.

He understood the price about four seconds before it finished, which was three and a half seconds later than he would have liked and exactly as long as the four men in that chapel had intended.

Records. That was the currency and it had always been the currency and it was written in the sealed part, and to take the marks off the world you have to put marks in, and the only marks available were the ones that said Charles Lambert had been here.

It took the vestry minutes first. He felt nineteen years of them go, every meeting he had ever taken down in a hand somebody would have recognised. Then the ordination roll. Then the marriage line in the parish book at Vaeloris, and the courtyard report, and the four hundred readings of it, and then the report itself, and Charles Lambert made a sound he had never made in his life and let it go and let it go and let it all go.

Kasumi went last of the writing.

The record of her, which was all the thing in his hand could reach. What was left afterward was the thing that had been under the record the whole time, unfiled, unweighed, unentered anywhere, in the District where it had always actually lived, and it was intact.

He had that for about a second and a half and it was enough.

He got his mouth open.

What came out of it was not entirely his voice. It came out doubled, layered under itself, his own and a second one over the top that was lower and drier and belonged to a man standing in ash eleven hundred miles away who had spent six weeks learning how the Works stand and had just been handed the trial he was not told about in advance.

"What did I tell you, kid."

It went up the narrows. It went past the ice and the ridges and the black and it did not go to any of them.

"You are not allowed to die on my watch."

Haruki paused for a moment, she felt something she hasn't felt before... the slight tugging against the threads. The deviation, she could feel the disturbance before it had fully set itself into stone. The fate had shifted much differently, the thread tracing to now as Kujo stood behind her, his hand around her neck. The harmonic pulse shifted as she focused. "So that is your strategy... hm... I see... that is interesting."

She said, her Musuhi and Danshi looked back at Kujo, it was in that moment her eyes began to shift as it experienced a moment where she could see it, just for a mere moment. The threads that Kujo inspected, traced, and even grappled. Then with her hand she shifted her sword, plunging into his center mass.

"You underestimate me, as you did before." She said, looking at Kujo, both of them caught in each other's locks. Kujo's hand around her neck, and Haruki's Blade connected through his torso. "I never needed you dead... I only needed your focus wavered, because when your focus wavered, that is where chaos had opened. And that is where I have calculated... and what I have observed." She said, her blade twisting as her eyes laid onto him, she kept pressure despite the adrenaline flowing through her veins.

"You may pull from multiple pasts, but I can see them all before they are acted. Just as how you explained it..." She said as her aether spread through his frame, locking him as the sensation began to burn, the binding wrapped around him as she smirked. "Do you see it now? My goal was never to harm you, only to weaken you, for them." She said, looking back at Sonzai and Sodoku, a smirk growing on her face.

"Do try to keep up with them, they won't be as forgiving as I am." She whispered to him, the bravery shining through along with her bravado. Her footing shifted, her face had begun to turn a red color from the grip Kujo had on her, but was lessened by the fact of the blade having been stabbed through him. The harmony remained strong, but her-will much stronger.

Kujo did not move when Haruki’s blade entered his center mass. The steel passed through Xevorith, flesh, and whatever deeper structure she had successfully reached, while her Aether spread through the wound and began establishing its lock. His hand remained around her throat, and for several seconds he simply looked down at the weapon buried through him as though she had placed an interesting instrument on a table.

Then the golden eyes opened across the black surrounding him.

Dozens at first, then hundreds, each yellow-gold iris staring into a different completed interval of the world behind them. Haruki’s Musuhi and Danshi could perceive those histories now, could watch Kujo selecting sequences and trace the relationships he intended to exploit. Kujo noticed her noticing them, and the realization made his mouth curl upward.

“So you can see where I am looking. Very good, Haruki. You have finally learned to observe the hand before it reaches into history.”

His fingers tightened slightly around her neck, though not enough to interrupt her concentration yet. The blade remained inside him, the burning restraint remained active, and Kujo made no foolish attempt to Unwind either while they were still maintaining a current causal claim upon his body. He understood his own limitation better than anyone standing before him.

“But you have mistaken observation for authority.”

The golden eyes around them shifted.

Haruki saw the histories clearly for one moment longer, the paths Kujo had touched lying before her perception like illuminated threads extending from past action into present consequence. Then those threads began blackening from their oldest portions forward, not disappearing from history but losing the capacity to tell her what Kujo’s present was obligated to become. Xevorith spread through the relationships between occurrence and consequence like ink poured through water.

“What you see in the past is true.”

Kujo smiled.

“It happened.”

His gaze lowered toward her.

“And it means nothing.”

The first golden eye closed.

A sequence Haruki had been watching remained completely intact behind it: Kujo moved, Kujo acted, Kujo chose, Kujo created a consequence. Yet the moment that history ceased maintaining an active claim upon his present state, Xevorith classified its authority as exhausted. The event remained available to memory and observation, but the present no longer owed it obedience.

Another eye closed. Then another. Then twenty at once.

“You think because you can see every road I have walked, you can calculate where those roads must bring me.”

Kujo’s voice softened.

“But I do not permit roads to own their destinations anymore.”

Haruki’s perception of his histories began becoming increasingly useless even while remaining perfectly accurate. She could see Kujo grasping her throat in an earlier encounter, see him using Xevorith against completed injuries, see him opening distances through absence, and see every previous application she had managed to identify. What vanished was the certainty that any of those witnessed precedents imposed a reliable rule upon the Kujo standing in front of her now.

“Memory is not causality.”

His smile widened.

“Observation is not causality. Prediction is merely faith dressed in mathematics.”

The black around Kujo’s body deepened while the golden eyes continued staring backward. Some observed seconds ago, others hours, days, months, and stranger intervals where sequence had become difficult to distinguish from location. Haruki could still perceive them all, but each past she touched reached the same terminal conclusion when carried toward the present.

**VOID.**

**NULL.**

Not erased. Not forgotten. Simply stripped of jurisdiction.

“You may inspect every past I have ever permitted myself to possess.”

Kujo tilted his head.

“Catalogue it. Compare it. Build ten thousand strategies around it.”

His fingers pressed more firmly against Haruki’s throat.

“But when that past arrives here, I decide whether it still has standing.”

The words carried into Xevorith itself.

The great golden eyes surrounding Kujo narrowed simultaneously, and the histories behind them seemed to terminate at the invisible boundary of the present rather than flowing naturally through it. Past action approached Kujo’s current existence and encountered something like a metaphysical court that accepted no precedent merely because precedent existed.

“All that has completed and no longer actively sustains me…” The blackness rippled. “…is void.”

Kujo’s gaze sharpened.

“All that has lost its present claim…” The golden eyes contracted into narrow slits. “…is null.”

Haruki’s blade remained inside him.

Kujo deliberately looked down at it again.

“This, however…” His smile became amused. “…is still happening.”

He gave the blade the respect of acknowledging exactly why he could not yet Unwind it. Her Aether was actively binding him, her steel remained embedded through his torso, and the relationship between weapon and wound still possessed present causal dependency. Kujo did not pretend otherwise, because a power without limits was merely another form of stupidity.

“You have done better than before.”

There was genuine approval in his voice.

“You created something that presently matters.”

Then Kujo laughed.

“That is why I am allowing you to enjoy it.”

The Crevice pulsed beneath the wound.

Gold-black fracture-light spread around the blade, not erasing Haruki’s current binding but beginning to pressure the structure from within while Kujo’s body remained caught inside it. The Regent’s attention never left Haruki, because he knew the truly important moment would come when she withdrew, when the lock broke, when the strike became finished enough to belong wholly to history.

“And when you are done…”

The golden eyes opened wider.

“When your blade leaves my body, when your restraint ceases maintaining itself, when this clever little moment finally becomes something that happened rather than something happening…”

His grin broadened.

“I will ask whether it deserves to have happened to me.”

The meaning settled between them.

Haruki could read the past.

Kujo could invalidate its authority.

Her advantage gave her awareness of the sequence, but awareness could not force that sequence to remain connected to Kujo once its active consequences ended. She had improved her ability to understand the mechanism without yet solving the mechanism itself.

“So yes, Haruki.”

Kujo leaned nearer despite the blade through him.

“Watch every past.”

The hand around her throat tightened another fraction.

“See them all.”

The surrounding golden eyes burned like stars embedded inside an endless black sea.

“Because when they reach the present…” Kujo’s smile vanished. “…all is void.”

Xevorith swallowed the light around his shoulders.

“All is null.”

He was still on his side in the ice when he worked out how it was being done.

Four hundred paces was too far to see anything and he did not need to see it. What the amulet had put into his hand was not sight. It was the register with the covers off, the whole of the Ashgate valley laid open in its filing, and from down there in the mud he could read the transaction the way he had read eleven hundred parish returns.

The grip up the road had not closed on a person.

*He has taken hold of a thread.*

*Something that is her and is also no part of her, and both of those true at once, and that is not a trick of language, it is the actual condition of the thing he is holding.*

*A person on that Plane is not an object. It is a bundle. Thousands of the small fluctuations running together, each one unique and each one a nothing on its own, and what we call a woman standing on a road is the place where enough of them keep company that the world starts filing them under one name.*

*So he does not need her. He needs one strand out of the bundle. He grasps it there, where it is a thread, and construes it wrongly out here, where it is a neck. Grasp it on the Plane, misread it outward into the physical, and the physical has to accept the reading.*

*Endless. That is the horror of the design. Every thread in a bundle is a separate handle and there is no end to them, and he does not have to find the right one, because there is no right one. Any of them will do. That is why nothing anyone has done to him today has cost him a single step.*

Charles Lambert lay in the ice with his ribs grinding and understood that there was exactly one way to contort a thing like that, and that it did not run through authority and it did not run through control.

Everything he had ever been taught said the opposite. The Bench weighs. The Court rules. The Church holds the Scale because the Scale decides. Thirty-eight years of correspondence in which the answer to every question was somebody with standing making a determination, and the creature at the end of that road had built its entire nature out of the same assumption and had simply appointed itself the court.

*You cannot out-rule him. There is no bench above his bench. He has already taken the seat and there is no appeal and there is no other appeal to be had, and if there were, he would take that seat too.*

*So do not appeal. Give the court more cases than it can hear.*

He had spent nineteen years watching the Bench at Altherion do exactly that to petitioners it disliked, and had drafted the letters, and had been ashamed of it every time. There is no ruling against a bench. There is only a docket long enough that the bench stops being a bench and becomes a room with paper in it.

The paradox came up out of the District and he had never once in his life been able to hold it and he held it now.

Everything came from nothing. That was the Church's line and it was in the fourth clause and Charles had read it aloud over open ground at eleven hundred burials.

And nothing had come from something. That was the part the fourth clause did not print. There was no empty anywhere. What the register called the void had never been an absence, because when he looked at it now with the Scale burned into his palm he could see it seething, the whole of it, everywhere, at every scale, the small unique fluctuations arriving and paying themselves back and arriving again in numbers that had no top, and that was the floor of existence and that was what the threads were made of.

*The void is not empty. It has never been empty. It is full, and what it is full of is possibility that has not been asked to decide yet.*

*He calls himself the Void and he is standing on a thing he has misfiled.*

Golden light came up out of Charles Lambert's chest and went along his arms.

It came through the skin. It had no source anywhere and no aim, and there was no heat in it that a hand would have recognised as heat, and the ice under him began to go regardless. It ran the length of both forearms and across the backs of the hands and out along every finger including the two that would not close, and where it ran it was not smooth.

It was banded.

Light and dark and light again, in fringes, laid across his own flesh in stripes the width of a hair, moving as he moved, and Charles looked down the length of his own arm and saw the pattern and understood what he was looking at with the last clear piece of his mind.

*Interference.*

*Two roads arriving at the same place and adding, and cancelling, and adding, and that only ever happens when nothing anywhere has kept a record of which road was taken.*

*The fringes are on me because there is no longer any record of me.*

*Nothing in the world can say which way I came. So both ways are still going, and where they land on the same inch of skin they add or they cancel, and that is a stripe, and I have them from the collarbone down.*

*I paid the whole file this morning and this is the receipt.*

Under the bands, his own arm looked wrong to him, and it took a moment to find why. There was no shadow. The gold went through the flesh instead of over it, and the hair on the forearm and the tendon under it and the vein along the inside were all lit from within at the same intensity, and a thing lit from within throws nothing.

He got his good hand under him and got up.

His ribs went and he let them go and stood on the frozen mud of the Ashgate road, fifty-three years old, an archbishop with no faculty and no working and no entry in any book in four worlds, gleaming in bands from the collarbone to the fingertips.

The question arrived while he was standing there, and it was an old one, and it had been sitting in him for ten years unanswered under everything else.

What is the Hand for.

The Scale weighs and the Bench reads the pan and the Court speaks, and all of that is machinery for deciding, and none of it had ever needed a hand. A hand belongs to the other half of the vocabulary. Holding, and taking, and doing.

Hand of Judgement, then. He had turned that over for a decade in cold rooms. Did it mean the killing of every man who had earned it. He had a list. He had built it without meaning to over nineteen years of correspondence and there were names on it he could have recited in his sleep, and the man who signed the order at Vaeloris was fourth.

Or did it mean the saving. Every soul that needed it, pulled out of the water. He had wanted that to be the answer since the day they put the chain on him, and had never been able to make it fit, because a hand that only saves is a hand that has decided who is worth the reach, and that is a verdict wearing gloves.

He stood in the ice with the fringes running on his skin and got it.

*Neither. Both of those are the Court's work in a different coat and I have been trying for ten years to make the Hand into a small Bench.*

*A hand holds.*

*That is the entire office. It does not weigh and it does not choose and it does not pass anything. It closes on a thing and it keeps that thing in place while somebody else does the work, and it takes whatever comes for as long as it can, and it is not required to be right.*

*I have been asking what I am permitted to do to other people. The answer was always what I am permitted to hold.*

He thought, briefly, of Dabney, and set that down, because a hand that is thinking about what it wants to hold has already dropped something.

He looked up the road and found the thread.

It came out of the bundle four hundred paces away and ran back down the narrows and there was a grip on it, and Charles Lambert could see the grip, and he could see that the thing doing the gripping had made one small assumption in order to hold on at all.

It had assumed the thread came with a name attached.

That is how a misconstrual works. You take hold on the Plane where the thing is only a fluctuation, and you carry it out here, and out here it has to arrive as somebody, and which somebody it arrives as is decided by the register.

The register no longer had an opinion about Charles Lambert.

No parish book, no ordination roll, no gate-roll, no minute, no report, no line anywhere in four worlds that said where he had been standing or that he had been standing at all. He was the only object on that field with nothing filed against him, and a thing with no file cannot be contradicted.

It occurred to him, in the part of him that was still an administrator, that this was the same operation as a substitution in a parish register, and that he had signed off on eleven of them in his career, and that all eleven had been fraud.

He reached out and put himself in the way.

There was no working in it and no chant and nothing that would have shown on any instrument. He simply offered the register the emptiest thing on the road at the moment it was deciding who was being held, and the register, having nothing to say against it, said nothing.

The world corrected.
